# Decolonisation of Africa and Asia - Live Learning Session

> **What this session teaches:** It teaches how, in roughly thirty years, the largest system of foreign rule the world has ever seen was dismantled - and why the dismantling produced sovereign states that were nevertheless not free to do very much. It begins from the position in 1945, when the nations of Europe still claimed ownership of vast areas of Asia and Africa, and it sets out exactly what each of the seven imperial powers held and in what legal form, because the difference between a colony, a protectorate, a settler territory and a mandate decides almost everything about how each one ended. It teaches the causation question as a genuine historical dispute rather than a list: the metropolitan thesis associated with Robert Holland, the counter-argument that in some cases the imperial power was simply expelled by force, and the reason the honest answer is that all these factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories. It teaches the five mechanisms by which the Second World War accelerated everything - the destruction of European military prestige by Japan, the guerrilla capacity that anti-Japanese resistance left behind, the political awakening of some 374,000 Africans recruited into the British armed forces, the wartime promises including the Atlantic Charter of 1941 and Churchill's later restriction of it, and the simple exhaustion of states that could no longer afford what Bernard Porter called a cheapskate empire. It teaches nationalism as a social phenomenon rather than a mood: where its leaders came from, why education in Britain and the United States produced simultaneously the vocabulary and the grievance, what the press, the party and the general strike actually did, and why the new towns mattered more than the countryside in West Africa. It teaches Pan-Africanism from Marcus Garvey and W. E. B. Du Bois through Nkrumah's federal project to its defeat at Addis Ababa in 1963 and the much weaker Organization of African Unity that replaced it; and it teaches Bandung in April 1955 and the Belgrade summit of September 1961 as the Afro-Asian and non-aligned expression of the same claim to agency. It teaches the outside pressures honestly, including the American market interest that sat behind American anti-imperialism, the Soviet denunciations, and the United Nations instrument of December 1960 that converted anti-colonialism into a standing institutional programme. It then teaches the routes, case by case, with the settler variable as the organising discriminator: the Gold Coast to Ghana in 1957 with its five years of supervised self-government that the account calls unique; Nigeria's careful federal preparation and the war it did not prevent; Kenya's land question, the Mau Mau uprising, the Hola camp and the figures that only became public in 2005; the Central African Federation of 1953-63 built to entrench a white minority and dissolved when its own African territories walked out; Southern Rhodesia from the unilateral declaration of November 1965 through sanctions that leaked to the Lancaster House settlement of 1979 and independence in April 1980; French Indo-China to Dien Bien Phu and Geneva in 1954; Tunisia and Morocco in 1956; Algeria from 1954 to July 1962, the war that destroyed the Fourth Republic and nearly produced a French civil war; de Gaulle's referendum of 1958, Guinea's ninety-five per cent refusal, and the neo-colonial architecture designed in advance for the eleven territories that said yes; Indonesia from Sukarno's declaration of 1945 to the Dutch recognition of 1949, West Irian in 1963 and the coup of 1965; Malaya's plural-society route through the Emergency of 1948-60, the Alliance, independence in 1957, the Federation of Malaysia in September 1963 and Singapore's separation in 1965; the Congo's six months' notice, its seventeen graduates, Katanga, Lumumba's murder and Mobutu; Portugal's three simultaneous wars consuming forty per cent of its budget by 1973, the coup of 1974 and the independences of 1974 and 1975; East Timor as the one case where decolonisation was reversed by an invasion; Spanish Sahara as the case where it was never completed; and the Italian territories and the Middle Eastern mandates, taken only to the boundary this topic owns. It teaches apartheid South Africa at length and in two parts, because it is the fullest available case of a legally constructed racial order, of its internal economic contradiction, of resistance changing form under repression, of international pressure that was loud long before it was effective, and of a negotiated transition to majority rule without civil war. It teaches the aftermath structurally - arbitrary frontiers, single-commodity economies, thin administrative classes, armies as the best-organised institution, debt and structural adjustment, and Cold War intervention - and then tests the structure against six named cases that each isolate a different mechanism. It closes with neo-colonialism as a concept rather than a complaint, the full verdict debate from Brendon, Orwell, Russell, Gott and Wood on one side to Ferguson on the other, and the answer architecture that converts thirty years and four continents into marks. Four boundaries are held throughout and stated in the lessons: India's transfer of power and partition belong to Modern Indian History and appear here only as the causation debate and the demonstration effect; the Cold War as a system belongs to the Cold War topic and appears here only where it entered a decolonising society; the communist regimes of China, Korea and Indochina belong to the topic on Asian communism; and the conquest that produced these empires, together with the drawing of the frontiers, belongs to the topic on the New Imperialism.

## How to read this session

Work through the lessons in order. The order is deliberately not the order of the chapters that supply most of the evidence. A chapter on the end of empire asks why the powers gave up, then works through Britain, then France, then the smaller empires, then delivers a verdict; a chapter on what followed then works through country after country. That arrangement teaches the facts and hides the argument. Here the causation question is opened in Lesson 3 as a dispute rather than closed as a list, because a script that recites five causes and stops will not get past the middle band, while a script that says which cause did what work, and in which colony, will. The social bases of nationalism come before any case study, because almost every later divergence is explained by who was mobilised and by what instrument. Pan-Africanism, Bandung and the United Nations instrument come next, so that the reader meets the colonised world as an actor with a project rather than as a set of territories to which things happened.

The cases are then arranged by route rather than by empire. This is the single most important structural decision in the session. Grouping by empire teaches that the British left peacefully and the French and Portuguese fought, which is false in both directions - Britain fought in Kenya and Malaya, and France left Tunisia and Morocco by negotiation in the same two years it was escalating in Algeria. Grouping by route teaches the variable. West Africa comes first because it is the clean case with the fewest settlers; East and Central Africa follow because they add the settler factor in increasing strength; the French cases follow because they run the same variable through a different metropolitan politics; the Dutch, Belgian, Portuguese and Spanish cases follow because they show what happens when a metropole has neither the strength to hold nor the resources to build an influence that would survive letting go.

Apartheid South Africa is given two lessons rather than one and placed after all the transfers, because it is not a transfer at all. It is the case where the settler population won, wrote its victory into statute, and then had to be dismantled from inside over forty-six years. Read in that position it answers a question the transfers cannot: what the colonial relation looks like when the coloniser does not go home.

The aftermath is taught last among the substantive lessons, and it is taught structurally before it is taught case by case, because the commonest failure in this topic is to explain post-colonial difficulty by the character of the people who inherited the state. The structures came first; the choices made inside them came second; and the session insists on both.

Inside a lesson, take the visual first, then the plain explanation, then the exact evidence, then the objection and the reply, and only then the examination use.

Four labels run through this session.

| Label | Meaning | Use in an answer |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Fact** | Directly supported by a verified source - the study material held for this topic, the world-history textbook account read for this session, the neighbouring subjects consulted for boundaries, the verified question ledgers, or a fact checked once and listed with its source at the end of this file | Assert it. Dates, named individuals, institutions, recorded policies and stated outcomes can be written flatly |
| ⚠️ **Analytical inference** | Interpretation, weighting, comparison or synthesis built on those facts | Argue it, signal it, and expect a counter-position |
| 📰 **Current linkage** | A dated official item, used exactly once in this session and limited there in the text itself | One dated sentence at most, and never as the substance of a history answer |
| **Attributed estimate** | A figure for deaths, killings, detentions or displacement, always carried with the name of whoever produced it and the period it covers | Attribute it in the sentence. Never state such a figure as an unowned fact |

That fourth label has no symbol because it must be visible in the sentence itself. ⚠️ This topic contains more contested mortality evidence than almost any other part of the syllabus - Kenya, Algeria, Indonesia, East Timor, Nigeria, the Congo, Angola, Rwanda and Burundi all carry figures that were produced under different conditions by different people counting different things. An answer that writes "the British killed a hundred thousand Kenyans" has made a claim no careful source makes in that form; an answer that writes "David Anderson and Caroline Elkins, whose work in 2005 first made the scale public, record more than a thousand Kikuyu hanged, some twenty thousand killed in combat and up to a hundred thousand deaths in detention camps during the Emergency" has made a checkable statement and has demonstrated exactly the historiographical control that a twenty-mark question rewards.

Seven disciplines are specific to this topic. Each exists because this is a part of the syllabus where moral feeling is strong, vocabulary is loose, and precision is rare.

**The two-question discipline.** ⚠️ Examiners on this topic ask two different questions and candidates routinely answer the wrong one. The first is *why empire ended*. The second is *why independence did not by itself produce stable, prosperous or genuinely self-determining states*. They have different answers, different evidence and different date ranges, and the second is not a verdict on the first. ✅ The textbook account is explicit that the problems which followed were post-colonial state-building crises and that many of their causes - artificial frontiers, single-commodity economies, a deliberate colonial policy of providing food and raw materials rather than industry, and an educated class kept deliberately small - were themselves colonial products. ❌ Never let the second question become an argument that the first should have been answered differently.

**The route discipline.** ⚠️ There is no single process called decolonisation. There are at least six recognisable routes, and the strongest single predictor of which route a territory took is not the identity of the coloniser but whether a substantial settler population with property claims stood between the metropole and withdrawal. ✅ The account itself organises British Africa on exactly this principle: relatively few Europeans in West Africa and those administrators rather than permanent settlers, so the move to independence was comparatively straightforward; a settler factor in East Africa, especially Kenya; and the settler factor at its most serious in Central Africa, especially Southern Rhodesia. ⚠️ Learn the variable, then the cases; not the cases, then a generalisation.

**The attribution discipline.** ⚠️ Every mortality, detention, refugee and displacement figure in this session travels with whoever produced it and the period it covers. ❌ No figure anywhere in this session is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated, combined across sources or converted from one bracket to another. ⚠️ Where the same account gives two incompatible figures for the same event - and it does, for East Timor - both are stated and the divergence is reported as itself a finding. ⚠️ Responsibility is attributed to named governments, parties, armies, militias and officials, never to a nation or a people.

**The terminology discipline.** ⚠️ The textbook account writes of "tribes" and "tribal differences", and it also notes in one place that these are the groups "as they now tend to be called" ethnic groups. This session uses *ethnic group* in its own analytical voice and preserves the source's own word only when reporting its formulation, because the vocabulary is not neutral: it encodes the assumption that African political divisions were primordial rather than made. ⚠️ The same care applies to the statutory racial categories of apartheid South Africa, which are reproduced as legal categories because that is exactly what they were, and not as descriptions.

**The boundary discipline.** Four neighbouring topics own material this session touches constantly, and the handovers are stated in the lessons rather than hidden. India's transfer of power, the negotiations, the partition and its violence belong to Modern Indian History; this session uses the Indian case only twice, once for the historiographical dispute over whether independence was granted or won, and once for its demonstration effect on Africa and on white South Africa. The Cold War as a system - containment, alliances, deterrence, the crises - belongs to the Cold War topic; this session teaches superpower intervention only at the point where it entered a decolonising or newly independent society. The communist regimes of China, Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos belong to the topic on Asian communism; this session teaches the anti-colonial war that produced Indochinese independence and stops at Geneva in July 1954. The conquest of these territories, the frontier-drawing of the late nineteenth century and the identity-fixing that colonial administration performed belong to the topic on the New Imperialism; this session inherits their consequences and says so.

**The date-and-status discipline.** ✅ The historical facts used here are stable: the Dutch recognised Indonesia in 1949, the Gold Coast became Ghana in 1957, the Congo became independent on 30 June 1960, Algeria in July 1962, the Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963, Angola and Mozambique became independent in 1975, Zimbabwe in April 1980, and South Africa's coalition government took office after the 1994 election. ❌ This session does **not** assert any current African or Asian economic indicator, growth rate, population figure, debt ratio, governing arrangement or diplomatic position, because none of those is stable and a history answer does not need them. ⚠️ Where a live institutional development is genuinely relevant, it appears exactly once, dated, in Lesson 6, and it is limited there in the text.

**The agency discipline.** ⚠️ The most damaging habit in answers on this topic is to write the colonised world as an object: Europe decided to leave, the superpowers competed, the new states failed. ✅ The evidence does not support it. ✅ Nkrumah's campaign used boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike; ✅ Azikiwe's general strike of 1945 was itself enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence; ✅ Guinea voted ninety-five per cent against de Gaulle's Community knowing that every franc of French aid would stop; ✅ Vietnamese nationalists had been campaigning against French rule since the 1920s; ✅ twenty-nine states met at Bandung in April 1955 to write their own principles of international conduct. ⚠️ Metropolitan calculation is real and must be argued, but it operated on a demand that colonised societies created.

## Roadmap

Twenty-four lessons. The count is set by the number of distinct routes and mechanisms the topic contains, not by a template, and the lessons are deliberately unequal: Lessons 15, 21 and 22 are long because Algeria and apartheid carry the densest evidence and the most contested judgements, while Lesson 12 on the Central African Federation is short because it teaches one mechanism cleanly and hands its consequence to the lesson that follows.

| # | Stage | Lesson | What it settles |
|---:|---|---|---|
| 1 | Foundation | The world of 1945: who held what, and in what legal form | Why the *form* of rule predicts the form of its ending |
| 2 | Foundation | The war as accelerant: five mechanisms, not one mood | What 1939-45 actually changed, and what it did not |
| 3 | Foundation | Who ended empire? The metropolitan thesis and its critics | How to argue causation instead of listing causes |
| 4 | Foundation | The social bases of nationalism: elite, press, strike, town | Why leadership and force came from different places |
| 5 | Foundation | Pan-Africanism: from Garvey and Du Bois to Addis Ababa 1963 | The project that failed, and the movements it built |
| 6 | Foundation | Outside pressures: Washington, Moscow and the United Nations | Anti-colonialism with interests attached |
| 7 | Foundation | Bandung 1955, Belgrade 1961 and Global South agency | The colonised world as author rather than object |
| 8 | Core | The route typology and the settler variable | The single discriminator that organises every case |
| 9 | Core | West Africa I - the Gold Coast to Ghana, 1947-57 | The best-prepared transfer in British Africa |
| 10 | Core | West Africa II - Nigeria, Sierra Leone and the Gambia | Why careful federal design did not hold |
| 11 | Core | East Africa - Kenya, Tanganyika and Uganda | Land, the settler factor and a counter-insurgency now known in full |
| 12 | Core | Central Africa - the Federation of 1953-63 | A union built to entrench a minority, dissolved by its own members |
| 13 | Core | Southern Rhodesia to Zimbabwe, 1965-80 | Sanctions that leaked, a war that worked, a question deferred |
| 14 | Core | The French empire I - Indo-China, Tunisia and Morocco | Three exits in three years, two of them negotiated |
| 15 | Core | The French empire II - Algeria, 1954-62 | The settler colony that broke a republic |
| 16 | Core | The French empire III - the 1958 referendum and after | Neo-colonialism designed before the flag came down |
| 17 | Core | The Netherlands and Indonesia, 1945-65 | A war the metropole could not afford, and what followed |
| 18 | Core | Malaya, Malaysia and the federation experiments | Plural society, insurgency and the successor-state question |
| 19 | Core | Belgium, the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi | Six months' notice and seventeen graduates |
| 20 | Core | Portugal, Spain, Italy and the unfinished cases | Late collapse, reversal at East Timor, and a referendum never held |
| 21 | Advanced | Apartheid I - building a racial order, 1910-1976 | A statutory system and the contradiction inside it |
| 22 | Advanced | Apartheid II - pressure, negotiation and after, 1976-94 | Why it ended, how, and what did not end with it |
| 23 | Advanced | After independence: five structures and six mechanisms | Inheritance, choice and the refusal of lazy explanation |
| 24 | Advanced | Neo-colonialism, the verdict debate and answer architecture | Converting thirty years and four continents into marks |

## Lesson 1 - The world of 1945: who held what, and in what legal form

Progress: 1/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the extent of the seven European empires in 1945; the difference between colony, protectorate, settler territory, mandate and overseas department; the Middle Eastern mandates and where they hand over; the Commonwealth as a designed continuity; and the thirty-year timetable by which most of it ended

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's opening summary for the chapter on the end of the European empires, listing the holdings of Britain, France, the Netherlands, Belgium, Portugal, Spain and Italy in 1945, the mandated territories held by Britain and France in the Middle East, and the complete year-by-year tables of the territories that became independent from each power
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in Lesson 6 and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson

### Visual: the seven empires in 1945, sorted by the form of rule they used

```text

   (largest in    enormous tracts of Africa,        + settler territory (Kenya,
    area)         Cyprus, Hong Kong, West Indies,   Rhodesias) + MANDATE
                  Falkland Islands, Gibraltar       (Transjordan, Palestine)
                  + Transjordan and Palestine

   FRANCE         Africa (West, Equatorial,         Colony + protectorate
   (second        the Maghreb), Indo-China,         (Tunisia, Morocco)
    largest)      the West Indies, Madagascar       + MANDATE (Syria)
                  + Syria                           + "extension of France"
                                                       (ALGERIA - decisive)

   NETHERLANDS    Dutch East Indies (Sumatra,       Colony, export-crop
                  Java, Celebes, West Irian,        economy, minimal
                  c. two-thirds of Borneo),         investment in living
                  Surinam, West Indian islands      standards

   BELGIUM        Congo, Ruanda-Urundi              Colony ruled through
                                                    (i) denial of advanced
                                                    education and (ii)
                                                    played-off ethnic rivalry

   PORTUGAL       Angola, Mozambique,               Colony under an
                  Portuguese Guinea, East Timor     authoritarian metropole
                                                    with no domestic
                                                    mechanism for conceding

   SPAIN          Spanish Sahara, Spanish           Colony under Franco;
                  Morocco, Ifni, Spanish Guinea     low metropolitan interest
                                                    except where phosphates

   ITALY          Libya, Somalia, Eritrea           STRIPPED BY DECISION
                                                    of the victors, 1947;
                                                    administered by France
                                                    and Britain pending
                                                    a UN disposal

   BY 1975: most of the above had gained independence.
   The FORM in the right-hand column predicts the ROUTE better than
   the name in the left-hand column does.
```

⚠️ Read the right-hand column, not the left. The column that tells you how a territory would end is the one describing what kind of thing it legally was and who lived in it. A crown colony administered by officials can be handed over in an afternoon once the decision is made. A territory legally defined as part of the metropole itself, populated by a million citizens of the metropole, cannot be handed over at all without a constitutional rupture - which is precisely what happened in Algeria.

### 1. Start with the scale, because the scale is the argument

✅ At the end of the Second World War in 1945, the nations of Europe still claimed ownership of vast areas of the rest of the world, particularly in Asia and Africa. ✅ Britain's empire was the largest in area, consisting of India, Burma, Ceylon, Malaya, enormous tracts of Africa and many assorted islands and other territories, such as Cyprus, Hong Kong, the West Indies, the Falkland Islands and Gibraltar. ✅ France had the second largest empire, with territories in Africa, Indo-China and the West Indies. ✅ Other important empires were those of the Netherlands, which held the Dutch East Indies; Belgium, which held the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi; Portugal, which held Angola, Mozambique and Guinea; Spain, which held Spanish Sahara, Ifni, Spanish Morocco and Spanish Guinea; and Italy, which held Libya, Somalia and Eritrea.

✅ Over the next thirty years remarkable changes took place: by 1975 most of these colonial territories had gained their independence.

⚠️ Hold those two sentences together for a moment, because the interval between them is the whole topic. Thirty years is not a long time for a global political order to be taken apart. It is roughly the working life of one colonial administrator. Whatever explanation you offer has to account not merely for the *fact* of the ending but for its *speed*, and speed is a much harder thing to explain than direction. A slow erosion could be explained by moral change or economic decline alone; a thirty-year collapse cannot.

### 2. Four legal forms, and what each one does to the exit

The word "colony" is used loosely, and the looseness costs marks. Four distinct forms appear in this material and each behaves differently.

**A colony** is territory administered directly by the metropole, usually through officials posted from home. ⚠️ Its defining feature for our purposes is that when the metropole decides to leave, the people who must physically depart are civil servants with pensions and return tickets. ✅ This is why the account records that in West Africa there were relatively few Europeans and they tended to be administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend, and that this made the move to independence comparatively straightforward.

**A protectorate** is territory with its own ruler whose external relations and effective government the metropole controls. ✅ Tunisia had a ruler known as the bey and Morocco had a Muslim king, Mohammed V, and both were protectorates. ⚠️ The exit problem here is different and, as it turned out, easier: an indigenous sovereign already exists, so independence can be arranged as a restoration rather than as an invention. ✅ In Morocco the French deposed the king in 1953, provoking violent demonstrations and a guerrilla campaign, and the settlement consisted of allowing him to return.

**A settler territory** is a colony in which a substantial European population has acquired land and legal privilege and regards the territory as home. ✅ In Algeria, Kenya, Tanganyika, Uganda and Rhodesia large numbers of whites had settled, and they were relentlessly hostile to independence, which would place them under black rule. ⚠️ Here withdrawal is not an administrative act at all. It is a transfer of property, security and political status away from people who vote in the metropole or have allies who do.

**A mandate** is territory taken from a defeated empire after the First World War and assigned to a victor under an international obligation. ✅ Britain and France still held land in the Middle East, taken from Turkey at the end of the First World War: Britain held Transjordan and Palestine, France held Syria. ✅ They were known as "mandated" territories, which meant that Britain and France were intended to "look after" them and prepare them for independence. ⚠️ The mandate is the form with a promise written into it. That promise is why decolonisation there was chronologically early - ✅ French Syria became independent in 1946 and ✅ Transjordan, as Jordan, in 1946 - and it is also why the failure of the promise in one case produced a conflict that outlived the empire.

> **Boundary.** ✅ Palestine's mandate ended in 1948. ⚠️ What followed - the creation of Israel, the Arab-Israeli wars, Suez in 1956 and everything after - is owned by the Middle East material and is not taught here. This session owns the *ending of the mandate form* and the independence dates the account records; it does not own the conflict that succeeded it. The same boundary applies to Egypt, which is outside the account's decolonisation chapter entirely, and to Sudan, recorded here only as becoming independent in 1956.

**A fifth form** appears once and matters enormously. ✅ Algeria continued to be treated not as a colony or a protectorate, but as an extension or province of metropolitan France itself. ⚠️ Lesson 15 is about what that single legal fact did to nine million people and to the French Republic.

### 3. The timetables, and what to actually memorise

The account gives full year-by-year tables. ⚠️ Do not memorise them entire; memorise the shape and about fifteen anchor dates.

| Power | Anchor independences (selected, all ✅) |
|---|---|
| Britain | ✅ Transjordan (Jordan) 1946; India and Pakistan 1947; Burma and Ceylon 1948; Palestine mandate ends 1948; Sudan 1956; Malaya and the Gold Coast (Ghana) 1957; Nigeria, Somaliland and Cyprus 1960; Tanganyika and Sierra Leone 1961; Jamaica, Trinidad and Tobago, Uganda 1962; Zanzibar (10 December) and Kenya 1963; Nyasaland (Malawi), Northern Rhodesia (Zambia) and Malta 1964; the Gambia 1965; British Guiana (Guyana), Barbados and Bechuanaland (Botswana) 1966; Aden (South Yemen) 1967; Southern Rhodesia (Zimbabwe) 1980 |
| France | ✅ Syria 1946; Indo-China 1954; Morocco and Tunisia 1956; **Guinea 1958**, alone and after voting no; and in 1960 the **eleven** West and Equatorial African territories that had voted yes, together with the separate cases of **Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar** - fourteen French-administered territories in that year, Guinea excluded |
| Netherlands | ✅ Dutch East Indies (Indonesia) 1949; Surinam 1975 |
| Belgium | ✅ Belgian Congo 1960 (called Zaire from 1971 to 1997); Ruanda-Urundi 1962, becoming Rwanda and Burundi |
| Spain | ✅ Spanish Morocco 1956; Spanish Guinea (Equatorial Guinea) 1968; Ifni 1969; Spanish Sahara 1975 |
| Portugal | ✅ Portuguese Guinea (Guinea-Bissau) 1974; Angola and Mozambique 1975; East Timor 1975, seized by Indonesia later the same year |
| Italy | ✅ Ethiopia liberated and Haile Selassie restored 1941, Italy renouncing its claims in the peace treaty of 1947; Libya 1951; Eritrea attached to Ethiopia 1952; Italian Somaliland merged into Somalia 1960 |

⚠️ Three shapes are visible in that table and each is worth a sentence in an answer.

⚠️ **First, Asia goes before Africa.** The British Asian territories are gone by 1948 and the Dutch East Indies by 1949, while sub-Saharan Africa barely begins before 1957. ⚠️ The reason is supplied in Lesson 2: the war was fought in Asia, and Asian nationalist movements were much older. ✅ The Indian National Congress had been agitating against British rule since 1885, and Vietnamese nationalists began to campaign against French rule during the 1920s.

⚠️ **Second, 1960 is a cliff-edge, not a slope.** ✅ Fourteen French-administered territories, Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus and the Belgian Congo all arrive in the same year. ⚠️ A process that produces that pattern is not a series of local negotiations reaching maturity at the same moment; it is a metropolitan decision taken once and applied wholesale, which is the strongest single piece of evidence for the metropolitan thesis you will meet in Lesson 3.

⚠️ **Third, the outliers are the settler cases.** ✅ Kenya waits until 1963, Angola and Mozambique until 1975, Southern Rhodesia until 1980. ⚠️ Every one of those delays has settlers or an authoritarian metropole or both in it. The outliers are not noise; they are the test of the theory.

### 4. The Commonwealth: continuity designed in advance

✅ Britain was prepared to grant independence when it was felt that individual territories were ready for it, and most of the new states retained a link with Britain by remaining in the British Commonwealth - a group of former British-controlled nations which agreed to continue associating together, mainly because there were certain advantages to be gained from doing so.

⚠️ Notice what this is. It is an institution built so that the ending of formal rule would not end the relationship. ✅ The account is direct about the calculation on the British side: the British were quite willing to allow independence and were confident that they would still be able to exercise influence through trade links, which they hoped to preserve by including the new states as members of the Commonwealth. ✅ This practice of exercising influence over former colonies after independence by economic means became known as **neo-colonialism**, and it became widespread in most of the new states of the Third World.

⚠️ That is a very important sentence and it appears in the source at the *start* of the British withdrawal from Africa, not at the end. Neo-colonialism, in this account, is not a later critique imposed on the process; it is a description of an intention present at the moment of transfer. Lesson 24 develops it into a concept. Here, note only that the successor structure was designed before the succession.

⚠️ The contrast completes the point. ✅ The Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal were, if anything, even more determined than France to hold on to their overseas possessions - probably because, being less wealthy than Britain and France, they lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism. ✅ There was no way they would be able to maintain the equivalent of the British Commonwealth or French influence over their former colonies, against competition from foreign capital. ⚠️ Read that carefully: the smaller empires fought *because* they could not expect influence after leaving. Poverty made them intransigent. That is a counter-intuitive and highly examinable mechanism.

### 5. What the form tells you before you know anything else

⚠️ Take an unfamiliar territory and ask three questions in this order, and you will predict its route with surprising accuracy.

1. **Is the metropole rich enough to expect post-independence influence?** ⚠️ If yes, it will prefer a negotiated exit with an institutional successor. If no, it will fight.
2. **Do Europeans live there permanently and own the best land?** ⚠️ If yes, expect delay, violence, or both, and expect the land question to survive the settlement.
3. **Is there a pre-existing indigenous political authority the metropole can hand to?** ⚠️ If yes - a sultan, a bey, a king, an established mass party - the exit can be constitutional. If no, the metropole must either create a successor or leave a vacuum.

⚠️ Run the Congo through those three questions and you will predict 1960 exactly: a metropole that could not sustain influence, no significant settler landowning class but over a hundred thousand Belgians present, and a nationalist movement less than a year old. ⚠️ Run Ghana through them and you will predict 1957.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The official General Studies Paper I syllabus covers world history from the eighteenth century onward and names, among its examples, the redrawal of national boundaries, colonisation and decolonisation. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the inventory without which no decolonisation answer can be specific: what was actually held, by whom, in what form, and on what timetable it ended.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ This material is almost never examined alone. It is the opening two or three sentences of any decolonisation answer, and its function is to establish scale and variety fast so that the rest of the answer can argue rather than describe. ⚠️ A script that writes "after the Second World War, European powers gradually withdrew from their colonies" has said nothing. A script that writes "in 1945 seven European states held territory across Asia and Africa in at least five distinct legal forms, and by 1975 most of it was independent" has earned the right to the analysis that follows.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating "the British Empire" and "the French Empire" as the only two. ✅ Belgium, the Netherlands, Portugal, Spain and Italy account for the Congo, Indonesia, Angola, Mozambique, Guinea-Bissau, East Timor, Western Sahara, Libya, Eritrea and Somalia - a very large share of the twentieth century's hardest cases.
2. ⚠️ Assuming decolonisation was uniformly post-1945 in origin. ✅ The Indian National Congress dates from 1885 and Vietnamese campaigning from the 1920s; the war accelerated an existing process in Asia and largely initiated one in much of Africa.
3. ❌ Calling Algeria a colony. ✅ It was legally an extension or province of metropolitan France, which is why its war had constitutional consequences in Paris.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting Italy. ✅ Its empire was removed by the decision of the victors in 1947, not by nationalist pressure - the one case in this material where the metropole's colonies were taken rather than given up.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ In 1945 Europe still claimed ownership of vast areas of Asia and Africa; by 1975 most of it was independent.
2. ✅ Britain: largest in area - India, Burma, Ceylon, Malaya, enormous tracts of Africa, Cyprus, Hong Kong, the West Indies, the Falklands, Gibraltar.
3. ✅ France: second largest - Africa, Indo-China, the West Indies.
4. ✅ Netherlands: the Dutch East Indies, Surinam, West Indian islands. Belgium: the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi. Portugal: Angola, Mozambique, Guinea, East Timor. Spain: Spanish Sahara, Spanish Morocco, Ifni, Spanish Guinea. Italy: Libya, Somalia, Eritrea.
5. ✅ Middle Eastern mandates taken from Turkey after the First World War: Britain held Transjordan and Palestine, France held Syria; Britain and France were intended to look after them and prepare them for independence.
6. ✅ Transjordan (Jordan) 1946, French Syria 1946, Palestine mandate ends 1948 - the mandate form goes first.
7. ✅ Settler territories named by the account: Algeria, Kenya, Tanganyika, Uganda and Rhodesia, where large numbers of whites had settled and were relentlessly hostile to independence under black rule.
8. ✅ Algeria was treated not as a colony or protectorate but as an extension or province of metropolitan France.
9. ✅ Britain granted independence when territories were felt to be ready; most new states stayed in the Commonwealth for the advantages to be gained.
10. ✅ Britain expected to keep influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership; this economic influence after independence became known as neo-colonialism and became widespread in most new Third World states.
11. ✅ The Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal were even more determined to hold on, probably because, being less wealthy, they lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism.
12. ✅ 1960 alone: fourteen French-administered territories - the **eleven** of French West and Equatorial Africa that had voted yes in 1958, together with **Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar** - plus Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus and the Belgian Congo. ⚠️ Guinea is not among them: it voted no and took independence in **1958**.
13. ✅ Italy lost its empire by a decision of 1947; Ethiopia had already been liberated and Haile Selassie restored in 1941, and in 1947 Italy renounced its claims; Libya 1951, Eritrea joined to Ethiopia 1952, Italian Somaliland merged into Somalia 1960.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: the legal form of rule and the presence of settlers predict the route better than the identity of the coloniser does.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two territories became independent from the same European power within five years of each other, by completely different processes: one by constitutional transfer, one after a war that helped destroy the metropolitan constitution. Identify the power and the two territories, and explain the difference using the legal form of rule rather than the personalities involved.

**Model answer:** The power is France; the territories are Tunisia, fully independent in March 1956 under Bourguiba, and Algeria, independent in July 1962 after a war beginning late in 1954. Tunisia was a protectorate with its own ruler, the bey, and a nationalist leadership the French could release from prison and negotiate with; independence there could be arranged as the recognition of an authority that already existed, and the French chose that route partly because keeping the moderate Bourguiba in jail was making the guerrilla movement more left-wing and less willing to negotiate. Algeria was not legally a colony or a protectorate at all but an extension or province of metropolitan France, containing over a million French settlers who controlled roughly a third of the most fertile land. There was therefore no legal act of transfer available that did not amount to dismembering France itself, and no French government dared consider independence because of the settlers and their supporters at home - Mendes-France himself said France without Algeria would be no France. The same metropole, the same decade and the same region produced opposite outcomes because the legal form and the settler population differed, not because French intentions differed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the list of imperial powers as the organising principle, so that answers are built as "British decolonisation was peaceful, French and Portuguese decolonisation was violent". Britain fought a twelve-year emergency in Malaya and a counter-insurgency in Kenya whose scale was only made public in 2005, while France withdrew from Tunisia and Morocco by negotiation in 1956. The variable that sorts the cases is the form of rule and the presence of settlers, which cuts across every empire.

---

## Lesson 2 - The war as accelerant: five mechanisms, not one mood

Progress: 2/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Japanese destruction of European military prestige in Asia; guerrilla capacity inherited from anti-Japanese resistance; the political education of some 374,000 African servicemen; wartime promises including the Atlantic Charter of 1941 and its later restriction; and the material weakening of the metropoles

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's section on the effects of the Second World War on nationalist movements, including the Japanese conquests, the recruitment figure for African servicemen, Sukarno's wartime administrative experience, the Dutch offer, the Atlantic Charter and Churchill's restriction, the Porter assessment of imperial cost, and the Macleod statement on the impossibility of holding Africa by force

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: five mechanisms and where each one bites

```text

   +-------------+-------------+-------------+-------------+
   |             |             |             |             |

   |             |             |             |             |
Japan defeats  Anti-Japanese  374,000       Atlantic      Metropoles
Britain,       resistance     Africans in   Charter 1941  militarily and
Holland,       trains         British       (2 clauses)   economically
France in      guerrillas     forces see    + Dutch offer weakened; cannot
Asia: they     who will not   the gap       of "some      hold empires
"failed        accept         between home  degree of     against really
miserably to   European       and service   independence" determined
protect their  rule again     conditions                  campaigns
subjects"        |              |              |             |
   |             |              |              |             |
BITES IN:     BITES IN:      BITES IN:      BITES IN:     BITES IN:
Asia first    Indo-China,    Africa,        everywhere    everywhere,
(the war was  Dutch East     esp. West      (Churchill    but unevenly
fought there) Indies,        Africa's new   later limits  (Britain first
              Malaya,        towns          it to Hitler's to recognise it)
              Burma                          victims)

   NOTE THE ASYMMETRY: mechanisms 1 and 2 are ASIAN.
   Mechanism 3 is AFRICAN. 4 and 5 are GENERAL.
   This is why Asia decolonised a decade before Africa.
```

⚠️ That asymmetry at the bottom of the diagram is the payoff. A question asking how the war caused decolonisation is really asking you to notice that it did different things in different places, and that the difference explains the timetable you memorised in Lesson 1.

### 1. The problem: why an accelerant is not a cause

Here is the trap this lesson exists to defuse. It is easy to write that the Second World War caused decolonisation. ⚠️ It is also wrong, and demonstrably so, because nationalist movements existed in many of Europe's overseas colonies, especially those in Asia, for many years before the war. ✅ The Indian National Congress Party had been agitating against British rule since 1885; ✅ Vietnamese nationalists began to campaign against French rule during the 1920s.

⚠️ But the opposite claim is equally wrong. ✅ Nationalism was not so strong in other areas, and progress towards independence would have been much slower without the boost provided by the Second World War.

⚠️ The precise formulation, and the one worth memorising, is that the war was an **accelerant acting on unevenly distributed material**. Where nationalism was already organised it removed the obstacles; where it was weak it supplied the conditions for organisation. Five mechanisms did this work, and naming them individually is what separates an analytical answer from a paragraph of atmosphere.

### 2. Mechanism one: the destruction of prestige

✅ Japanese forces conquered Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong and Burma, the Dutch East Indies and French Indo-China. ✅ Although the Japanese were eventually defeated, the nationalists, many of whom had fought against the Japanese, had no intention of tamely accepting European rule again. ✅ The reason the account gives is not military but legitimating: Britain, France and Holland had failed miserably to protect their subjects, thus destroying any claim to legitimacy they might have had.

⚠️ Pause on the logic, because it is better than it first looks. Imperial rule in Asia rested on a bargain that was mostly implicit: you accept foreign government, and in exchange you receive order and protection. The Japanese conquests did not merely defeat European armies; they falsified the bargain publicly, in front of the population, and then the Europeans came back and asked for the arrangement to resume on the old terms. ⚠️ A claim to rule that has been tested and failed cannot simply be reasserted, and this is why 1945 in Asia was not a restoration but a confrontation.

### 3. Mechanism two: capacity, not just resentment

Resentment does not by itself remove a government. Organised armed men do. ✅ If necessary, nationalists would continue to fight against the Europeans, using the guerrilla tactics they had learned fighting the Japanese. ✅ This is exactly what happened in Indo-China, the Dutch East Indies, Malaya and Burma.

⚠️ This is the most underrated sentence in the whole causation section and it is worth a clause in any answer. The anti-Japanese resistance was a training programme. It distributed weapons, created command structures, taught terrain-based irregular warfare against a technologically superior enemy, and conferred the political legitimacy that comes from having fought the occupier when the colonial power could not. ⚠️ When the Europeans returned they faced not a grievance but an army.

⚠️ And notice the asymmetry of outcomes from the same input, because examiners like it. ✅ In Indo-China the anti-Japanese resistance became the force that defeated France. ✅ In Malaya, the Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng, who had played a leading role in the resistance to the Japanese, began to stir up strikes and violence against the British - and were defeated. ⚠️ Identical wartime origin, opposite result. Lesson 18 explains why.

> **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Indochinese and Malayan communist movements as *regimes and ideologies* belong to the topic on Asian communism. This session teaches them only as anti-colonial forces and stops where the colonial relationship ends: at Geneva in July 1954 for Indo-China, and at independence in 1957 for Malaya.

### 4. Mechanism three: 374,000 men who had seen a comparison

✅ Asians and Africans became more aware of social and political matters as a result of their involvement in the war. ✅ Some 374,000 Africans were recruited into the British armed forces. ✅ The vast majority of them had never left their homeland before, and they were appalled at the contrast between the primitive living conditions in Africa and the relatively comfortable conditions they experienced even as members of the armed forces.

⚠️ Why does this matter more than it looks? Because the grievance it creates is comparative and factual rather than ideological, and comparative grievances are politically durable. A man who has been told that colonial rule brings development, and has then seen what an ordinary soldier's rations and quarters look like in the metropole's own army, has acquired a standard of measurement he cannot unsee. ⚠️ He has also acquired discipline, literacy in many cases, organisational experience, and a network of men with the same standard.

✅ The Kenyan case shows the mechanism working directly. ✅ Africans there resented the discrimination and the colour bar between blacks and whites, under which they were treated as inferior, second-class citizens; ✅ this was especially unacceptable since many Africans had served in the army during the Second World War and had received equal treatment and respect from whites. ⚠️ The war produced men who had experienced racial equality as a practical arrangement and then returned to a territory where it was illegal. Lesson 11 shows where that went.

### 5. Mechanism four: promises, and the attempt to withdraw them

Three wartime commitments raised expectations, and at least one of them was later restricted in a way that did more damage than silence would have.

✅ Some Asian nationalist leaders worked with the Japanese, thinking that after the war there would be more chance of independence being granted by the Japanese than by the Europeans; ✅ many of them, like Dr Sukarno in the Dutch East Indies, gained experience helping to govern the occupied areas, and Sukarno declared an independent republic on **18 August 1945** and was Indonesia's first president from that date, the Netherlands recognising Indonesian independence only in **1949**. ⚠️ The textbook account dates his presidency to 1949; that is the date of Dutch recognition, not of the presidency, and the two must be kept apart.

✅ The Dutch government, shocked that people were so ready to co-operate with the Japanese in the East Indies, offered them some degree of independence as soon as the Japanese were defeated.

✅ The 1941 Atlantic Charter set out joint Anglo-American thinking about how the world should be organised after the war. ✅ Two of its points were that nations should not expand by taking territory from other nations, and that all peoples should have the right to choose their own form of government. ✅ Though Churchill later said that this only applied to victims of Hitler's aggression, the hopes of Asian and African peoples had been raised.

⚠️ That last sentence repays slow reading. The restriction did not work, and the reason it did not work is instructive: a universal principle stated in public cannot be privately narrowed. Once "all peoples" has been said, the person who wants to exclude some peoples has to explain the exclusion, and the only available explanations are racial. ⚠️ The Atlantic Charter therefore did more than raise hopes; it forced the imperial powers to defend empire on grounds they could no longer state out loud. ⚠️ That is delegitimation, and it is one of the two or three strongest analytical moves available in this topic.

✅ It also had a concrete diplomatic consequence, because the Americans took the wording seriously. ✅ President Roosevelt made it clear that he took the Atlantic Charter to apply to all peoples, not just those taken over by the Germans, and he and his successor Truman pressurised the British government to speed up independence for India.

### 6. Mechanism five: a state that could no longer afford itself

✅ The war weakened the European states, so that in the end they were not militarily or economically strong enough to hold on to their far-flung empires in the face of really determined campaigns for independence. ✅ The British were the first to recognise this.

✅ Bernard Porter's assessment is the one to quote, because it explains why British collapse came first and fastest: the British Empire had always been a cheapskate affair; governments had never wanted to spend money on it or commit more than the minimum of personnel to it, or trouble the British people with it too much; the best way to manage things was to devolve the ruling of colonial possessions, and the expense, to settlers or local traditional rulers - which had its advantages but also diluted Britain's power.

⚠️ Read that as a structural argument rather than a criticism. An empire run cheaply through settlers and intermediaries is an empire whose coercive capacity is not actually in metropolitan hands. When the demand for coercion rises, such an empire cannot scale up: it can only lean harder on intermediaries whose interests are now diverging from its own. ⚠️ This is exactly what happened in Central Africa and in Kenya, where the settlers the British had devolved power to became the principal obstacle to British policy.

✅ The British conclusion was drawn openly by Iain Macleod, Colonial Secretary: "we could not possibly have held by force our territories in Africa; the march of men towards freedom cannot be halted; it can only be guided."

⚠️ And notice the second half of that sentence, which is about presentation. ✅ The British concentrated on making their withdrawals look good; it was important to give the impression that they were in control of the process, that it was something they had intended all along, and that they were not scuttling away. ⚠️ This matters for evidence handling, because it means British official accounts of decolonisation are an interested party's narrative. Lesson 3 shows Indian historians making exactly that objection.

✅ The other powers drew the opposite conclusion first. ✅ The French, Dutch, Spanish and Portuguese reacted differently and seemed determined to preserve their empires; but this involved them in costly military campaigns, and eventually they all had to admit defeat.

### Comparative note: what the war did not do

⚠️ Three things are often credited to the war and should not be.

⚠️ It did not create anti-colonial nationalism, which predated it by decades in India and by twenty years in Vietnam.

⚠️ It did not produce a uniform European decision to withdraw. ✅ The French position in 1944 was the opposite: the Brazzaville Declaration stated that the colonising work of France made it impossible to accept any idea of autonomy for the colonies or any possibility of development outside the French Empire, and that even at a distant date there would be no self-government in the colonies.

⚠️ And it did not make withdrawal inevitable on any particular date. ✅ Portugal, which was neutral in the war, kept its African empire until a military coup at home in 1974 - twenty-nine years after the mechanism supposedly operated. ⚠️ The war changed the odds in every territory; it determined the outcome in none.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The syllabus names both world wars and decolonisation, and this lesson is the join between them. ⚠️ A question on the consequences of the Second World War that omits the end of empire is incomplete, and a question on decolonisation that treats the war as background is weak.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The standard demand is "assess the reasons for the growth of nationalism in the European colonies after the Second World War" or "how important was the war in bringing about decolonisation". ⚠️ Both reward the accelerant formulation plus named mechanisms plus the asymmetry between Asia and Africa. Both punish a paragraph of general statements about weakened powers.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that the war caused decolonisation. ✅ Congress dates from 1885; Vietnamese campaigning from the 1920s. The war accelerated, and it accelerated different things in different continents.
2. ⚠️ Using "European exhaustion" as a complete explanation. ⚠️ Exhaustion reduces the supply of coercion; it does not by itself generate a demand for withdrawal, and an exhausted state can also choose reform, delay or brutality - as France, the Netherlands and Portugal all did.
3. ⚠️ Forgetting that some nationalist leaders collaborated with Japan. ✅ Sukarno gained administrative experience under Japanese occupation, declared an independent republic on 18 August 1945 and was Indonesia's first president from that date. This is uncomfortable and examinable, and it explains why the Dutch attempted a pre-emptive offer.
4. ❌ Quoting the Atlantic Charter without Churchill's restriction. ✅ The restriction is the interesting half, because it shows the principle escaping the control of the man who signed it.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Japanese forces conquered Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong, Burma, the Dutch East Indies and French Indo-China.
2. ✅ Britain, France and Holland had failed miserably to protect their subjects, destroying any claim to legitimacy they might have had.
3. ✅ Nationalists would continue to fight using guerrilla tactics learned fighting the Japanese - in Indo-China, the Dutch East Indies, Malaya and Burma.
4. ✅ Some 374,000 Africans were recruited into the British armed forces; most had never left home and were appalled at the contrast between conditions in Africa and in the forces.
5. ✅ Some Asian leaders worked with the Japanese expecting better independence prospects; Sukarno gained governing experience, declared an independent republic on 18 August 1945 and was its first president from that date, the Netherlands recognising Indonesian independence only in 1949.
6. ✅ The Dutch, shocked at co-operation with Japan, offered some degree of independence as soon as the Japanese were defeated.
7. ✅ Atlantic Charter 1941: no territorial expansion at others' expense; all peoples to have the right to choose their own form of government.
8. ✅ Churchill later said it applied only to victims of Hitler's aggression, but hopes had already been raised; Roosevelt insisted it applied to all peoples and, with Truman, pressed Britain on India.
9. ✅ The war left European states not militarily or economically strong enough to hold their empires against really determined campaigns; Britain recognised this first.
10. ✅ Porter: the British Empire had always been a cheapskate affair, managed by devolving rule and expense to settlers or chiefs, which diluted Britain's power.
11. ✅ Macleod: "we could not possibly have held by force our territories in Africa; the march of men towards freedom cannot be halted; it can only be guided."
12. ✅ The British concentrated on making withdrawals look good - in control, intended all along, not scuttling away.
13. ✅ The French 1944 Brazzaville Declaration ruled out autonomy or self-government even at a distant date; France, the Netherlands, Spain and Portugal fought first and conceded later.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: mechanisms one and two were Asian, mechanism three was African, mechanisms four and five were general - which is why Asia went first and Africa followed a decade later.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that the Second World War caused the collapse of the European empires. Correct the claim so that everything true in it survives, and use the correction to explain why Asian territories became independent roughly a decade before most African ones.

**Model answer:** The correction is that the war accelerated an unevenly distributed process rather than originating it, and that it did so through five separable mechanisms which were not equally present on the two continents. Nationalism predated the war where it mattered most: the Indian National Congress had been agitating since 1885 and Vietnamese nationalists since the 1920s, so in Asia the war removed obstacles to movements that already existed. Two of the five mechanisms were specifically Asian. The Japanese conquest of Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong, Burma, the Dutch East Indies and French Indo-China destroyed the European claim to protect its subjects, and the anti-Japanese resistance left behind trained guerrillas who had no intention of accepting European rule again, as happened in Indo-China, the Dutch East Indies, Malaya and Burma. The third mechanism was chiefly African and slower-acting: some 374,000 Africans served in the British forces and returned with a comparative standard by which colonial conditions could be judged, but with no armed organisation. The remaining two - the raised expectations of the Atlantic Charter, which Churchill could not successfully narrow, and the material weakening of metropoles that Porter describes as having always run empire cheaply through settlers and chiefs - were general. Asia therefore had both prestige collapse and armed capacity in 1945; Africa had political awakening without either, and required a further decade of party-building, strikes and boycotts before the same pressure existed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating "European exhaustion" as sufficient. Exhaustion lowers the supply of coercion but does not create the demand for withdrawal, and an exhausted state has other options. France, the Netherlands and Portugal were all exhausted and all chose to fight first - Portugal for nearly three decades after the war it did not fight in. The war changed the cost of holding empire; colonised societies determined how high that cost would be driven.

## Lesson 3 - Who ended empire? The metropolitan thesis and its critics

Progress: 3/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the opening of the archives in the 1990s; Robert Holland and the metropolitan thesis; the counter-case from nationalist agency and expulsion by force; the "no simple answer" resolution; the Indian historiographical dispute as the model of the argument; and how to convert a causation dispute into examination structure

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

P. Menon and A. N. Wilson

CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: one dispute, two poles, and the evidence each side points to

```text

   POLE A: THE METROPOLITAN THESIS        POLE B: NATIONALIST AGENCY
   ================================       ==========================
```
