```text
   POINTS TO:                             POINTS TO:
   - 1960: 11 French territories           - Indo-China: eight-year war,
     + Togo, Cameroun, Madagascar;          Dien Bien Phu May 1954
     Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus
     and the Belgian Congo, all in        - Algeria: 700,000 French
     one year                               troops by 1960, and still
   - de Gaulle's 1958 referendum:           independence in July 1962
     a metropolitan design applied        - Indonesia: four years of
     to twelve territories at once          Dutch military effort, 1949
   - Britain's expectation of             - Portugal: 40% of the budget
     continued influence through            on three wars by 1973; the
     trade and Commonwealth                 metropolitan regime fell first
   - Italy 1947: empire removed by        - Nkrumah's general strike 1950;
     the decision of the victors            Azikiwe's general strike 1945

   THE RESOLUTION THE ACCOUNT GIVES:
   "there is no simple answer. What can be said with certainty is that
    ALL these factors were present IN VARYING DEGREES in ALL colonial
    territories."
   => The examinable skill is not choosing a pole. It is saying WHICH
      factor was decisive IN WHICH TERRITORY, and WHY the mix differed.
```

⚠️ Most candidates treat historiography as decoration - a name dropped to look well-read. It is not. In this topic the dispute *is* the analytical structure, because the two poles generate different predictions and the predictions can be tested against the cases you will meet in Lessons 9 to 22.

### 1. Why this dispute exists at all

✅ During the 1990s more documents dealing with decolonisation became available, enabling historians to investigate more deeply the motives of the European powers in giving up their colonies and the different ways in which they carried out their withdrawals.

⚠️ That single sentence explains the whole shape of the field, and it is worth understanding why. The documents that became available in the 1990s were *metropolitan* documents: cabinet minutes, colonial office files, treasury assessments. They record what London, Paris and Brussels were thinking. ⚠️ A sudden flood of evidence about one side of a two-sided process will naturally produce a wave of scholarship emphasising that side. The metropolitan thesis is, in part, an artefact of which archives opened.

⚠️ Notice also what this implies about balance. The nationalist side of the story is documented too, but differently - in party records, newspapers, strike reports, memoirs and oral testimony rather than in state files. ⚠️ A historian who weighs only what is easiest to cite will systematically underweight the colonised. This is a genuine methodological point and it is worth one sentence in a twenty-mark answer.

### 2. Pole A: the metropolitan thesis, stated exactly

✅ The main debate that has developed is about the extent to which decolonisation was caused by local nationalist movements, and how far it was brought about by outside political and economic considerations.

✅ Robert Holland is the leading exponent of what has become known as the **metropolitan thesis**, and his formulation deserves to be quoted rather than paraphrased: the great colonial powers divested themselves of their subordinate possessions, not because internal pressures within their colonies left them with no other choice, but in the wake of a revisionist process whereby imperial roles came to be seen as incongruent with more "modern" goals in the fields of foreign and economic policy.

⚠️ Unpack the claim, because its strength is in the second half. Holland is not saying the colonised were passive. He is saying that the *decision* was taken in Europe, for European reasons, and that those reasons were about Europe's own self-image and strategy: empire came to look old-fashioned, expensive and inconsistent with where the metropolitan elite thought its future lay - in European integration, in the Atlantic alliance, in modern industrial economies rather than in tropical administration.

⚠️ The thesis has real evidence behind it, and an answer that dismisses it is weaker than one that argues with it.

- ✅ The 1960 cluster is hard to explain any other way. Fourteen French-administered territories, Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus and the Belgian Congo all became independent in a single calendar year, and they had nothing in common except the continent they sat on.
- ✅ De Gaulle's 1958 plan was a metropolitan design applied wholesale: twelve colonies offered self-government inside a French Community in which France would take all important decisions about taxation and foreign affairs, with a referendum in each and no aid for any that refused.
- ✅ Britain expected to exercise influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership - a calculation about post-imperial position, not a response to pressure.
- ✅ And Italy's empire was removed in 1947 by a decision of the victorious powers, with no nationalist agency involved at all.

### 3. Pole B: agency, and the question that tests it

✅ Other historians feel that more credit must be given to the strength of local nationalist movements, and they acknowledge that in some cases the imperial power was quite simply expelled by sheer force.

✅ The account then poses the decisive test question, and it is worth carrying into an examination hall verbatim in substance: would the British have left East and Central Africa for purely "metropolitan" reasons if there had been no nationalist movements in these areas?

⚠️ That question is well chosen, and you should be able to say why. East and Central Africa are exactly the regions where the metropolitan calculation pointed the other way - towards staying. ✅ The British thought independence for East Africa was not so necessary as for West Africa, and expected multiracial governments in which European and Asian settlers would play a significant part. ✅ In Central Africa they permitted, in 1953, the creation of a federation whose aim was to preserve the supremacy of the white minority. ⚠️ A pure metropolitan thesis has to explain why a metropole that was designing white-minority constitutions in 1953 was conceding black majority rule in Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia by 1961-2 and dissolving its own federation in December 1963. The obvious explanation is what happened in between.

⚠️ The agency evidence is strongest where the metropole paid a price it had not chosen to pay.

- ✅ In Indo-China an eight-year armed struggle culminated in the French defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954, which was a humiliating blow, caused a political crisis and brought down the government.
- ✅ In Algeria, on the textbook account's figure, France had 700,000 troops engaged in a massive anti-terrorist operation by 1960 - other estimates of French strength are lower - and granted independence in July 1962 anyway.
- ✅ In Indonesia the Dutch were still a long way from complete victory in 1949 after four years of fighting, and the expense of the campaign was crippling for a small country.
- ✅ In Portugal's three African wars the government was spending forty per cent of its budget by 1973, and the regime at home was overthrown by a military coup in 1974 before the colonies were conceded.
- ✅ And in the unambiguously "peaceful" cases the pressure was still real and still applied from below: ✅ Nkrumah's campaign involved boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950, and the British released him from prison realising that he had mass support; ✅ Azikiwe showed he meant business by organising an impressive general strike in 1945, which was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence.

⚠️ That last pair is the sharpest available answer to the metropolitan thesis, because it shows nationalist action changing the *timetable* in the cases usually cited as examples of voluntary withdrawal.

### 4. The resolution, and why it is not a fudge

✅ Of course there is no simple answer. ✅ What can be said with certainty is that all these factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories.

⚠️ A candidate reading that sentence thinks: useless, it says everything mattered. That reading throws away the marks. The operative words are **in varying degrees**. The sentence is not a refusal to decide; it is an instruction to decide *per territory* rather than in general. ⚠️ The examinable skill is to hold a scale in your head and place cases on it.

| Territory | ⚠️ Where it sits | ✅ Decisive evidence |
|---|---|---|
| Italian Africa, 1947-52 | Pure metropolitan - in fact pure *external* | ✅ Empire removed by decision of the victors; the UN placed the territories under governments sympathetic to western interests |
| French Community territories, 1958-60 | Strongly metropolitan | ✅ A referendum designed in Paris, offered to twelve territories simultaneously, with aid as the incentive |
| Gold Coast, 1950-57 | Balanced, tilting to agency | ✅ Boycotts, demonstrations and a general strike; ✅ the British released Nkrumah on realising he had mass support and conceded a new constitution with universal adult suffrage |
| Kenya, 1952-63 | Strongly agency, though the movement was defeated militarily | ✅ The uprising was defeated by 1960, yet the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign is recorded as one of the reasons the British changed their attitude |
| Algeria, 1954-62 | Agency, at maximum cost | ✅ 700,000 French troops by 1960; ✅ even if the army won militarily, the FLN had the support of most Algerians, and while that lasted French control could never be secure |
| Portuguese Africa, 1961-75 | Agency operating on the metropole itself | ✅ Guerrilla war the army could not suppress; ✅ forty per cent of the budget by 1973; ✅ the dictatorship overthrown in 1974 and the colonies released immediately afterwards |

⚠️ Look at the Kenyan row, because it contains the most interesting causal shape in the table. The movement lost and its objective was achieved. That is only paradoxical if you think nationalism works by winning battles. It works by raising costs, and a defeated insurgency can raise costs high enough to change a metropolitan calculation. ⚠️ This is the single most transferable mechanism in the topic and it deserves a name: **cost imposition**. Colonial nationalism's decisive achievement was usually not victory but expense.

### 5. The Indian dispute as the model of the argument

> **Boundary.** ⚠️ India's negotiations, its partition, its leaders and its violence are owned by the Modern Indian History material and are not taught here. What follows is used *only* as the clearest available example of how a causation dispute is structured, because the account presents it that way.

✅ The reasons why the British decided to grant Indian independence have been the subject of lively debate.

✅ **The official version:** independence was the culmination of a process going back to the Government of India Act of 1919 - a process by which the British carefully prepared India for independence.

✅ **The challenge:** some Indian historians, including Sumit Sarkar and Anita Inder Singh, have argued that Indian independence was never a long-term goal of the British, and that the Government of India Acts of 1919 and 1935 were designed not to prepare the way for independence but to postpone it. ✅ Independence was not a gift from the British; it was "the hard-won fruit of struggle and sacrifice".

✅ **The economic reading:** other historians have suggested that India was no longer of any value to Britain - instead of being a source of profit it was now a drain on British resources - so that the aim was to get out in a way that did not look too much like a humiliation and that kept India within the British financial network and Commonwealth.

✅ **The middle view:** Howard Brasted defended the Labour government against the accusation that it made policy up as it went along, showing that the Labour Party had drawn up a clear policy of withdrawal before the Second World War, discussed by Attlee with Nehru in 1938.

✅ **The assessments of the handover itself diverge too:** ✅ Attlee argued that Britain could not be blamed for the violence, which was due to "the failure of the Indians to agree among themselves"; ✅ V. P. Menon believed Britain's decision to leave "earned for Britain universal respect and goodwill"; ✅ Brasted agreed that less sensitive handling could have produced an even worse bloodbath; ✅ while A. N. Wilson believes there could have been less violence had Mountbatten provided peacekeeping forces to protect the migrant populations and taken more care in deciding the frontiers.

⚠️ Now extract the structure, which is what you are here for. Four kinds of claim are in play and they are not competing versions of the same claim:

1. a claim about **intention** (did Britain plan this?);
2. a claim about **interest** (did Britain still profit?);
3. a claim about **causation** (what actually moved the date?); and
4. a claim about **conduct** (was the execution competent or culpable?).

⚠️ Candidates lose marks by mixing these. Britain can have had no intention of leaving, have found India unprofitable, have been moved primarily by mass pressure, and have handled the exit either well or badly - all four at once, with no contradiction. ⚠️ Separating the four is worth more than knowing any one answer.

### Comparative note: the presentation problem

⚠️ One further reason to distrust any single account is that one party was deliberately managing the story. ✅ The British concentrated on making their withdrawals look good, giving the impression that they were in control of the process, that it was something they had intended all along, and that they were not scuttling away.

⚠️ So the "careful preparation" narrative is not merely one interpretation among several: it is the interpretation the withdrawing power actively cultivated. ⚠️ That does not make it false - ✅ the Gold Coast really did receive five years of supervised self-government, and the account calls that experience unique to Ghana. ⚠️ But it does mean the narrative requires independent corroboration, and that where preparation was genuinely absent, as in the Congo, the same power's claims should be read with the same scepticism.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Decolonisation is a named syllabus term, and causation is the commonest demand on any named process. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the structure that turns a list of causes into an argument.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "assess the reasons", "analyse the causes", "how far would you agree that", or a quotation stating one pole and inviting evaluation. ⚠️ The strongest structure is: state the dispute in one sentence; give the strongest evidence for the metropolitan pole; give the strongest evidence for agency, preferring cases where the metropole paid a price it had not chosen; introduce cost imposition as the mechanism that connects them; then place three named territories at different points on the scale and finish with the "varying degrees in all territories" formulation as a *conclusion you have earned* rather than an opening hedge.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Naming Holland without stating what the metropolitan thesis claims. ✅ It claims the decision was European and was driven by a revisionist process in which imperial roles came to look incongruent with modern foreign and economic goals.
2. ⚠️ Treating "there is no simple answer" as the answer. ⚠️ It is an instruction to differentiate by territory, and an answer that does not differentiate has not followed it.
3. ⚠️ Assuming a defeated nationalist movement failed. ✅ Kenya's uprising was defeated by 1960 and the expense of defeating it is recorded among the reasons Britain changed its attitude.
4. ❌ Importing Indian negotiation or partition detail into a world-history answer. ⚠️ Use the Indian case for the *shape* of the historiographical dispute and for its demonstration effect, and hand the narrative to Modern Indian History.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ More documents became available during the 1990s, enabling deeper investigation of European motives and methods of withdrawal.
2. ✅ The main debate: how far decolonisation was caused by local nationalist movements and how far by outside political and economic considerations.
3. ✅ Holland's metropolitan thesis: powers divested themselves not because internal pressures left no choice, but through a revisionist process making imperial roles look incongruent with modern foreign and economic goals.
4. ✅ Counter-position: more credit to local nationalist movements; in some cases the imperial power was simply expelled by sheer force.
5. ✅ The test question: would Britain have left East and Central Africa for purely metropolitan reasons if there had been no nationalist movements there?
6. ✅ Resolution: no simple answer; all these factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories - an instruction to differentiate per territory.
7. ⚠️ Cost imposition is the mechanism that joins the poles: nationalism usually worked by making retention expensive rather than by winning militarily.
8. ✅ Metropolitan-pole evidence: the 1960 cluster; de Gaulle's 1958 referendum design; Britain's expectation of influence through trade and Commonwealth; Italy's empire removed by the victors in 1947.
9. ✅ Agency-pole evidence: Dien Bien Phu May 1954; 700,000 French troops in Algeria by 1960; four years of Dutch war to 1949; forty per cent of Portugal's budget on three wars by 1973; Nkrumah's 1950 general strike; Azikiwe's 1945 general strike.
10. ✅ Indian dispute, used only as a model: official "careful preparation" version; Sarkar and Anita Inder Singh's rejoinder that the 1919 and 1935 Acts postponed rather than prepared, and that independence was "the hard-won fruit of struggle and sacrifice"; the drain-on-resources reading; Brasted's middle view citing Labour's pre-war policy and the 1938 Attlee-Nehru discussion.
11. ✅ Conduct assessments diverge: Attlee blaming Indian disagreement; Menon crediting Britain with universal respect and goodwill; Brasted noting worse was possible; Wilson faulting Mountbatten on peacekeeping and frontiers.
12. ⚠️ Four separable claims: intention, interest, causation, conduct. They can all be true at once.
13. ✅ The British deliberately made withdrawals look controlled and intended, which is why their own preparation narrative needs corroboration.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: the decision was usually taken in Europe, but the *price* that made the decision necessary was usually set in the colony.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why a nationalist movement that was militarily defeated can still be the decisive cause of its territory's independence, and name a case where this happened.

**Model answer:** Because nationalism operates on the imperial power's cost calculation rather than on the battlefield result. An insurgency that is suppressed still forces the metropole to raise troop numbers, fund a sustained campaign, accept casualties, defend its conduct publicly and divert resources from priorities it values more - and once withdrawal becomes cheaper than retention, the colony is conceded regardless of who won the fighting. Kenya is the clear case. The uprising organised by the Mau Mau secret society from 1952 had been defeated by 1960, and the state of emergency involved Operation Anvil in April 1954, a sweep of Nairobi by about five thousand troops and a thousand police in which over fifty thousand people were screened. Yet the account records that by then the British, encouraged by the wind of change and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude; Macmillan faced up to the fact that it was impossible and indefensible to continue prolonging the privileged position of a group which made up no more than five per cent of the population, and moved Kenya towards independence, which came in December 1963. Cost and the loss of a defensible justification did the work that military victory did not. The same mechanism appears in reverse in Portugal, where the army could not suppress the guerrillas, forty per cent of the budget was going on three wars by 1973, and the metropolitan regime fell before the colonies were released.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the metropolitan thesis and nationalist agency as rival answers to one question, so that an answer must pick a side. They answer different questions. The metropolitan thesis explains where and when the decision was formally taken; agency explains why the situation in which that decision was rational came about. The account's own resolution is that all the factors were present in varying degrees in all territories, which is an instruction to weight them case by case rather than a refusal to judge.

---

## Lesson 4 - The social bases of nationalism: elite, press, strike, town

Progress: 4/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: education abroad and the simultaneous supply of vocabulary and grievance; the named leaders and their formation; the newspaper, the party and the general strike as instruments; the receptiveness of the new towns; labour and peasant grievances in the record; and an honest statement of what the evidence base does and does not carry on women

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's statement on the rapid post-1945 spread of African nationalism through education in Britain and the United States, the receptiveness of Africans in the new towns, the records of Nkrumah, Azikiwe, Nyerere and Kenyatta, the general strikes of 1945 and 1950, the French arrests of nationalist leaders and trade unionists in 1949, and the land and labour grievances recorded for Kenya, Algeria and South Africa
CA search: not sought - the session's one current linkage is placed in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two supplies, one movement

```text
   WHO SUPPLIED WHAT TO THE NATIONALIST MOVEMENT?

   THE EDUCATED ELITE                    THE URBAN AND RURAL MASS
   ==================                    ========================
   supplies:                             supplies:
     * the vocabulary of rights          * DISRUPTIVE CAPACITY
     * legal and constitutional          * numbers at demonstrations
       technique                         * the general strike
     * the newspaper                     * the boycott of European goods
     * the political party               * the risk of ungovernability
     * an international audience
     * negotiating legitimacy            derived from:
                                           * new towns: "the mass of
   formed by:                               Africans in the new towns
     * education in Britain and             were particularly receptive
       the USA, "where they were            to nationalist ideas"
       made aware of racial                * wage labour in mines,
       discrimination"                       factories, ports, railways
                                           * land loss (Kenya highlands,
   named: NKRUMAH (London and USA,           Algeria's fertile third)
   CPP from 1949) . AZIKIWE (USA,           * returning servicemen
   editor, newspapers from 1937)
   . NYERERE (Edinburgh, TANU)             * ex-resistance fighters (Asia)
   . KENYATTA (Britain in the 1930s,
   KAU) . GARVEY and DU BOIS (the
   pan-African antecedent, outside
   Africa)

   THE JOIN: education produced BOTH the argument AND the grievance,
   because the same journey that taught the vocabulary of rights
   delivered the experience of racial discrimination.

   THE TEST: neither supply worked alone. Nkrumah in prison achieved
   nothing until a general strike proved mass support; Azikiwe's
   newspapers had run since 1937, but it was the 1945 general strike
   that "was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria
   for independence".
```

⚠️ The middle box is the lesson. A question on the role of Western-educated Africans in the rise of African nationalism is answered badly by listing leaders and well by explaining that education supplied the argument while urban mass action supplied the pressure, and that the British timetable moved only when the two combined.

### 1. The mechanism inside "education abroad"

✅ African nationalism spread rapidly after 1945; this was because more and more Africans were being educated in Britain and the USA, where they were made aware of racial discrimination. ✅ Colonialism was seen as the humiliation and exploitation of blacks by whites, and the mass of Africans in the new towns were particularly receptive to nationalist ideas.

⚠️ Read that first sentence twice, because there is a causal claim hidden inside it that most candidates miss. It does not say that education abroad produced nationalism because it taught liberal ideas. It says that education abroad produced nationalism because it was **where they were made aware of racial discrimination**.

⚠️ That is a much more interesting mechanism, and it is the one to write. A student who travels from a colony to the metropole undergoes two experiences simultaneously. He acquires the metropole's own political vocabulary - self-determination, representation, consent, rights - in its most authoritative form. And he encounters, in person and often for the first time as an explicit social fact rather than a colonial administrative arrangement, the proposition that he belongs to an inferior category. ⚠️ The two experiences arrive together and they cannot be reconciled. The resulting position is not a borrowed ideology; it is the metropole's own principle turned against the metropole's own practice, argued by someone with personal standing to make the charge.

⚠️ This also explains a detail of tone that runs through the movement. The demand was almost never for something exotic. ✅ The Freedom Charter proclaimed at Kliptown in 1955 demanded equality before the law, freedom of assembly, movement, speech, religion and the press, the right to vote, equal pay for equal work, a forty-hour week, a minimum wage, unemployment benefit, free medical care and free compulsory equal education. ⚠️ There is nothing in that list a British or American liberal could disown. That is the point of the list.

### 2. The four leaders, and what their formation predicts

| Leader | ✅ Formation | ✅ Instrument | ⚠️ What the formation predicts |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Kwame Nkrumah** (Gold Coast) | ✅ Educated in London and the USA; leader of the Convention People's Party from 1949 | ✅ Boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations, a general strike in 1950; then constitutional politics after release | ⚠️ A leader formed in the metropole and in the pan-African diaspora will pursue both national independence *and* a continental project - which Nkrumah did, and which failed (Lesson 5) |
| **Nnamdi Azikiwe** (Nigeria) | ✅ Educated in the USA; newspaper editor in the Gold Coast; returned to Nigeria in 1937 and founded a series of newspapers | ✅ The press, then an impressive general strike in 1945 | ⚠️ A leader whose instrument is the newspaper builds a reading public across regions, which is exactly what a federation needs and exactly what Nigeria's regional structure would later strain |
| **Julius Nyerere** (Tanganyika) | ✅ Educated at the University of Edinburgh; leader of the Tanganyika African National Union | ✅ Insisted the government must be African while making it clear that whites had nothing to fear from black rule | ⚠️ A leader who neutralises settler fear removes the coloniser's strongest argument for delay; ✅ Macmillan's government, impressed by his ability and sincerity, conceded independence with black majority rule in 1961 |
| **Jomo Kenyatta** (Kenya) | ✅ Born 1894, Kikuyu, a veteran among African nationalists; spent time in Britain during the 1930s; returned to Kenya in 1947; leader of the **Kenya African Union** | ✅ Hoped to win African majority rule gradually, first gaining more African seats on the Legislative Council | ⚠️ A gradualist leader in a settler colony will be outflanked by his own radicals - ✅ the more radical wing of his party, calling themselves the Forty Group, wanted to drive the British out by force - and will then be punished for their actions |

⚠️ That last row contains the cruellest irony in the topic and it is examinable. ✅ Kenyatta was arrested, found guilty of terrorism and kept in jail for six years although he had publicly condemned violence and insisted the KAU had not been involved in organising the rebellion. ⚠️ The settler strategy of refusing to negotiate with the moderate produced exactly the radicalisation it claimed to fear, and then the moderate was imprisoned for it.

⚠️ The French produced the same effect deliberately and then noticed. ✅ In Tunisia the French banned New Destour and imprisoned Bourguiba in 1952, and then became aware of a disturbing trend: with Bourguiba and other moderate leaders in jail, the guerrilla movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate. ✅ They concluded that with a moderate like Bourguiba at the head of the country there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence, released him, and let him form a government.

⚠️ Generalise it, because it recurs: **imprisoning a moderate nationalist transfers the movement to people the metropole cannot negotiate with, and the metropole eventually has to release him on worse terms.** ✅ The British did the same calculation in the Gold Coast, releasing Nkrumah on realising that he had mass support. ✅ And they did it in Kenya, where Kenyatta was at last released in 1961 and was prime minister by May 1963.

### 3. The three instruments, and why the strike did the work

⚠️ The educated elite did not invent new political weapons. It took three metropolitan ones and used them against the metropole.

**The newspaper.** ✅ Azikiwe worked as a newspaper editor in the Gold Coast and after returning to Nigeria in 1937 founded a series of newspapers, soon gaining enormous prestige. ⚠️ A newspaper does something a party cannot: it creates a public that thinks of itself as one public. In a territory assembled from regions with different languages and histories, that is nation-building in the most literal sense.

**The party.** ✅ Nkrumah led the Convention People's Party from 1949, and in the 1951 elections - the first under the new constitution - the CPP won thirty-four seats out of thirty-eight. ⚠️ A party converts sentiment into a countable number, and a countable number is what makes a nationalist an unavoidable negotiating partner rather than an agitator.

**The general strike.** ⚠️ This is the instrument that actually moved dates, and it is the most under-used piece of evidence in student answers. ✅ Azikiwe in 1945 showed he meant business by organising an impressive general strike, which was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence. ✅ Nkrumah's campaign used boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950; he and other leaders were imprisoned, but the British, realising that he had mass support, soon released him and agreed to a new constitution which included the vote for all adults.

⚠️ Why the strike and not the demonstration? Because a colonial economy is an export economy, and an export economy is a chain: mine, railway, port, ship. ⚠️ A general strike does not persuade the administration; it stops the revenue. It also proves, in a form no governor can dispute in a despatch home, that the man claiming to speak for the population can actually move it.

⚠️ The colonial powers understood this perfectly, which is why organised labour was targeted directly. ✅ In 1949 the French government decided to clamp down on all nationalist movements, and many nationalist leaders **and trade unionists** were arrested, often denounced as communist agitators though without much evidence to support the accusations. ✅ In Morocco the new trade unions played an important role in the nationalist movement. ✅ And in South Africa the point was made in law a generation earlier: by a law of 1911 black workers were forbidden to strike and were barred from holding skilled jobs.

⚠️ Line those three facts up and a thesis appears: colonial states treated industrial action as the principal political threat, which is the best available evidence that it was.

### 4. Land, labour and the rural grievance

⚠️ The educated elite is the visible half of the movement. The grievances that made mass action possible were mostly about land and work, and the account records them precisely enough to use.

- ✅ **Kenya.** The main African grievance was the land situation: the most fertile farming land was on the highland plateau, but only white settlers were allowed to farm there. ⚠️ The resolution proves the grievance was accurate: ✅ when Britain changed course, Africans were allowed to settle in the fertile highland plateau, restrictions were lifted on what the Kikuyu could grow, and as a result coffee became one of the main crops. ⚠️ The restrictions had been protecting settler producers from African competition, which is a statement about economics, not about administration.
- ✅ **Algeria.** Over a million French settlers controlled something like a third of all the most fertile land, taken from the original Algerian owners during the century before 1940; the whites exported most of the crops they produced and used some of the land to grow vines for winemaking, which meant there was less food available for the growing African population, whose standard of living was clearly falling. ⚠️ That is a mechanism, not a complaint: export agriculture plus viticulture on the best land, against a rising population, produces measurable immiseration.
- ✅ **The Dutch East Indies.** The Dutch operated in a way similar to the French in Algeria - they grew crops for export and did very little to improve the living standards of the East Indians.
- ✅ **South Africa.** Reserved areas made up only about seven per cent of the total area and were not large enough to enable Africans to produce sufficient food for themselves and pay all their taxes; black Africans were forbidden to buy land outside the reserves; men mostly lived in barracks accommodation away from their wives and children; in the gold-mining industry Africans had to live in single-sex compounds with sometimes as many as ninety men sharing a dormitory.
- ✅ **The general colonial economy.** Most African territories had very little industry; this had been a deliberate policy by the colonial powers, whose intention was that the colonies should buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and provide food and raw materials.

⚠️ Put the last item first in any answer on economic grievance, because it converts a set of local complaints into a single designed structure. ⚠️ The absence of industry was not backwardness; it was policy. Lesson 23 shows what that policy cost after independence.

### 5. An honest statement about women in this evidence base

⚠️ This session is asked to teach women's participation where the evidence supports it, and integrity requires saying plainly what the evidence does and does not carry.

⚠️ What the record here **does** support is that colonial and racial systems structured women's lives in specific, documented ways, and that these are legitimate evidence in an answer on the social effects of colonial rule.

- ✅ In South Africa the pass system and the migrant-labour compound separated men from their wives and children by design.
- ✅ Marriage and sexual relations between whites and non-whites were forbidden, and police spied on anybody suspected of breaking the rules - a prohibition that operated on women's lives and bodies as its primary object.
- ✅ Black children went to separate schools and were given a much inferior education, which is a statement about the reproduction of the system across generations.
- ✅ The Freedom Charter of 1955 demanded equal pay for equal work, free medical care and free, compulsory and equal education - demands whose incidence is heavily gendered.
- ✅ After independence the same issue reappears in policy: in Burundi, new policies were introduced to safeguard the rights of women and children and to provide free education for primary-school children; ✅ and in Nigeria the adoption of Sharia law into the legal systems of twelve northern states by the end of 2002 produced punishments recorded as especially severe on women, including a death sentence by stoning imposed in Katsina state, a sentence overturned on appeal in Sokoto state, and the federal government's statement that it was totally opposed to such sentences.

⚠️ What this evidence base **does not** carry is a record of women's organisation *inside* the nationalist movements - the women's wings, market-women's associations, boycott committees and rural protests that a specialist history of African or Asian nationalism would document. ❌ No such organisation is asserted here, because it is not in the sources read for this session. ⚠️ It is left open rather than filled from memory. ⚠️ In an answer, use the structural evidence above with confidence and do not invent named women's movements to satisfy a rubric.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The syllabus's decolonisation clause covers the process and its effects on society, and this lesson is where "society" becomes concrete: who mobilised, through what institutions, over what grievances.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The recurring demand is on the role of Western-educated leaders in the rise of nationalism. ⚠️ The answer that scores has three moves: education supplied vocabulary and grievance simultaneously; the elite's instruments were the press, the party and the strike, all learned from the metropole; and the timetable moved only when elite leadership was joined to urban mass action, evidenced by the 1945 and 1950 general strikes and by Nkrumah's release.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Listing leaders and calling it an answer. ✅ Each name must carry a mechanism: Nkrumah the mass campaign, Azikiwe the press and the strike, Nyerere the neutralisation of settler fear, Kenyatta the gradualist outflanked by his own radicals.
2. ⚠️ Treating the elite as sufficient. ✅ The British released Nkrumah on realising he had mass support; the 1945 strike, not the newspapers running since 1937, prompted preparation for Nigerian independence.
3. ⚠️ Treating the elite as unnecessary. ⚠️ Mass grievance without an organisation that can negotiate produces repression, not transfer; Kenya's uprising was defeated and its political settlement still required Kenyatta's release.
4. ❌ Inventing evidence about women's organisations to balance an answer. ⚠️ Use the documented structural evidence and say what is not established.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ African nationalism spread rapidly after 1945 because more Africans were educated in Britain and the USA, where they were made aware of racial discrimination.
2. ✅ Colonialism was seen as the humiliation and exploitation of blacks by whites; the mass of Africans in the new towns were particularly receptive to nationalist ideas.
3. ✅ Nkrumah: educated in London and the USA; Convention People's Party from 1949; boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950; imprisoned, then released once the British realised he had mass support.
4. ✅ Azikiwe ("Zik"): educated in the USA; newspaper editor in the Gold Coast; returned to Nigeria 1937 and founded a series of newspapers; his 1945 general strike was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence.
5. ✅ Nyerere: educated at the University of Edinburgh; TANU; insisted the government must be African while assuring whites they had nothing to fear; Macmillan's government conceded independence with black majority rule in 1961.
6. ✅ Kenyatta: born 1894, Kikuyu, in Britain during the 1930s, returned 1947, led the KAU; wanted gradual majority rule; the radical Forty Group wanted to drive the British out by force.
7. ✅ Kenyatta was jailed six years although he had publicly condemned violence and said the KAU had not organised the rebellion.
8. ✅ France banned New Destour and jailed Bourguiba in 1952, then found the guerrilla movement becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate, and released him.
9. ⚠️ General rule: imprisoning the moderate transfers the movement to people the metropole cannot negotiate with.
10. ✅ Three instruments: the newspaper, the party (CPP won 34 of 38 seats in 1951) and the general strike.
11. ✅ France arrested nationalist leaders and trade unionists in 1949, often denouncing them as communist agitators without much evidence; Moroccan trade unions played an important role; South Africa's law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs.
12. ✅ Land and labour grievances: Kenya's highland plateau reserved to white settlers; Algeria's settlers holding about a third of the most fertile land and growing export crops and vines while African living standards fell; South Africa's seven per cent reserves, the ban on buying land outside them, barracks accommodation and single-sex compounds of up to ninety men.
13. ✅ Very little industry in most African territories was a deliberate colonial policy: colonies were to buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and provide food and raw materials.
14. ✅ Women in this evidence base: pass laws and compounds separating families; the prohibition of interracial marriage and sexual relations with police surveillance; inferior schooling; Freedom Charter demands on equal pay, medical care and education; post-independence policies for women and children in Burundi; Sharia punishments recorded as especially severe on women in twelve northern Nigerian states by the end of 2002, opposed by the federal government.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: the educated elite supplied the argument, the legitimacy and the international audience; the towns, the mines and the strike supplied the pressure; neither moved a timetable alone.

### Concept check

**Question:** A colonial governor in 1949 is told that a nationalist leader has founded three newspapers and won a large following, and separately that dock and railway workers have called a general strike. Which report will move his government's policy, and why? Support your answer with two named cases.

**Model answer:** The strike will move policy, because a colonial economy is an export chain and a general strike stops the revenue while also proving, in a form the governor cannot dispute in a despatch home, that nationalist leadership can actually move the population. The newspapers build the public that makes the strike possible, but they do not by themselves impose a cost. Nigeria is the first case: Azikiwe had been founding newspapers since his return in 1937 and had gained enormous prestige, yet it was the impressive general strike he organised in 1945 that was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence. The Gold Coast is the second: Nkrumah's campaign combined boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950, and although he was imprisoned, the British realised he had mass support, released him and agreed to a new constitution giving the vote to all adults, an elected Assembly and an eleven-man Executive Council with eight members chosen by the Assembly. The colonial powers' own behaviour confirms the ranking: in 1949 the French arrested nationalist leaders and trade unionists together, and South Africa had forbidden black workers to strike by a law of 1911.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that the educated elite were therefore unimportant and that mass action alone explains independence. Mass grievance without an organisation capable of negotiating produces repression rather than transfer - Kenya's uprising was militarily defeated - and the settlement still required a leader the British could deal with, which is why Kenyatta was released in 1961 and was prime minister by May 1963. The elite supplied the party, the constitutional technique and the negotiating legitimacy; the correct formulation is that neither half moved a timetable without the other.

---

## Lesson 5 - Pan-Africanism: from Garvey and Du Bois to Addis Ababa 1963

Progress: 5/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the diaspora origin of Pan-Africanism; Marcus Garvey and W. E. B. Du Bois; the students who carried it home; Nkrumah's federal project and the states that backed it; why the majority refused; the retreat to the Organization of African Unity in 1963; and the honest assessment of what the movement did achieve

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's section on Pan-Africanism, including its diaspora origins with Garvey and Du Bois, its adoption by African students mainly from British colonies, the federal United States of Africa ambition, Nkrumah's conferences, the states supporting and opposing, the Addis Ababa decision of 1963 and the Organization of African Unity, and the separate record in the Ghana section of the economic union with Guinea and Mali
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session sits in the next lesson and is limited there
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the argument that travelled home and then shrank

```text
   STAGE 1  DIASPORA ORIGIN (early 20th century)
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   Claim: all people of African descent, WHEREVER THEY LIVED,
          are united by the same cultural and spiritual heritage
   Carriers: MARCUS GARVEY - self-educated Jamaican, founder of
             the Universal Negro Improvement Association
             W. E. B. DU BOIS - first African American to earn
             a doctorate from Harvard
   Note: "first publicized by people of African origin living
          OUTSIDE Africa"
                      |
                      v
   STAGE 2  TRANSMISSION (by the end of the Second World War)
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   Some African students, MAINLY FROM BRITISH COLONIES, take it up
                      |
                      v
   STAGE 3  THE DOUBLE AMBITION
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   (a) encouragement to independence            <- ACHIEVED
   (b) after independence: abandon the
       artificial frontiers set up by the
       Europeans and build a federal
       "United States of Africa"                <- NOT ACHIEVED
                      |
                      v
   STAGE 4  THE POLITICAL TEST (Nkrumah's conferences)
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   FOR:      Guinea, Mali, Morocco
   AGAINST:  Ethiopia and Liberia (independent for generations),
             Nigeria, Sierra Leone, almost all former French colonies
   REASON:   "having just won their independence, they saw little
             point in surrendering a large proportion of it"
   SUSPICION: some leaders suspected Nkrumah of developing
             delusions of grandeur
                      |
                      v
   STAGE 5  THE SETTLEMENT (Addis Ababa, 1963)
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   The prospect of a United States of Africa had DISAPPEARED.
   Instead: the ORGANIZATION OF AFRICAN UNITY - "a much less
   binding arrangement, while still displaying a sort of unity"
                      |
                      v
   VERDICT: "But pan-Africanism had not been totally irrelevant -
   it had been an important influence on the rise of nationalist
   movements in many of the former colonies."
```

⚠️ The shape of that diagram - a large claim arriving from outside, achieving one of its two objectives completely and the other not at all - is the shape of the answer. Do not write Pan-Africanism as a failure and do not write it as a triumph. Write it as a movement whose *instrumental* aim succeeded and whose *constitutive* aim was defeated by the success of the first.

### 1. The idea, and why it came from outside Africa

✅ Early in the twentieth century there was an important development in African thinking which emphasised that all people of African descent, wherever they lived, were united by the same cultural and spiritual heritage. ✅ Pan-Africanism, as it became known, was first publicised by people of African origin living outside Africa.

⚠️ Stop on that last clause, because it is causally important rather than merely biographical. A person living in a colony in the Gold Coast in 1910 experiences a specific administration, a specific district officer and a specific set of local grievances. A person of African descent living in Jamaica, London or the United States experiences something else: a category. He is treated as a member of a group defined by descent across continents, by people who have never heard of his district. ⚠️ Being treated as a race is the experience that makes a *racial* political claim thinkable. The territorial subject experiences colonialism; the diaspora subject experiences the global colour line. Pan-Africanism is the political theory of the second experience, which is precisely why it was formulated by people outside Africa and exported into it.

✅ At the forefront were **Marcus Garvey**, a self-educated Jamaican who had founded the Universal Negro Improvement Association, and **W. E. B. Du Bois**, the first African American to earn a doctorate from Harvard.

⚠️ Note the contrast in those two descriptions - self-educated and Harvard-doctorate - because it is not decorative. The movement was carried by a mass-organisational tradition and by a scholarly one simultaneously, and that combination is what allowed it to be at once a popular identity and an intellectual programme. ⚠️ It also anticipates the split you will see in Lesson 4's terms: argument and pressure, supplied from different directions.

### 2. Transmission: the students, and a very specific route

✅ Gradually these ideas spread, and by the end of the Second World War some African students, mainly from British colonies, had taken up Pan-Africanism.

⚠️ "Mainly from British colonies" is a small phrase carrying a large consequence. ⚠️ Recall from Lesson 4 that African nationalism spread rapidly after 1945 because more Africans were being educated in Britain and the USA. The same educational pipeline that produced the nationalist leadership produced the pan-African commitment, which is why the two arrived together in the same people.

⚠️ This also explains an asymmetry you will meet at Stage 4. ✅ Almost all the former French colonies were among the strongest opponents of the federal project. ⚠️ Their leaders had been formed in a different metropolitan system - one that treated the territories as if they were part of France, offered assimilation rather than separate development, and in 1958 offered membership of a French Community. ⚠️ A leadership educated inside a project of union with Europe was unlikely to want an immediate union with Africa, and in the French African case the alternative was not abstract: it came with aid attached.

### 3. The double ambition, and why the second half was radical

✅ Not only was Pan-Africanism an encouragement to their ambitions of independence, it also inspired them to think beyond that: if all Africans shared the same social and cultural ties, it meant that the ultimate goal after independence must be to abandon the artificial frontiers set up by the Europeans and have a sort of federal United States of Africa along the same lines as the United States of America.

⚠️ Take the phrase **artificial frontiers set up by the Europeans** seriously, because it is the intellectual bridge between this lesson and Lesson 23. Pan-Africanism was the only major political programme of the period that proposed to *solve* the border problem rather than inherit it. ⚠️ Everyone agreed the frontiers were arbitrary; only the pan-Africanists proposed to do something about it at the level of the state system itself.

⚠️ And that is precisely why it lost. Abolishing arbitrary frontiers requires the states created by those frontiers to dissolve themselves, and those states were the prize that the nationalist movements had just won. ⚠️ The programme required its own beneficiaries to give away what they had spent thirty years obtaining.

### 4. Nkrumah's campaign and the vote against it

✅ Kwame Nkrumah, who was to become the first prime minister of a semi-independent Gold Coast and then the first president of Ghana, was a strong believer in Pan-Africanism. ✅ He wasted no time before organising meetings and conferences of African leaders in which he pressed the advantages of African unification. ✅ He also supported the Organization of African Unity, set up in 1963, and ✅ as a start an economic union was formed with Guinea and Mali, though nothing much came of it, while his dream of an African federal state quickly faded.

✅ Some states supported the idea, including Guinea, Mali and Morocco, ✅ but a majority were not impressed - having just won their independence, they saw little point in surrendering a large proportion of it by entering a huge political federation. ✅ Some of the other leaders suspected that Nkrumah was developing delusions of grandeur, seeing himself as the president of a federal Africa. ✅ Strongest in their opposition were Ethiopia and Liberia, which had been independent for generations, together with Nigeria, Sierra Leone and almost all the former French colonies.

⚠️ That list of opponents is worth reading as an argument rather than a roll-call, because each group refused for a different reason and the reasons are examinable.

| ✅ Opponent | ⚠️ Reason for refusing | ⚠️ What it reveals |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Ethiopia and Liberia | Independent for generations | Sovereignty long held is valued more, not less; states with the longest experience of independence were the least willing to pool it |
| ✅ Nigeria | The largest British African colony, with a population of over sixty million and its own federal problem | A large state gains least from federation and risks most; Nigeria was already struggling to federate three regions and had no interest in federating a continent |
| ✅ Sierra Leone | A small West African neighbour of Ghana | Small neighbours of an ambitious state fear absorption more than they fear isolation |
| ✅ Almost all the former French colonies | Recently offered, and in eleven cases accepted, a French Community with aid attached | A territory that has just chosen one union does not immediately join a rival one |
| ✅ Guinea, Mali, Morocco (the supporters) | Guinea had refused de Gaulle's plan and lost all French aid; Mali and Morocco had their own reasons for seeking alternatives | ⚠️ Support for pan-African union correlates with having no metropolitan patron to lose |

⚠️ That final row is the analytical prize, and it is a genuinely strong line in an answer: **the states that backed continental federation were disproportionately those that had already cut themselves off from metropolitan support.** ✅ Guinea, under Sékou Touré, returned a ninety-five per cent vote against de Gaulle's plan, was given independence immediately in 1958 and had all French aid stopped; ✅ an economic union with Guinea and Mali was Nkrumah's first practical step. ⚠️ Pan-African union was, among other things, the alternative available to states that had refused neo-colonial dependence - which tells you both why they wanted it and why the states still receiving French aid did not.

⚠️ The suspicion of Nkrumah's motives should also be reported, not suppressed, because it is a real obstacle rather than a slur. ⚠️ A federal project needs a first president, and the man proposing it is the obvious candidate; any leader advocating continental union is therefore vulnerable to the charge that he is advocating his own elevation. ⚠️ This is a structural problem for all federal projects proposed by an individual, and it has nothing specifically to do with Nkrumah's character.

### 5. Addis Ababa 1963: what was actually agreed

✅ By 1963 the prospect of a United States of Africa had disappeared when a conference of African countries at Addis Ababa, in Ethiopia, decided that the best way forward would be for them all to join an Organization of African Unity - a much less binding arrangement, while still displaying a sort of unity.

⚠️ Read the venue as part of the outcome. ✅ Addis Ababa is the capital of Ethiopia, and Ethiopia was among the strongest opponents of federation. ⚠️ The continental body was founded in the capital of the state most committed to keeping continental bodies weak, and the result was an organisation that displayed unity without binding anyone.

⚠️ The OAU's subsequent record confirms the design. Three findings from this session's later lessons should be gathered here, because together they form a judgement:

- ✅ Neither the UN, the Commonwealth nor the Organization of African Unity was able to mediate in the Nigerian civil war of 1967-70.
- ✅ The OAU sent a peacekeeping force to Burundi - the first time it had ever taken such action - and was unable to prevent the continuing massacres and ethnic cleansing.
- ✅ In Rwanda in 1994 the Organization of African Unity did not even condemn the genocide, let alone try to prevent it, nor did any other African state take any action or issue public condemnation.

⚠️ These are severe findings and they should be stated plainly. ⚠️ But they should be stated as consequences of the 1963 design decision rather than as a moral failing of African statesmanship: an organisation deliberately constituted as non-binding, by states that had just refused to pool sovereignty, does not acquire enforcement capacity later merely because a crisis arrives.

⚠️ One qualification prevents the judgement from becoming a caricature. ✅ The OAU condemned apartheid, was particularly critical of the continued South African occupation of South West Africa, ✅ and Nyerere's prestige was at its height when he was chosen as its chairman for 1984-5. ⚠️ On the one question where all its members agreed and no member's sovereignty was at stake, the OAU was an effective diplomatic instrument. That is exactly what a non-binding organisation of sovereign states is good for.

### 6. The verdict the account itself offers

✅ But Pan-Africanism had not been totally irrelevant - it had been an important influence on the rise of nationalist movements in many of the former colonies.

⚠️ That is the balanced formulation and it is worth adopting. ⚠️ Expressed as a mechanism: Pan-Africanism supplied the *pre-national* identity that made national movements possible. Before a Gold Coast nationalism could exist, there had to be a claim that Africans were a people with standing to demand self-government, and that claim was continental and diasporic before it was territorial. ⚠️ Having done that work, the idea was discarded by the very movements it had enabled, because they had become governments with borders to defend.

### Comparative note: the two continental projects of the 1960s

⚠️ It is worth setting Pan-Africanism beside the other unification project of the same decade, because the comparison is instructive and is the kind of value addition that lifts an answer.

⚠️ European integration in the same years proceeded from the opposite direction: states that had recently fought each other pooled *specific functions* - coal, steel, tariffs - while retaining sovereignty, and deferred political union indefinitely. ⚠️ Nkrumah proposed the reverse: political federation first, on the basis of a shared identity, with functional co-operation to follow. ✅ What actually survived was the functional fragment - an economic union with Guinea and Mali, though nothing much came of it.

⚠️ The lesson is not that federation is impossible but that the order matters: shared identity is a weaker foundation for union than shared functional interest, because identity does not by itself tell any government what it would gain next year.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The syllabus's decolonisation clause covers the redrawal of national boundaries, and Pan-Africanism is the one major movement of the period that proposed to undo the redrawal rather than inherit it. ⚠️ It is also the necessary background to any question on African unity, regional organisation or the intellectual origins of African nationalism.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect it inside a broader question on African nationalism, on the role of ideas in decolonisation, or on why post-colonial African states retained colonial borders. ⚠️ The high-value move in all three is the same: state the double ambition, show that the first half succeeded and the second failed, and explain that the second failed *because* the first succeeded.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating Pan-Africanism as an African idea in origin. ✅ It was first publicised by people of African origin living outside Africa - Garvey in the Caribbean tradition and Du Bois in the American one - and reached Africa through students, mainly from British colonies.
2. ⚠️ Treating the OAU as the fulfilment of Pan-Africanism. ✅ It was the settlement that followed the *disappearance* of the federal prospect, and was explicitly a much less binding arrangement.
3. ⚠️ Explaining the refusal of federation by African disunity. ✅ The stated reason is that states, having just won independence, saw little point in surrendering a large proportion of it - a rational sovereignty calculation, not a failure of solidarity.
4. ❌ Attributing the term *neo-colonialism* to Pan-Africanism as a theory. ⚠️ The account records it as the name given to a practice of post-independence economic influence, and separately quotes Nkrumah's own formulation from his book on the subject. Lesson 24 handles this carefully.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Pan-Africanism: all people of African descent, wherever they lived, united by the same cultural and spiritual heritage.
2. ✅ First publicised by people of African origin living outside Africa.
3. ✅ Marcus Garvey: self-educated Jamaican, founder of the Universal Negro Improvement Association.
4. ✅ W. E. B. Du Bois: first African American to earn a doctorate from Harvard.
5. ✅ By the end of the Second World War some African students, mainly from British colonies, had taken it up.
6. ✅ Double ambition: encouragement to independence, and, after independence, abandoning the artificial frontiers set up by the Europeans for a federal United States of Africa on the American model.
7. ✅ Nkrumah, first prime minister of a semi-independent Gold Coast and first president of Ghana, was a strong believer and organised conferences of African leaders.
8. ✅ Supporters: Guinea, Mali and Morocco. ✅ An economic union was formed with Guinea and Mali, though nothing much came of it.
9. ✅ Opponents: a majority, who having just won independence saw little point in surrendering a large proportion of it; strongest were Ethiopia and Liberia, independent for generations, together with Nigeria, Sierra Leone and almost all the former French colonies.
10. ✅ Some leaders suspected Nkrumah of developing delusions of grandeur, seeing himself as president of a federal Africa.
11. ✅ By 1963 the prospect had disappeared; a conference at Addis Ababa decided on the Organization of African Unity, a much less binding arrangement while still displaying a sort of unity.
12. ✅ OAU limits in practice: unable to mediate in Nigeria's civil war; its first-ever peacekeeping deployment, to Burundi, could not prevent the massacres; it did not even condemn the Rwandan genocide.
13. ✅ OAU effectiveness where interests aligned: it condemned apartheid and was particularly critical of South Africa's continued occupation of South West Africa; Nyerere chaired it in 1984-5.
14. ✅ The account's verdict: Pan-Africanism had not been totally irrelevant - it had been an important influence on the rise of nationalist movements in many of the former colonies.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: Pan-Africanism supplied the pre-national identity that made territorial nationalism possible, and was then defeated by the territorial states it had helped to create.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the paradox that Pan-Africanism helped to produce independent African states and was then rejected by them, and identify the one group of states that did support continental federation and what they had in common.

**Model answer:** The paradox resolves once the movement's two aims are separated. Its first aim was to encourage independence, and for that purpose it supplied something territorial nationalism could not generate on its own: a claim that all people of African descent shared a heritage and therefore had standing to demand self-government. Its second aim was to abandon the artificial frontiers set up by the Europeans and create a federal United States of Africa. The second aim could only be achieved by the governments the first aim had brought into existence, and those governments were precisely the beneficiaries of the frontiers in question - which is why a majority were not impressed, seeing little point in surrendering a large proportion of independence they had just won. Ethiopia and Liberia, independent for generations, were among the strongest opponents, along with Nigeria, Sierra Leone and almost all the former French colonies, and by 1963 the Addis Ababa conference had replaced federation with the deliberately much less binding Organization of African Unity. The supporters were Guinea, Mali and Morocco, and what the first two had in common is decisive: Guinea under Sekou Toure had voted ninety-five per cent against de Gaulle's plan in 1958, was granted immediate independence and had all French aid stopped, and Nkrumah's first practical step was an economic union with Guinea and Mali. Continental union was most attractive to states with no metropolitan patron left to lose.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Pan-Africanism was therefore a failure. The account's own verdict is that it had not been totally irrelevant and had been an important influence on the rise of nationalist movements in many former colonies, and it also supplied the OAU, which was effective on the one question where members' interests aligned and no sovereignty was at stake - the condemnation of apartheid and of South Africa's occupation of South West Africa. The accurate judgement is that its instrumental aim succeeded completely and its constitutive aim failed completely, and that the second failure was produced by the first success.
