## Lesson 6 - Outside pressures: Washington, Moscow and the United Nations

Progress: 6/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: American anti-imperialism and the market interest behind it; the containment argument and Porter's qualification; Soviet denunciation; the United Nations demand for a step-by-step programme and the instrument of December 1960; the Special Committee on Decolonization; and the single dated current linkage carried by this session

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's section on outside pressures, including American hostility to imperialism and its 1776 precedent, Roosevelt's and Truman's pressure on Britain over India, Peter Clarke on Churchill's imperialism irritating the Americans, the communism argument and Bernard Porter's qualification of it for Africa, the American interest in new markets and in ending imperially protected preference, the United Nations demand for a step-by-step programme of decolonization, and the Soviet denunciations
CA search: "United Nations Special Committee on Decolonization 2026 session Non-Self-Governing Territories" - a genuine linkage exists because the institutional programme created in 1960-61 is still running and still has a defined caseload
CA found: United Nations News, 16 February 2026, reporting the opening of the 2026 session of the Special Committee on Decolonization
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three external actors, three motives, one instrument

```text
        WASHINGTON                MOSCOW                 UNITED NATIONS
        ==========                ======                 ==============

 STATED   hostile to           constantly              firmly against
 POSITION imperialism,         denounced               imperialism;
          remembering 1776     imperialism             demanded a
                                                       STEP-BY-STEP
                                                       PROGRAMME for
                                                       decolonization

 ACTUAL   (a) fear that        (a) ideological         "under American
 INTERESTS    delay would          opposition           influence"
              encourage       (b) competitive
              COMMUNISM           advantage among      => the instrument,
   BUT: Porter - in Africa        new states              14 December 1960:
       still comparatively                                the Declaration on
       little communist                                   the Granting of
       influence                                          Independence to
                                                          Colonial Countries
          (b) NEW MARKETS                                 and Peoples
              "potential markets                          [resolution 1514 (XV)]
              into which they
              could force their                      => the machinery, 1961:
              way and establish                         the Special Committee
              both economic and                         on Decolonization
              political influence"                       (the C-24)
          (c) imperially
              protected markets
              gave Britain and
              other Europeans
              "an unfair advantage"

 EFFECT ON THE COLONIES: "As well as putting the European states under
 pressure, this encouraged nationalists all over the world to INTENSIFY
 their campaigns."
```

⚠️ Read the middle row of the American column. Anti-imperialism with a market interest attached is not hypocrisy to be denounced; it is a structure to be described. ⚠️ And it is the structure that explains how a world of formally sovereign states could coexist with continuing economic dependence - which is Lesson 24's subject.

### 1. Why external pressure is a separate cause and not a footnote

⚠️ It is tempting to fold outside pressure into either the metropolitan thesis or nationalist agency. Resist it, because it does something neither does. ✅ The account is explicit about its dual effect: as well as putting the European states under pressure, it encouraged nationalists all over the world to intensify their campaigns.

⚠️ That dual action is the whole point. External anti-colonialism raised the cost of holding empire *and* lowered the cost of resisting it, simultaneously, for every territory at once. ⚠️ It is the only factor in this topic that operates on both sides of the equation at the same time, which is why a global process produced synchronised results - the 1960 cluster you noticed in Lesson 1.

### 2. Washington: four motives, honestly ranked

✅ **The precedent.** The USA, no doubt remembering that they had been the earliest part of the British Empire to declare independence in 1776, was hostile to imperialism - the building up of empires and owning of colonies.

✅ **The charter argument.** During the war, President Roosevelt made it clear that he took the Atlantic Charter to apply to all peoples, not just those taken over by the Germans. ✅ He and his successor Truman pressurised the British government to speed up independence for India.

✅ **The personal friction.** Peter Clarke points out that Churchill's imperialism irritated the Americans to such an extent that they were determined not to do anything that would help Britain to keep its empire.

✅ **The containment argument.** One reason given by the Americans for wanting to see the end of the European empires was that delays in granting independence to European colonies in Asia and Africa would encourage the development of communism in those areas.

⚠️ Now the qualification, which is where the marks are. ✅ While there was clearly some truth in this argument in the case of Asia, Bernard Porter was convinced that in the case of Africa there was still comparatively little communist influence.

⚠️ That is a historian correcting a contemporary justification, and you should be able to say what follows from the correction. If the communist threat was real in Asia and largely absent in Africa, then the containment argument was a *description* in one continent and a *rationalisation* in the other. ⚠️ Which raises the question of what the American interest in African decolonisation actually was - and the account answers it directly.

✅ **The market motive.** More important was the fact that the Americans looked on the newly-independent nations as potential markets into which they could force their way and establish both economic and political influence. ✅ In the eyes of the USA, imperially protected markets gave the British and other Europeans an unfair advantage.

⚠️ Read those two sentences slowly. They say that the leading anti-colonial power of the period had a direct commercial interest in the dissolution of European empires, because empire operated as a system of preferential trade that excluded American goods. ⚠️ American anti-imperialism and American commercial expansion were the same policy seen from two angles.

⚠️ Two consequences follow and both are examinable.

⚠️ **First**, it predicts that American support for self-determination would be conditional on the political character of the successor. ⚠️ The record bears this out with painful consistency. ✅ In Indonesia the USA, operating via the CIA, was involved in the 1965 coup against Sukarno, because they did not like his toleration of the Communist Party or the way in which he was acting as leader of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World. ✅ In Ghana the American CIA gave the 1966 coup against Nkrumah its full backing, because the USA disapproved of Nkrumah's links with communist states. ✅ In Angola the Americans backed the FNLA with advisers, cash and armaments against the Marxist MPLA. ✅ In East Timor, President Ford gave Suharto the go-ahead because FRETILIN was thought to be Marxist and an independent East Timor might have socialist or even communist leanings. ✅ In Western Sahara, just when it seemed the Moroccans were prepared to negotiate peace, President Reagan encouraged them to continue the fight and stepped up aid to Morocco.

⚠️ **Second**, it explains the general pattern the account states at the end of the chapter: ✅ in countries where new governments were prepared to introduce socialist policies, such as nationalising resources or foreign businesses, or where governments showed any sign of being pro-communist, the western countries disapproved, often responding by cutting off aid or helping to destabilise the government, and in some cases even overthrowing governments - ✅ which happened in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica.

⚠️ State this carefully in an answer, because the careless version is wrong. ❌ The claim is not that the United States opposed decolonisation. ✅ It pressed for it, repeatedly and effectively. ⚠️ The claim is that it supported the transfer of *formal* sovereignty while intervening against successor governments whose economic policy threatened access - which is a precise statement of what "political independence without economic decolonisation" means in practice.

### 3. Moscow: the other denunciation

✅ The USSR also added its voice to the chorus and constantly denounced imperialism.

⚠️ The account says little more, and this session will not invent more. ⚠️ Two observations are safely supported by evidence elsewhere in this material and are worth making.

⚠️ First, Soviet support was materially real where it was offered. ✅ The nationalists in the Portuguese colonies, who all had strong Marxist connections, received economic and military aid from the communist bloc. ✅ The MPLA in Angola received Russian weapons and a Cuban army, which enabled it to defeat both invading forces by March 1976.

⚠️ Second, Soviet involvement carried a cost for the recipient that had nothing to do with the merits of the case. ✅ In Western Sahara, the fact that Sahara had been officially recognised by the USSR was enough to arouse American suspicions, and the account's own verdict on the result is blunt: ✅ yet another new Third World country had become a victim of superpower self-interest.

⚠️ That is the mechanism to write: **recognition by one superpower converted a colonial question into a Cold War question, and a Cold War question could not be settled on its own terms.** Lesson 23 shows the same mechanism operating in Angola and the Congo.

### 4. The United Nations: from denunciation to machinery

✅ The United Nations Organization, under American influence, came out firmly against imperialism and demanded a step-by-step programme for decolonisation.

⚠️ That sentence names an outcome without naming the instrument, and the instrument is standard examinable knowledge. Three items complete it.

✅ **The Declaration.** The Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples was adopted by the United Nations General Assembly on **14 December 1960**, as **resolution 1514 (XV)**. ✅ It was adopted without a single expressed dissent in the General Assembly. ✅ Its substantive principles include that the subjection of peoples to alien subjugation, domination and exploitation constitutes a denial of fundamental human rights; that all peoples have the right to self-determination, including the right freely to determine their political status and freely to pursue their economic, social and cultural development; that all armed action or repressive measures against dependent peoples shall cease; and that immediate steps shall be taken in Trust and Non-Self-Governing Territories to transfer all powers to the peoples of those territories. ✅ It also warns against any attempt aimed at the partial or total disruption of the national unity and the territorial integrity of a country.

⚠️ That last clause is the one to notice, because it is the point at which the United Nations chose the frontiers over the Pan-Africanists. ⚠️ The Declaration granted self-determination to peoples *inside the existing colonial boundaries* and simultaneously protected those boundaries against disruption. ⚠️ Lesson 19 shows why: the Congo's Katanga secession was live at exactly that moment. ⚠️ The consequence is one of the most important structural facts in this entire topic: **the international order underwrote the arbitrary colonial map at the moment it dismantled colonial rule**, and every secession crisis from Biafra to Eritrea ran into that decision.

✅ **Why 1960 and not earlier.** The General Assembly's composition had changed. ✅ Sixteen new states had been admitted in one step in 1955, bringing the total to seventy-six; ✅ and **seventeen** more were admitted during 1960 itself - sixteen on **20 September** and **Nigeria on 7 October** - taking membership from **eighty-two to ninety-nine**. ✅ The resulting voting coalition was variously styled the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77, the Bandung group, or the developing or Third World countries, and it supplied both the cohesion and the tactical competence to secure adoption.

⚠️ This is the neatest illustration in the topic of decolonisation becoming self-reinforcing. Each new independent state was a vote; enough votes changed the institution; the changed institution accelerated the creation of more states. ⚠️ Lesson 7 shows the same coalition meeting outside the United Nations five years earlier.

✅ **The machinery.** The Special Committee on Decolonization, known as the **C-24**, was established by the General Assembly in **1961** to monitor progress towards granting independence to territories that had not yet attained a full measure of self-government, as defined under Chapter XI of the United Nations Charter; its mandate stems from the 1960 Declaration.

### 5. The single current linkage in this session

📰 **United Nations News, 16 February 2026.** 📰 At the opening of the 2026 session of the Special Committee on Decolonization, the Secretary-General, in remarks delivered by his Chef de Cabinet Courtenay Rattray, recorded that since 1945 more than eighty former colonies comprising some 750 million people have gained independence, while **seventeen Non-Self-Governing Territories remain on the United Nations list**, home to nearly two million people and ranging from Western Sahara in Africa to small island territories in the Caribbean and Pacific. 📰 He stated that the legacy of colonialism has left profound scars through deeply rooted mechanisms of economic exploitation, as well as in the form of racism, inequality and persistent exclusion from decision-making bodies, and set three priorities for the remaining process: inclusive dialogue among the Territories, administering Powers and Member States, addressing each case individually and in accordance with the Charter, the 1960 Declaration and the relevant General Assembly resolutions; the central role of young people; and climate action, since the majority of the Territories are small islands facing rising sea levels, coastal erosion and more frequent extreme weather.

⚠️ **The limit on this item, stated here in the text.** This is the only current linkage in this session and it is not used again. ⚠️ It is an official institutional statement and is therefore reliable evidence of the United Nations' own stated position, procedure and caseload; it is not independent evidence about the merits of any individual territorial dispute, and nothing in it is a substitute for historical evidence. ❌ No answer on historical decolonisation should be built on it. ⚠️ Its legitimate use is one sentence: the programme created in 1960-61 is a standing institutional process with a defined and still unfinished caseload, which is why Western Sahara appears in this session as an unfinished case rather than a closed one.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Decolonisation is a named syllabus term, and international pressure is one of the three standard causal blocks alongside metropolitan weakness and colonial nationalism. ⚠️ The United Nations instrument is also the bridge between this topic and any question on international institutions and self-determination.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually as one component of a causes question. ⚠️ The discriminating move is to state the *interest* behind each external actor's declared position, and then to point out that the same American interest that accelerated decolonisation also produced interventions against successor governments - which converts a list of causes into a causal argument that runs through to Lesson 24.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Presenting American anti-imperialism as purely principled. ✅ The account ranks the market motive above the containment argument in importance, and records that imperially protected markets were seen as giving Europeans an unfair advantage.
2. ⚠️ Presenting it as purely cynical. ✅ Roosevelt's reading of the Atlantic Charter and the pressure he and Truman applied over India were real and had effects.
3. ⚠️ Accepting the communism argument as uniform. ✅ Porter was convinced that in Africa there was still comparatively little communist influence, even though there was some truth in the argument for Asia.
4. ❌ Writing that the United Nations "granted" independence. ⚠️ It demanded a step-by-step programme, adopted a Declaration in December 1960 and created a monitoring committee in 1961; the transfers were made by the administering powers.
5. ⚠️ Missing the territorial-integrity clause. ⚠️ The 1960 Declaration protects the inherited frontiers in the same breath as it grants self-determination, which is why secession after independence had almost no international support.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The USA was hostile to imperialism, remembering that it had been the earliest part of the British Empire to declare independence in 1776.
2. ✅ Roosevelt took the Atlantic Charter to apply to all peoples; he and Truman pressed Britain to speed up Indian independence.
3. ✅ Peter Clarke: Churchill's imperialism irritated the Americans so much they were determined not to help Britain keep its empire.
4. ✅ American containment argument: delays would encourage communism in Asia and Africa. ✅ Porter's qualification: some truth for Asia, but comparatively little communist influence in Africa.
5. ✅ More important was the American view of new nations as potential markets into which they could force their way and establish economic and political influence; imperially protected markets gave Europeans an unfair advantage.
6. ✅ Pattern of intervention against successor governments: Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica.
7. ✅ Named instances: the CIA's involvement in the 1965 Indonesian coup; American encouragement of the 1966 coup against Nkrumah, which the textbook account calls full CIA backing though the claim is contested; American advisers, cash and armaments to the FNLA in Angola; Ford's go-ahead to Suharto over East Timor; Reagan's encouragement to Morocco in Western Sahara.
8. ✅ The USSR constantly denounced imperialism; the communist bloc supplied economic and military aid to the Portuguese-colony nationalists; the MPLA received Russian weapons and a Cuban army and defeated both invasions by March 1976.
9. ✅ Soviet recognition of Sahara aroused American suspicions; the account's verdict is that yet another new Third World country had become a victim of superpower self-interest.
10. ✅ The United Nations, under American influence, came out firmly against imperialism and demanded a step-by-step programme for decolonisation.
11. ✅ Resolution 1514 (XV), the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples, adopted 14 December 1960 without a single expressed dissent.
12. ✅ Its principles: alien subjugation denies fundamental human rights; all peoples have the right to self-determination including economic, social and cultural development; armed action against dependent peoples to cease; immediate steps to transfer all powers in Trust and Non-Self-Governing Territories; and a warning against disrupting the national unity and territorial integrity of a country.
13. ✅ It became possible because the Assembly had changed: sixteen new states admitted in 1955 taking the total to seventy-six, and seventeen more during 1960 - sixteen on 20 September and Nigeria on 7 October - taking membership from eighty-two to ninety-nine, forming a coalition styled variously the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77, the Bandung group or the Third World.
14. ✅ The Special Committee on Decolonization (C-24) was established in 1961 to monitor progress under Chapter XI of the Charter.
15. 📰 One dated current item, 16 February 2026: seventeen Non-Self-Governing Territories remain; more than eighty former colonies with some 750 million people have gained independence since 1945. Used once, here, and not carried into any historical argument.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: external pressure was the only factor operating on both sides of the equation at once, raising the cost of holding empire and lowering the cost of resisting it - which is why the process synchronised globally.

### Concept check

**Question:** The United Nations Declaration of December 1960 is usually remembered for granting the right of self-determination. It contains a second provision that shaped African and Asian politics for the next forty years in the opposite direction. Identify it and explain its consequences.

**Model answer:** The second provision is the warning against any attempt aimed at the partial or total disruption of the national unity and the territorial integrity of a country. The Declaration therefore granted self-determination to colonial peoples within their existing colonial boundaries while simultaneously protecting those boundaries against any further division. The context explains the drafting: the Congo had become independent on 30 June 1960 and mineral-rich Katanga had declared itself independent under Tshombe within weeks, so the Assembly was legislating with an active secession in front of it. The consequence was that the arbitrary frontiers which the Pan-Africanists had wanted to abolish were underwritten by international law at the very moment colonial rule was being dismantled, and every subsequent secession attempt faced an international presumption against it. Biafra is the clearest illustration: when Ojukwu declared the eastern region independent in May 1967, neither the United Nations, the Commonwealth nor the Organization of African Unity was able or willing to mediate, and the war ran to January 1970 with federal unity preserved. The deeper point is that self-determination was defined territorially rather than ethnically, so that the unit entitled to a state was whatever unit the colonial power had administered.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading American anti-imperialism as either wholly principled or wholly cynical. Both readings lose marks because the evidence supports a structure rather than a motive: the United States genuinely pressed for the transfer of formal sovereignty - Roosevelt on the Atlantic Charter, pressure on Britain over India, the United Nations position under American influence - while its more important interest, as the account ranks it, was access to new markets closed by imperial preference. That combination produced precisely the outcome it implies: support for independence alongside intervention against successor governments in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica.

## Lesson 7 - Bandung 1955, Belgrade 1961 and Global South agency

Progress: 7/24 | Stage: Foundation | Subtopic: the Asian-African Conference of April 1955 and its ten principles; the non-aligned summit at Belgrade in September 1961; the voting bloc these meetings created at the United Nations; the limits of the project; and the boundary between this topic, the Cold War topic and Indian foreign policy

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the study material for this topic places Bandung and Non-Aligned Movement detail outside the world-history decolonisation file, and the textbook account's decolonisation chapters do not narrate either conference; the connecting facts used here were therefore fixed in advance and verified once, with their sources listed at the end, and the United Nations legal commentary read for Lesson 6 independently records the resulting Assembly coalition
CA search: not sought - this session carries exactly one current linkage and it was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: two conferences, one claim, and what each added

```text
   BANDUNG, Indonesia           BELGRADE, Yugoslavia
   18-24 April 1955             1-6 September 1961
   ==================           ====================
   29 countries                 25 founding states
   AFRO-ASIAN                   NON-ALIGNED
   (identity-based:             (posture-based:
    the formerly and             refusal to join
    still colonised)             either Cold War bloc)

   Associated figures: Tito (Yugoslavia), Nehru (India),
   Nasser (Egypt), Nkrumah (Ghana), Sukarno (Indonesia)

   THE TEN PRINCIPLES adopted at Bandung (Dasasila Bandung),
   in substance:
     1. human rights and the UN Charter
     2. sovereignty and territorial integrity of all nations
     3. equality of all races and of all nations, large and small
     4. non-intervention in internal affairs
     5. the right of self-defence, singly or collectively,
        under the Charter
     6. no use of collective defence arrangements to serve
        big-power interests; no pressure by one country on another
     7. no aggression or threat of force against territorial
        integrity or political independence
     8. peaceful settlement of disputes
     9. promotion of mutual interests and co-operation
    10. respect for justice and international obligations

   NOTICE WHAT PRINCIPLES 2, 3, 6 AND 7 ARE DOING:
   they take the rules the great powers wrote and apply them
   to the great powers. Sovereignty, equality and non-intervention
   were not new ideas. The claim was that they applied to
   everybody - "all nations large and small".

   THE INSTITUTIONAL RESULT, recorded by UN legal commentary:
   an Assembly coalition "variously styled as the Non-Aligned bloc,
   the Group of 77, the Bandoeng group, the Developing or Third
   World countries" - which then carried resolution 1514 (XV)
   in December 1960.
```

⚠️ The middle block is the argument. Bandung did not propose an alternative international order. It proposed that the existing one mean what it said. That is a much harder demand to refuse, and a much stronger position to argue from.

### 1. Why this lesson exists where it does

⚠️ A word about honesty first, because it matters for how you use this material. ⚠️ The textbook account read for this session narrates the end of the European empires and the problems that followed; it does not narrate Bandung or the Belgrade summit, and the study material for this topic places Afro-Asian and non-aligned detail elsewhere. ⚠️ The connecting facts below were therefore identified as a gap before any search was made, verified once, and are listed with their sources at the end of this file. ⚠️ They are used here for one purpose: to establish that the decolonising world acted collectively and on its own account, which is the claim Lessons 1 to 6 have been building towards and which the case studies from Lesson 9 onwards would otherwise obscure.

> **Boundary.** ⚠️ Non-alignment as a *Cold War* posture - the bargaining leverage it gave, the aid it attracted, the way it exposed states to pressure from both blocs - is owned by the Cold War material. India's own non-aligned foreign policy is owned by the Indian polity and foreign-policy material. ⚠️ This session owns one thing only: Bandung and Belgrade as evidence of Global South agency during decolonisation, and the Assembly coalition they produced.

### 2. Bandung, April 1955

✅ The Asian-African Conference met at Bandung, in Indonesia, from **18 to 24 April 1955**. ✅ Twenty-nine countries took part. ✅ It adopted a ten-point declaration known as the **Dasasila Bandung**, the Bandung Ten Principles, summarised in the visual above.

⚠️ Three observations convert this from a fact into an argument.

⚠️ **First, look at the host.** ✅ Indonesia had been recognised as independent by the Netherlands only in 1949, after four years of fighting, and ✅ its president Sukarno had begun his political career under Dutch imprisonment and Japanese occupation. ⚠️ A conference of twenty-nine states convened six years after independence by a state that had fought for it is itself the argument about agency; it does not need to be made separately.

⚠️ **Second, look at principle 6.** ✅ It calls for abstention from the use of arrangements of collective defence to serve the particular interests of any of the big powers, and abstention by any country from exerting pressures on other countries. ⚠️ In 1955 that clause had specific addressees. Alliance systems were being built across Asia at exactly that moment, and the principle says, in diplomatic language, that a small state's accession to a great-power alliance should not be procured by pressure. ⚠️ This is the germ of non-alignment: not neutrality between good and evil, but a refusal to have one's foreign policy determined by somebody else's confrontation.

⚠️ **Third, look at what is absent.** There is no new economic order in the ten principles, no programme of expropriation, no theory of imperialism. ⚠️ The document is legal and conservative in form, and deliberately so. ⚠️ It stakes the claim of the formerly colonised on the *existing* rules of sovereignty, equality and non-intervention, which places the burden of explanation on anybody who wants to apply those rules selectively. ⚠️ Compare the Freedom Charter of the same decade, from Lesson 4, which likewise demanded nothing a metropolitan liberal could disown. ⚠️ The pattern is consistent and it is the strongest rhetorical position available to a weak party: demand the enforcement of the strong party's own stated principles.

### 3. Belgrade, September 1961, and the shift from identity to posture

✅ The first summit of the Non-Aligned Movement was held at **Belgrade** in Yugoslavia from **1 to 6 September 1961**, with **twenty-five** founding states, and is associated with **Tito** of Yugoslavia, **Nehru** of India, **Nasser** of Egypt, **Nkrumah** of Ghana and **Sukarno** of Indonesia.

⚠️ The difference between the two meetings is the examinable point and it is easy to state. ⚠️ Bandung was **Afro-Asian**: membership was defined by who you were - a people of Asia or Africa, formerly or presently colonised. ⚠️ Belgrade was **non-aligned**: membership was defined by what you did - decline to join either bloc. ⚠️ That is why Yugoslavia, a European state with no colonial history, could host and lead the second and not the first.

⚠️ Why does the shift matter? Because a movement defined by identity has a fixed maximum size and no leverage, while a movement defined by posture can recruit and can bargain. ⚠️ Non-alignment converted the moral claim of Bandung into a negotiating position: a state that has not yet chosen a side has something to withhold.

⚠️ Note also the overlap in personnel with earlier lessons. ✅ Nkrumah, the pan-Africanist of Lesson 5, and ✅ Sukarno, the Indonesian nationalist of Lessons 2 and 17, both appear here. ⚠️ Pan-Africanism, Afro-Asian solidarity and non-alignment were not three separate movements with three separate memberships; they were three successive framings of one project by substantially the same generation of leaders, each framing broader and less binding than the last. ⚠️ That progression - continental federation, then Afro-Asian conference, then non-aligned posture - is itself an argument about what sovereign states will and will not do, and it runs exactly parallel to the retreat from a United States of Africa to the Organization of African Unity in 1963.

### 4. What the coalition actually achieved

⚠️ It is easy to treat these conferences as gestures. The evidence from Lesson 6 says otherwise, and it is the strongest evidence available for Global South agency in the whole session.

✅ By 1960 the United Nations General Assembly's composition had been transformed: sixteen new states admitted in one step in 1955 brought the total to seventy-six, and seventeen more were admitted during 1960 itself - sixteen on 20 September and Nigeria on 7 October - taking membership from eighty-two to ninety-nine. ✅ The resulting neutralist or uncommitted majority coalition was variously styled the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77, the **Bandung group**, or the developing or Third World countries, ✅ and it was this informal electoral alliance that provided the intellectual cohesiveness and the political-tactical competence to secure the adoption of resolution 1514 (XV) without a single expressed dissent.

⚠️ Read that as a chain and it becomes the decisive passage in this lesson:

```text
   decolonisation creates states
        -> states become UN members
        -> members organise as a bloc (Bandung group, 1955 onwards)
        -> the bloc carries the Declaration of 14 December 1960
        -> the Declaration becomes the standing programme
        -> the programme accelerates further decolonisation
        -> which creates more states
```

⚠️ That is a self-reinforcing loop driven from the bottom, and it is the clearest single refutation of the view that decolonisation was something done to Asia and Africa. ⚠️ The formerly colonised did not merely receive independence; they organised, took control of a chamber, and legislated the norm that governed the remainder of the process.

### 5. Limits, honestly stated

⚠️ An answer that presents Bandung and Belgrade without limits will read as advocacy. Four limits are supported by material in this session.

⚠️ **The members disagreed with each other, sometimes violently.** ✅ Indonesia, the host of Bandung, invaded and annexed East Timor in 1975 within weeks of its neighbour's decolonisation, and ✅ the account records that the USA continued to supply military goods to the Indonesians, who were guilty of appalling atrocities both during and after the war. ⚠️ Non-alignment was a posture towards the superpowers, not a rule of conduct towards neighbours.

⚠️ **Non-alignment did not prevent intervention.** ✅ Sukarno was displaced from 1965 by a military takeover in which the USA, operating via the CIA, was involved - authority passing to Suharto under the Supersemar order of 11 March 1966 and the presidency being removed by the MPRS in March 1967 - and one of the stated American objections was precisely the way in which he was acting as leader of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World. ⚠️ Leading the movement was itself a reason for being removed.

⚠️ **Collective diplomacy did not become collective capacity.** ✅ The Organization of African Unity, the continental expression of the same impulse, could not mediate in Nigeria, could not stop the killings in Burundi and did not condemn the Rwandan genocide.

⚠️ **And the economic claim went largely unmet.** ⚠️ The ten principles are political and legal; they contain no mechanism for altering the terms of trade. ✅ The condition the account describes - economies relying on exports of only one or two commodities, with a fall in the world price a major disaster, and loans leaving states heavily in debt - was untouched by any of it. ⚠️ Lesson 24 explains why sovereignty without economic restructuring produced neo-colonialism, and this is where that argument begins.

### Comparative note: three framings, one retreat

| Framing | ✅ Moment | ⚠️ Binding force | ⚠️ Why it narrowed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Continental federation | ✅ Nkrumah's conferences, to 1963 | Would have dissolved member states | ⚠️ Required the beneficiaries of independence to surrender it |
| Afro-Asian conference | ✅ Bandung, 18-24 April 1955, 29 states | Declaratory principles only | ⚠️ Identity-based membership cannot expand or bargain |
| Non-aligned posture | ✅ Belgrade, 1-6 September 1961, 25 states | A shared stance, no obligations | ⚠️ Posture is the most a set of sovereign states will actually commit to |
| Institutional settlement | ✅ OAU 1963; the Assembly bloc from 1955 | Non-binding; but the bloc could legislate norms | ⚠️ Where a vote was the instrument, the coalition was genuinely powerful |

⚠️ The pattern across the table is the topic's recurring lesson about sovereignty: newly independent states would co-operate to the exact extent that co-operation did not diminish what they had just acquired - and no further.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The syllabus's decolonisation clause covers the process and its effects, and these conferences are the principal evidence that the decolonising world was a collective political actor rather than a set of objects. ⚠️ They also bridge to the international-relations material on non-alignment, and the boundary must be respected.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ In a world-history answer this belongs as a short block - three or four sentences at most - inside a question on the causes of decolonisation, on Afro-Asian solidarity, or on the relationship between decolonisation and the Cold War. ⚠️ Its highest-value use is the causal chain in section 4: the states created by decolonisation captured the General Assembly and legislated the norm that governed the rest of the process.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating Bandung and the Non-Aligned Movement as the same thing. ⚠️ Bandung in April 1955 was Afro-Asian and identity-based with twenty-nine states; Belgrade in September 1961 was non-aligned and posture-based with twenty-five founding states, and Yugoslavia could lead the second only.
2. ⚠️ Importing Indian foreign-policy content. ⚠️ That belongs to Indian foreign policy; this session takes only the fact of collective Afro-Asian action.
3. ⚠️ Presenting non-alignment as neutrality. ✅ The sixth Bandung principle is against collective defence arrangements serving big-power interests and against pressure by one country on another - a refusal of *procurement*, not an absence of opinion.
4. ⚠️ Claiming the movement achieved economic independence. ⚠️ Its instruments were legal and diplomatic; commodity dependence and debt were untouched, which is exactly the gap Lesson 24 names as neo-colonialism.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Asian-African Conference, Bandung, Indonesia, 18-24 April 1955; twenty-nine countries.
2. ✅ It adopted a ten-point declaration, the Dasasila Bandung.
3. ✅ The principles cover human rights and the Charter; sovereignty and territorial integrity; equality of all races and of all nations large and small; non-intervention; the right of self-defence under the Charter; abstention from collective defence arrangements serving big-power interests and from pressure between countries; no aggression or threat of force; peaceful settlement; mutual interests and co-operation; and respect for justice and international obligations.
4. ⚠️ The document claims the *existing* rules of sovereignty and equality for everybody rather than proposing a new order - the strongest available position for a weak party.
5. ✅ First Non-Aligned summit, Belgrade, Yugoslavia, 1-6 September 1961; twenty-five founding states.
6. ✅ Associated leaders: Tito, Nehru, Nasser, Nkrumah and Sukarno.
7. ⚠️ Bandung was identity-based (Afro-Asian); Belgrade was posture-based (non-aligned), which is why Yugoslavia could lead the second.
8. ⚠️ A posture-based movement can recruit and bargain; an identity-based one cannot.
9. ✅ Sixteen new UN members in 1955 took the total to seventy-six; seventeen more joined during 1960 - sixteen on 20 September and Nigeria on 7 October - taking membership from eighty-two to ninety-nine.
10. ✅ The resulting coalition was styled the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77, the Bandung group or the Third World, and it carried resolution 1514 (XV) in December 1960 without a single expressed dissent.
11. ⚠️ The self-reinforcing chain: decolonisation creates states, states form a bloc, the bloc legislates the norm, the norm accelerates decolonisation.
12. ✅ Limits: Indonesia, Bandung's host, annexed East Timor in 1975; Sukarno was displaced from 1965 by a military takeover involving the CIA - Supersemar in March 1966, the MPRS removing him in March 1967 - with his leadership of the non-aligned movements among the stated American objections; the OAU could not mediate in Nigeria, could not stop the Burundi massacres and did not condemn the Rwandan genocide.
13. ⚠️ The economic claim went unmet: the principles contain no mechanism for altering commodity dependence or debt.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: three successive framings by the same generation - continental federation, Afro-Asian conference, non-aligned posture - each broader and less binding, because sovereign states co-operate exactly as far as co-operation does not diminish sovereignty.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain the difference between the Bandung conference and the Belgrade summit in terms of how each defined membership, and say why that difference made the second more useful to its members.

**Model answer:** Bandung, held from 18 to 24 April 1955 with twenty-nine participating countries, defined membership by identity: it was the Asian-African Conference, and belonging depended on being a people of Asia or Africa with a colonial past or present. Belgrade, held from 1 to 6 September 1961 with twenty-five founding states, defined membership by posture: belonging depended on declining to align with either Cold War bloc, which is why Yugoslavia under Tito could host and lead it although it was a European state with no colonial history, alongside Nehru, Nasser, Nkrumah and Sukarno. The difference matters because an identity-based association has a fixed maximum membership and nothing to offer or withhold, whereas a posture-based one can expand and can bargain: a state that has not yet chosen a side possesses something both sides want. Non-alignment therefore converted the moral claim of Bandung - that sovereignty, equality and non-intervention apply to all nations large and small - into a usable negotiating position. The institutional proof is that the same coalition, styled variously the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77 or the Bandung group, held a working majority in the General Assembly by 1960 and carried the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples on 14 December 1960 without a single expressed dissent.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating non-alignment as protection. It was a diplomatic posture, not a shield, and the record in this session shows both its external and internal limits: Sukarno, who hosted Bandung, was overthrown in 1965 in a coup in which the CIA was involved, with his leadership of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements among the American objections; and Indonesia itself invaded and annexed East Timor in 1975. Membership of the movement constrained neither superpower behaviour towards members nor member behaviour towards neighbours.

## Lesson 8 - The route typology and the settler variable

Progress: 8/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: six recognisable routes out of empire; the settler variable as the strongest single predictor; the account's own three-region classification of British Africa as an empirical test; the secondary variables of strategic value, metropolitan regime type and successor-state design; and the method for applying the typology to an unfamiliar case

### Visual: six routes, and the question that sorts them

       able to        six months,          S. Rhodesia       metropolitan

    1951-57;   independence promised        1948-94)

    1961;      insurgents)

    1954-60)

   CROSS-CUTTING VARIABLE: an AUTHORITARIAN metropole has no
   domestic mechanism for conceding, so it fights until the
   metropolitan regime itself falls (Portugal: 40% of budget
   by 1973; coup at home 1974; colonies released 1974-75).

⚠️ Work the decision tree downward for any territory and you will predict the route. That is the whole apparatus of Lessons 9 to 22 in one diagram, and it should be memorised.

### 1. The argument for the settler variable

⚠️ The claim is that among all the possible predictors - the identity of the coloniser, the size of the territory, its religion, its resources, the date, the strength of its nationalist party - the single best predictor of *how* a territory became independent is whether Europeans lived there permanently and owned its best land.

⚠️ The strongest evidence for this is that the account itself organises British Africa on exactly that principle, without argument, as though it were obvious.

✅ **West Africa** - the Gold Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and the Gambia: here there were relatively few Europeans, and they tended to be administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend; this made the move to independence comparatively straightforward.

✅ **East Africa** - Kenya, Uganda and Tanganyika: here, especially in Kenya, things were complicated by the settler factor - the presence of European and Asian settlers, who feared for their future under black governments.

✅ **Central Africa** - Nyasaland, Northern and Southern Rhodesia: here, especially in Southern Rhodesia, the settler factor was at its most serious; this was where European settlers were most firmly entrenched, owning huge and profitable estates, and confrontation between white settlers and African nationalists was most bitter.

⚠️ Now test the classification against outcomes. ✅ West Africa: the Gold Coast 1957, Nigeria 1960, Sierra Leone 1961, the Gambia 1965 - four constitutional transfers, no colonial war. ✅ East Africa: Tanganyika 1961 and Uganda 1962 by negotiation, but Kenya 1963 after a state of emergency declared in 1952 and a counter-insurgency lasting to 1960. ✅ Central Africa: Malawi and Zambia in 1964 after the federation broke, but Southern Rhodesia not until 1980, after a unilateral declaration of independence in November 1965 and a guerrilla war.

⚠️ The classification predicts the outcomes with no exceptions in British Africa. That is an unusually clean result in history and it deserves to be stated as such in an answer.

### 2. Why settlers change everything: the mechanism, in four steps

⚠️ Do not merely assert the correlation. Explain it, because the explanation is where the marks are.

**Step one: settlers convert withdrawal from an administrative act into an expropriation.** ⚠️ Recalling officials costs the metropole pensions and passages. Ending settler rule costs settlers their land, their labour supply, their legal status and often their personal safety - none of which the metropole can compensate.

**Step two: settlers have metropolitan political representation and allies.** ✅ In Kenya it was the white settlers who had the political influence over the British government, and they were supported by certain big-business interests in Britain. ✅ In Algeria no French government dared consider independence, since this would incur the wrath of the settlers and their supporters in France. ⚠️ An African nationalist party can lobby the Colonial Office; a settler lobby can threaten a governing majority.

**Step three: settlers have a moral narrative of their own.** ✅ The Kenyan settlers pointed out that they had worked hard and devoted their lives to making their farms successful, that they now saw themselves as white Africans, and that Kenya was their homeland. ⚠️ Take that seriously as a political fact, whatever you think of it, because it is what made compromise so hard: this is not a claim about profit but about belonging, and a claim about belonging cannot be bought out.

**Step four: settlers can act independently of the metropole, including against it.** ✅ The Kenyan settlers refused to negotiate with Kenyatta and were determined to prolong their rule; they provoked a confrontation, hoping that violence would destroy the African Party. ✅ In Algeria, Generals Massu and Salan organised demonstrations in Algiers in May 1958 and prepared Operation Resurrection, a plan to airlift paratroops from Algiers into Paris to occupy government buildings; ⚠️ the operation was never carried out, and the government resigned under the threat of it. ✅ In Southern Rhodesia, Ian Smith declared independence unilaterally, against the wishes of Britain, in November 1965. ✅ And in South Africa the settlers simply won: the Afrikaner Nationalist Party took power in 1948 and wrote their supremacy into statute.

⚠️ Step four is the decisive one and it is the one most candidates miss. **The settler population is a third actor.** The standard two-party model - coloniser against colonised - cannot describe Kenya, Algeria, Rhodesia or South Africa, because in each the settlers pursued a policy against their own metropole. ⚠️ Rhodesia is the purest case: a white minority declared independence *from Britain* in order to prevent Britain from conceding African majority rule.

### 3. The six routes, with their defining case and their signature

| Route | ✅ Defining cases | ⚠️ Decisive condition | ⚠️ Signature in the record |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Negotiated constitutional transfer** | ✅ Gold Coast/Ghana 1951-57; Tanganyika 1961; Uganda 1962; Nigeria 1954-60 | Few settlers; an organised movement able to be a negotiating partner; a metropole expecting continued influence | ⚠️ A sequence of constitutions, elections and stages of self-government; the leader jailed then released and invited to form a government |
| **2. Delayed transfer with a counter-insurgency** | ✅ Malaya 1948-57 | Britain intended to leave but would not leave to the insurgents | ⚠️ A state of emergency running alongside an explicit independence promise to a different community |
| **3. Hurried exit into a vacuum** | ✅ Belgian Congo 1959-60; ✅ Spanish Sahara 1975 | A metropole with no plan, no successor class and a sudden fright | ⚠️ A timetable measured in months; no prepared administrative class; immediate secession or partition |
| **4. Settler revolt or prolonged delay** | ✅ Kenya 1952-63; ✅ Southern Rhodesia 1965-80 | Settlers strong enough to obstruct but not legally fused with the metropole | ⚠️ A state of emergency, a counter-insurgency, a unilateral act against the metropole, and a late negotiated settlement |
| **5. Settler-state war** | ✅ Algeria 1954-62 | The territory legally part of the metropole; settlers counted in hundreds of thousands | ⚠️ Metropolitan constitutional crisis; army mutiny or near-mutiny; mass settler departure at the end |
| **6. The state that never left** | ✅ South Africa 1948-94 | Settlers won power outright and legislated their supremacy | ⚠️ No transfer at all; an internal struggle over decades ending in a negotiated constitution |

⚠️ Two cases sit awkwardly in the table and the awkwardness is instructive rather than a defect.

⚠️ **Portuguese Africa** is a settler-poor case that nonetheless produced long wars, which looks like a refutation. ⚠️ It is not, because the variable that operated there was the *metropolitan regime type*: ✅ the right-wing Portuguese government of Salazar blithely ignored nationalist developments in the rest of Africa and stepped up its repressive policies, and only when the dictatorship was overthrown by a military coup in 1974 were the colonies released. ⚠️ An authoritarian metropole has no domestic mechanism - no election, no opposition party, no free press - through which withdrawal can become policy. The pressure therefore accumulates until it breaks the regime rather than changing its mind.

⚠️ **Malaya** is a settler-poor case with a twelve-year emergency, which also looks like a refutation. ⚠️ It is not, because the obstacle there was not settlers but the *successor-state question*: ✅ the population was multiracial, mostly Malays and Chinese with some Indians and Europeans as well, and ✅ the insurgents were Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng. ⚠️ Where the colonised society is plural and no group holds an uncontested claim to inherit, decolonisation becomes a contest over the successor state's boundaries and franchise, and the coloniser's departure is the easy part. Lesson 18 develops this into a full route in its own right.

### 4. The secondary variables, briefly

⚠️ Three further variables refine the prediction and each is worth a clause.

⚠️ **Strategic value.** ✅ Eden decided to drop the idea of independence for Cyprus, believing that Britain needed the island as a military base to protect her interests in the Middle East, and announced that Cyprus must remain permanently British. ⚠️ A base is a metropolitan interest that no local negotiation can satisfy; ✅ the settlement finally reached in 1960 gave Cyprus full independence while Britain retained two military bases, which is exactly what you would predict.

⚠️ **Economic value.** ✅ Franco resisted longest in Spanish Sahara because the country was a valuable source of phosphates. ✅ The wealthiest part of the Congo, Katanga with its copper-mining industry, seceded within weeks - and ✅ many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga, which would be easier for them to influence, and wanted to continue their control of the copper mining. ⚠️ Where the value is concentrated in one province, expect that province to be detached rather than the whole territory retained.

⚠️ **Metropolitan wealth.** ✅ The Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal were even more determined than France to hold on, probably because, being less wealthy, they lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism, and could not maintain the equivalent of the British Commonwealth or French influence against competition from foreign capital. ⚠️ This is the counter-intuitive one and it is worth stating explicitly: **the poorer the metropole, the more violently it defended formal rule, because informal influence was not available to it as a substitute.**

### 5. Using the typology under examination conditions

⚠️ Given an unfamiliar territory or a comparison you have not rehearsed, run this sequence.

1. ⚠️ **Count the settlers and ask what they own.** This decides the branch. ✅ Kenya: some 66,000 white settlers against 10 million Africans, holding the fertile highland plateau. ✅ Southern Rhodesia: fewer than 200,000 whites, about 20,000 Asians and 4 million black Africans. ✅ The Central African Federation: about 300,000 Europeans out of a total population of about 8.5 million. ✅ Algeria: over a million settlers and 9 million Muslim Algerians, with the settlers holding about a third of the most fertile land. ✅ Tunisia about 250,000 settlers and Morocco about 300,000 in 1945.
2. ⚠️ **Ask what the metropole expects afterwards.** Influence expected means negotiation; influence impossible means resistance.
3. ⚠️ **Ask whether an organised successor exists.** ✅ A party that has won thirty-four of thirty-eight seats is a negotiating partner; ✅ a movement less than a year old, in a country with seventeen graduates, is not.
4. ⚠️ **Ask whether the metropolitan regime can change its mind.** A democracy can lose an election; a dictatorship must be overthrown.
5. ⚠️ **Then, and only then, describe the outcome** - and make the description an explanation, by naming which of the four answers produced it.

⚠️ The sentence that examiners reward, and which is worth carrying whole: **the form decolonisation took was determined less by the coloniser's intentions than by whether settlers, strategic assets or an unsettled successor question made withdrawal negotiable.**

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Comparison is the standard high-mark demand on a syllabus term covering four continents and thirty years. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the comparative apparatus; Lessons 9 to 22 supply the cases it sorts.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Typical demands: compare a peaceful and a violent case of decolonisation; explain why decolonisation took different forms in different territories; account for the difference between British and French experience. ⚠️ All three are answered by naming the variable first and the cases second. ⚠️ The commonest failure is two case narratives side by side with no variable connecting them, which reads as description however accurate it is.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Organising by empire. ⚠️ Britain fought in Kenya and Malaya; France negotiated in Tunisia and Morocco in 1956. The coloniser's name is a weak predictor.
2. ⚠️ Treating settlers as merely an obstacle. ⚠️ They are a third actor with independent agency - Algiers in May 1958, Salisbury in November 1965, Pretoria from 1948.
3. ⚠️ Forcing Portuguese Africa into the settler model. ⚠️ The operative variable there is the authoritarian metropole's inability to change policy without regime change.
4. ⚠️ Forcing Malaya into the settler model. ⚠️ The operative variable there is plural society and the contested successor state.
5. ❌ Assuming a rich metropole fights harder. ✅ The opposite is recorded: the poorer powers fought because neo-colonial influence was unavailable to them.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ British Africa was classified by settler presence: West Africa with relatively few Europeans, and those administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend, making the move comparatively straightforward.
2. ✅ East Africa - Kenya, Uganda, Tanganyika - complicated, especially in Kenya, by the settler factor: European and Asian settlers who feared for their future under black governments.
3. ✅ Central Africa - Nyasaland, Northern and Southern Rhodesia - where the settler factor was at its most serious, settlers most firmly entrenched, owning huge and profitable estates, and confrontation most bitter.
4. ✅ Outcomes match: West Africa 1957-65 with no colonial war; Tanganyika 1961 and Uganda 1962 by negotiation but Kenya 1963 after an emergency from 1952; Malawi and Zambia 1964 but Southern Rhodesia 1980.
5. ⚠️ Four-step mechanism: settlers turn withdrawal into expropriation; they hold metropolitan political influence; they have a claim of belonging that cannot be bought out; and they can act against their own metropole.
6. ✅ Settler autonomy in evidence: Kenyan settlers refusing to negotiate with Kenyatta and provoking confrontation; Massu and Salan's Operation Resurrection of May 1958; Smith's unilateral declaration of November 1965; the Nationalist victory of 1948 in South Africa.
7. ⚠️ Six routes: negotiated transfer; delayed transfer with counter-insurgency; hurried exit into a vacuum; settler revolt or long delay; settler-state war; and the state that never left.
8. ⚠️ Portuguese Africa is explained by metropolitan regime type, not settlers: the Salazar regime had no mechanism for conceding, so pressure broke the regime in 1974 before the colonies were released.
9. ⚠️ Malaya is explained by plural society and the contested successor state, not settlers.
10. ✅ Secondary variable - strategic value: Eden judged Cyprus necessary as a military base and said it must remain permanently British; the 1960 settlement gave independence while Britain retained two bases.
11. ✅ Secondary variable - concentrated economic value: Spanish Sahara's phosphates; Katanga's copper, with many Belgians preferring an independent Katanga they could influence.
12. ✅ Secondary variable - metropolitan wealth, inverted: the Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal were more determined to hold on, probably because, being less wealthy, they lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism.
13. ✅ Settler numbers to quote: Kenya some 66,000 whites against 10 million Africans, plus around 200,000 Indians and 35,000 Muslim Arabs; Southern Rhodesia fewer than 200,000 whites, about 20,000 Asians and 4 million Africans; the Central African Federation about 300,000 Europeans out of about 8.5 million; Algeria over a million settlers and 9 million Muslim Algerians; Tunisia about 250,000 and Morocco about 300,000 in 1945.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: the form decolonisation took was determined less by the coloniser's intentions than by whether settlers, strategic assets or an unsettled successor question made withdrawal negotiable.

### Concept check

**Question:** Two cases appear to contradict the settler thesis: Portuguese Africa, where wars lasted thirteen years with comparatively few settlers, and Malaya, where a twelve-year emergency followed a colonial power that intended to leave. Show that neither actually refutes the thesis, and state what each adds to it.

**Model answer:** Neither refutes it, because each is governed by a different variable that the thesis does not claim to exclude. In Portuguese Africa the operative variable is metropolitan regime type. The Salazar government ignored nationalist developments elsewhere in Africa and responded to the fighting that began in Angola in 1961 by stepping up repression, and it kept doing so until the army was spending forty per cent of the state budget on three colonial wars by 1973. An authoritarian metropole has no election, opposition or free press through which withdrawal can become policy, so the accumulated pressure broke the regime rather than changing its mind: the dictatorship was overthrown by a military coup in 1974 and Guinea-Bissau, Mozambique and Angola were released in 1974 and 1975. In Malaya the operative variable is the contested successor state. The population was multiracial, mostly Malays and Chinese with some Indians and Europeans, and the insurgency was led by Chinese communist guerrillas under Chin Peng, so the unresolved question was not whether Britain would leave but which community would inherit. Britain's answer combined counter-insurgency with a credible timetable: it declared a state of emergency in 1948 and resettled suspected supporters into guarded villages, while making clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready, which kept the Malay majority firmly pro-British. Together the two cases add a rule: violence is produced by any condition that makes withdrawal non-negotiable, of which settler property is the commonest but not the only form.

**Misconception to avoid:** Organising the comparison by imperial power, so that British decolonisation is written as peaceful and French, Belgian and Portuguese decolonisation as violent. Britain declared emergencies in Malaya in 1948, Kenya in 1952, Cyprus in 1955 and Nyasaland and Southern Rhodesia in 1959, while France conceded Tunisia and Morocco by negotiation in 1956 in the middle of the Algerian war. The predictive variable is the condition in the territory, not the flag flying over it.
