## Lesson 9 - West Africa I: the Gold Coast to Ghana, 1947-57

Progress: 9/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: why West Africa moved first; Nkrumah and the Convention People's Party; the campaign of boycott, demonstration and general strike; imprisonment and release; the constitution of universal adult suffrage; the 1951 election and the five years of supervised government the account calls unique; and what "preparation" did and did not guarantee

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's West Africa section, including the description of the Gold Coast as the first black African state south of the Sahara to win independence after the Second World War, the detail of Nkrumah's campaign and imprisonment, the constitutional terms conceded, the 1951 election result, the five years of experience under British supervision described as unique to Ghana, and the 1957 independence
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the ladder the Gold Coast climbed, rung by rung

```text
   1949   CPP founded; Nkrumah its leader
            (educated in London and the USA)
     |
     v
   1950   THE CAMPAIGN
            * boycotts of European goods
            * violent demonstrations
            * a GENERAL STRIKE
            -> Nkrumah and other leaders IMPRISONED
     |
     v
   ...    THE BRITISH CALCULATION
            "realizing that he had mass support,
             soon released him"
            and AGREED A NEW CONSTITUTION:
              - the vote for ALL ADULTS
              - an ELECTED ASSEMBLY
              - an ELEVEN-MAN EXECUTIVE COUNCIL,
                EIGHT of them chosen by the Assembly
     |
     v
   Feb    FIRST ELECTION UNDER THE NEW CONSTITUTION
   1951     CPP wins 34 seats out of 38
            -> NKRUMAH RELEASED FROM PRISON at once,
               invited to form a government as
               LEADER OF GOVERNMENT BUSINESS
     |
     v
   1952   Nkrumah becomes PRIME MINISTER
            => "This was self-government but not yet
                full independence."
     |
     v
   1952-57  FIVE YEARS OF SUPERVISED GOVERNMENT
            "a small but well-educated group of politicians
             and other professionals, who, for the next five
             years, gained experience of government under
             British supervision"
            "This experience was UNIQUE TO GHANA"
     |
     v
   1957   FULL INDEPENDENCE as GHANA
            - the first British territory in Africa to win
              independence
            - "a great source of inspiration for other
               African colonies"
```

⚠️ The ladder has eight rungs in eight years, and every rung is a British concession made after a demonstration of African strength. That is the shape to reproduce in an answer: not "Britain granted independence to Ghana in 1957" but a sequence in which each concession follows a specific pressure.

### 1. Why West Africa, and why first

⚠️ Before the narrative, the structural reason, because it is the thing an examiner is actually testing.

✅ In West Africa - the Gold Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone and the Gambia - there were relatively few Europeans, and they tended to be administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend. ✅ This made the move to independence comparatively straightforward.

⚠️ Turn that into a mechanism. In West Africa there was no third actor. The contest was between a colonial administration and a nationalist movement, and an administration can be instructed from London to concede in a way that a settler community cannot. ⚠️ There was also nothing for the metropole to defend beyond trade, and trade survives independence - ✅ indeed the British were confident that they would still be able to exercise influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership. ⚠️ Where the asset is trade rather than land, withdrawal is compatible with keeping the asset.

⚠️ One further West African advantage should be recorded because it is the mirror image of a Central African disadvantage. ✅ In Central Africa the numbers of well-educated Africans were much smaller than in West Africa, because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided. ⚠️ Settlers did not merely obstruct independence directly; they obstructed it indirectly by suppressing the education that produced the class capable of negotiating it. ⚠️ West Africa's educated professional group existed because there were no settlers with an interest in preventing it.

### 2. The campaign: what "pressure" actually consisted of

✅ The Gold Coast was the first black African state south of the Sahara to win independence after the Second World War, taking the name Ghana in 1957. ✅ It was achieved fairly smoothly, though not without some incident.

⚠️ That phrase - "not without some incident" - conceals a genuine confrontation, and a good answer unpacks it.

✅ The nationalist leader was **Kwame Nkrumah**, educated in London and the USA and since 1949 leader of the **Convention People's Party**. ✅ There were boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a **general strike in 1950**, and Nkrumah and other leaders were imprisoned for a time.

⚠️ Three instruments, each doing different work, and the distinction is examinable.

⚠️ A **boycott of European goods** attacks the colonial economy's import side and, more importantly, disciplines the participating population: it requires thousands of individual acts of self-denial and therefore demonstrates commitment rather than mere opinion.

⚠️ **Demonstrations** produce the visible crowd and force the administration to choose between conceding and using force - and using force against unarmed crowds in a colony whose metropole has just fought a war for self-determination creates precisely the delegitimation described in Lesson 2.

⚠️ The **general strike** is the decisive instrument, for the reason given in Lesson 4: it stops the export economy and proves that the leadership commands the workforce.

⚠️ And notice what the British did with the information. ✅ They imprisoned Nkrumah, and then, realising that he had mass support, soon released him. ⚠️ The campaign's function was not to defeat the administration. It was to supply the administration with a fact - that this man could move the country - which changed what the administration thought its options were.

### 3. The constitution: read the details, because they are the concession

✅ The British agreed to allow a new constitution which included the vote for all adults; an elected Assembly; and an eleven-man Executive Council, of which eight were chosen by the Assembly.

⚠️ Take those three clauses one at a time, because each is a specific surrender of a specific power and candidates who quote the constitution without analysing it gain nothing.

⚠️ **The vote for all adults.** Universal adult suffrage in a colony is not a procedural detail; it is the end of the argument. Every gradualist scheme of the period - qualified franchises, reserved seats, multiracial councils - depended on restricting the electorate so that the numerical majority did not become the political majority. ✅ Compare Kenya, where the only British concession for years was to allow six Africans to join a Legislative Council of fifty-four members, or ✅ Algeria, where one million whites elected sixty members of the assembly and nine million Muslims elected the other sixty. ⚠️ Conceding universal suffrage in a territory without settlers concedes the outcome.

⚠️ **An elected Assembly.** This creates the institution in which a nationalist party can demonstrate its majority in a form the metropole must recognise.

⚠️ **Eight of eleven on the Executive Council chosen by the Assembly.** This is the transfer of the executive, not merely the legislature - and it is the rung that makes the next five years meaningful, because it means Africans were *governing* rather than advising.

✅ In the elections of **February 1951**, the first under the new constitution, the CPP won **thirty-four seats out of thirty-eight**. ✅ Nkrumah was released from prison in **February 1951**, immediately after the result, was invited to form a government as Leader of Government Business, and became prime minister in **1952**.

⚠️ That sequence - imprisoned as a criminal, released, elected, invited to form a government - happened in under two years, and it is the single most efficient illustration in this topic of the mechanism from Lesson 4: imprisoning the moderate is a tactic the metropole must eventually reverse.

### 4. The five years, and the word "unique"

✅ This was self-government but not yet full independence. ✅ The Gold Coast had a small but well-educated group of politicians and other professionals who, for the next five years, gained experience of government under British supervision. ✅ This experience was **unique to Ghana**; had it been repeated in other newly independent states, it might possibly have helped to avoid chaos and mismanagement. ✅ In 1957 Ghana, as it became known, received full independence.

⚠️ That judgement deserves careful handling, because it is simultaneously the strongest evidence for the value of preparation and the most misused sentence in the topic.

⚠️ **What it correctly establishes.** There was a five-year interval in which an African government exercised real executive power under supervision, with a trained professional class available to staff it. ⚠️ The contrast with the Congo, where ✅ the Belgians suddenly announced that the Congo could become independent in six months, ✅ there was no experienced group of Africans to which power could be handed over, ✅ there were only seventeen graduates in the entire country, and ✅ the Congolese National Movement had existed less than a year - is total, and it is the comparison to make.

⚠️ **What it does not establish.** That preparation guarantees stability. ⚠️ And the decisive refutation is Ghana itself. ✅ Nkrumah ruled Ghana from independence in 1957 until his removal by the army in 1966; ✅ from 1959 onwards opponents could be deported or imprisoned for up to five years without trial; ✅ in 1964 all parties except Nkrumah's were banned; ✅ a steep fall in the world price of cocoa left Ghana with a huge balance-of-payments deficit; ✅ and the coup that removed him is said by the textbook account to have received the full backing of the American CIA. ⚠️ Carry that as a **contested allegation, not a settled fact**: American hostility to Nkrumah and American foreknowledge and encouragement of his removal are well attested, but "full backing" states more than the declassified record establishes, and the extent of operational American involvement in February 1966 remains disputed.

⚠️ So the best-prepared transfer in British Africa produced a one-party state inside seven years and a military coup inside nine. ⚠️ This is why Lesson 23 insists that post-colonial difficulty cannot be explained by unreadiness: the most ready state failed too, for reasons - commodity dependence, the closing of politics, external interest - that preparation did not address. ⚠️ Note the forward reference and do not import the 1957-66 narrative into an answer about the transfer; it belongs to the second of the two questions from the opening disciplines.

⚠️ One further caution about the word "unique". ⚠️ It is the account's own assessment, and it is offered with a conditional - *might possibly have helped*. ⚠️ Reproduce the conditional. An answer that writes "Ghana succeeded because it was prepared" has converted a qualified counterfactual into a causal claim the evidence does not support.

### 5. The demonstration effect

✅ It was a further ten years before the Gold Coast became the first British territory in Africa to win independence; ✅ this became a great source of inspiration for other African colonies.

⚠️ That is a causal claim about the *spread* of decolonisation, and it links this lesson back to Lesson 3's causation dispute. ⚠️ A metropolitan thesis has to explain why a transfer in one small West African territory should change the expectations of Africans two thousand miles away who were dealing with a different administration. ⚠️ The answer is demonstration: independence ceased to be an aspiration and became a precedent, with a date attached. ✅ The same effect is recorded elsewhere in the material: by 1960 the nationalists in the Portuguese colonies were greatly encouraged by the large number of other African states winning independence.

⚠️ And it operated in the opposite direction too, on the people who feared it. ✅ When India and Pakistan were given independence in 1947, white South Africans became alarmed at the growing racial equality within the Commonwealth, and they were determined to preserve their supremacy. ⚠️ The demonstration effect of one decolonisation produced apartheid in a territory two oceans away. Lesson 21 develops that point; note here only that precedent travels in both directions.

### Comparative note: the Gold Coast against the Congo, on one variable

⚠️ Place the two 1950s transfers side by side and the variable that separates them is not wealth, size, resources or the identity of the coloniser but the existence of a trained successor class and the time allowed to use it.

| Variable | ✅ Gold Coast / Ghana | ✅ Belgian Congo |
|---|---|---|
| Nationalist organisation at the point of decision | ✅ CPP founded 1949; won 34 of 38 seats in 1951 | ✅ The MNC under Lumumba had existed less than a year; there were over fifty different groups |
| Educated professional class | ✅ A small but well-educated group of politicians and other professionals | ✅ Only seventeen graduates in the entire country; no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers |
| Interval between decision and transfer | ✅ Five years of government under British supervision, 1952-57 | ✅ Six months |
| Colonial education policy | ⚠️ Not restricted by settlers; a professional class existed | ✅ Deliberate denial of advanced education, to prevent contact with nationalist ideas and deprive Africans of a professional class who could lead them to independence |
| Immediate outcome | ✅ Orderly transfer, 1957 | ✅ Everything went wrong shortly after independence; the country was plunged into a disastrous civil war |

⚠️ The fourth row is the one to emphasise, because it shows that the difference was produced by colonial policy rather than by anything about the two societies. ⚠️ Belgium's Congo lacked a successor class because Belgium had decided it should lack one.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Decolonisation is a named syllabus term, and the Gold Coast is the standard reference case for the negotiated constitutional route. ⚠️ It is also the necessary counterweight to answers that treat decolonisation as uniformly violent.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Most often as one half of a comparison - peaceful against violent, or prepared against unprepared. ⚠️ The high-value handling is to describe the transfer as a sequence of concessions extracted by demonstrable pressure, rather than as a gift, and then to use Ghana's own post-1957 trajectory to refuse the easy conclusion that preparation ensures stability.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that Ghana became independent peacefully, full stop. ✅ There were boycotts, violent demonstrations, a general strike and the imprisonment of the leadership. "Comparatively straightforward" is a relative judgement, not an account of an absence of conflict.
2. ⚠️ Quoting "unique to Ghana" as proof that preparation works. ⚠️ The account's own claim is conditional - it might possibly have helped avoid chaos - and Ghana itself produced a one-party state by 1964 and a coup in 1966.
3. ⚠️ Missing the constitutional content. ✅ The vote for all adults, an elected Assembly, and eight of eleven Executive Council members chosen by the Assembly - each is a distinct surrender of power.
4. ⚠️ Confusing the dates. ✅ 1949 CPP; 1950 general strike; February 1951 election with 34 of 38 seats and release from prison; 1952 prime minister; 1957 full independence.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ West Africa - the Gold Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone, the Gambia - had relatively few Europeans, and those were administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend, making the move comparatively straightforward.
2. ✅ The Gold Coast was the first black African state south of the Sahara to win independence after the Second World War, taking the name Ghana in 1957.
3. ✅ It was the first British territory in Africa to win independence, and became a great source of inspiration for other African colonies.
4. ✅ Kwame Nkrumah: educated in London and the USA; leader of the Convention People's Party since 1949.
5. ✅ The campaign: boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950; Nkrumah and other leaders imprisoned for a time.
6. ✅ The British, realising he had mass support, soon released him and agreed a new constitution.
7. ✅ The constitution: the vote for all adults; an elected Assembly; an eleven-man Executive Council of which eight were chosen by the Assembly.
8. ✅ February 1951 elections, the first under the new constitution: the CPP won 34 seats out of 38.
9. ✅ Nkrumah released in February 1951 immediately after the result, invited to form a government, prime minister in 1952 - self-government but not yet full independence.
10. ✅ A small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals gained five years' experience of government under British supervision; this experience was unique to Ghana and might possibly have helped avoid chaos and mismanagement elsewhere.
11. ✅ Full independence in 1957.
12. ⚠️ Contrast with the Congo: six months' notice, only seventeen graduates, no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers, and a nationalist movement less than a year old.
13. ✅ Central Africa had far fewer well-educated Africans than West Africa because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided - settlers obstructed independence indirectly as well as directly.
14. ⚠️ Preparation did not guarantee stability: Ghana banned all parties except Nkrumah's in 1964 and its government was removed by the army in 1966 with American encouragement and, on the textbook account's contested reading, full CIA backing.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: the Gold Coast transfer was a ladder of concessions each following a demonstration of strength, and the five supervised years were an unusual advantage whose limits Ghana itself then demonstrated.

### Concept check

**Question:** The account describes the Gold Coast's five years of supervised self-government as an experience unique to Ghana that might have helped other states avoid chaos and mismanagement. Assess that judgement using evidence from Ghana itself.

**Model answer:** The judgement is well founded as a comparative statement and unreliable as a prediction. As comparison it is strong: between 1952 and 1957 an African prime minister and a small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals exercised executive power under supervision, with eight of the eleven Executive Council members chosen by an Assembly elected on universal adult suffrage, and nothing resembling this existed in the Belgian Congo, which was given six months' notice, had only seventeen graduates and no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers, and whose main nationalist movement was less than a year old. As prediction it fails on its own test case. Nkrumah ruled from 1957 until removed by the army in 1966; the Preventive Detention Act of 1958 allowed detention without trial and from 1959 opponents could be deported or jailed without trial; in 1964 all parties except his own were banned; a steep fall in the world cocoa price produced a huge balance-of-payments deficit because Ghana remained dependent on a single export; and the 1966 coup had American encouragement, which the textbook account records as full CIA backing though the claim is contested. None of these causes - commodity dependence, the closing of political competition, and external interest in the government's alignment - is one that five years of administrative experience could have addressed. The correct formulation is that preparation reduced the risk of immediate administrative collapse, which is what it was designed to do, and had no bearing on the structural and external pressures that actually destroyed the first republic.

**Misconception to avoid:** Describing the Gold Coast's independence as simply granted, on the strength of the phrase "comparatively straightforward". The sequence was a ladder of concessions, each following pressure: boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950 led to imprisonment, imprisonment was reversed once the British realised Nkrumah had mass support, and the new constitution with universal adult suffrage followed. The relative smoothness lies in the absence of a settler population to obstruct the process, not in an absence of African pressure to drive it.

---

## Lesson 10 - West Africa II: Nigeria, Sierra Leone and the Gambia

Progress: 10/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: Nigeria's scale and regional structure; Azikiwe, the press and the general strike of 1945; the federal constitution of 1954 and the sequence of regional then national self-government; why the careful preparation did not hold; and the two quiet West African transfers

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's Nigeria section, covering its size and population, the regional division between the Muslim north dominated by the Hausa and Fulani, the western Yoruba region and the eastern Ibo region, Azikiwe's education, newspapers and general strike, the federal constitution of 1954, the sequence of regional and then national self-government, independence in 1960, the 1967 secession, and the independence dates for Sierra Leone and the Gambia
CA search: not sought - the single current linkage in this session is in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a territory that had to be designed before it could be transferred

```text
   THE PROBLEM: "easily the largest of Britain's African colonies,
   with a population of over 60 million" and "a more difficult
   proposition than Ghana because of its great size, and because
   of its regional differences"

   +-----------------------------------------------------------+
   |  NORTH: "the vast Muslim north, dominated by the Hausa     |
   |         and Fulani tribes"                                 |
   |         -> supplies the prime minister, Balewa             |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------+
   |  WEST: "the western region (Yorubas)"                      |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------+
   |  EAST: "the eastern region (Ibos)"                         |
   |        -> supplies the leading nationalist, Azikiwe        |
   |        -> and the oil, in the eastern coastal area         |
   +-----------------------------------------------------------+

   THE DESIGN ANSWER (1954 constitution):
        local assemblies for the THREE REGIONS
                     +
        a central (FEDERAL) government in LAGOS

   THE SEQUENCE:
        regions assume self-government FIRST
                     ->
        the country as a whole becomes independent in 1960

   THE OUTCOME:
        "Sadly, in spite of the careful preparations for
         independence, tribal differences caused civil war to
         break out in 1967 when the Ibos declared the eastern
         region independent with the name Biafra."

   THE EXAMINABLE QUESTION IS NOT "did Britain prepare?"
   It is: WHY DID A FEDERAL DESIGN, WHICH IS THE STANDARD
   REMEDY FOR REGIONAL DIVERSITY, FAIL HERE?
```

⚠️ Keep the question at the bottom of the diagram in view throughout. Nigeria is not evidence that the British failed to prepare; it is evidence that a particular remedy did not work, and an answer earns marks by explaining the failure of the remedy rather than by lamenting the outcome.

### 1. Scale as a political problem

✅ Nigeria was easily the largest of Britain's African colonies, with a population of over sixty million. ✅ It was a more difficult proposition than Ghana because of its great size, and because of its regional differences between the vast Muslim north, dominated by the Hausa and Fulani, the western region of the Yoruba and the eastern region of the Ibo.

⚠️ Scale is not merely a logistical difficulty and it is worth saying why. ⚠️ A small territory with one dominant political movement can be transferred to that movement. A very large territory containing several populations of comparable weight cannot be transferred to anybody without simultaneously deciding the relations between them. ⚠️ The colonial power is therefore forced to act as a constitutional designer rather than as a departing administrator, and it must make that design with a specific and short deadline while having no legitimacy to impose it after it has gone.

⚠️ Note also what the diagram records about the distribution of assets, because it drives everything after 1960. ✅ Nigeria was potentially a wealthy state, extensive oil resources having been discovered in the eastern coastal area; ✅ the north supplied the prime minister, the capable and moderate Sir Abubakar Tafawa Balewa; ✅ the leading nationalist and later president was Nnamdi Azikiwe, an Ibo from the east. ⚠️ Political authority and mineral wealth were located in different regions, which is the classic precondition for a secession crisis.

### 2. Azikiwe: the press before the party

✅ The leading nationalist was **Nnamdi Azikiwe**, popularly known to his supporters as "Zik". ✅ He was educated in the USA and for a time worked as a newspaper editor in the Gold Coast. ✅ After his return to Nigeria in 1937 he founded a series of newspapers and became involved in the nationalist movement, soon gaining enormous prestige. ✅ In 1945 he showed he meant business by organising an impressive **general strike**, which was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence.

⚠️ Three points from that paragraph deserve development.

⚠️ **The Gold Coast connection.** ✅ Azikiwe edited a newspaper in the Gold Coast before returning to Nigeria. ⚠️ West African nationalism was a single intellectual world with people, arguments and newspapers moving between territories that were administratively separate. ⚠️ That mobility is part of why the region moved together and why the Gold Coast's independence in 1957 functioned as a precedent rather than as a foreign event.

⚠️ **The order of instruments.** ⚠️ Azikiwe had newspapers from 1937 and prestige for years before anything changed. ✅ It was the general strike of 1945 that was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence. ⚠️ Eight years of the most influential press in West Africa moved no timetable; one general strike did. ⚠️ Set that beside the Gold Coast's 1950 strike and the generalisation from Lesson 4 stands on two cases rather than one.

⚠️ **The timing.** ✅ 1945 is the year the war ended. ⚠️ A general strike in that year draws on demobilised servicemen, wartime inflation, wartime labour discipline and the wartime rhetoric of self-determination all at once. ⚠️ It is the clearest single illustration of Lesson 2's third mechanism turning into political action.

### 3. The federal design and its sequence

✅ It was decided that a federal system would be most suitable. ✅ In 1954 a new constitution introduced local assemblies for the three regions, with a central federal government in Lagos, the capital. ✅ The regions assumed self-government first, and the country as a whole became independent in **1960**.

⚠️ That sequence - regions first, centre second - is the detail most often skipped and it repays analysis. ⚠️ Read one way it is prudent: build the units before the union, so that self-government is learned at a manageable scale. ⚠️ Read another way it is dangerous: it gives each region an established government, a payroll and a political class with an institutional interest *before* the national government exists, so that the centre arrives as a latecomer negotiating with entrenched powers rather than as the source of their authority.

⚠️ Compare the effect with the Malayan sequence in Lesson 18, where ✅ each state had its own legislature for local affairs and the sultans retained some power, but **the central government had firm overall control**. ⚠️ The order of institutional creation, and the location of residual power, differed - and so did the two federations' capacity to survive a crisis.

✅ Nigeria became a republic in 1963 with Azikiwe as president and Balewa as prime minister.

### 4. Why the design failed: four pressures, only one of them ethnic

⚠️ The account's own summary is that ✅ in spite of the careful preparations for independence, tribal differences caused civil war to break out in 1967 when the Ibos declared the eastern region independent with the name Biafra. ⚠️ But the fuller narrative identifies four distinct pressures, and an answer that names only the ethnic one has read the topic sentence and not the paragraph.

⚠️ **Pressure one: a structural complaint against the centre.** ✅ Although the constitution was a federal one in which each of the three regions had its own local government, the regions felt that the central government in Lagos did not safeguard their interests sufficiently. ✅ Balewa came from the Muslim north where the Hausa and Fulani were powerful, and the Yoruba of the west and the Ibo of the south and east constantly complained about northern domination, even though Azikiwe was an Ibo.

⚠️ That final clause matters. ⚠️ Having an Ibo head of state did not dissolve the complaint of northern domination, because the complaint was about where effective power lay rather than about which individuals held ceremonial office. ⚠️ Representation at the top is not the same as security at the level of resources and appointments, and confusing the two is a standing error in answers on federal politics.

⚠️ **Pressure two: economic recession.** ✅ By 1964 prices had risen by fifteen per cent, unemployment was rising, and wages were on average well below what had been calculated as the minimum living wage.

⚠️ This is the most under-used fact in the Nigerian case and it should be foregrounded. ⚠️ Ethnic competition is continuous and usually contained. What converts it into a crisis is a contraction, because a contraction makes distribution zero-sum: every appointment, contract and subsidy that one region gains is now visibly taken from another. ⚠️ The Nigerian collapse of 1966-67 followed two years of falling real wages, and any answer that omits this is explaining a political rupture with a permanent variable.

⚠️ **Pressure three: the loss of procedural legitimacy.** ✅ Criticism of the government mounted and Balewa replied by arresting Chief Awolowo, who had been premier of the western region until 1959 and was by then **leader of the opposition in the federal parliament**; the western region for a time seemed likely to break away from the federation. ⚠️ The textbook account calls Awolowo "prime minister of the western region" at the time of his arrest; he had left that office in 1959, and he was arrested in 1962 as federal opposition leader. ✅ The central government was also accused of corruption after blatantly trying to "fix" the results of the 1964 elections.

⚠️ Here is the decisive mechanism, and it should be stated as a rule: **a federation survives by procedure, so an election that is not believed removes the only non-violent method of settling the distribution question.** ⚠️ Once the ballot is discredited, a region seeking redress has the courts, the street or the army - and in 1966 Nigeria the army was the instrument chosen.

⚠️ **Pressure four: the militarisation of the dispute.** ✅ In January 1966 there was a military coup carried out by mainly Ibo officers in which Balewa and some other leading politicians were killed. ✅ After this the situation deteriorated steadily: in the north there were savage massacres of Ibos who had moved into the region for better jobs. ✅ The new leader, General Ironsi, himself an Ibo, was murdered by northern soldiers. ✅ When a northerner, Colonel Yakubu Gowon, emerged supreme, almost all the Ibos fled from other parts of Nigeria back to the east, whose leader, Colonel Ojukwu, announced that the eastern region had seceded from Nigeria to become the independent state of **Biafra in May 1967**. ✅ Gowon launched what he described as a "short surgical police action" to bring the east back into Nigeria.

⚠️ Two observations. ⚠️ First, notice the phrase "who had moved into the region for better jobs": the victims of the northern massacres were internal economic migrants, which means the violence targeted exactly the population that the single national labour market had created. ⚠️ Colonial-era and post-independence mobility produced minorities in every region, and those minorities became the point of attack the moment the federal frame lost authority. ⚠️ Second, Ironsi's murder disproves any simple reading of the coup as an ethnic seizure: an Ibo general who had taken power was killed by northern soldiers, and it was that second killing rather than the first that made the east's position untenable.

> **Forward reference.** ⚠️ The war itself - its conduct, its external arming, its famine and its remarkable aftermath - is taught in Lesson 23 as one of six post-independence mechanisms, so that the structural argument can be made once rather than three times. Do not narrate the war here.

### 5. The two quiet transfers

✅ The other two British colonies in West Africa achieved independence without serious incident: **Sierra Leone in 1961** and **the Gambia in 1965**.

⚠️ These are one sentence in the source and they should be one sentence in an answer too - but the right sentence. ⚠️ Their value is as controls. ⚠️ They share West Africa's defining condition, the absence of settlers, and they lack Nigeria's defining condition, three regionally concentrated populations of comparable weight contesting a single federal centre. ⚠️ They therefore produce the plain version of the route: a transfer without incident. ⚠️ Their quietness is evidence, not an absence of evidence, and citing them briefly prevents an answer from suggesting that West African independence was generally troubled.

### Comparative note: Ghana and Nigeria, same region, opposite difficulties

| Variable | ✅ Ghana | ✅ Nigeria |
|---|---|---|
| Scale | ✅ Small; a single dominant party won 34 of 38 seats in 1951 | ✅ Over sixty million people, the largest British African colony |
| Political structure at transfer | Unitary self-government under one prime minister | ✅ Federal: three regional assemblies from 1954 plus a central government in Lagos |
| Sequence | ✅ Self-government 1952, then independence 1957 - centre first | ✅ Regions self-governing first, then national independence in 1960 |
| Resource base | ✅ Cocoa, with gold and bauxite | ✅ Extensive oil in the eastern coastal area |
| Where power and wealth sat | Together | ⚠️ Apart: prime minister from the north, oil in the east |
| Principal post-independence danger | ⚠️ A commodity price collapse and the closing of politics - Lesson 23, case 1 | ⚠️ A distributive dispute becoming a secession question - Lesson 23, case 2 |
| What it proves about preparation | ⚠️ The best preparation in British Africa did not prevent a coup in 1966 | ⚠️ The most careful constitutional design did not prevent a war in 1967 |

⚠️ The last row is the payoff, and it is the reason these two lessons sit together. ⚠️ Britain tried the two available remedies - administrative apprenticeship in one territory, constitutional engineering in the other - and both territories broke down within a decade. ⚠️ That is not an argument that the remedies were worthless; it is an argument that the causes of post-colonial instability lay outside the range of what a transfer process could address. Lesson 23 identifies what they were.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Nigeria is the standard case for the redrawal of boundaries and for federalism as a response to colonial-era plurality, and it is one of the two cases the account itself names where ethnic differences helped to cause civil war after independence.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually inside a question on post-independence African instability, or on whether colonial powers prepared their colonies adequately. ⚠️ The discriminating move is to refuse the "unprepared" framing and to ask instead why a specific and reasonable remedy failed, then to answer with the four pressures - structural complaint, recession, discredited election, militarisation - in that order.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Explaining Biafra by ethnic difference alone. ✅ The account records a fifteen per cent price rise by 1964, wages below the calculated minimum living wage, and a government accused of blatantly trying to fix the 1964 elections; these are the conditions that turned a permanent division into a rupture.
2. ⚠️ Treating the January 1966 coup as a straightforward Ibo seizure of power. ✅ General Ironsi, himself an Ibo, was murdered by northern soldiers, and Gowon, a northerner, emerged supreme.
3. ⚠️ Ignoring the sequence of self-government. ✅ Regions first, then the centre - which left the federal government negotiating with established regional powers.
4. ❌ Asserting Nigerian oil revenue figures for the 1960s. ⚠️ The account's oil-dependence statistic belongs to the later period and is used in Lesson 23; do not project it backwards.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Nigeria was easily the largest British African colony, with a population of over sixty million.
2. ✅ More difficult than Ghana because of great size and regional differences: the vast Muslim north dominated by the Hausa and Fulani, the western region of the Yoruba, the eastern region of the Ibo.
3. ✅ Extensive oil resources had been discovered in the eastern coastal area.
4. ✅ Nnamdi Azikiwe, "Zik": educated in the USA, newspaper editor in the Gold Coast, returned to Nigeria in 1937, founded a series of newspapers, gained enormous prestige.
5. ✅ His impressive general strike of 1945 was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence.
6. ✅ A federal system was judged most suitable; the 1954 constitution created local assemblies for the three regions with a central federal government in Lagos.
7. ✅ The regions assumed self-government first; the country as a whole became independent in 1960; Nigeria became a republic in 1963 with Azikiwe as president and Balewa as prime minister.
8. ✅ The regions felt the central government in Lagos did not safeguard their interests sufficiently; Yoruba and Ibo complained of northern domination even though Azikiwe was an Ibo.
9. ✅ By 1964 prices had risen fifteen per cent, unemployment was rising and wages were well below the calculated minimum living wage.
10. ✅ Balewa arrested Chief Awolowo - premier of the western region until 1959 and by then federal leader of the opposition - and the western region for a time seemed likely to break away; the central government was accused of corruption after blatantly trying to fix the 1964 elections.
11. ✅ January 1966: a military coup by mainly Ibo officers killed Balewa; savage massacres of Ibos followed in the north; General Ironsi, an Ibo, was murdered by northern soldiers; Colonel Gowon, a northerner, emerged supreme.
12. ✅ Ibos fled back to the east; Colonel Ojukwu announced secession as Biafra in May 1967; Gowon launched a "short surgical police action".
13. ✅ Sierra Leone became independent in 1961 and the Gambia in 1965, without serious incident.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: a federation survives by procedure, and the 1964 election destroyed the procedure; ethnic difference supplied the fault line, recession supplied the pressure and the discredited ballot removed the remedy.

### Concept check

**Question:** Britain gave Nigeria a federal constitution precisely because of its regional diversity, and the federation collapsed into war within seven years of independence. Explain why the standard remedy failed, without relying on ethnic difference as the explanation.

**Model answer:** It failed because federalism distributes power territorially, and where identity is also territorial every distributive dispute becomes a question about the survival of the union rather than a question about policy. Three specific conditions turned that structural weakness into a rupture. First, the design was already contested: although each of the three regions had its own local government under the 1954 constitution, the regions felt the central government in Lagos did not safeguard their interests sufficiently, and having an Ibo president in Azikiwe did not answer the complaint because the complaint concerned where effective power lay. Second, a contraction made distribution zero-sum: by 1964 prices had risen by fifteen per cent, unemployment was rising and wages were on average well below the calculated minimum living wage. Third, and decisively, the procedure that federations depend on was destroyed when the central government was accused of blatantly trying to fix the 1964 elections, having already arrested Chief Awolowo - premier of the Western Region until 1959 and, at his arrest in 1962, leader of the opposition in the federal parliament - when criticism mounted. Once the ballot could not settle the distribution question, the remaining instruments were the street and the army, and in January 1966 the army was used. The sequence of self-government compounded the problem, because the regions assumed it before the national government existed, so the centre negotiated with entrenched regional powers rather than being the source of their authority.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that Britain failed to prepare Nigeria. The account itself records careful preparations - a federal constitution in 1954, regional self-government, then national independence in 1960, under a capable and moderate prime minister with the veteran nationalist Azikiwe as president. The failure was of a specific remedy under specific pressures, not of effort. This matters because the same period shows the opposite experiment producing the same result: Ghana had the best-prepared administrative transfer in British Africa and produced a one-party state by 1964 and a coup in 1966. Neither administrative apprenticeship nor constitutional engineering could reach the causes that actually destabilised post-colonial states.

## Lesson 11 - East Africa: Kenya, Tanganyika and Uganda

Progress: 11/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the settler factor in Kenya; the land question and the colour bar; Kenyatta, the Kenya African Union and the Forty Group; the Mau Mau uprising and the state of emergency; Operation Anvil, the Hola camp and what became known in 2005; Macmillan's arithmetic and the "wind of change"; the route to December 1963; and the contrasting transfers in Tanganyika and Uganda

Book context: queried - the textbook account's East Africa section, covering British expectations of multiracial government, Macmillan's Cape Town speech of 1960, Nyerere and TANU, Uganda and the kabaka, the Kenyan population figures, Kenyatta's formation and the Forty Group, the land and colour-bar grievances, the settler strategy of provoking confrontation, the 1952 uprising and emergency, Operation Anvil, the Hola camp scandal, the figures published by David Anderson and Caroline Elkins in 2005, Macmillan's five-per-cent judgement, the stages of African representation, and the route through KANU and KADU to independence in December 1963

⚠️ Note the two arrows at the bottom of the "what broke it" box. The uprising and the arithmetic are not alternative explanations; they are the same explanation seen from two ends. The arithmetic became compelling *because* defending five per cent of the population had turned out to require a hundred thousand troops and a system of detention camps.

### 1. British expectations, and the speech that changed them

✅ The British thought that independence for the colonies of East Africa was not so necessary as for West Africa, and that when independence did come it would be in the form of multiracial governments in which the European and Asian settlers would play a significant part.

⚠️ That sentence records a policy, and the policy is the thing that had to be abandoned. ⚠️ "Multiracial government" sounds inclusive and was in practice a formula for entrenched minority weight: a constitution in which communities are represented as communities gives a small community a share out of all proportion to its numbers. ⚠️ It is the same device as Algeria's split assembly and Southern Rhodesia's weighted federal parliament, and it is the standard settler-colony constitutional proposal of the period.

✅ But during Harold Macmillan's government of 1957-63 an important change took place in British policy towards both East and Central Africa. ✅ Macmillan had come to realise the strength of black African nationalist feeling; in a famous speech in Cape Town in **1960** he said: "the wind of change is blowing through the continent. Whether we like it or not, this growth of national consciousness is a political fact, and our national policies must take account of it."

⚠️ Three things about that speech are worth an answer.

⚠️ **Where it was delivered.** Cape Town, not Nairobi or London - in the capital of the one African state where a white minority government had already won and was consolidating. ⚠️ The speech was therefore addressed to the settler interest across the continent and, most pointedly, to the government that embodied it. ✅ Lesson 21 records what happened next: at the 1961 Commonwealth Conference criticism of South Africa was intense, and Verwoerd withdrew its application for continued membership.

⚠️ **What it claims.** Not that majority rule is right, but that national consciousness is "a political fact" which policy "must take account of". ⚠️ It is an argument from necessity rather than from justice, which is exactly what a Conservative prime minister needed in order to bring his own party's settler sympathisers with him.

⚠️ **What it does not say.** It sets no dates and concedes no principle beyond acknowledgement. ⚠️ Its force came from the identity of the speaker and the audience, not from its content - which is a useful general lesson about political speech.

### 2. The Kenyan deadlock: land, the colour bar, and a third actor

✅ Kenya was the most difficult area of East Africa to deal with because of the presence of a significant non-African population. ✅ As well as the ten million Africans there were some sixty-six thousand white settlers who were violently opposed to black majority rule, around two hundred thousand Indians and thirty-five thousand Muslim Arabs. ✅ But it was the white settlers who had the political influence over the British government.

✅ The main African grievance was the land situation: the most fertile farming land was on the highland plateau, but only white settlers were allowed to farm there. ✅ Africans also resented the discrimination and the colour bar between blacks and whites, under which they were treated as inferior, second-class citizens. ✅ This was especially unacceptable since many Africans had served in the army during the Second World War and had received equal treatment and respect from whites. ✅ Moreover it was clear that the whites expected to keep all their privileges even if they had to agree to independence.

⚠️ That is a very tightly constructed grievance and it should be reproduced with its internal logic intact. ⚠️ It is not a general objection to foreign rule. It is a specific complaint that the best land is reserved by race, that legal inferiority is imposed by race, and - the sharpest element - that the men who had experienced racial equality in the metropole's own army were subject to that inferiority on returning home. ⚠️ Lesson 2's third mechanism, the political education of servicemen, becomes a concrete political grievance here and nowhere more clearly.

✅ The settlers' own case is also recorded and should not be omitted: they pointed out that they had worked hard and devoted their lives to making their farms successful, that they now saw themselves as white Africans, and that Kenya was their homeland.

⚠️ Report that fairly, because doing so strengthens the analysis. ⚠️ It is a claim about belonging, not about profit, and a claim about belonging admits no compensation. ⚠️ This is why settler questions are structurally harder than commercial ones: an expropriated company can be paid; a community that regards a territory as its homeland cannot be bought out of that conviction.

✅ And the settlers acted, against the British as much as against the Africans. ✅ The white settlers refused to negotiate with Kenyatta and were determined to prolong their rule; ✅ they provoked a confrontation, hoping that violence would destroy the African Party. ✅ The British government was under pressure from both sides, and the white settlers were supported by certain big-business interests in Britain; even so, it did not handle the situation with much imagination. ✅ The KAU was able to make little progress, the only British concession being to allow six Africans to join the Legislative Council of fifty-four members.

⚠️ Six out of fifty-four, for a population of ten million against sixty-six thousand. ⚠️ Quote that ratio; it does more analytical work than any adjective.

### 3. Kenyatta, and the punishment of the moderate

✅ The main Kenyan African leader was **Jomo Kenyatta**. ✅ Born in 1894, he was a member of the Kikuyu and a veteran among African nationalists; he spent some time in Britain during the 1930s and returned to Kenya in 1947, becoming leader of the **Kenya African Union (KAU)**, which consisted mostly of members of the dominant Kikuyu. ⚠️ The textbook account calls it the "Kenya African Unity Party"; its name was the Kenya African Union, and that is the form used here. ✅ He hoped to win African majority rule gradually, first of all gaining more African seats on the Legislative Council. ✅ However, the more radical wing of his party - calling themselves the **Forty Group** - wanted to drive the British out by force, if necessary.

⚠️ Hold the two halves of that together, because their relationship is the mechanism. ⚠️ A gradualist strategy requires an interlocutor. When the settlers refused to negotiate and the only concession available was six seats in fifty-four, gradualism had nothing to show for itself, and the radical wing's argument - that force was the only remaining method - became empirically stronger with every year of refusal. ⚠️ The settlers produced the radicalism they cited as the reason for refusing.

✅ In 1952 African impatience burst out in an uprising against the British, with attacks on European-owned farms and on black workers. ✅ It was organised by the **Mau Mau** secret society, whose members were mainly Kikuyu. ✅ A state of emergency was declared in 1952; Kenyatta and other nationalist leaders were arrested and found guilty of terrorism. ✅ Kenyatta was kept in jail for six years although he had publicly condemned violence and insisted that the KAU had not been involved in organising the rebellion.

⚠️ Two details in that passage should be carried into any answer. ⚠️ First, the uprising attacked European-owned farms **and black workers** - which is a statement that the conflict had an internal dimension and was not a simple racial front. ⚠️ Second, the state's response was to imprison the man who had publicly condemned violence, on a charge the account records alongside his denial. ⚠️ That is the clearest illustration available of a colonial state removing its own best available negotiating partner, and it cost Britain nine years: ✅ Kenyatta was at last released in 1961 and was prime minister by May 1963.

### 4. The counter-insurgency, and the evidence that arrived fifty years late

⚠️ This section requires the attribution discipline more strictly than any other in the session. Read the figures with their source attached and never separate them from it.

✅ In **April 1954** the British launched **Operation Anvil**, a cordon-and-search sweep of Nairobi carried out by about **five thousand British troops with roughly a thousand police**, supported by the Home Guard, in which **more than fifty thousand people were screened** and large numbers were detained - ✅ though the Africans regarded themselves as freedom fighters, not terrorists. ⚠️ The textbook account describes Anvil as a deployment of one hundred thousand troops; that figure is not supportable and is not used anywhere in this session.

⚠️ That parenthesis is the account's own, and it is worth keeping, because it marks a genuine problem of historical language rather than a rhetorical flourish. The term used depends on the writer's position, and a candidate should signal awareness of that rather than adopting one side's vocabulary silently.

✅ There was a scandal in 1959 with revelations of brutal treatment of prisoners at the **Hola detention camp**, where savage beatings left **eleven dead**.

✅ However, the British government managed to hide from people at home the scale of what was going on in Kenya. ✅ It was only in **2005** that the scale was set out, in **two separate books** by the historians **David Anderson** and **Caroline Elkins**. ⚠️ The two books are not a joint finding and their figures must never be run together. ✅ **Anderson** records that the British hanged **more than a thousand** Kikuyu and that some **twenty thousand** were killed in the fighting. ✅ **Elkins** separately advanced a far larger estimate - that **up to one hundred thousand** people died in the detention camps, where there was a culture of brutality, routine beatings, killings and torture of the most grotesque kinds. ⚠️ **Elkins's camp-mortality estimate is her own and it is contested.** Other scholars, working from census and demographic evidence, have put the excess mortality far lower - in the tens of thousands rather than the hundreds of thousands - and **no reconciled total exists**. ❌ Do not average the competing estimates, and ❌ do not attribute the hundred-thousand figure jointly to "Anderson and Elkins". ✅ One police chief later admitted that conditions in the camps were far worse than he had suffered as a prisoner of war in Japan. ✅ By contrast, fewer than a hundred whites were killed.

⚠️ Four points of method, all examinable.

⚠️ **Attribute, and attribute separately.** These are figures advanced by named historians working in 2005, not contemporary official statistics, and they are not one agreed set. ⚠️ Write "Anderson records more than a thousand hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting", and write "Elkins estimated up to a hundred thousand deaths in the camps, an estimate that other scholars have contested on demographic grounds" - both sentences are then checkable. ❌ Write "one hundred thousand Kenyans died in British camps" and it is an unowned assertion; ❌ write "Anderson and Elkins record a hundred thousand deaths" and it is a misattribution.

⚠️ **Note the concealment as itself a finding.** ✅ The British government managed to hide the scale from people at home. ⚠️ This is decisive evidence against the metropolitan-thesis assumption that British decolonisation was a considered policy adjustment carried out in public view. ⚠️ It also bears directly on Lesson 3: a state that concealed the conduct of its withdrawal is not a reliable narrator of its motives for withdrawing.

⚠️ **Note the ratio.** ✅ Fewer than a hundred whites killed against the figures above. ⚠️ Contemporary British and settler discourse treated Mau Mau as an existential threat to Europeans; the casualty distribution records something else entirely, and stating the ratio is the most economical way to make that point.

⚠️ **Note the date.** ⚠️ The public record of this episode is younger than the independence of most African states. ⚠️ Histories written before 2005 could not have incorporated it, which is a specific instance of the general problem raised in Lesson 3 about evidence availability shaping interpretation.

### 5. Why Britain changed course, and the ladder that followed

✅ The uprising had been defeated by 1960 - ✅ but by then, ironically, the British, encouraged by the "wind of change" and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude.

⚠️ That single sentence contains the whole argument of Lesson 3's cost-imposition mechanism and is worth committing to memory. ⚠️ The movement lost; the objective was achieved; expense did the work.

✅ **Harold Macmillan**, who became prime minister in January 1957, faced up to the fact that it was impossible and indefensible to continue trying to prolong the privileged position of a group which made up no more than **five per cent** of the population.

⚠️ Notice the two adjectives, because they are doing different jobs. **Impossible** is the cost argument: the campaign had shown what holding the position required. **Indefensible** is the legitimacy argument: after the Hola scandal of 1959, the position could not be defended in Parliament or in public. ⚠️ A good answer names both, because either alone is a weaker explanation.

⚠️ The concessions that followed track the grievances of section 2 exactly, which is itself evidence that the grievances had been correctly identified all along.

✅ Africans were allowed to settle in the fertile highland plateau. ✅ Restrictions were lifted on what the Kikuyu could grow, and as a result **coffee** became one of the main crops. ✅ In 1957 elections were held for eight African seats in the Legislative Council, and the following year plans were announced to increase African membership. ✅ In 1960 Africans became the majority group on the council and were given four out of ten seats in the Council of Ministers. ✅ In 1961 Kenyatta was at last released.

⚠️ Stop on the coffee. ⚠️ Restrictions on what African farmers could grow had nothing to do with agronomy and everything to do with competition: settler producers were protected from African producers by law. ⚠️ Their removal, and the immediate emergence of coffee as a main crop, is the most economical proof available that the colonial land system was an economic arrangement dressed as an administrative one.

### 6. The successor-state question, in miniature

✅ Progress towards independence was held up by rivalry and disagreement between the different groups. ✅ While Kenyatta had been in prison new leaders had emerged; the larger groups wanted a strong, centralised government which would be dominated by them, ✅ while a number of smaller groups who did not relish being controlled by the Kikuyu and Luo, led by **Ronald Ngala**, formed a rival party, the **Kenya African Democratic Union (KADU)**, and wanted a federal form of government which would enable them to have more control over their own affairs.

✅ Both parties worked together to form a coalition government in 1962 in preparation for elections held in May 1963. ✅ **KANU** won a clear majority and Kenyatta became prime minister of a self-governing Kenya. ✅ It was decided to abandon the idea of a federal system of government. ✅ Kenya became fully independent in **December 1963**, and a year later a republic with Kenyatta as its first president and **Odinga** as vice-president.

⚠️ The federal question here is the same question Lesson 10 met in Nigeria and Lesson 18 will meet in Malaya, and the three answers differ. ⚠️ Nigeria adopted federalism and it did not hold. ⚠️ Kenya considered it and abandoned it. ⚠️ Malaya adopted a federation in which the central government had firm overall control. ⚠️ A comparison across the three is one of the strongest available pieces of value addition in this topic.

✅ To his great credit, in spite of his harsh treatment by the British, Kenyatta favoured reconciliation: whites who decided to stay on after independence were fairly treated provided they took Kenyan citizenship, and **Kenya became one of the most pro-British of the former colonies**.

⚠️ That outcome deserves emphasis because it is genuinely surprising and is a strong closing line in an answer. ⚠️ The territory where British conduct had been worst produced one of the most pro-British successor states, and the decision was taken personally by the man Britain had imprisoned for six years on a charge he denied. ⚠️ It is also a caution against assuming that post-colonial alignment can be predicted from the violence of the colonial ending.

✅ Sadly, the internal divisions continued to cause problems after independence: the Luo believed that the Kikuyu were receiving special treatment from the government, Kenyatta and Odinga fell out, **Mboya** was assassinated in 1969, and Odinga was sacked and spent two years in prison.

### 7. The two East African contrasts

⚠️ Kenya is the exception in its own region, and the contrast is what proves the settler thesis rather than merely illustrating it.

✅ **Tanganyika.** The nationalist campaign was conducted by the **Tanganyika African National Union** led by **Dr Julius Nyerere**, who had been educated at the University of Edinburgh. ✅ He insisted that the government must be African, but he also made it clear that whites had nothing to fear from black rule. ✅ Macmillan's government, impressed by Nyerere's ability and sincerity, conceded independence with black majority rule in **1961**. ✅ The island of Zanzibar was later united with Tanganyika and the country took the name **Tanzania in 1964**. ✅ Nyerere was president until his retirement in 1985.

⚠️ Nyerere's method is worth naming because it is a technique rather than a temperament: he removed the coloniser's strongest argument for delay. ⚠️ The case for postponement in East Africa rested on protecting settlers; a nationalist who guarantees settler safety publicly and credibly leaves the metropole with no stated reason to wait.

✅ **Uganda.** Independence was delayed for a time by internal disputes: the ruler of the Buganda area, known as the **kabaka**, objected to the introduction of democracy. ✅ Eventually a solution was found in a federal constitution which allowed the kabaka to retain some powers in Buganda. ✅ Uganda itself became independent in **1962** with **Dr Milton Obote** as prime minister.

⚠️ Uganda is the third variant of the successor-state problem and it is distinct from both Nigeria's and Kenya's. ⚠️ The obstacle was not a settler population or a rival ethnic party but a *pre-colonial monarchy* whose authority was incompatible with an elected national government. ⚠️ The remedy was asymmetric federalism - special powers for one unit - which is a constitutional device worth knowing by name.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Kenya is the account's own named exception to the judgement that Britain handled decolonisation better than others, and it is the principal British case of settler obstruction, counter-insurgency and land as the core colonial grievance.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ As the violent half of a comparison; as evidence in a question on the costs of empire; or inside a question on why decolonisation took different forms. ⚠️ The strongest structure is: the deadlock (land, colour bar, six seats in fifty-four); the third actor provoking confrontation; the uprising and its suppression with attributed figures; the two adjectives in Macmillan's judgement; and the ladder from 1957 to December 1963, ending with the reconciliation that followed.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Stating the Kenyan figures without attributing them, or attributing them jointly. ✅ Anderson gives more than a thousand hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting; Elkins separately estimated up to a hundred thousand deaths in the camps, an estimate that is contested; before 2005 the British government had hidden the scale from people at home.
2. ⚠️ Treating the uprising as a failure because it was defeated. ✅ It was defeated by 1960, and the expense of defeating it is recorded among the reasons Britain changed its attitude.
3. ⚠️ Presenting Kenya as typical of British Africa. ✅ Tanganyika conceded in 1961 and Uganda in 1962 by negotiation; Kenya is the account's explicit exception.
4. ⚠️ Forgetting that the settlers acted against Britain. ✅ They refused to negotiate with Kenyatta and provoked a confrontation hoping violence would destroy the African party.
5. ❌ Describing the highland restrictions as an agricultural policy. ✅ Their removal produced coffee as a main crop, which shows they were protection for settler producers.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Britain expected East African independence to come later than West Africa's and to take the form of multiracial governments in which European and Asian settlers played a significant part.
2. ✅ Macmillan, Cape Town, 1960: "the wind of change is blowing through the continent... this growth of national consciousness is a political fact, and our national policies must take account of it."
3. ✅ Kenya's population as recorded: ten million Africans, some sixty-six thousand white settlers violently opposed to black majority rule, around two hundred thousand Indians, thirty-five thousand Muslim Arabs; the settlers held the political influence over the British government.
4. ✅ The main grievance was land: the most fertile farming land was on the highland plateau and only white settlers were allowed to farm there; plus the colour bar, made more unacceptable by wartime service in which Africans had received equal treatment.
5. ✅ Settlers claimed they had worked hard, saw themselves as white Africans and regarded Kenya as their homeland, and expected to keep all their privileges even under independence.
6. ✅ Settlers refused to negotiate with Kenyatta and provoked a confrontation, hoping violence would destroy the African party; they were supported by certain big-business interests in Britain.
7. ✅ The only British concession for years: six Africans on a Legislative Council of fifty-four members.
8. ✅ Kenyatta: born 1894, Kikuyu, in Britain during the 1930s, returned 1947, led the KAU, wanted gradual majority rule; the radical Forty Group wanted to drive the British out by force.
9. ✅ 1952 uprising organised by the Mau Mau secret society, attacking European-owned farms and black workers; state of emergency declared 1952; Kenyatta jailed six years despite publicly condemning violence and denying KAU involvement.
10. ✅ Operation Anvil, April 1954: about five thousand British troops and a thousand police, with the Home Guard; over fifty thousand people screened and large numbers detained. ✅ 1959 Hola detention camp scandal: savage beatings left eleven dead.
11. ✅ The British government hid the scale at home; the evidence was published in 2005 in two separate books. ✅ Anderson: more than a thousand Kikuyu hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting. ✅ Elkins, separately and contestedly: up to one hundred thousand dead in detention camps, where there was a culture of brutality, routine beatings, killings and torture. ⚠️ Demographic critiques put camp-related excess mortality far lower and no reconciled total exists. ✅ Fewer than a hundred whites killed.
12. ✅ The uprising was defeated by 1960, but by then the British, encouraged by the wind of change and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude.
13. ✅ Macmillan judged it impossible and indefensible to prolong the privileged position of a group making up no more than five per cent of the population.
14. ✅ Concessions: African settlement on the fertile highland plateau; restrictions lifted on Kikuyu crops, making coffee a main crop; eight African seats in 1957; an African majority on the council and four of ten ministries in 1960; Kenyatta released 1961.
15. ✅ KANU wanted a strong centralised government; KADU under Ronald Ngala wanted federalism; coalition 1962; KANU won in May 1963; federalism abandoned; full independence December 1963; a republic a year later with Kenyatta president and Odinga vice-president.
16. ✅ Kenyatta favoured reconciliation; whites who took Kenyan citizenship were fairly treated; Kenya became one of the most pro-British former colonies. ✅ Internal divisions persisted: Mboya assassinated 1969, Odinga sacked and imprisoned for two years.
17. ✅ Tanganyika: Nyerere, educated at Edinburgh, led TANU, insisted on African government while assuring whites; independence with black majority rule 1961; united with Zanzibar as Tanzania in 1964; president until 1985.
18. ✅ Uganda: delayed by the kabaka of Buganda's objection to democracy; solved by a federal constitution leaving the kabaka some powers; independent 1962 under Milton Obote.
19. ⚠️ Master formulation: Kenya shows a defeated insurgency achieving its objective by cost imposition, and shows the settler community acting as a third party against both the colonised and its own metropole.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Kenyan uprising was militarily defeated by 1960, and Kenya became independent in December 1963. Explain the causal link, and say precisely what Macmillan's judgement about "five per cent of the population" was based on.

**Model answer:** The link is cost imposition rather than victory. The uprising forced Britain to conduct an expensive and sustained campaign, including Operation Anvil in April 1954, a sweep of Nairobi by about five thousand troops and a thousand police in which over fifty thousand people were screened, and the account states directly that by 1960 the British, encouraged by the wind of change and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude. Macmillan's judgement rested on two distinct grounds, and both should be named. The first was practical impossibility: the campaign had demonstrated what defending the settler position actually required, against a population of ten million Africans and fewer than sixty-six thousand settlers. The second was that the position had become indefensible in public, which follows directly from the Hola detention camp scandal of 1959, where revelations of brutal treatment left eleven dead and made the policy impossible to justify in Parliament. What was not known at the time is important to the historiography rather than to Macmillan's decision: the British government managed to hide the scale of the campaign from people at home, and the evidence appeared only in 2005, Anderson recording more than a thousand Kikuyu hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting, and Elkins separately and contestedly estimating up to one hundred thousand deaths in the detention camps, against fewer than a hundred whites killed. The concessions that followed tracked the original grievances precisely: African settlement on the fertile highland plateau and the lifting of restrictions on what the Kikuyu could grow, after which coffee became a main crop.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the settlers simply as beneficiaries of British policy rather than as an independent actor opposed to it. In Kenya the settlers refused to negotiate with Kenyatta, were determined to prolong their rule, and deliberately provoked a confrontation in the hope that violence would destroy the African party - a strategy aimed at forcing the British government's hand, not at executing its instructions. Recognising the settler community as a third party with its own objectives is what makes Kenya, Algeria, Southern Rhodesia and South Africa intelligible as a family of cases.

## Lesson 12 - Central Africa: the Federation of 1953-63

Progress: 12/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the settler strategy of combining resources; the Central African Federation and the arithmetic behind its parliament; Banda, Kaunda and Nkomo; the emergency of 1959 and the Monckton Commission of 1960; the constitutions of 1961-62; the dissolution of December 1963; and the independence of Malawi and Zambia in 1964

Book context: queried - the textbook account's Central Africa section, covering the region's greater settler entrenchment, the suppression of African education by settlers, the 1953 creation of the Central African Federation and its population arithmetic, the weighting of the federal parliament at Salisbury, the settler hope of dominion status, the three African leaders, the 1959 emergency and mass arrests, British Labour and Macleod's sympathy, the Monckton Commission recommendations, the 1961-62 constitutions, the termination of December 1963 and the independence of Malawi and Zambia in 1964
