### Visual: a federation designed as a defensive weapon

```text
   THE SETTLER PROBLEM, 1953:
   "Alarmed at the spread of nationalism, the whites decided that
    their best policy was to COMBINE RESOURCES."

   THE INSTRUMENT: they persuade Churchill's government (1953) to
   allow a union of THREE colonies:

        NYASALAND  +  NORTHERN RHODESIA  +  SOUTHERN RHODESIA
                                              (where settlers are
                                               most entrenched)
                        = THE CENTRAL AFRICAN FEDERATION

   THE AIM, stated: "to preserve the supremacy of the white minority"

   THE ARITHMETIC:
        about    300,000  Europeans
        out of about 8,500,000  total population
        => roughly 1 in 28

   THE DESIGN:
        federal parliament at SALISBURY (capital of Southern
        Rhodesia) "heavily weighted to favour the whites"
        settler hope: full independence from Britain WITH
        DOMINION STATUS

   THE AFRICAN RESPONSE:
        Dr HASTINGS BANDA      (Nyasaland)
        KENNETH KAUNDA         (Northern Rhodesia)   campaign for
        JOSHUA NKOMO           (Southern Rhodesia)   BLACK MAJORITY
                                                     RULE
   1959  violence -> state of emergency in Nyasaland and Southern
         Rhodesia, MASS ARRESTS of Africans
   1960  MONCKTON COMMISSION recommends: votes for Africans, an end
         to racial discrimination, and THE RIGHT OF TERRITORIES TO
         LEAVE THE FEDERATION
   1961-62 new constitutions in Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia
         "in effect, allowed the Africans their own parliaments"
   Dec 1963 BOTH want to leave -> FEDERATION TERMINATED
         = "signalling DEFEAT FOR THE SETTLERS"
   1964  Nyasaland -> MALAWI ;  Northern Rhodesia -> ZAMBIA
         (Southern Rhodesia: see Lesson 13)
```

⚠️ The instructive feature of this case is that the settlers lost by their own instrument. The federation was built to aggregate white strength across three territories; the Monckton Commission then attached an exit right to it, and the two territories with the weakest settler presence used that right to leave and to become independent under African governments a year later.

### 1. The educational consequence of settlement

⚠️ Before the politics, one structural point that connects this lesson to Lesson 9 and should open any answer on the region.

✅ Central Africa was the most troublesome area for Britain to deal with because this was where the settlers were most numerous and most deeply entrenched, particularly in Southern Rhodesia. ✅ Another problem was that the numbers of well-educated Africans were much smaller than in West Africa, because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided.

⚠️ That is a compound disadvantage and it is worth naming as such. ⚠️ Settlers obstructed independence directly, by holding political power and refusing to negotiate; and they obstructed it indirectly, by suppressing the education that produces the professional class with which a metropole can negotiate a transfer. ⚠️ Compare the Gold Coast's small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals from Lesson 9, and the Belgian denial of advanced education in Lesson 19, and a general proposition emerges: **the availability of a successor class was a policy variable, not a natural fact, and it was set by whoever benefited from its absence.**

### 2. The federation as a defensive combination

✅ Alarmed at the spread of nationalism, the whites decided that their best policy was to combine resources. ✅ They persuaded Churchill's government in **1953** to allow them to set up a union of the three colonies - Nyasaland and Northern and Southern Rhodesia - to be known as the **Central African Federation**. ✅ Their aim was to preserve the supremacy of the white minority, about three hundred thousand Europeans out of a total population of about eight and a half million. ✅ The federal parliament in **Salisbury**, the capital of Southern Rhodesia, was heavily weighted to favour the whites, who hoped that the federation would soon gain full independence from Britain, with dominion status.

⚠️ Three analytical points, each worth a sentence.

⚠️ **This is the settler strategy in its purest form.** The settlers of Southern Rhodesia, who were numerous and entrenched, aggregated themselves with the much smaller settler populations of two territories where the African majority was overwhelming. ⚠️ Aggregation does not change any territory's internal ratio, but it changes the unit in which the ratio is counted, and the unit is what a constitution applies to. ⚠️ Federation was, in this case, a device for making a local majority into a minority inside a larger frame - the exact inverse of the reason federations are usually recommended.

⚠️ **The prize was dominion status.** ⚠️ Dominion status meant self-government under the Crown without British power to intervene - the constitutional position of Canada, Australia or, since 1910, South Africa. ⚠️ The settlers were not seeking to preserve colonial rule; they were seeking to end it on their own terms, before Africans could end it on theirs. ⚠️ This is the same objective Ian Smith pursued by other means in Lesson 13 and that the Afrikaner Nationalists had already achieved in Lesson 21.

⚠️ **Britain consented.** ✅ Churchill's government allowed it in 1953. ⚠️ Any answer claiming that Britain steadily advanced African interests must reckon with the fact that the metropole authorised the creation of a state whose declared aim was white supremacy, seven years before the wind-of-change speech was delivered.

### 3. The African response and the emergency

✅ The Africans watched with growing distrust, and their leaders - **Dr Hastings Banda** in Nyasaland, **Kenneth Kaunda** in Northern Rhodesia and **Joshua Nkomo** in Southern Rhodesia - began to campaign for black majority rule. ✅ As violence developed, a state of emergency was declared in Nyasaland and Southern Rhodesia, with mass arrests of Africans, in **1959**.

⚠️ 1959 should be filed as a year of simultaneous crises in British Africa, because the coincidence is causally significant: ✅ the Hola camp revelations in Kenya, ✅ the emergency and mass arrests in Central Africa, and - just across the Belgian frontier - ✅ widespread rioting in Leopoldville in January 1959 that produced the Congo's six-month timetable. ⚠️ Macmillan's Cape Town speech of 1960 follows all three. ⚠️ Policy did not change because of an idea; it changed because three separate coercive commitments became visible within a year.

✅ However, there was much support for the Africans in Britain, especially in the Labour Party, and the Conservative colonial secretary **Iain Macleod** was sympathetic.

⚠️ Note that metropolitan politics is here a genuine variable rather than background. ⚠️ Settler strength in a colony depends on metropolitan allies; when the metropolitan political balance shifts, so does the settlers' effective power, without anything changing on the ground. ⚠️ Macleod is the same man quoted in Lesson 2 saying that Africa could not possibly have been held by force.

### 4. The Monckton Commission, and the clause that killed the federation

✅ The **Monckton Commission** of **1960** recommended votes for Africans, an end to racial discrimination, and **the right of territories to leave the Federation**.

⚠️ The third recommendation is the one that decided the outcome, and an answer should say why. ⚠️ A federation created to lock three territories into a single white-dominated unit is destroyed by a right of exit, because exit converts the constitutional question from "who controls the federal parliament?" - which the settlers had rigged - to "does each territory wish to remain?", which they could not rig. ⚠️ The first question is answered by the weighted parliament in Salisbury; the second is answered by the territorial populations, and in two of the three the settler population was small.

✅ The British introduced new constitutions in Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia which, in effect, allowed the Africans their own parliaments in **1961-62**. ✅ Both wanted to leave the Federation, which was therefore terminated in **December 1963**, signalling defeat for the settlers. ✅ The following year Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia became fully independent, taking the names **Malawi** and **Zambia**.

⚠️ So the sequence is: aggregate to entrench; acquire an exit clause under pressure; grant African parliaments in the two weaker territories; lose the vote on remaining; dissolve. ⚠️ Ten years from creation to termination.

⚠️ And the residue is the subject of the next lesson. ⚠️ The dissolution left Southern Rhodesia - the territory where the settlers were most entrenched and which had hosted the federal capital - alone, with its settler political class intact, its African parties banned and its ambition for independence unsatisfied. ✅ When Zambia and Malawi were given independence, the whites assumed that Southern Rhodesia would get the same treatment and put in a formal request for independence. ⚠️ The Federation's collapse therefore did not merely fail to solve the Southern Rhodesian problem; it concentrated it.

### Comparative note: three federations of the decolonising period

⚠️ This is the point at which to assemble the federation comparison, because three attempts occur within five years and all three are in this session's scope.

| Federation | ✅ Dates | ⚠️ Purpose | ✅ Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|
| ✅ **West Indies Federation** | ✅ Set up 1958; survived until 1962 | ⚠️ To make small, scattered territories viable as a single independent state | ✅ Never functioned successfully; constant squabbles about budget contributions and representation; Jamaica voted to leave in September 1961 and Trinidad withdrew in January 1962, and Britain abandoned the federation in 1962, granting independence separately |
| ✅ **Central African Federation** | ✅ 1953 to December 1963 | ✅ To preserve the supremacy of the white minority | ✅ Terminated when Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia chose to leave - defeat for the settlers |
| ✅ **Federation of Malaysia** | ✅ Proclaimed September 1963 | ⚠️ To assemble a viable state from territories of differing composition, with the central government holding firm overall control | ✅ Singapore left to become an independent republic in 1965, and the rest of the Federation continued successfully |

⚠️ Read the table for the variable. ⚠️ The West Indian federation failed because its members could not agree on the terms of sharing. ⚠️ The Central African federation failed because its members did not consent to its purpose. ⚠️ The Malaysian federation survived a major separation because the central government had firm overall control and because the departure of one member removed the contested element rather than the union itself. ⚠️ Federation, in short, works where the members want the same state and fails where it is an instrument for settling a question the members have not agreed on.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This is the account's own case of the settler factor at its most serious, and it is one of the clearest examples in the syllabus's decolonisation clause of the redrawal of boundaries being attempted *during* decolonisation rather than before it.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Rarely alone; usually as evidence in a question on settler colonies, on why some territories took longer, or on federation as a decolonising device. ⚠️ The high-value handling is to present the Federation as a defensive weapon, give the arithmetic, identify the exit clause as the fatal provision, and note that its dissolution concentrated the problem in Southern Rhodesia.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the Federation as an administrative convenience. ✅ Its stated aim was to preserve the supremacy of the white minority, and its parliament was heavily weighted to favour the whites.
2. ⚠️ Forgetting that Britain authorised it. ✅ Churchill's government allowed it in 1953.
3. ⚠️ Missing the education mechanism. ✅ Well-educated Africans were far fewer than in West Africa because the settlers had ensured very little education was provided.
4. ⚠️ Treating the dissolution as the end of the regional problem. ⚠️ It concentrated the settler question in Southern Rhodesia, which then declared independence unilaterally in November 1965.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Central Africa was the most troublesome region for Britain because settlers were most numerous and most deeply entrenched, particularly in Southern Rhodesia.
2. ✅ Well-educated Africans were far fewer than in West Africa because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided.
3. ✅ Alarmed at the spread of nationalism, the whites decided to combine resources and persuaded Churchill's government in 1953 to allow a union of Nyasaland and Northern and Southern Rhodesia.
4. ✅ The Central African Federation's aim was to preserve the supremacy of the white minority - about three hundred thousand Europeans out of a total population of about eight and a half million.
5. ✅ Its federal parliament at Salisbury, the capital of Southern Rhodesia, was heavily weighted to favour the whites, who hoped for full independence from Britain with dominion status.
6. ✅ African leaders: Dr Hastings Banda in Nyasaland, Kenneth Kaunda in Northern Rhodesia, Joshua Nkomo in Southern Rhodesia, campaigning for black majority rule.
7. ✅ 1959: violence, a state of emergency in Nyasaland and Southern Rhodesia, and mass arrests of Africans.
8. ✅ There was much support for the Africans in Britain, especially in the Labour Party, and the Conservative colonial secretary Iain Macleod was sympathetic.
9. ✅ The Monckton Commission of 1960 recommended votes for Africans, an end to racial discrimination, and the right of territories to leave the Federation.
10. ✅ New constitutions in Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia in 1961-62 in effect allowed the Africans their own parliaments.
11. ✅ Both wanted to leave, and the Federation was terminated in December 1963 - defeat for the settlers.
12. ✅ The following year Nyasaland became Malawi and Northern Rhodesia became Zambia, fully independent.
13. ⚠️ Mechanism: aggregation cannot change a territory's internal ratio, only the unit in which it is counted; an exit clause therefore destroys a federation built on aggregation.
14. ⚠️ Consequence: dissolution concentrated the settler problem in Southern Rhodesia, whose whites then assumed they would receive the same treatment and requested independence.
15. ⚠️ Master formulation: the Central African Federation was a constitutional weapon rather than a constitutional arrangement, and it was defeated by a procedural right its own sponsors had been forced to accept.

### Concept check

**Question:** A federation is normally recommended as a way of accommodating diversity. Explain how the Central African Federation inverted that purpose, and identify the single provision that destroyed it.

**Model answer:** It inverted the purpose by using aggregation as a defensive weapon rather than as an accommodation. Alarmed at the spread of nationalism, the settlers decided their best policy was to combine resources, and persuaded Churchill's government in 1953 to unite Nyasaland with Northern and Southern Rhodesia with the stated aim of preserving the supremacy of the white minority - about three hundred thousand Europeans out of a total population of about eight and a half million. Aggregation cannot alter any territory's internal ratio, but it alters the unit to which a constitution applies, and the federal parliament at Salisbury was heavily weighted to favour the whites, who hoped the Federation would soon achieve independence from Britain with dominion status. The provision that destroyed it was the Monckton Commission's 1960 recommendation of the right of territories to leave the Federation, alongside votes for Africans and an end to racial discrimination. An exit right changes the operative question from who controls the federal parliament, which the settlers had rigged, to whether each territory consents to remain, which they could not rig. Once new constitutions in 1961-62 in effect gave Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia their own parliaments, both chose to go; the Federation was terminated in December 1963, signalling defeat for the settlers, and the two territories became Malawi and Zambia in 1964.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the dissolution as the resolution of the region's settler problem. It concentrated it. Southern Rhodesia had hosted the federal capital, had the most entrenched settler population and had banned the African parties, and when Zambia and Malawi were given independence its whites assumed they would receive the same treatment and formally requested it. Britain's refusal to grant independence without constitutional change for the African majority led directly to Ian Smith's unilateral declaration in November 1965 and to fifteen further years before majority rule.

Progress: 13/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Rhodesia Front and Ian Smith; the unilateral declaration of November 1965; why sanctions leaked and who leaked them; the Commonwealth crisis and the front-line states; the guerrilla war and the changes of 1975-78; nationalist division and the Patriotic Front; the Lancaster House settlement of 1979; and independence in April 1980

Book context: queried - the textbook account's Southern Rhodesia section, covering the population figures, the Rhodesia Front and the banning of African parties, the British condition of at least a third of parliamentary seats for Africans, Smith's refusal, the unilateral declaration of November 1965 and the five recorded reactions to it, the sanctions-evasion detail including Rhodesian chrome, the Commonwealth split and the front-line states, the effect of Mozambique's independence in June 1975 and of the called-off South African invasion of Angola in October 1975, the guerrilla advance by 1978, the four nationalist parties, the Geneva conference of 1976 and the Patriotic Front, the Zimbabwe/Rhodesia scheme of April 1979, and the Lancaster House terms and the 1980 election result; together with the account's separate section on Mugabe's first decade

### Visual: fifteen years, and the five things that finally moved

```text
   1964 Apr   Ian Smith becomes prime minister
              (the Rhodesia Front: "a right-wing white racist party,
               determined never to surrender control of the country
               to black African rule"; African parties BANNED)
   POPULATION: fewer than 200,000 whites | about 20,000 Asians
               | 4,000,000 black Africans

   BRITAIN'S CONDITION: independence only if the constitution is
   changed to allow black Africans AT LEAST A THIRD of the seats
   in parliament.   SMITH: refuses. WILSON (1964-70): still refuses.

   1965 Nov   UDI - UNILATERAL DECLARATION OF INDEPENDENCE
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   THE FIVE REACTIONS, as recorded:
   1. Britain rules out force; hopes ECONOMIC SANCTIONS will work;
      stops buying Rhodesian sugar and tobacco
   2. The UN condemns UDI and calls for a COMPLETE TRADE EMBARGO
   3. SOUTH AFRICA and PORTUGAL (Mozambique) refuse to comply
      -> Rhodesia trades THROUGH them
   4. Many countries publicly condemn and privately evade: the USA
      buys Rhodesian CHROME "because it was the cheapest available";
      British oil companies among those breaking sanctions
   5. The COMMONWEALTH is seriously shaken: Ghana and Nigeria want
      force and offer troops; Zambia and Tanzania prefer sanctions -
      and ZAMBIA SUFFERS MORE FROM THEM THAN RHODESIA DOES
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   1966/1968  Wilson meets Smith aboard HMS TIGER and HMS FEARLESS;
              Smith rejects both sets of proposals
   1969       World Council of Churches programme to combat racism
   1970       Rhodesia declares itself a REPUBLIC; black citizens'
              rights whittled away

   WHAT ACTUALLY CHANGED, 1975-78
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   * MOZAMBIQUE independent from Portugal, June 1975 -> guerrillas
     can operate from Mozambique; sanctions bite harder
   * The FRONT-LINE STATES - Zambia, Botswana, Tanzania, Mozambique -
     support the armed struggle and provide TRAINING CAMPS
   * South Africa becomes less inclined to support Rhodesia after its
     invasion of Angola (Oct 1975) is called off on AMERICAN ORDERS;
     the USA and South Africa urge Smith to concede
   * By 1978 nationalist guerrillas control large areas of the
     countryside; white farmers attacked; rural schools closed or burnt
   => "It became clear that the defeat of the whites was only a
      matter of time."

   1979 Sep-Dec  LANCASTER HOUSE CONFERENCE, London
                 * a new constitution allowing the black majority to rule
                 * a 100-SEAT parliament with 20 SEATS RESERVED FOR
                   WHITES (uncontested); the other 80 elected
                 * Muzorewa to step down; the guerrilla war to end
   1980 Apr      ZANU wins 57 of the 80 black African seats;
                 Mugabe prime minister; ZIMBABWE INDEPENDENT
```

⚠️ The diagram is organised to answer one question: why did fifteen years of international sanctions achieve nothing and three years of guerrilla war plus regional realignment achieve everything? Hold that question in view.

### 1. The residue of the Federation

⚠️ Lesson 12 ended by noting that the Federation's dissolution concentrated the settler problem rather than resolving it, and this lesson begins there.

✅ Southern Rhodesia took much longer to deal with, and it was 1980 before the colony achieved independence with black majority rule. ✅ It was in Rhodesia, as it was now known, that the white settlers fought most fiercely to preserve their privileged position. ✅ There were fewer than two hundred thousand whites, about twenty thousand Asians and four million black Africans, but the **Rhodesia Front**, a right-wing white racist party, was determined never to surrender control of the country to black African rule. ✅ The black African parties were banned.

✅ When Zambia and Malawi were given independence, the whites assumed that Southern Rhodesia would get the same treatment and put in a formal request for independence. ✅ The British Conservative government refused and made it clear that independence would be granted only if the constitution was changed to allow black Africans at least a third of the seats in parliament. ✅ **Ian Smith**, who became prime minister of Southern Rhodesia in April 1964, rejected this idea and refused to make any concessions.

⚠️ Note how modest the British condition was. ⚠️ A third of the seats for a population that was roughly ninety-five per cent African is not majority rule; it is a guaranteed permanent minority. ⚠️ That Smith refused even this is the measure of the Rhodesia Front's position, and quoting the ratio against the demand is the most efficient way to establish it.

✅ Smith's own justification is recorded and should be reported, because it links this case to Lesson 23: he argued that continued white rule was essential in view of the problems being faced by the new black governments in other African states, and because the Zimbabwe nationalists seemed bitterly divided.

⚠️ That argument deserves a direct answer rather than dismissal, because versions of it recur. ⚠️ The first half uses post-colonial difficulty elsewhere as a reason to deny self-government here - which is exactly the inference the account forbids, since those difficulties were themselves largely colonial inheritances. ⚠️ The second half is self-serving in a specific way: ✅ the nationalist divisions were real, but ✅ Smith was able to present the divisions between the nationalist leaders as his excuse for the lack of progress, and a regime that bans African parties and then cites African disunity has manufactured part of its own justification.

✅ **Harold Wilson**, the new British Labour prime minister from 1964 to 1970, continued to refuse independence unless the constitution was changed to prepare for black majority rule.

### 2. UDI, November 1965, and the anatomy of a failed sanctions regime

✅ Since no compromise seemed possible, Smith declared Southern Rhodesia independent, against the wishes of Britain - a unilateral declaration of independence, or **UDI** - in **November 1965**.

⚠️ Register what this is before analysing its consequences. ⚠️ A settler minority seized sovereignty from its own imperial metropole in order to prevent that metropole from extending political rights to the African majority. ⚠️ This is the purest available demonstration of the Lesson 8 proposition that settlers are a third actor: the rebellion was against Britain.

✅ **First reaction.** At first there seemed very little Britain could do about it, once the government had decided not to use force against the illegal Smith regime. ✅ It was hoped to bring the country to its knees by economic sanctions, and Britain stopped buying sugar and tobacco from Rhodesia.

⚠️ The decision not to use force is the hinge and it should be examined rather than assumed. ⚠️ Britain had used force in Kenya against an African uprising thirteen years earlier and would not use it against a white rebellion. ⚠️ Whatever the operational reasons, the asymmetry is a fact of the record and it was noticed at the time - ✅ which is exactly why Ghana and Nigeria wanted Britain to use force and offered to supply troops.

✅ **Second reaction.** The UN condemned UDI and called on all member states to place a complete trade embargo on Rhodesia.

✅ **Third reaction.** South Africa, also ruled by a white minority government, and Portugal, which still controlled neighbouring Mozambique, were sympathetic to the Smith regime and refused to obey the Security Council resolution. ✅ This meant that Rhodesia was able to continue trading through these countries.

⚠️ This is the structural reason sanctions failed and it generalises. ⚠️ A landlocked state's sanctions regime is only as strong as its neighbours' compliance, and Rhodesia's neighbours to the south and east were the two other white-minority-governed territories in the region. ⚠️ The sanctions could not work while the regional configuration held, which tells you in advance what would have to change - and in 1975 and 1980 it did.

✅ **Fourth reaction.** Many other countries, while publicly condemning UDI, privately evaded the embargo; the USA, for example, bought Rhodesian chrome because it was the cheapest available. ✅ Companies and businessmen in many countries, including British oil companies, continued to break sanctions, and although the Rhodesian economy suffered to some extent, it was not serious enough to topple the Smith regime.

⚠️ That is a very precise indictment and it should be used precisely. ⚠️ It is not the claim that sanctions are useless; it is the claim that *these* sanctions failed because the states imposing them permitted their own firms to break them where the commodity was cheap. ⚠️ Lesson 22 records the identical pattern against South Africa - ✅ Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public but continued to trade - and the repetition across two cases makes it a general finding rather than an anecdote.

✅ **Fifth reaction.** The Commonwealth was seriously shaken. ✅ Ghana and Nigeria wanted Britain to use force and offered to supply troops. ✅ Zambia and Tanzania hoped that economic sanctions would suffice; relations with the British became extremely cool when it seemed that they were deliberately soft-pedalling sanctions, ✅ especially as Zambia was suffering more from them than Rhodesia.

⚠️ That final clause is the most under-used fact in this case. ⚠️ Zambia, newly independent and landlocked, had its trade routes through Rhodesia; enforcing sanctions therefore cost Zambia more than it cost the target. ⚠️ Sanctions imposed on a state whose neighbours depend on it transfer the cost to the enforcers, and the enforcers here were among the poorest participants. ⚠️ This is a strong, transferable point about the political economy of sanctions.

✅ Wilson twice met Smith aboard **HMS Tiger in 1966** and **HMS Fearless in 1968** to put new proposals, and there was a howl of protest in case he betrayed the black Rhodesians; ✅ perhaps fortunately for the future of the Commonwealth, Smith rejected both sets of proposals. ✅ The World Council of Churches set up a programme to combat racism in 1969, giving the nationalists encouragement and support both morally and financially. ✅ In 1970 Rhodesia declared itself a republic, and the rights of black citizens were gradually whittled away until they were suffering similar treatment to that experienced by blacks in South Africa.

### 3. What actually changed: 1975 as the hinge year

✅ In 1976 the first signs began to appear that the whites would have to compromise.

⚠️ Four changes account for it and all four are regional or international rather than domestic. ⚠️ That is itself the finding: the Rhodesian settlers were not defeated by internal African political pressure of the kind that moved the Gold Coast, but by the collapse of the regional system that had sustained them.

⚠️ **Change one: Mozambique.** ✅ Mozambique's independence from Portugal in **June 1975** was a blow to the effectiveness of sanctions and allowed nationalist guerrillas to operate from Mozambique. ⚠️ One of the two neighbours breaking the embargo had just become an African-governed state hostile to Rhodesia, and had simultaneously become a base area. ⚠️ Decolonisation in one territory therefore directly caused decolonisation in another - the clearest instance of the demonstration and contagion effect having a *material* rather than merely inspirational form.

⚠️ **Change two: the front-line states.** ✅ The front-line states - which included Zambia, Botswana and Tanzania, as well as Mozambique - supported the armed struggle and provided training camps for the resistance movement. ✅ Thousands of black guerrillas were soon active in Rhodesia, straining the white security forces to their limits and forcing Smith to hire foreign mercenaries.

⚠️ The detail about mercenaries is the clearest single index of settler weakness: a population of fewer than two hundred thousand cannot generate an army adequate to hold four million people against externally based insurgency, and hiring foreigners is the admission of that arithmetic.

⚠️ **Change three: South Africa's calculation.** ✅ The South Africans became less inclined to support Rhodesia after their invasion of Angola in **October 1975** had been called off on American orders. ✅ The Americans were afraid that the USSR and Cuba might become involved in Rhodesia unless some compromise could be found; together with South Africa, they urged Smith to make concessions to the blacks before it was too late.

⚠️ Read that carefully, because it shows the Cold War operating in an unexpected direction. ⚠️ Superpower competition is usually cited as prolonging African conflicts, and Lesson 23 will show it doing so in Angola. ⚠️ Here the fear of Soviet and Cuban involvement made the United States push its own side's ally towards concession. ⚠️ The same variable produced opposite effects in two adjacent countries within a year, which is precisely why a blanket claim that the Cold War prolonged African wars is unsafe.

⚠️ **Change four: the countryside.** ✅ By 1978 nationalist guerrillas controlled large areas of the Rhodesian countryside; farming was adversely affected as white farmers were attacked; schools in rural areas were closed and sometimes burnt down. ✅ It became clear that the defeat of the whites was only a matter of time.

⚠️ A settler economy is an agricultural economy, and an agricultural economy cannot be defended from fortified towns. ⚠️ That is why guerrilla control of the countryside was decisive here in a way that fifteen years of sanctions were not: it attacked the asset the settlers were defending rather than the currency they were earning.

### 4. Nationalist division, and the conference that reduced it

✅ Smith still tried everything he knew to delay black majority rule as long as possible, and the divisions between the nationalist leaders were a genuine problem.

| ✅ Party | ✅ Leader | ✅ Base as recorded |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **ZAPU** - Zimbabwe African People's Union | ✅ Joshua Nkomo, the veteran nationalist | ✅ Later recorded as supported by the Ndebele people in Matabeleland |
| ✅ **ZANU** - Zimbabwe African National Union | ✅ The Reverend Ndabaningi Sithole | ✅ Later recorded as drawing its main support from the Shona people |
| ✅ **UANC** - United African National Council | ✅ Bishop Abel Muzorewa | ⚠️ The most moderate of the nationalist parties |
| ✅ ZANU's guerrilla wing | ✅ **Robert Mugabe**, who eventually emerged as ZANU's unchallenged leader | ⚠️ Armed capacity, which is what determined the outcome |

✅ ZAPU and ZANU, representing different groups, seemed to be bitter enemies. ✅ The divisions were reduced to some extent as a result of the **1976 Geneva Conference**, when ZAPU and ZANU came together loosely in the **Patriotic Front**; after this the parties were referred to as ZANU-PF and PF-ZAPU.

✅ Smith then tried to compromise by introducing his own scheme, the **Internal Settlement signed on 3 March 1978**: a joint government of whites and the UANC, the most moderate of the nationalist parties, leading to elections in **April 1979**, after which Bishop Muzorewa became prime minister of a state renamed **Zimbabwe/Rhodesia** in **June 1979**. ⚠️ The textbook account dates the settlement itself to April 1979; the agreement was March 1978, and April 1979 is the date of the elections it produced. ✅ However, it was ZANU-PF and PF-ZAPU which had mass support, and they continued the guerrilla war.

⚠️ The Internal Settlement of March 1978, and the Muzorewa government it produced in 1979, is a case worth naming in its own right. ⚠️ It is the standard late-colonial device: transfer office to the most accommodating nationalist while retaining the substance of power, and present the result as majority rule. ⚠️ It failed for the reason such devices usually fail - the parties with mass support and armed capacity were excluded, and their exclusion meant the war continued, so the settlement did not deliver the one thing it was supposed to buy.

### 5. Lancaster House, and the question it did not settle

✅ Smith soon had to admit defeat and the British called the **Lancaster House Conference** in London, from **September to December 1979**, which agreed the following points. ✅ There should be a new constitution which would allow the black majority to rule. ✅ In the new Republic of Zimbabwe there would be a **hundred-seat parliament with twenty seats reserved for whites**, uncontested; the remaining eighty MPs were to be elected, and it was expected that they would be black, since the vast majority of the population was black. ✅ Muzorewa would step down as prime minister and the guerrilla war would end.

✅ In the elections which followed, Mugabe's ZANU won a sweeping victory, taking **fifty-seven out of the eighty black African seats**. ✅ This gave him a comfortable overall majority, enabling him to become prime minister when Zimbabwe officially became independent in **April 1980**. ✅ The transference to black majority rule was welcomed by all African and Commonwealth leaders as a triumph of common sense and moderation.

⚠️ Note what Lancaster House settled and what it did not.

⚠️ **It settled the political question.** Twenty reserved seats out of a hundred is a transitional guarantee, not a veto, and it was abolished in 1987 when ✅ ZAPU and ZANU merged and Mugabe became the country's first executive president. ⚠️ Fifteen years of settler resistance ended in a constitutional transfer within four months of negotiation, once the military and regional facts had changed.

⚠️ **It did not settle the land question**, and this is the examinable omission. ✅ About four thousand white farmers stayed on in Zimbabwe after independence, and between them they owned about half the country's arable land. ⚠️ A settlement that transfers political power while leaving the colonial distribution of productive assets untouched has deferred the central grievance of a settler colony, not resolved it.

⚠️ The deferral had a price, and it was paid twenty years later. ⚠️ **That narrative is owned by Lesson 23**, where the farm occupations from February 2000, the dispute about the Lancaster House compensation promise and the electoral context are set out with their evidence. ⚠️ What belongs here is only the structural point: the settlement of 1979 transferred the parliament and left the land, and a grievance left in place at a transfer remains available to whoever later needs it.

⚠️ State the structural point carefully, and leave the rest to Lesson 23. ⚠️ The unresolved land question of 1979 was real and was a legitimate grievance; what was later done with that grievance, and the electoral circumstances in which it was done, are examined where the evidence for them is set out.

⚠️ For balance, the early record must also be given. ✅ Mugabe soon showed that he was capable of moderation and pledged himself to work for reconciliation and unity, which calmed the fears of the white farmers and businessmen who had remained and who were necessary for the economy to flourish; ✅ he formed a coalition with Nkomo's ZAPU; ✅ he kept the Lancaster House promise of twenty guaranteed white seats; ✅ and measures were introduced to alleviate the poverty of the black population - wage increases, food subsidies and better social services, health care and education. ⚠️ Do not read 1980 backwards from 2000.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Southern Rhodesia is the longest-delayed British decolonisation and the clearest case of settler rebellion against the metropole; it is also the standard case for the effectiveness of international sanctions.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Inside questions on settler colonies, on the role of international pressure, or as the violent counterpart to a negotiated case. ⚠️ The discriminating structure is: the arithmetic and the modesty of the British demand; UDI as rebellion against the metropole; the five reactions, with sanctions failing for identifiable reasons; the four regional changes of 1975-78; and Lancaster House settling the political question while deferring the land question.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Saying sanctions failed, without saying why. ✅ Neighbouring white-minority states refused to comply; other states evaded privately; the USA bought Rhodesian chrome as the cheapest available; British oil companies broke sanctions; and Zambia suffered more from them than Rhodesia did.
2. ⚠️ Treating UDI as a colonial government resisting nationalists. ⚠️ It was a settler rebellion against Britain, which had demanded that Africans receive at least a third of parliamentary seats.
3. ⚠️ Crediting the outcome to international opinion. ⚠️ The decisive changes were Mozambican independence in June 1975, the front-line states' training camps, the South African recalculation after the Angolan invasion was called off in October 1975, and guerrilla control of large areas of the countryside by 1978.
4. ⚠️ Treating Lancaster House as a complete settlement. ✅ About four thousand white farmers still owned about half the arable land; what followed from that omission is examined in Lesson 23.
5. ❌ Reading Mugabe's later record back into 1980. ✅ The early record includes reconciliation, the retention of white farmers necessary to the economy, the twenty reserved seats honoured, and wage increases, food subsidies and improved services.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Southern Rhodesia: fewer than 200,000 whites, about 20,000 Asians, 4 million black Africans; the Rhodesia Front, a right-wing white racist party, determined never to surrender control; African parties banned.
2. ✅ Britain would grant independence only if the constitution gave black Africans at least a third of parliamentary seats; Smith, prime minister from April 1964, refused; Wilson from 1964 also refused without preparation for majority rule.
3. ✅ Smith's stated reasons: the problems of new black governments elsewhere in Africa, and the bitter divisions among Zimbabwe nationalists.
4. ✅ UDI declared November 1965.
5. ✅ Britain ruled out force and relied on sanctions, stopping purchases of Rhodesian sugar and tobacco; the UN condemned UDI and called for a complete trade embargo.
6. ✅ South Africa and Portuguese-ruled Mozambique refused to comply, so Rhodesia traded through them; many states evaded privately; the USA bought Rhodesian chrome as the cheapest available; British oil companies broke sanctions; the economy suffered but not enough to topple Smith.
7. ✅ The Commonwealth was shaken: Ghana and Nigeria wanted force and offered troops; Zambia and Tanzania preferred sanctions; Zambia suffered more from them than Rhodesia.
8. ✅ Wilson met Smith aboard HMS Tiger (1966) and HMS Fearless (1968); Smith rejected both sets of proposals.
9. ✅ 1969: the World Council of Churches programme to combat racism encouraged and financed the nationalists. ✅ 1970: Rhodesia declared itself a republic and black citizens' rights were whittled away.
10. ✅ Mozambique's independence in June 1975 was a blow to sanctions and allowed guerrillas to operate from there; the front-line states - Zambia, Botswana, Tanzania and Mozambique - supported the armed struggle and provided training camps.
11. ✅ South Africa became less inclined to support Rhodesia after its October 1975 invasion of Angola was called off on American orders; the USA and South Africa urged Smith to concede, fearing Soviet and Cuban involvement.
12. ✅ By 1978 guerrillas controlled large areas of the countryside; white farmers were attacked; rural schools closed or burnt; Smith hired foreign mercenaries.
13. ✅ Parties: ZAPU (Nkomo), ZANU (Sithole), UANC (Muzorewa), with Mugabe emerging as ZANU's unchallenged leader; the 1976 Geneva Conference produced the loose Patriotic Front, hence ZANU-PF and PF-ZAPU.
14. ✅ Smith's Internal Settlement, signed on 3 March 1978, produced elections in April 1979 and a Zimbabwe/Rhodesia under Muzorewa as prime minister from June 1979, but the parties with mass support continued the war.
15. ✅ Lancaster House, September-December 1979: a new constitution for black majority rule; a hundred-seat parliament with twenty seats reserved for whites, uncontested; Muzorewa to step down; the war to end.
16. ✅ ZANU won 57 of the 80 black African seats; Mugabe became prime minister at independence in April 1980; ZANU and ZAPU merged in 1987 and Mugabe became executive president.
17. ✅ Deferred question: about 4,000 white farmers owned about half the arable land at independence, and the settlement left that distribution untouched. ⚠️ What was later done with the grievance belongs to Lesson 23, where the evidence for it is set out.
18. ⚠️ Master formulation: sanctions failed because the region and the sanctioning states' own firms did not enforce them; the war succeeded because it attacked the settlers' agricultural asset once the regional buffer had gone.

### Concept check

**Question:** Fifteen years of international sanctions failed to end the Rhodesian rebellion, and the settlers conceded within four years of 1975. Explain the difference, naming the changes that mattered.

**Model answer:** Sanctions failed for reasons of geography and enforcement rather than of design. Rhodesia was landlocked and its two relevant neighbours were white-minority-ruled: South Africa and Portuguese Mozambique refused to obey the Security Council resolution, so Rhodesia simply traded through them. Enforcement elsewhere was nominal - many countries condemned UDI publicly and evaded the embargo privately, the United States bought Rhodesian chrome because it was the cheapest available, and companies including British oil companies continued to break sanctions - so the economy suffered but not enough to topple Smith. The burden also fell on the wrong parties, since Zambia suffered more from the sanctions than Rhodesia did. Four changes from 1975 altered this. Mozambique's independence from Portugal in June 1975 removed one of the two sanctions-breaking neighbours and simultaneously gave the guerrillas a base. The front-line states - Zambia, Botswana, Tanzania and Mozambique - supported the armed struggle and provided training camps. South Africa became less inclined to support Rhodesia after its invasion of Angola was called off on American orders in October 1975, and both the United States and South Africa then urged Smith to concede for fear of Soviet and Cuban involvement. And by 1978 guerrillas controlled large areas of the countryside, attacking white farmers and closing rural schools, so that the defeat of the whites was only a matter of time. The decisive shift was from attacking Rhodesia's export earnings, which its neighbours could replace, to attacking the agricultural asset the settlers actually held.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the Lancaster House settlement as a complete resolution because it delivered majority rule. It settled the political question - a hundred-seat parliament with twenty reserved, uncontested white seats, abolished at the Unity Accord in 1987 - while leaving the distribution of land untouched, so that about four thousand white farmers still owned roughly half the country's arable land. That deferred grievance remained available for political use long afterwards, and what was made of it is examined in Lesson 23 with the evidence for it. Both the grievance and the later political use of it are real, and an answer needs both, taken from the lesson that owns each.

---

## Lesson 14 - The French empire I: Indo-China, Tunisia and Morocco

Progress: 14/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Brazzaville Declaration of 1944; the eight-year war in Indo-China to Dien Bien Phu in May 1954 and the Geneva Conference; the protectorate route in Tunisia with New Destour and Bourguiba; Morocco, Istiqlal, the deposed king and the trade unions; and the mechanism by which imprisoning moderates forced negotiation

Book context: queried - the textbook account's French empire section, including the list of possessions, the 1944 Brazzaville Declaration, the eight-year armed struggle culminating at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954, the fall of the French government and Mendes-France's withdrawal, the Geneva agreement of July 1954, the settler numbers in Tunisia and Morocco, the New Destour and Bourguiba sequence, and the Istiqlal, deposition and restoration sequence in Morocco; the extraction of the page opening the Indo-China subsection is damaged, and that gap is left open
CA search: not sought - this session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three French exits in three years, by three different mechanisms

```text
   Yet within TWELVE YEARS all three of the following were gone:

   INDO-CHINA                 TUNISIA                MOROCCO

   MAY 1954                         |                 with KING MOHAMMED

   PIERRE MENDES-FRANCE,           |                  role

                                                      1956

   THE COMMON MECHANISM in Tunisia and Morocco:
   France chose the cheaper of two costs. Independence with a
   negotiable partner cost less than an indefinite anti-guerrilla
   war - AND preserved the prospect of post-independence influence.
   THE CONTRAST WITH ALGERIA (Lesson 15) is that there the
   settlers made that calculation politically impossible.
```

⚠️ The whole point of putting these three cases together is the last line of the diagram. France's leaders were capable of a rational cost calculation and made it three times between 1954 and 1956. Algeria is not explained by French irrationality; it is explained by a variable that suspended the calculation.

### 1. The starting position, and how far it was from the outcome

✅ The French began by trying to suppress all nationalist agitation, regarding it as high treason. ✅ As the 1944 **Brazzaville Declaration** put it: "The colonising work of France makes it impossible to accept any idea of autonomy for the colonies or any possibility of development outside the French Empire. Even at a distant date, there will be no self-government in the colonies."

⚠️ That is an unusually useful document for an examination answer, for two reasons.

⚠️ **First, its date.** 1944 - while the war was still being fought, and three years after the Atlantic Charter had declared that all peoples should have the right to choose their own form of government. ⚠️ Place the two statements side by side and the tension inside the Allied position is visible: the same coalition contained a charter promising self-determination and a declaration ruling out self-government even at a distant date.

⚠️ **Second, its absoluteness.** "Even at a distant date" forecloses the gradualist option that Britain used throughout Africa. ⚠️ A power that has ruled out eventual self-government has also ruled out the apprenticeship model - stages of self-rule, elected assemblies, executive councils - which means that when the position becomes untenable there is no intermediate arrangement to fall back on. ⚠️ Absolutism at the start produces abruptness at the end, and that is exactly the French pattern: nothing, then everything, in a very short time.

✅ But gradually the French were influenced by Britain's moves towards decolonisation, and after their defeat in Indo-China in 1954 they too were forced to bow to the "wind of change".

### 2. Indo-China: the defeat that changed French policy everywhere

> **Boundary.** ⚠️ The Vietnamese revolution, Ho Chi Minh's party, the communist regimes of Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia, and the American war that followed are owned by the Asian communism and Cold War material. ⚠️ This session teaches Indo-China as an anti-colonial war against France and stops at Geneva in July 1954.

✅ Vietnamese nationalists had begun to campaign against French rule during the 1920s. ✅ Vietnamese independence was unacceptable to the French, and an **eight-year armed struggle** began which culminated in the French defeat at **Dien Bien Phu in May 1954**.

✅ The defeat was a humiliating blow for the French and it caused a political crisis. ✅ The government resigned, and the new and more liberal premier **Pierre Mendes-France**, realising that public opinion was turning against the war, decided to withdraw. ✅ At the **Geneva Conference in July 1954** it was agreed that Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia should become independent.

✅ Unfortunately this was not the end of the troubles: although the French had withdrawn, the Americans were unwilling to allow the whole of Vietnam to come under the rule of the communist Ho Chi Minh, and an even more bloody struggle developed.

⚠️ Four consequences of Dien Bien Phu belong to this topic, and they are the reason it is taught here rather than being handed over entirely.

⚠️ **It brought down a government and changed a policy.** ⚠️ This is the mechanism from Lesson 3: a colonial defeat converted into a metropolitan political event. ⚠️ Mendes-France's calculation was about French public opinion, not about Vietnam.

⚠️ **It set the timetable for the Maghreb.** ✅ Under pressure at the same time in Indo-China and Morocco, the French realised that they would have to give way in Tunisia. ⚠️ A metropole fighting on three fronts must concede on at least two, and Indo-China's collapse is what made the Tunisian and Moroccan concessions of 1956 rational.

⚠️ **It encouraged the FLN.** ✅ Encouraged by the French defeat in Indo-China, a more militant nationalist group was formed in Algeria - the National Liberation Front - which launched a guerrilla war towards the end of 1954. ⚠️ The demonstration effect operated *within* an empire and in favour of armed methods: Dien Bien Phu in May, an Algerian insurgency by the end of the same year.

⚠️ **It fixed the army's psychology.** ✅ The French army, after its defeats in the Second World War and Indo-China, saw the Algerian war as a chance to restore its reputation and refused to contemplate surrender. ⚠️ Lesson 15 shows what an army in that condition does to a republic.

### 3. Tunisia: the protectorate route, and the moderate-in-jail mechanism

✅ Both Tunisia and Morocco were protectorates - Tunisia had a ruler known as the **bey**, Morocco a Muslim king, **Mohammed V**. ✅ Nationalists resented French control and had been campaigning for real independence since before the Second World War. ✅ The situation was complicated by the presence of large numbers of European settlers: Tunisia had about **two hundred and fifty thousand** and Morocco about **three hundred thousand** in 1945, and they were committed to maintaining the connection with France, which guaranteed their privileged position.

⚠️ Register those settler numbers, because they are the comparative control for Lesson 15. ⚠️ A quarter of a million settlers was enough to complicate matters and not enough to prevent a negotiated exit. ⚠️ Over a million, in a territory legally part of France, was enough to prevent one. ⚠️ The settler variable is not binary; it has a threshold, and these three territories bracket it.

✅ In Tunisia the main nationalist group was the **New Destour**, led by **Habib Bourguiba**. ✅ They had widespread support among both rural dwellers and townspeople who believed independence would improve their living standards. ✅ A guerrilla campaign was launched against the French, who responded by banning New Destour and imprisoning Bourguiba in **1952**; ✅ **seventy thousand French troops** were deployed against the guerrillas but failed to crush them.

✅ The French then became aware of a disturbing trend: with Bourguiba and other moderate leaders in jail, the guerrilla movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate. ✅ Under pressure at the same time in Indo-China and Morocco, the French realised that they would have to give way. ✅ With a moderate like Bourguiba at the head of the country, there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence. ✅ He was released from jail and Mendes-France allowed him to form a government. ✅ In **March 1956** Tunisia became fully independent under Bourguiba's leadership.

⚠️ This is the cleanest statement in the whole session of the mechanism first met in Lesson 4, and it should be quoted rather than summarised, because the French reasoning is recorded explicitly. ⚠️ The sequence is: imprison the moderate; discover that the movement radicalises; release him in order to have someone to negotiate with; and choose independence under him because independence under him preserves influence. ⚠️ Note that the final step is not a concession to justice; it is a calculation about post-independence leverage, and it is the clearest early sign of the neo-colonial design Lesson 16 describes in full.

⚠️ Note also the mass base. ✅ New Destour had widespread support among both rural dwellers and townspeople who believed independence would improve their living standards. ⚠️ This is an economic rather than a purely political motivation, and it belongs in any answer on the social bases of nationalism from Lesson 4: independence was expected to raise living standards, and that expectation is what made mass support durable enough to outlast the leadership's imprisonment.

### 4. Morocco: the same pattern with a monarchy attached

✅ In Morocco the pattern of events was remarkably similar. ✅ There was a nationalist party calling itself **Istiqlal**, meaning Independence, and King Mohammed himself seemed to be in the forefront of opposition to the French. ✅ The new trade unions also played an important role. ✅ The French deposed the king in **1953**, provoking violent demonstrations and a guerrilla campaign. ✅ Faced with the prospect of yet another long and expensive anti-guerrilla war, the French decided to bow to the inevitable: the king was allowed to return and Morocco became independent in **1956**.

⚠️ Two features distinguish Morocco from Tunisia and both are examinable.

⚠️ **The monarch was the nationalist.** ⚠️ Where a protectorate's indigenous ruler leads the independence movement, the colonial power has no option of governing through a compliant traditional authority - the standard device of indirect rule. ⚠️ Deposing him then converts a political dispute into a question of national humiliation, which is why the deposition of 1953 produced violent demonstrations and a guerrilla campaign, and why the settlement consisted simply of letting him come back. ⚠️ France spent three years arriving where it had started.

⚠️ **The trade unions.** ✅ The new trade unions also played an important role. ⚠️ This is a further instance of the Lesson 4 finding, and it explains ✅ why the French government in 1949 clamped down on all nationalist movements and arrested many nationalist leaders **and trade unionists**, often denouncing them as communist agitators though without much evidence. ⚠️ Colonial administrations targeted organised labour because organised labour was the instrument that worked.

### 5. Reading the three cases together

⚠️ The comparative payoff of this lesson is a table that a candidate can carry whole into a question on French decolonisation.

| Variable | ✅ Indo-China | ✅ Tunisia | ✅ Morocco |
|---|---|---|---|
| Legal form | Colony | ✅ Protectorate under a bey | ✅ Protectorate under a king |
| Settlers | ⚠️ Not a settler colony of the Maghreb type | ✅ About 250,000 in 1945 | ✅ About 300,000 in 1945 |
| Indigenous authority available to France | ⚠️ None that could carry nationalist legitimacy | ✅ Bourguiba, once released | ✅ Mohammed V, once restored |
| French method first tried | ✅ Eight-year war | ✅ Ban the party, jail the leader, deploy 70,000 troops | ✅ Depose the king |
| Why it was abandoned | ✅ Defeat at Dien Bien Phu, May 1954; the government resigned; public opinion turning | ✅ Radicalisation of the movement; simultaneous pressure in Indo-China and Morocco | ✅ The prospect of yet another long and expensive anti-guerrilla war |
| Outcome | ✅ Geneva, July 1954: Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia to become independent | ✅ Bourguiba released, forms a government, full independence March 1956 | ✅ King returns; independence 1956 |
| ⚠️ What France retained or hoped to retain | ⚠️ Nothing | ✅ The chance of maintaining influence after independence through a moderate leader | ⚠️ The same calculation, unstated |

⚠️ The final row is the thread that runs into Lessons 15 and 16. ⚠️ France abandoned formal rule wherever it could see a way of retaining informal influence, and fought wherever it could not. ⚠️ In Algeria it could not, because the settlers were the influence and they could not be represented by any Algerian government. ⚠️ In sub-Saharan Africa it could, and Lesson 16 shows the machinery being built in advance.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ French decolonisation is the principal comparative counterpart to British decolonisation, and these three cases give the full range within a single empire and a thirty-month window.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually inside a comparison of imperial powers, or as evidence that the coloniser's identity is a weak predictor. ⚠️ The strongest use is to present Brazzaville in 1944 and then the three exits of 1954-56, and to ask what changed - the answer being cost, not principle, plus the discovery that influence could survive withdrawal.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Narrating the Vietnam War inside a decolonisation answer. ⚠️ The French exit ends at Geneva in July 1954; what followed belongs to the topic on Asian communism.
2. ⚠️ Treating French policy as uniformly intransigent. ✅ Brazzaville in 1944 was intransigent; 1954-56 was a rapid series of negotiated withdrawals.
3. ⚠️ Missing the moderate-in-jail mechanism. ✅ The French explicitly noticed that jailing Bourguiba made the movement more left-wing and less willing to negotiate, and released him to preserve influence.
4. ❌ Assuming Tunisia and Morocco had no settlers. ✅ About 250,000 and 300,000 respectively in 1945 - enough to complicate, not enough to prevent.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The French began by trying to suppress all nationalist agitation, regarding it as high treason.
2. ✅ Brazzaville Declaration, 1944: the colonising work of France makes it impossible to accept any idea of autonomy, and even at a distant date there will be no self-government in the colonies.
3. ✅ Vietnamese nationalists had campaigned against French rule since the 1920s; Vietnamese independence was unacceptable to the French; an eight-year armed struggle culminated in the French defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954.
4. ✅ The defeat was a humiliating blow and caused a political crisis; the government resigned; Mendes-France, realising public opinion was turning against the war, decided to withdraw.
5. ✅ Geneva Conference, July 1954: Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia to become independent. ✅ The Americans were unwilling to allow all Vietnam under Ho Chi Minh and a more bloody struggle developed - owned elsewhere.
6. ⚠️ Four consequences of Dien Bien Phu for this topic: it changed a metropolitan government; it forced the Maghreb concessions; it encouraged the formation of the FLN later in 1954; and it committed the French army to restoring its reputation in Algeria.
7. ✅ Tunisia and Morocco were protectorates, under a bey and a king respectively; settlers numbered about 250,000 and about 300,000 in 1945 and were committed to the French connection that guaranteed their privileged position.
8. ✅ Tunisia: New Destour under Bourguiba, with widespread support among rural dwellers and townspeople who believed independence would improve living standards; a guerrilla campaign; the party banned and Bourguiba jailed in 1952; 70,000 French troops deployed and failing to crush the guerrillas.
9. ✅ The French noticed that with the moderates jailed the movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate, and that with a moderate like Bourguiba in charge there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence.
10. ✅ Bourguiba was released, Mendes-France allowed him to form a government, and Tunisia became fully independent in March 1956.
11. ✅ Morocco: Istiqlal; King Mohammed himself in the forefront of opposition; the new trade unions playing an important role; the king deposed in 1953, provoking violent demonstrations and a guerrilla campaign.
12. ✅ Faced with the prospect of yet another long and expensive anti-guerrilla war, the French bowed to the inevitable; the king returned and Morocco became independent in 1956.
13. ✅ In 1949 the French clamped down on all nationalist movements and arrested many nationalist leaders and trade unionists, often denouncing them as communist agitators without much evidence.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: France abandoned formal rule wherever informal influence could be preserved through a negotiable leader, and fought wherever it could not - which is why the same government conceded Tunisia and Morocco in 1956 while escalating in Algeria.

### Concept check

**Question:** In 1952 France banned Tunisia's main nationalist party and imprisoned its leader; in 1956 it made that same leader head of an independent state. Explain the reasoning behind both decisions, and state what the reversal reveals about French decolonisation more generally.

**Model answer:** The 1952 decision followed the standard colonial reflex: New Destour had launched a guerrilla campaign, so the party was banned, Bourguiba was jailed and seventy thousand French troops were deployed. The reversal followed from two discoveries. First, the troops failed to crush the guerrillas, so repression had been tried and had not worked. Second, and decisively, the French became aware that with Bourguiba and the other moderate leaders in jail the guerrilla movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate - that is, imprisonment was destroying the very interlocutor France would eventually need. Pressure in Indo-China and Morocco at the same moment removed the option of waiting. The conclusion France drew was explicitly about post-independence leverage: with a moderate like Bourguiba at the head of the country there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence. He was released, Mendes-France allowed him to form a government, and Tunisia became fully independent in March 1956. What the reversal reveals is the organising principle of French decolonisation: France conceded formal sovereignty wherever informal influence could be preserved through a negotiable successor, and fought where it could not. The same government that released Bourguiba and restored Mohammed V in 1956 was escalating in Algeria, where no Algerian government could have represented the settler interest that France was actually defending.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the French record as uniformly intransigent on the strength of the Brazzaville Declaration of 1944, which ruled out any idea of autonomy or self-government even at a distant date. Within twelve years France had left Indo-China after defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954 and had negotiated Tunisian and Moroccan independence in 1956. The intransigence was real but it was conditional, and identifying the condition - whether withdrawal could leave influence behind - explains both the concessions and the war that ran alongside them.

## Lesson 15 - The French empire II: Algeria, 1954-62

Progress: 15/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the legal fusion of Algeria with France; the settler economy and its demographic effect; the failure of peaceful nationalism; the rigged assembly; the FLN's war from late 1954 and its escalation to 700,000 troops; the effects in France including the collapse of the Fourth Republic; de Gaulle's return in May 1958 and the historians' dispute about it; the OAS; Evian and independence in July 1962; and the savage aftermath, with figures attributed

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
