### Visual: the structure that made compromise impossible

```text
   THE LEGAL FACT
   "Algeria continued to be treated NOT AS A COLONY OR A
    PROTECTORATE, but as an EXTENSION OR PROVINCE OF
    METROPOLITAN FRANCE ITSELF."
   => there is no legal act of "granting independence" available.
      Independence means DIVIDING FRANCE.

   THE DEMOGRAPHIC AND ECONOMIC FACT
   -------------------------------------------------------------
   over 1,000,000  French settlers ("PIEDS NOIRS", "black feet")
       9,000,000  Muslim Arab Algerians
   The settlers control "SOMETHING LIKE A THIRD OF ALL THE MOST
   FERTILE LAND", taken from the original Algerian owners during
   the century before 1940.
   They EXPORT most of the crops and use some land for VINES FOR
   WINEMAKING
        -> "less food available for the growing African population,
            whose standard of living was CLEARLY FALLING"

   THE POLITICAL FACT: an assembly designed to produce one answer
   -------------------------------------------------------------
        ALGERIAN ASSEMBLY: 120 members, LIMITED POWERS
           1,000,000 whites      elect 60 members
           9,000,000 Muslims     elect 60 members
        plus: "Corruption on the part of the Europeans usually
               meant that they had a majority in the assembly."
        => a Muslim vote is worth ONE NINTH of a European vote,
           and even that is not delivered.

   THE CONSEQUENCE
   -------------------------------------------------------------
   Messali Hadj leads an "active, though PEACEFUL, nationalist
   movement"; after almost TEN YEARS of campaigning following the
   end of the war they had "achieved ABSOLUTELY NOTHING".
        -> "the stubbornness of the settlers and their refusal even
            to talk meant that the struggle would be decided by
            the EXTREMISTS."
        -> FLN formed, led by BEN BELLA; guerrilla war from late 1954
        -> by 1960, 700,000 FRENCH TROOPS engaged
```

⚠️ Everything in this lesson follows from the top box. Algeria is not a harder version of Tunisia; it is a different category of problem, because there was no legal mechanism by which the thing being demanded could be given.

### 1. The legal fact, and why it foreclosed the normal route

✅ Algeria continued to be treated not as a colony or a protectorate, but as an extension or province of metropolitan France itself. ✅ But that did not mean that the nine million Muslim Arab Algerians were treated as equals with ordinary French people: they were allowed no say in the government of their country.

⚠️ Take the two halves of that statement together, because the contradiction between them is the political core of the case. ⚠️ Algeria was legally France, and Algerians legally were not French in any sense that carried political rights. ⚠️ The result was a claim that could be advanced in two mutually exclusive ways: either make the legal fiction real and enfranchise nine million Muslims inside the French Republic, which would transform French politics; or separate, which would dismember the Republic.

⚠️ That is why every other route in this session was unavailable here. ⚠️ There was no bey or sultan to restore as in Morocco, no Legislative Council to expand seat by seat as in Kenya, no constitution to revise in stages as in the Gold Coast, no dominion status to offer. ⚠️ ✅ In spite of what had happened in Indo-China, Tunisia and Morocco, no French government dared consider independence for Algeria, since this would incur the wrath of the settlers and their supporters in France - ✅ and even Mendes-France, who had withdrawn from Indo-China and released Bourguiba, declared: "France without Algeria would be no France."

⚠️ When the man who executed two withdrawals says that about a third territory, the variable has been identified.

### 2. The settler economy and its human effect

✅ There were over a million French settlers, known as **pieds noirs**, "black feet", who controlled something like a third of all the most fertile land in Algeria, taken from the original Algerian owners during the century before 1940. ✅ The whites exported most of the crops they produced and also used some of the land to grow vines for winemaking; this meant there was less food available for the growing African population, whose standard of living was clearly falling.

⚠️ That is a complete economic mechanism in three clauses and it deserves to be written out as one, because it is exactly the kind of causal specificity that distinguishes a strong answer.

```text
   best land held by settlers
        -> output directed to EXPORT and to VITICULTURE
           (wine is not food, and it occupies arable land)
        -> domestic food supply constrained
        -> while the African population GROWS
        -> living standards FALL
        -> a grievance that is measurable, material and worsening
           year by year, independent of any political argument
```

⚠️ Note the contrast with Kenya, where the same structure existed but where the eventual remedy was available and was taken: ✅ restrictions were lifted on what the Kikuyu could grow, and coffee became a main crop. ⚠️ In Algeria no such adjustment was on offer, because ✅ the French settlers would make no concessions whatsoever, continuing to dominate the economy with their large farms and treating the Algerians as second-class citizens.

⚠️ And their strategic assumption is recorded, which is useful because it turned out to be false: ✅ they firmly believed that fear of the full might of the French army would be enough to dissuade the nationalists from becoming violent.

### 3. The failure of peaceful nationalism, and what follows from it

✅ There was an active, though peaceful, nationalist movement led by **Messali Hadj**, but after almost ten years of campaigning following the end of the Second World War, they had achieved absolutely nothing.

⚠️ This sentence is the hinge of the case and it should be used explicitly in an answer on the origins of violent decolonisation. ⚠️ Ten years of peaceful campaigning producing absolutely nothing is not a neutral fact; it is the empirical premise of the argument for armed struggle, and it was supplied by the settlers' refusal to concede anything at all.

✅ Tragically, the stubbornness of the settlers and their refusal even to talk meant that the struggle would be decided by the extremists.

⚠️ That is the account's own judgement and it is the generalisable proposition of this lesson: **where no concession is available to moderates, the movement passes to those who do not need concessions to justify themselves.** ⚠️ Compare Tunisia, where the French noticed the same dynamic in time and reversed it by releasing Bourguiba; compare Kenya, where refusal to negotiate with Kenyatta strengthened the Forty Group. ⚠️ Algeria is the case where nobody reversed it.

✅ Encouraged by the French defeat in Indo-China, a more militant nationalist group was formed - the **National Liberation Front (FLN)**, led by **Ben Bella**, which launched a guerrilla war towards the end of **1954**. ✅ At the same time, however, they promised that when they came to power the pieds noirs would be treated fairly.

⚠️ That last clause should not be dropped. ⚠️ Even the organisation that took up arms offered the settlers a guarantee, which means the refusal to negotiate was not a response to an existential threat but a refusal on principle. ⚠️ ✅ The settlers were still confident that with the support of the French army they could overcome the guerrillas.

### 4. The war's effect on France: four channels

✅ The war gradually escalated as the French sent more forces. ✅ By 1960 they had **seven hundred thousand troops** engaged in a massive anti-terrorist operation. ✅ The war was having profound effects in France itself.

✅ **Channel one - the strategic realisation.** Many French politicians realised that even if the army won the military struggle, the FLN still had the support of most of the Algerian people, and while this lasted French control of Algeria could never be secure.

⚠️ This is the single most transferable sentence in the topic and it deserves to be memorised. ⚠️ It states the counter-insurgency paradox exactly: military success does not produce political security where the insurgent retains popular support, because the population is the thing being contested and it cannot be captured. ⚠️ It is also the precise reason cost imposition works - the metropole must fund an indefinite occupation rather than a finite campaign.

✅ **Channel two - the split in public opinion.** The war split public opinion in France between those who wanted to continue supporting the white settlers and those who thought the struggle was hopeless; at times feelings ran so high that France itself seemed on the verge of civil war.

✅ **Channel three - the army's institutional stake.** The French army, after its defeats in the Second World War and Indo-China, saw the Algerian war as a chance to restore its reputation and refused to contemplate surrender. ✅ Some generals were prepared to stage a military coup against any government that decided to give Algeria independence.

⚠️ This is the most dangerous of the four because it makes the army an autonomous political actor with a veto. ⚠️ Note the causal chain running back through Lesson 14: the defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954 produced an army determined never to accept another, and that determination then threatened the Republic in May 1958.

✅ **Channel four - the mutiny of May 1958.** Suspecting that the government was about to give way, as it had in Tunisia and Morocco, **Generals Massu and Salan** organised demonstrations in Algiers in **May 1958** and demanded that **General de Gaulle** should be called in to head a new government. ✅ They were convinced that the general, a great patriot, would never agree to Algerian independence. ✅ They prepared their plan - codenamed **Resurrection** - to airlift paratroops from Algiers into Paris, where it was intended that they should occupy government buildings. ⚠️ **The operation was prepared and threatened but never carried out**: no troops occupied Paris, and it was the credible threat rather than its execution that did the work. ✅ Civil war seemed imminent; the government could see no way out of the deadlock and consequently resigned.

⚠️ Stop and state what has happened, because candidates routinely under-describe it. ⚠️ A colonial war produced a military conspiracy to seize the capital of the metropole, and the metropolitan government fell rather than face it. ⚠️ Compare Lesson 8's fourth step: settlers can act against their own metropole. ⚠️ This is the most extreme instance in the entire session, and it was executed not by settlers alone but by the metropolitan army in alliance with them.

### 5. De Gaulle: the return, the dispute and the pragmatism

✅ De Gaulle cleverly used the media to reinforce his case; he condemned the weakness of the Fourth Republic and its "regime of the parties", which he claimed was incapable of dealing with the problem, and, looking back to 1940, said: "Not so long ago, the country, in its hour of peril, trusted me to lead it to salvation. Today, with the trials that face it once again, it should know that I am ready to assume the powers of the Republic."

✅ President Coty called upon de Gaulle, who agreed to become prime minister on condition that he could draw up a new constitution. ✅ This turned out to be the end of the Fourth Republic.

⚠️ Record the historians' dispute, because it is a live one and the account presents it as such. ✅ Historians have had a great debate about de Gaulle's role: how much had he known about Resurrection? Had he or his supporters actually planned it themselves so that he could return to power? Was he simply using the situation in Algeria as a way of destroying the Fourth Republic, which he thought was weak? ✅ What does seem clear is that he knew about the plan and had dropped hints to Massu and Salan that if President Coty refused to allow him to take power, he would be happy for Resurrection to go ahead so that he could take power in that way.

⚠️ That is a carefully limited finding and it should be reproduced with its limits. ⚠️ The account does not say de Gaulle organised the conspiracy; it says he knew of it and signalled that he would accept its success. ⚠️ Writing more than that is fabrication; writing less loses the point that the Fifth Republic was founded in the shadow of a threatened coup.

✅ De Gaulle soon produced his new constitution, giving the president much more power, and was elected president of the Fifth Republic in **December 1958**, a position he held until his resignation in April 1969. ✅ His enormous prestige was demonstrated when the constitution was put to a referendum on **28 September 1958**: in France itself over **eighty per cent** voted in favour, while in Algeria, where Muslim Algerians were allowed to vote on equal terms with whites for the first time, the yes vote was **96.6 per cent**. ⚠️ The textbook account gives the Algerian figure as "over seventy-six per cent"; the recorded result was 96.6 per cent, and the corrected figure is the one used here.

⚠️ That Algerian referendum detail is easy to skim and it is important. ⚠️ The first occasion on which nine million Muslim Algerians voted on equal terms with a million settlers was a referendum on a French constitution, in the fifth year of a war for independence. ⚠️ It is simultaneously evidence of how long equal suffrage had been withheld and a caution against reading the result as a verdict on independence, which was not the question asked.

✅ Having gained power, de Gaulle was now expected to deliver a solution. ✅ But how could he achieve this when any attempt at compromise would be seen as total betrayal by the very people who had helped him to power? ✅ De Gaulle was the great pragmatist: as the vicious fighting continued, with both sides committing atrocities, he must have realised that outright military victory was out of the question, and he no doubt hoped that his popularity would enable him to force a settlement.

⚠️ Note the phrase **both sides committing atrocities**, and keep it. ⚠️ An answer on Algeria that describes atrocity in one direction only has misreported the source.

### 6. The OAS, and the collapse of the settler position

✅ When de Gaulle showed a willingness to negotiate with the FLN, the army and the settlers were incensed; this was not what they had expected from him. ✅ Led by General Salan, they set up **l'Organisation de l'Armee Secrete (OAS)** in 1961, which began a terrorist campaign, blowing up buildings and murdering critics both in Algeria and in France. ✅ Several times they attempted to assassinate de Gaulle; in **August 1962**, after independence had been granted, he and his wife narrowly escaped death when their car was riddled with bullets.

✅ When it was announced that peace talks would begin at **Evian**, the OAS seized power in Algeria. ✅ This was going too far for most French people and for many of the army too. ✅ When de Gaulle appeared on television dressed in his full general's uniform and denounced the OAS, the army split, and the rebellion collapsed.

⚠️ The mechanism of that collapse is worth naming. ⚠️ The settler position had always depended on the army; when the army divided, the settlers had no independent capacity. ⚠️ Compare Rhodesia, where the settlers had their own security forces and could therefore sustain a rebellion for fifteen years, and South Africa, where they controlled the state outright. ⚠️ The Algerian settlers were the most numerous and the most legally privileged of all the settler communities in this session, and they were also the most dependent, because their privilege rested on being part of France rather than on holding a state of their own.

### 7. Independence, and the aftermath handled with attribution

✅ The French public was sick of the war and there was widespread approval when **Ben Bella**, who had been in prison since 1956, was released to attend peace talks at Evian. ✅ It was agreed that Algeria should become independent in **July 1962**, and Ben Bella was elected as its first president the following year.

⚠️ Note once more, and for the third time in two lessons, the moderate-in-jail mechanism completing its cycle: the leader imprisoned in 1956 was released in 1962 to sign the settlement.

✅ About **eight hundred thousand settlers left the country**, and the new government took over most of their land and businesses.

⚠️ That figure records the largest single population movement in this session outside South Asia, and it is the measure of what "settler colony" meant. ⚠️ It also answers the land question that Zimbabwe deferred and South Africa never resolved: in Algeria the colonial distribution of productive assets ended at independence, because the owners left.

✅ **The aftermath of the struggle was savage.** ✅ Algerian Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some **two hundred thousand who had served in the French army**, were now denounced by the FLN as traitors. ✅ **Nobody knows how many were executed or murdered, but some estimates put the total as high as one hundred and fifty thousand.**

⚠️ Apply the attribution discipline exactly. ⚠️ The phrase to preserve is "nobody knows how many... but some estimates put the total as high as". ❌ Do not write "150,000 harkis were killed" as a flat fact; the source does not, and the uncertainty is part of the evidence. ⚠️ Note also what the figure counts: Algerians killed by Algerians after independence, for having served the colonial power. ⚠️ A war of national liberation produced a lethal settling of accounts with those on the wrong side of it, and an answer that omits this has given a partial account.

✅ Of all the wars of independence waged against a colonial power, this was one of the most bloody.

### 8. The verdict on de Gaulle, given in full

✅ Some historians have criticised de Gaulle for his handling of the Algerian situation and for the enormous bloodshed that was caused. ✅ Yet, given the intransigence of the white settlers and the rebel elements of the army, and eventually that of the FLN, it is difficult to imagine any other politician who could have handled it any better. ✅ It may have been a flawed process, but arguably it was one that saved France from civil war.

⚠️ That is a genuinely balanced judgement and it names three sources of intransigence rather than one - settlers, rebel army elements, and eventually the FLN. ⚠️ Reproduce all three. An answer that blames only the settlers has removed the difficulty that makes the judgement defensible.

⚠️ And note the counterfactual embedded in it: the achievement claimed is not a good outcome in Algeria but the avoidance of civil war in France. ⚠️ That is a metropolitan standard of success, and recognising it as such is exactly the kind of critical reading Lesson 3 asked for.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Algeria is the account's own example of one of the most bloody wars of independence and the canonical case of settler colonialism making withdrawal non-negotiable; it is also the clearest case of decolonisation transforming the metropole's own constitution.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ As the violent half of a route comparison, or inside a question on why some decolonisations were violent, or on the effects of decolonisation on the imperial powers themselves. ⚠️ The structure that scores: the legal fusion; the settler economy's measurable effect; ten years of peaceful nationalism achieving absolutely nothing; the war and its four effects in France; the fall of the Fourth Republic; de Gaulle's pragmatism against the OAS; Evian; and the attributed aftermath.

**Traps.**

1. ❌ Calling Algeria a French colony. ✅ It was treated as an extension or province of metropolitan France, which is why no ordinary act of transfer was available.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the effect on France. ✅ The Fourth Republic ended; a general's conspiracy to airlift troops into Paris was in operation; France itself seemed on the verge of civil war.
3. ⚠️ Stating the aftermath figure flatly. ✅ Nobody knows how many were executed or murdered; some estimates put the total as high as one hundred and fifty thousand, among those who had remained loyal to France including some two hundred thousand who had served in the French army.
4. ⚠️ Writing atrocity in one direction. ✅ Both sides committed atrocities, and the OAS conducted a terrorist campaign in France as well as in Algeria.
5. ⚠️ Treating de Gaulle's return as a constitutional event. ✅ It followed Operation Resurrection, and he knew of the plan and had dropped hints that he would accept power obtained that way.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Algeria was treated not as a colony or protectorate but as an extension or province of metropolitan France; the nine million Muslim Arab Algerians were allowed no say in the government of their country.
2. ✅ Over a million French settlers, the pieds noirs, controlled something like a third of all the most fertile land, taken from the original Algerian owners during the century before 1940.
3. ✅ The settlers exported most crops and grew vines for winemaking, so there was less food for a growing African population whose standard of living was clearly falling.
4. ✅ The Algerian assembly had 120 members with limited powers: a million whites elected sixty, nine million Muslims elected the other sixty, and European corruption usually gave them a majority anyway.
5. ✅ Messali Hadj led an active but peaceful nationalist movement that achieved absolutely nothing in almost ten years after the war.
6. ✅ The stubbornness of the settlers and their refusal even to talk meant the struggle would be decided by the extremists.
7. ✅ The FLN, led by Ben Bella, was formed after the French defeat in Indo-China and launched a guerrilla war towards the end of 1954, while promising that the pieds noirs would be treated fairly.
8. ✅ The textbook account states that by 1960 France had 700,000 troops engaged; estimates of French strength vary, so the figure should always be given with its source.
9. ✅ Four effects in France: the realisation that military victory could not secure control while the FLN retained popular support; a split in public opinion bringing France to the verge of civil war; an army determined to restore its reputation after the Second World War and Indo-China, with some generals ready to stage a coup; and the May 1958 Algiers demonstrations by Massu and Salan with Operation Resurrection.
10. ✅ The government resigned; de Gaulle condemned the Fourth Republic's regime of the parties and declared himself ready to assume the powers of the Republic; Coty called him; he became prime minister on condition of drawing up a new constitution; the Fourth Republic ended.
11. ✅ Historians dispute how far he knew of Resurrection; what seems clear is that he knew of the plan and had hinted to Massu and Salan that he would be happy for it to proceed if Coty refused him power.
12. ✅ Constitutional referendum, 28 September 1958: over eighty per cent in favour in France and 96.6 per cent in favour in Algeria, where Muslim Algerians voted on equal terms with whites for the first time. ✅ De Gaulle was elected president of the Fifth Republic in December 1958.
13. ✅ The OAS was set up under Salan in 1961 and ran a terrorist campaign in Algeria and France, repeatedly attempting to assassinate de Gaulle, including in August 1962 after independence.
14. ✅ The OAS seized power in Algeria when the Evian talks were announced; de Gaulle appeared on television in full general's uniform and denounced it; the army split and the rebellion collapsed.
15. ✅ Ben Bella, imprisoned since 1956, was released for the Evian talks; independence was agreed for July 1962 and he was elected first president the following year.
16. ✅ About 800,000 settlers left in and around 1962 and the new government took over most of their land and businesses. ⚠️ Reconcile the two settler numbers rather than letting them sit in contradiction: the settler population is given as over a million, while the 800,000 is a **departure** figure for 1962 and after, so the two count different things and neither is a correction of the other.
17. ✅ The aftermath was savage: Algerian Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some 200,000 who had served in the French army, were denounced as traitors; nobody knows how many were executed or murdered, but some estimates put the total as high as 150,000.
18. ✅ Of all the wars of independence waged against a colonial power, this was one of the most bloody; the account judges that, given the intransigence of the settlers, rebel army elements and eventually the FLN, it is difficult to imagine any other politician handling it better, and that the flawed process arguably saved France from civil war.
19. ⚠️ Master formulation: where a territory is legally part of the metropole and a million settlers hold its best land, there is no act of transfer available, so the only routes are enfranchisement that would transform the metropole or separation that would divide it - and the second required a war.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes that Algeria's war happened because France was more determined to keep its empire than Britain was. Refute this using evidence from French policy in the same years, and give the correct explanation.

**Model answer:** The claim fails because France conceded two other territories by negotiation in the middle of the Algerian war. Tunisia became fully independent in March 1956 with Bourguiba, released from the jail France had put him in, as its head, and Morocco became independent in 1956 with the king France had deposed in 1953 allowed to return; both decisions were taken because the French preferred negotiation to yet another long and expensive anti-guerrilla war, and in Tunisia's case explicitly because a moderate leader offered a better chance of maintaining French influence after independence. France had also withdrawn from Indo-China after Dien Bien Phu in May 1954. The correct explanation is structural. Algeria was treated not as a colony or a protectorate but as an extension or province of metropolitan France, so no ordinary act of transfer existed; over a million settlers held about a third of the most fertile land and had the political support to make any concession fatal to a French government, which is why even Mendes-France said France without Algeria would be no France; and the settlers refused even to talk, so that ten years of Messali Hadj's peaceful campaigning achieved absolutely nothing and the struggle passed to the extremists. What followed proves the point about determination: the war brought down the Fourth Republic, Generals Massu and Salan prepared to airlift troops into Paris in May 1958, and de Gaulle - brought back by people convinced he would never concede - granted independence in July 1962 anyway.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing the aftermath as though only one side committed atrocities. The account records that both sides committed atrocities during the fighting; that the OAS ran a terrorist campaign of bombings and murders in France as well as Algeria, repeatedly attempting to kill de Gaulle; and that after independence Algerian Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some two hundred thousand who had served in the French army, were denounced by the FLN as traitors, with nobody knowing how many were executed or murdered though some estimates put the total as high as one hundred and fifty thousand. The uncertainty in that last figure must be carried with it.

## Lesson 16 - The French empire III: the 1958 referendum and neo-colonialism by design

Progress: 16/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: French West and Equatorial Africa and the mandated territories; assimilation as a sham; the clampdown of 1949; internal self-government in 1956; de Gaulle's French Community and the referendum of 1958; Guinea's refusal under Sekou Toure and the withdrawal of aid; the independences of 1960; the overseas departements; and neo-colonialism as an intended design rather than an unintended outcome

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's section on the rest of the French empire, covering the eight colonies of French West Africa, the four of French Equatorial Africa, the mandated territories of Cameroun and Togo, Madagascar, the policy of treating the territories as part of France and its description as a sham, the 1949 clampdown and arrests, the internal self-government of 1956, de Gaulle's five-point plan of 1958 and the referendum results, Guinea's ninety-five per cent refusal under Sekou Toure and the stopping of all French aid, the independences of 1960, the statement that de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism, and the status of the three overseas departements
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the referendum of 1958 as a designed choice

```text
   WHAT WAS ON OFFER (de Gaulle's plan, 1958):
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   * the 12 colonies keep SELF-GOVERNMENT, each with its own
     parliament for local affairs
   * all become members of a new union: THE FRENCH COMMUNITY
   * FRANCE TAKES ALL IMPORTANT DECISIONS ABOUT TAXATION AND
     FOREIGN AFFAIRS
   * all members receive ECONOMIC AID from France
   * a REFERENDUM in each colony
   * colonies opting for full independence CAN HAVE IT -
     BUT WILL RECEIVE NO FRENCH AID
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   "De Gaulle was confident that NONE OF THEM WOULD DARE FACE
    THE FUTURE WITHOUT FRENCH HELP."

   THE RESULT
   ------------------------------------------------------------
        11 colonies       VOTE FOR THE PLAN
         1 colony         VOTES AGAINST: GUINEA, under SEKOU TOURE,
                          by NINETY-FIVE PER CENT
                              |
                          Guinea given independence IMMEDIATELY (1958)
                          ALL FRENCH AID STOPPED
                              |
                          "Guinea's brave stand encouraged the other
                           11, as well as Togo, Cameroun and
                           Madagascar: they all demanded full
                           independence and DE GAULLE AGREED. They
                           all became independent republics during 1960."

   THE OUTCOME DE GAULLE ACTUALLY OBTAINED
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   "this new independence was not quite so complete as the new
    states had hoped - DE GAULLE WAS INTENT ON NEO-COLONIALISM -
    ALL THE STATES EXCEPT GUINEA found that France still influenced
    their economic and foreign policies, and any independent action
    was almost out of the question."

   => the vote was lost and the objective was achieved.
```

⚠️ The last line is the lesson. A referendum designed to prevent independence produced independence for every participant within two years, and the power that lost it retained most of what it had been trying to protect. ⚠️ This is the single clearest demonstration in the session that formal sovereignty and effective control are separable, and it is where the concept of neo-colonialism stops being an accusation and becomes a description of an intention.

### 1. The territories, and the doctrine that governed them

✅ The French possessions in Africa south of the Sahara were **French West Africa**, consisting of eight colonies - Dahomey, Guinea, Ivory Coast, Mauretania, Niger, Senegal, Sudan and Upper Volta; **French Equatorial Africa**, consisting of four colonies - Chad, Gabon, Middle Congo and Oubangui-Shari; a third group consisting of **Cameroun and Togo**, former German colonies given to France to be looked after as mandates in 1919; and the island of **Madagascar**.

✅ French policy after 1945 was to treat these territories as if they were part of France. ✅ Yet this was a sham, since the Africans were not treated on equal terms with Europeans, and any moves towards more privileges for the Africans were opposed by the French settlers.

⚠️ That word - sham - is the account's own and it should be used, because it names precisely the structure Lesson 15 identified in Algeria. ⚠️ The doctrine of assimilation offers membership of the metropolitan nation instead of national independence, and it is a genuine alternative only if the membership is real. ⚠️ Where the membership is withheld in practice, assimilation becomes the most effective possible obstacle to independence: it denies the premise on which independence would be demanded without delivering the equality that would make the denial acceptable.

✅ And when the territories organised anyway, the response was repression with a Cold War label attached. ✅ In 1949 the French government decided to clamp down on all nationalist movements, and many nationalist leaders and trade unionists were arrested; ✅ often they were denounced as communist agitators, though without much evidence to support the accusations.

⚠️ Note the two things happening in that sentence at once. ⚠️ First, the targeting of trade unionists alongside politicians, which is the Lesson 4 finding again. ⚠️ Second, the pre-emptive use of anti-communism as a justification for colonial repression, without evidence - which is exactly the pattern Lesson 6 identified in the American containment argument and which Porter qualified for Africa. ⚠️ A candidate who can connect the French clampdown of 1949 to the American willingness to accept anti-communist justifications has made a genuinely strong cross-lesson link.

### 2. The reluctant concessions of 1956

✅ Gradually the French were forced by events in Indo-China and the Maghreb, together with the fact that Britain was preparing the Gold Coast and Nigeria for independence, to change their policy. ✅ In **1956** the twelve colonies of West and Equatorial Africa were each given self-government for internal affairs, but they continued to press for full independence.

⚠️ Three separate pressures are named in that first sentence and it is worth separating them, because it is a compact illustration of Lesson 3's causation argument working in combination.

⚠️ **Defeat elsewhere in the same empire.** ✅ Indo-China, meaning Dien Bien Phu and Geneva in 1954.

⚠️ **Simultaneous pressure elsewhere in the same region.** ✅ The Maghreb, meaning the Tunisian and Moroccan settlements of 1956 and the Algerian war from late 1954.

⚠️ **The demonstration effect of a rival empire.** ✅ Britain preparing the Gold Coast and Nigeria for independence. ⚠️ This is a metropolitan-level demonstration effect rather than a popular one: French policy changed partly because British policy had changed, and a colonial power that is seen to be more generous than its neighbour creates a problem for the neighbour.

### 3. The plan of 1958, read as an instrument

✅ When de Gaulle came to power in 1958 he proposed a new plan, hoping to keep as much control over the colonies as possible. ✅ The twelve colonies would continue to have self-government, each with its own parliament for local affairs; ✅ they would all be members of a new union, the **French Community**, and France would take all important decisions about taxation and foreign affairs; ✅ all members of the Community would receive economic aid from France; ✅ there would be a referendum in each colony to decide whether the plan should be accepted; ✅ and colonies opting for full independence could have it, but would receive no French aid.

⚠️ Examine the design, because it is a very carefully constructed instrument and its construction is the examinable content.

⚠️ **What it gives away.** Local parliaments and internal self-government - the visible attributes of self-rule.

⚠️ **What it retains.** Taxation and foreign affairs. ⚠️ Those two are not a random selection; they are the fiscal and the external sovereignty of a state. ⚠️ A polity whose taxes and foreign policy are decided elsewhere has a parliament and does not have a state.

⚠️ **How consent is procured.** By a referendum - which supplies democratic legitimacy - with aid conditional on the answer. ⚠️ The alternative on offer is independence *without* aid, in territories whose economies the account elsewhere describes as underdeveloped, with very little industry by deliberate colonial design, dependent on one or two commodity exports.

⚠️ **The prediction.** ✅ De Gaulle was confident that none of them would dare face the future without French help.

⚠️ This is worth stating as a general proposition, because it recurs throughout the post-colonial period: **where aid is conditional on a constitutional choice, the referendum measures dependence rather than preference.** ⚠️ Any answer on the limits of formal decolonisation can use this as its sharpest single example.

### 4. Guinea, and what one refusal cost and achieved

✅ He was almost right: eleven colonies voted in favour of his plan, but one, **Guinea**, under the leadership of **Sekou Toure**, returned a **ninety-five per cent** vote against. ✅ Guinea, the only one of the **twelve** territories that voted to do so, was given independence immediately in **1958**, and all French aid was stopped; ✅ the **eleven** that had voted yes became independent during **1960**.

⚠️ Take the two halves of that seriously. ⚠️ Ninety-five per cent is not a narrow rejection of a bad offer; it is a categorical refusal of the terms themselves, made by a population that knew the stated penalty in advance. ⚠️ And the penalty was imposed in full and immediately.

⚠️ This is the strongest single piece of evidence in the session for the agency argument of Lesson 3, and it should be deployed there as well as here. ⚠️ A metropolitan thesis holds that decolonisation was decided in Europe for European reasons. ⚠️ Guinea decided otherwise, against the explicit design of the European decision, and paid the announced price.

✅ However, Guinea's brave stand encouraged the other eleven, as well as Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar: they all demanded full independence and de Gaulle agreed. ✅ They all became independent republics during **1960**.

⚠️ So the referendum failed completely within two years, and it failed by demonstration. ⚠️ One territory showed that the threatened penalty was survivable, and once it was survivable the choice offered in 1958 ceased to be a choice.

⚠️ Note also where Guinea appears in Lesson 5. ✅ Guinea, Mali and Morocco were the states supporting Nkrumah's federal project, and ✅ an economic union was formed with Guinea and Mali. ⚠️ A state that has just lost all metropolitan aid needs an alternative structure, which explains both Guinea's pan-African commitment and the correlation noticed in Lesson 5 between support for continental union and the absence of a patron.

### 5. Neo-colonialism, stated as a design

✅ However, this new independence was not quite so complete as the new states had hoped - **de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism** - ✅ all the states except Guinea found that France still influenced their economic and foreign policies, and any independent action was almost out of the question.

⚠️ This sentence should be committed to memory, because it settles a question that answers usually leave open. ⚠️ Neo-colonialism is often presented as an unintended consequence: formal independence arrived, economies remained dependent, and influence persisted by default. ⚠️ Here it is recorded as an **intention held by the head of the metropolitan state during the transfer**. ⚠️ Combine it with the British equivalent from Lesson 1 - ✅ the British were confident that they would still be able to exercise influence through trade links, which they hoped to preserve by including the new states as members of the Commonwealth, and ✅ this practice became known as neo-colonialism and became widespread in most of the new states of the Third World - and the finding is that both major imperial powers designed a successor relationship before the transfer, and that both designs substantially worked.

⚠️ Two further observations sharpen this.

⚠️ **The exception proves the design.** ✅ All the states except Guinea found that France still influenced their economic and foreign policies. ⚠️ The one territory that refused the Community and lost aid was also the one that escaped the influence. ⚠️ That is not a coincidence; it is the price structure working exactly as designed, in both directions.

⚠️ **The smaller powers could not do this.** ✅ The Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism and could not maintain the equivalent of the British Commonwealth or French influence against competition from foreign capital, which is why they were even more determined to hold on. ⚠️ So the wealth of a metropole determined whether it could afford to leave, and the design of neo-colonial arrangements determined what it kept when it did.

### 6. The territories that never left

✅ Three French possessions outside Africa - **Martinique, Guadeloupe and French Guiana** - were not given independence. ✅ They continued to be treated as extensions of the mother country and their official status was "overseas departements", a sort of county or province. ✅ Their peoples voted in French elections and their representatives sat in the French National Assembly in Paris.

⚠️ This is a short passage with a long implication and it is worth a sentence in any answer on the completeness of decolonisation.

⚠️ Assimilation, which was a sham in Africa, was implemented in the Caribbean and South America. ⚠️ The populations of these three territories did receive metropolitan citizenship with real voting rights and parliamentary representation, and they did not become independent. ⚠️ The comparison is instructive rather than decisive: it suggests that assimilation could function as a genuine alternative to independence where the territory was small and the metropole was willing to deliver the equality it promised, and that its failure in Algeria and in French West Africa was a failure of delivery rather than of the concept.

⚠️ It is also the point at which this session connects to the current position recorded in Lesson 6: ✅ seventeen Non-Self-Governing Territories remain on the United Nations list, and the majority are small islands. ⚠️ Decolonisation as a process concluded for large territories and did not conclude for small ones, and Martinique, Guadeloupe and French Guiana show one of the routes by which a small territory could exit the process without becoming independent.

### Comparative note: two designs for keeping influence

| Feature | ✅ British design | ✅ French design |
|---|---|---|
| Named vehicle | ✅ The Commonwealth - former British-controlled nations agreeing to continue associating, mainly for the advantages to be gained | ✅ The French Community, proposed 1958 |
| Stated expectation | ✅ Confidence that Britain would still exercise influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership | ✅ Hoping to keep as much control over the colonies as possible |
| What was retained formally | ⚠️ Nothing formal; membership was voluntary and carried no reserved powers | ✅ All important decisions about taxation and foreign affairs |
| Instrument of consent | ⚠️ Accession at independence | ✅ A referendum in each colony, with aid withheld from any that refused |
| Outcome of the formal scheme | ⚠️ Membership was widely retained | ✅ The Community was superseded within two years by full independence for all participants |
| ⚠️ Outcome of the underlying objective | ✅ The practice of exercising influence over former colonies by economic means became known as neo-colonialism and became widespread | ✅ All the states except Guinea found France still influenced their economic and foreign policies, and any independent action was almost out of the question |

⚠️ Read the last two rows together. ⚠️ The British scheme survived formally and the French scheme collapsed formally, and both metropoles obtained substantially what they had wanted. ⚠️ The lesson is that the institutional wrapper was not what did the work; the aid, the trade and the commodity dependence did.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This lesson supplies the syllabus's decolonisation clause with its most explicit evidence that the transfer of sovereignty was designed to be partial, and it is the necessary foundation for any answer on neo-colonialism, which Lesson 24 completes.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Inside questions on whether decolonisation was genuine, on the limits of independence, or on French versus British methods. ⚠️ The decisive move is to present the 1958 referendum as an instrument with a price attached, note that the aim was stated rather than inferred, and then show that the vote was lost and the objective achieved.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating neo-colonialism as a later critique. ✅ It is recorded as a British expectation at the start of the African withdrawal and as de Gaulle's intention in 1958-60.
2. ⚠️ Forgetting the mandated territories. ✅ Cameroun and Togo were former German colonies given to France as mandates in 1919, and they joined the demand for full independence in 1960.
3. ⚠️ Missing what the Community retained. ✅ Taxation and foreign affairs - the fiscal and external sovereignty of a state.
4. ⚠️ Treating Guinea as a minor exception. ⚠️ Its refusal by ninety-five per cent, and its survival without aid, destroyed the scheme within two years and encouraged fourteen other territories.
5. ❌ Asserting figures for French aid or for Guinea's economic losses. ⚠️ None is given in the sources read for this session.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ French West Africa: eight colonies - Dahomey, Guinea, Ivory Coast, Mauretania, Niger, Senegal, Sudan, Upper Volta.
2. ✅ French Equatorial Africa: four colonies - Chad, Gabon, Middle Congo, Oubangui-Shari. ✅ Plus Cameroun and Togo, former German colonies held as mandates from 1919, and Madagascar.
3. ✅ French policy after 1945 was to treat these territories as if they were part of France - a sham, since Africans were not treated on equal terms and moves towards more privileges were opposed by the French settlers.
4. ✅ In 1949 the French clamped down on all nationalist movements, arresting many nationalist leaders and trade unionists, often denounced as communist agitators without much evidence.
5. ✅ Pressure from Indo-China, the Maghreb and Britain's preparation of the Gold Coast and Nigeria forced a change; in 1956 the twelve colonies each received self-government for internal affairs, but pressed for full independence.
6. ✅ De Gaulle's 1958 plan: self-government with local parliaments; membership of a new French Community; France to take all important decisions on taxation and foreign affairs; economic aid for members; a referendum in each colony; independence available but with no French aid.
7. ✅ De Gaulle was confident none would dare face the future without French help.
8. ✅ Twelve territories voted; eleven voted in favour; Guinea under Sekou Toure returned a ninety-five per cent vote against, took independence immediately in 1958 and had all French aid stopped, while the eleven that voted yes became independent during 1960.
9. ✅ Guinea's brave stand encouraged the other eleven plus Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar; all demanded full independence, de Gaulle agreed, and all became independent republics during 1960.
10. ✅ The new independence was not quite so complete as hoped - de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism - and all the states except Guinea found France still influenced their economic and foreign policies, with independent action almost out of the question.
11. ✅ Martinique, Guadeloupe and French Guiana were not given independence; they became overseas departements, voting in French elections with representatives in the National Assembly.
12. ⚠️ Comparison: the British Commonwealth design survived formally and the French Community collapsed formally, and both metropoles substantially achieved the underlying objective, because the work was done by aid, trade and commodity dependence rather than by the institutional wrapper.
13. ⚠️ Master formulation: where aid is made conditional on a constitutional choice, a referendum measures dependence rather than preference - which is why de Gaulle's plan won eleven votes to one and collapsed within two years once one territory proved the penalty survivable.

### Concept check

**Question:** De Gaulle's 1958 referendum was won in eleven of twelve territories and the French Community had ceased to matter within two years. Explain why the scheme failed so quickly, and why France nevertheless got most of what it wanted.

**Model answer:** It failed because Guinea disproved its central assumption. The plan offered self-government with local parliaments inside a French Community in which France would take all important decisions about taxation and foreign affairs, with economic aid for members and none for any territory choosing full independence, and de Gaulle was confident that none of them would dare face the future without French help. Guinea, under Sekou Toure, returned a ninety-five per cent vote against, was granted independence immediately in 1958 and had all French aid stopped - and survived. Once the announced penalty had been shown to be survivable, the choice offered in 1958 was no longer a choice: Guinea's stand encouraged the other eleven colonies together with Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar, all of them demanded full independence, de Gaulle agreed, and all became independent republics during 1960. France nevertheless obtained its objective because the Community was only the wrapper. The account states that the new independence was not quite so complete as the new states had hoped, that de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism, and that all the states except Guinea found France still influencing their economic and foreign policies with independent action almost out of the question. The exception is the proof: the one territory that refused the aid was the one that escaped the influence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating neo-colonialism as a retrospective accusation made by critics of decolonisation. In this evidence base it is recorded twice as a stated intention held during the transfer itself - as British confidence that influence would continue through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership, described in the source as the practice that became known as neo-colonialism, and as de Gaulle's own purpose in 1958-60. The concept therefore describes a designed successor relationship, not an accidental one, and an answer that presents it as an unintended side-effect has understated what the sources say.

---

## Lesson 17 - The Netherlands and Indonesia, 1945-65

Progress: 17/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Dutch East Indies and the export-crop model; Sukarno's arrest, release under Japanese occupation and declaration of 1945; four years of Dutch military effort and the five reasons for negotiating; recognition in 1949; West Irian and the break from the Union; and the coup of 1965 with its mass killing and its Cold War reception

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's Netherlands section, covering the extent of the East Indies empire, the comparison of Dutch methods with the French in Algeria, the nationalist campaigns of the 1930s and Sukarno's arrest, his release and administrative role under Japanese occupation, the declaration of an independent republic in 1945, the four-year war and the five recorded reasons for the Dutch decision to negotiate, the recognition of 1949 and the exclusion of West Irian, the break from the Netherlands-Indonesia Union, the West Irian settlement of 1963, and the 1965 coup with the CIA role, the New Order purge and the western reaction; together with the East Timor material used at the boundary
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: four years of war, and the five reasons it ended

```text
   1942  Japanese invade; RELEASE Sukarno and others; allow them a
         part in the administration; promise independence after the war
                              |
   1945  Japanese defeated. SUKARNO DECLARES AN INDEPENDENT REPUBLIC
         OF INDONESIA, "not expecting any resistance from the Dutch,
         who had been defeated and their country occupied by the
         Germans"
                              |
   1945-49  "Dutch troops soon arrived and made determined efforts to
            regain control." The war drags on. They are "still a long
            way from complete victory in 1949".
                              |
   1949  THE FIVE REASONS FOR NEGOTIATING, as recorded:
         1. "The expense of the campaign was CRIPPLING for a small
            country like the Netherlands."
         2. "Outright victory still seemed a long way off."
         3. "They were under strong pressure from the UN to reach
            agreement."
         4. "Other countries, including the USA and Australia, were
            pressing the Dutch to grant independence SO THAT THEY
            COULD EXERT THEIR INFLUENCE IN THE AREA, once exclusive
            Dutch control ended."
         5. "The Dutch hoped that by making concessions, they would be
            able to PRESERVE THE LINK between Holland and Indonesia
            and maintain some influence."
                              |
   1949  Netherlands recognises THE UNITED STATES OF INDONESIA, with
         Sukarno as president - BUT NOT INCLUDING WEST IRIAN.
         Sukarno accepts a Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the
         Dutch crown; Dutch troops withdraw.
                              |
   1950  Sukarno BREAKS AWAY from the Union; pressurises the Dutch
         over West Irian; seizes Dutch-owned property; expels Europeans
                              |
   1963  The Dutch give way: WEST IRIAN becomes part of Indonesia
                              |
   1965  Sukarno overthrown in a RIGHT-WING MILITARY COUP
```

⚠️ Reason four in the middle box is the one to notice and the one most answers miss. The United States and Australia pressed for Indonesian independence *so that they could exert their influence in the area once exclusive Dutch control ended.* That is Lesson 6's market motive stated in a single sentence, applied to a specific territory, by the account itself.

### 1. The Dutch colonial model, and its Algerian resemblance

✅ Before the Second World War the Netherlands had a huge empire in the East Indies, including the large islands of Sumatra, Java and Celebes, West Irian - part of the island of New Guinea - and about two-thirds of the island of Borneo. ✅ They also owned some islands in the West Indies, and Surinam on the mainland of South America.

✅ It was in the valuable East Indies that the first challenge came to Dutch control, even before the war. ✅ The Dutch operated in a way similar to the French in Algeria - they grew crops for export and did very little to improve the living standards of the East Indians.

⚠️ That comparison is the account's own and it is analytically useful, so use it. ⚠️ The Algerian mechanism from Lesson 15 - best land directed to export production while the domestic population's standard of living falls - is here described as the Dutch model too, and it recurs in Lesson 23 as the general colonial economic structure: ✅ most African territories had very little industry, this being a deliberate policy by the colonial powers whose intention was that the colonies should buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and provide food and raw materials.

⚠️ So three continents, three empires, one design. ⚠️ Stating that as a single proposition - **colonial economies were built to export primary products and to import manufactures, and the resulting structure outlived the political relationship** - is worth more than three separate descriptions.

✅ Nationalist groups campaigned throughout the 1930s, and many leaders, including **Ahmed Sukarno**, were arrested.

### 2. The Japanese interval, and why it produced a republic in 1945

✅ When the Japanese invaded in 1942 they released Sukarno and others and allowed them to play a part in the administration of the country, promising independence when the war was over.

⚠️ This is the Lesson 2 mechanism in its most consequential form, and it should be handled without embarrassment. ⚠️ The Japanese occupation supplied the Indonesian nationalist leadership with three things the Dutch had denied it: liberty, administrative experience, and a public promise of independence. ⚠️ ✅ Sukarno declared the republic on 18 August 1945 and was its first president from that date; the Netherlands recognised that independence only in 1949.

⚠️ It also explains a Dutch policy noted in Lesson 2: ✅ the Dutch government, shocked that people were so ready to co-operate with the Japanese in the East Indies, offered them some degree of independence as soon as the Japanese were defeated. ⚠️ The pre-emptive offer was an attempt to compete with the occupier's promise, and it failed because a promise made under duress in 1945 is worth less than experience of office acquired in 1942.

✅ With the Japanese defeat, Sukarno declared an independent Republic of Indonesia on **18 August 1945** and was its **first president from that date**, not expecting any resistance from the Dutch, who had been defeated and their country occupied by the Germans. ✅ However, Dutch troops soon arrived and made determined efforts to regain control.

⚠️ That expectation - and its disappointment - is worth a sentence. ⚠️ Sukarno's reasoning was that a state which had itself been occupied and liberated could not coherently reimpose occupation elsewhere. ⚠️ He was wrong about the Netherlands as he would have been wrong about France, and the reason is the one Lesson 2 gave: the war's effect on metropolitan capacity was real, but capacity is not the same as willingness, and defeated metropoles frequently chose to fight in order to restore standing they had lost in Europe.

### 3. Why the Dutch stopped, and who wanted them to

✅ Although the Dutch had some success, the war dragged on, and they were still a long way from complete victory in 1949, when they at last decided to negotiate.

⚠️ The five recorded reasons repay individual attention because each maps onto a different lesson in this session.

⚠️ **Expense.** ✅ The expense of the campaign was crippling for a small country like the Netherlands. ⚠️ Cost imposition (Lesson 3), with the metropole's size as the multiplier.

⚠️ **Military prospects.** ✅ Outright victory still seemed a long way off. ⚠️ The counter-insurgency paradox (Lesson 15): territory could be taken and control could not be secured.

⚠️ **The United Nations.** ✅ They were under strong pressure from the UN to reach agreement. ⚠️ The institutional pressure of Lesson 6, operating a full decade before the 1960 Declaration.

⚠️ **Third-party interest.** ✅ Other countries, including the USA and Australia, were pressing the Dutch to grant independence so that they could exert their influence in the area, once exclusive Dutch control ended. ⚠️ Lesson 6's market and influence motive, stated flatly.

⚠️ **Retained influence.** ✅ The Dutch hoped that by making concessions they would be able to preserve the link between Holland and Indonesia and maintain some influence. ⚠️ Lesson 16's neo-colonial calculation, attempted here by a power too small to sustain it - ✅ recall that the Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism.

⚠️ The five together are the best single inventory in this session of the pressures that ended a colonial war, and they can be transposed to almost any case.

### 4. Recognition, the withheld province and the break

✅ The Netherlands agreed to recognise the independence of the **United States of Indonesia in 1949**, with Sukarno continuing as president, **but not including West Irian**. ⚠️ 1949 is the date of **Dutch recognition**, not of the presidency, which dates from 18 August 1945; keep the two apart, because the gap between them is four years of war. ✅ Sukarno agreed to a **Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the Dutch crown**, and Dutch troops were withdrawn.

⚠️ Two devices appear here and both are familiar from other cases.

⚠️ **A retained province.** ⚠️ Withholding West Irian preserved a Dutch foothold and a bargaining asset. ⚠️ Compare the British retention of two military bases in Cyprus and the Belgian preference for an independent Katanga that would be easier to influence. ⚠️ Partial withdrawal with a retained enclave is a recurring metropolitan device and it consistently produces a subsequent dispute.

⚠️ **A union under the Crown.** ⚠️ This is the French Community's device in miniature, four years earlier: formal independence inside a continuing constitutional link with the metropole.

⚠️ Both failed within a year. ✅ The following year Sukarno broke away from the Union and began to pressurise the Dutch to hand over West Irian, seizing Dutch-owned property and expelling Europeans. ✅ Eventually in **1963** the Dutch gave way and allowed West Irian to become part of Indonesia.

⚠️ Note the instrument Sukarno used: ✅ seizing Dutch-owned property and expelling Europeans. ⚠️ Where a metropole's residual leverage is economic, the successor state's counter-leverage is expropriation - which is precisely why, as Lesson 6 records, ✅ western countries disapproved where new governments were prepared to nationalise resources or foreign businesses and responded by cutting off aid, destabilising or in some cases overthrowing governments.

### 5. 1965: the coup, the killings, and the silence

✅ Important developments began in **1965**, when the army moved against the left after the attempted coup of 30 September and Sukarno was progressively displaced in what amounted to a **right-wing military takeover**, apparently because he was thought to be too much under the influence of communist China and the Indonesian Communist Party - **the largest communist party outside the USSR and China**. ⚠️ Be exact about the sequence, because the textbook account compresses it into a single year. ✅ Sukarno was not removed in 1965: he was compelled to transfer executive authority to **Suharto by the order of 11 March 1966**, known as **Supersemar**, and was stripped of the presidency by the provisional assembly, the **MPRS**, in **March 1967**.

✅ The USA, operating via the CIA, was involved in these events, because they did not like Sukarno's toleration of the Communist Party, or the way in which he was acting as leader of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World.

⚠️ Read the second clause of that sentence beside Lesson 7. ⚠️ The host of the Bandung conference was removed with the involvement of a superpower that objected, among other things, to his leadership of the non-aligned movement. ⚠️ Non-alignment was not merely unprotective; it was itself a stated reason for intervention.

✅ The Americans welcomed Sukarno's successor, **General Suharto**, who obligingly introduced what he called his **"New Order"**. ✅ This involved a purge of communists, during which **at least half a million people were murdered**, and the Communist Party was broken. ✅ The regime had all the hallmarks of a brutal military dictatorship, but there were few protests from the West because, in the Cold War atmosphere, Suharto's anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable.

⚠️ Apply the attribution discipline. ⚠️ The figure is recorded as **at least half a million people murdered** during the purge. ❌ Do not round it, give it a range, or attach a different total. ⚠️ Attribute responsibility as the account does - to the regime conducting the purge - and record the western reaction, which is part of the historical fact rather than a comment on it.

⚠️ The general proposition, which connects this lesson to Lessons 6 and 23, is this: **the Cold War supplied not only intervention but permission.** ⚠️ Mass killing by an anti-communist government in a strategically valuable state attracted few protests from the West because of the alignment of the killers, and that silence is a documented feature of the period rather than an inference.

> **Boundary and forward reference.** ⚠️ Indonesia's own invasion and annexation of East Timor in 1975 is taught in Lesson 20, where the Portuguese decolonisation that preceded it belongs. ⚠️ The Indonesian Communist Party as an ideological formation, and Asian communism generally, belong to the topic on Asian communism.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Indonesia is the principal Southeast Asian decolonisation after India in this material, the main Dutch case, and the clearest instance of a metropole defeated by cost rather than on the battlefield.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Usually as the Asian counterpart in a comparison, or inside a question on the Second World War's effect on decolonisation, or on the Cold War and the new states. ⚠️ The five reasons for the Dutch decision make an unusually well-structured paragraph and should be used as such.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating the Japanese occupation as merely an interruption. ✅ It released the nationalist leadership, gave it administrative experience and promised independence, and Sukarno declared a republic on the Japanese defeat.
2. ⚠️ Presenting the 1949 settlement as complete. ✅ West Irian was withheld until 1963, and the Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the Dutch crown was broken by Sukarno within a year.
3. ⚠️ Treating American pressure for Indonesian independence as anti-colonial principle alone. ✅ The USA and Australia pressed the Dutch so that they could exert their own influence once exclusive Dutch control ended.
4. ⚠️ Omitting 1965. ⚠️ A decolonisation answer that stops at 1949 misses the point that the state which emerged was overthrown sixteen years later with foreign involvement, and that at least half a million people were murdered in the purge that followed.
5. ❌ Inventing figures for the war of 1945-49. ⚠️ None is given in the sources read for this session, and the gap is left open rather than filled.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ The Dutch East Indies included Sumatra, Java, Celebes, West Irian and about two-thirds of Borneo; the Netherlands also held West Indian islands and Surinam.
2. ✅ The Dutch operated in a way similar to the French in Algeria: growing crops for export and doing very little to improve living standards.
3. ✅ Nationalist groups campaigned throughout the 1930s and many leaders, including Sukarno, were arrested.
4. ✅ The Japanese invaded in 1942, released Sukarno and others, allowed them a part in the administration and promised independence after the war.
5. ✅ On the Japanese defeat in 1945 Sukarno declared an independent Republic of Indonesia, not expecting resistance from a Netherlands that had itself been defeated and occupied; Dutch troops soon arrived and made determined efforts to regain control.
6. ✅ The five reasons for negotiating in 1949: crippling expense for a small country; outright victory still a long way off; strong UN pressure; the USA and Australia pressing so that they could exert their own influence once exclusive Dutch control ended; and Dutch hopes of preserving the link and maintaining some influence.
7. ✅ The Netherlands recognised the independence of the United States of Indonesia in 1949 with Sukarno as president, excluding West Irian; Sukarno accepted a Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the Dutch crown and Dutch troops withdrew.
8. ✅ The following year Sukarno broke away from the Union, pressed for West Irian, seized Dutch-owned property and expelled Europeans; in 1963 the Dutch gave way and West Irian became part of Indonesia.
9. ✅ From 1965 Sukarno was displaced by a right-wing military takeover - executive authority transferred to Suharto by the Supersemar order of 11 March 1966 and the presidency removed by the MPRS in March 1967 - apparently because he was thought too much under the influence of communist China and the Indonesian Communist Party, the largest communist party outside the USSR and China.
10. ✅ The USA, operating via the CIA, was involved, objecting to his toleration of the Communist Party and to his leadership of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World.
11. ✅ General Suharto's "New Order" involved a purge of communists during which at least half a million people were murdered and the Communist Party was broken.
12. ✅ The regime had all the hallmarks of a brutal military dictatorship, but there were few protests from the West because in the Cold War atmosphere the anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable.
13. ✅ Surinam became an independent republic in 1975; the Dutch West Indian islands were treated as part of the Netherlands with some control over internal affairs.
14. ⚠️ Master formulation: Indonesia shows a small metropole defeated by cost rather than in battle, a partial settlement leaving a retained province to be fought over for fourteen years, and a Cold War that supplied both intervention and permission.

### Concept check

**Question:** The United States pressed the Netherlands to grant Indonesian independence in the 1940s and was involved in the military displacement of Indonesia's first president from 1965. Explain why these are not contradictory policies.

**Model answer:** They follow from a single consistent interest, which is access and influence rather than the constitutional status of the territory. In 1949 the account records that other countries including the USA and Australia were pressing the Dutch to grant independence so that they could exert their influence in the area once exclusive Dutch control ended - that is, American pressure for decolonisation was directed against the exclusivity of Dutch control, not against control as such. The wider pattern is the same: the Americans looked on newly independent nations as potential markets into which they could force their way and establish both economic and political influence, and regarded imperially protected markets as giving the Europeans an unfair advantage. The 1965 coup follows from the same interest under changed conditions. Sukarno was thought to be too much under the influence of communist China and of the Indonesian Communist Party, the largest outside the USSR and China, and the USA operating via the CIA was involved in his overthrow because it objected both to his toleration of that party and to the way he was acting as leader of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World. The resulting New Order purged the communists, with at least half a million people murdered, and attracted few protests from the West because in the Cold War atmosphere an anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable. Support for formal sovereignty and intervention against a government's orientation are the same policy applied at two moments.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the 1949 recognition as the completion of Indonesian decolonisation. The Netherlands withheld West Irian and obtained Sukarno's agreement to a Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the Dutch crown; he broke away from the Union the following year and pressed for West Irian by seizing Dutch-owned property and expelling Europeans, and the Dutch did not give way until 1963. Partial withdrawal with a retained enclave was a standard metropolitan device - compare the two British military bases retained in Cyprus - and it consistently produced a further dispute rather than a settlement.

---

## Lesson 18 - Malaya, Malaysia and the successor-state question

Progress: 18/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: a territory that had to be assembled before it could be transferred; the Federation of Malaya of 1948 and the exclusion of Singapore; the communist insurgency and the Emergency of 1948-60; resettlement combined with a credible independence promise; the Alliance and the election of 1955; independence in 1957; the Federation of Malaysia of September 1963, Brunei's refusal and Singapore's separation in 1965; and the two control cases of the West Indies and Cyprus

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's section on the West Indies, Malaya and Cyprus, covering the definition of a federation, the composition and complexity of Malaya, the multiracial population and the rubber and tin economy, the Federation of Malaya of 1948 with Singapore left out, the franchise and its communal effect, Chin Peng's guerrillas and the Emergency of 1948-60 with the resettlement tactic and the independence promise, the Alliance Party and the 1955 election result, independence and Commonwealth admission in 1957, the proposal and proclamation of the Federation of Malaysia in September 1963 after a UN investigation, Brunei's refusal and 1984 independence, Singapore's separation in 1965, and the parallel West Indian and Cypriot experiments
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
