### Visual: what had to be decided before independence could be granted

```text
   THE RAW MATERIAL, 1945
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   "a complex area which would be difficult to organize":
       NINE STATES, each ruled by a SULTAN
       TWO BRITISH SETTLEMENTS: MALACCA and PENANG
       SINGAPORE, "a small island less than a mile from the mainland"
   POPULATION: "multiracial: mostly MALAYS and CHINESE, but with some
   INDIANS and EUROPEANS as well"
   ECONOMY: "based on exports of RUBBER and TIN" and "THE MOST
   PROSPEROUS IN SOUTH-EAST ASIA"

   TWO QUESTIONS HAD TO BE ANSWERED FIRST
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   Q1  WHAT IS THE UNIT?          Q2  WHO INHERITS IT?
       |                              |
   1948 FEDERATION OF MALAYA:     ALL ADULTS HAVE THE VOTE
   states + settlements grouped   in the chosen unit
   SINGAPORE LEFT OUT as a        -> "this meant that the MALAYS,
   separate colony                   THE LARGEST GROUP, USUALLY
   each state keeps a legislature    DOMINATED AFFAIRS"
   for local affairs; sultans
   retain some power; BUT "the    => the two answers are ONE answer.
   central government had FIRM       Excluding Singapore was a
   OVERALL CONTROL"                  DEMOGRAPHIC decision as much
                                     as an administrative one.

   THE CHALLENGE TO BOTH ANSWERS
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   CHIN PENG's Chinese communist guerrillas - "who had played a
   leading role in the resistance to the Japanese" - stir up strikes
   and violence in support of an INDEPENDENT COMMUNIST STATE
        -> STATE OF EMERGENCY declared 1948, in force until 1960
        -> tactic: "RESETTLE INTO SPECIALLY GUARDED VILLAGES all
           Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas"
        -> AND, decisively: "it was made clear that INDEPENDENCE
           WOULD FOLLOW AS SOON AS THE COUNTRY WAS READY FOR IT;
           this ensured that the MALAYS remained firmly pro-British
           and gave very little help to the communists, who were
           Chinese"

   THE POLITICAL ROUTE OUT
   ------------------------------------------------------------
   TUNKU ABDUL RAHMAN's Malay Party joins the main Chinese and
   Indian groups -> the ALLIANCE PARTY
        -> wins 51 OF 52 SEATS in the 1955 elections
        -> "This seemed to suggest stability and the British were
           persuaded to grant full independence in 1957"
        -> Malaya admitted to the Commonwealth
   1963 Sept  FEDERATION OF MALAYSIA proclaimed (Malaya + Singapore +
              North Borneo/Sabah + Sarawak), after a UN investigation
              team reported a large majority in favour
              BRUNEI decides NOT to join (independent 1984)
   1965       SINGAPORE leaves to become an independent republic;
              "the rest of the Federation continued successfully"
```

⚠️ The instruction at the centre of the diagram - that the two questions are one answer - is the analytical core of this lesson. In a plural society, deciding the boundaries of the successor state decides who will govern it, so the constitutional question and the communal question cannot be separated.

### 1. A unit that did not exist

✅ Malaya was liberated from Japanese occupation in 1945, but there were two difficult problems to be faced before the British were prepared to withdraw.

✅ The first was that it was a complex area which would be difficult to organise. ✅ It consisted of nine states each ruled by a sultan, two British settlements - Malacca and Penang - and Singapore, a small island less than a mile from the mainland. ✅ The population was multiracial: mostly Malays and Chinese, but with some Indians and Europeans as well.

⚠️ Begin an answer here, because this is what distinguishes Malaya from every case so far. ⚠️ In the Gold Coast, Nigeria, Kenya and Algeria the territory existed as an administrative unit and the question was who would rule it. ⚠️ In Malaya there was no single polity to transfer. ⚠️ The British were therefore obliged to *create* the successor state before they could hand anything over, and creating it meant drawing a boundary - and drawing a boundary in a plural society is a decision about demography.

⚠️ Add the economic stake, which raises what was at issue. ✅ The economy was based on exports of rubber and tin and was the most prosperous in south-east Asia. ⚠️ This is the only territory in this session described as the most prosperous in its region, and prosperity raises the value of inheriting the state rather than reducing the conflict over it.

### 2. The 1948 settlement, and the demography inside the constitution

✅ In preparation for independence it was decided to group the states and the settlements into the **Federation of Malaya in 1948**, while **Singapore remained a separate colony**. ✅ Each state had its own legislature for local affairs; the sultans retained some power, but **the central government had firm overall control**. ✅ All adults had the vote, and this meant that the **Malays, the largest group, usually dominated affairs**.

⚠️ Three observations, each of which is a mark.

⚠️ **The exclusion of Singapore was a demographic act.** ⚠️ Singapore was, as the account records, less than a mile from the mainland, and it was left out of the unit being assembled. ⚠️ Its exclusion altered the communal balance of the electorate in the Federation, and the account states the consequence directly in the next breath: universal adult suffrage in the chosen unit meant that the Malays, as the largest group, usually dominated affairs. ⚠️ The franchise was universal; the electorate was selected by drawing the boundary.

⚠️ **Compare that with Nigeria and Kenya.** ⚠️ Nigeria adopted federalism with regions self-governing before the centre, and the centre proved too weak to hold the union. ⚠️ Kenya considered federalism at KADU's request and abandoned it. ⚠️ Malaya adopted a federation in which the central government had firm overall control. ⚠️ Three answers to one question, with three different outcomes, and the variable is where residual power was placed.

⚠️ **The sultans were retained.** ⚠️ Nine indigenous rulers kept some power, which converted potential opponents of a centralising nationalist state into stakeholders in it. ⚠️ Compare Uganda's asymmetric federal solution for the kabaka in Lesson 11, and Morocco's restoration of Mohammed V in Lesson 14: incorporating a pre-colonial authority is a recurring and generally successful device.

### 3. The insurgency, and the counter-insurgency that worked

✅ **Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng**, who had played a leading role in the resistance to the Japanese, now began to stir up strikes and violence against the British in support of an independent communist state.

⚠️ That opening clause is Lesson 2's second mechanism arriving in its most direct form: the anti-Japanese resistance becoming the anti-British insurgency. ⚠️ It is the same sequence as Indo-China and it produced the opposite result, which is exactly why the pair is worth comparing.

✅ The British decided to declare a **state of emergency in 1948**, and in the end they dealt with the communists successfully, though it took time, and the state of emergency remained **in force until 1960**. ✅ Their tactics were to **resettle into specially guarded villages all Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas**. ✅ It was made clear that **independence would follow as soon as the country was ready for it**; this ensured that the Malays remained firmly pro-British and gave very little help to the communists, who were Chinese.

⚠️ The last sentence is the decisive one in the whole lesson and it deserves to be stated as a mechanism rather than narrated.

```text
   The insurgents demand:  INDEPENDENCE (under a communist state)
   The British respond by:  PROMISING INDEPENDENCE - to a different
                            community
        -> the majority community now has MORE to gain from British
           victory than from insurgent victory
        -> the insurgency loses the population it would need
        -> counter-insurgency + a credible political timetable
           defeats it
   NOTE: repression alone did not do this. RESETTLEMENT was coercive
   and it worked only because it was paired with a POLITICAL OFFER
   that made the majority a stakeholder in the outcome.
```

⚠️ Two cautions belong with that mechanism, and an answer is stronger for including them.

⚠️ **The resettlement was coercive and communal.** ✅ Those resettled into guarded villages were Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas - a population selected by ethnicity plus suspicion. ⚠️ The measure that helped win the Emergency also deepened the communal division that the constitutional settlement was designed to manage.

⚠️ **The promise was addressed to a community, not to the territory.** ⚠️ Britain did not promise independence to everyone equally; it promised independence in a unit and on a franchise that would deliver Malay dominance, to a community that therefore had reason to defend the arrangement. ⚠️ That is a sophisticated political instrument and it should be described as such rather than praised.

⚠️ Finally, place this beside Algeria and the contrast is total. ⚠️ In Algeria ten years of peaceful campaigning achieved absolutely nothing, so the struggle passed to the extremists and a war followed. ⚠️ In Malaya, an armed insurgency was defeated because the coloniser conceded, in advance and credibly, the thing the insurgents were fighting for - to somebody else. ⚠️ **A credible political timetable is a counter-insurgency weapon, and its absence is an insurgent recruiting instrument.** That proposition, supported from both cases, is among the most transferable in this session.

### 4. The Alliance, and independence in 1957

✅ The move towards independence was accelerated when the Malay Party, under their able leader **Tunku Abdul Rahman**, joined forces with the main Chinese and Indian groups to form the **Alliance Party**, which won **fifty-one out of the fifty-two seats** in the **1955** elections. ✅ This seemed to suggest stability and the British were persuaded to grant full independence in **1957**, when Malaya was admitted to the Commonwealth.

⚠️ Notice what the Alliance is and is not. ⚠️ It is not a single-nation mass movement of the kind that the Convention People's Party or the Tanganyika African National Union represented. ⚠️ It is a **coalition of communal parties**, in which each community's organisation retains its identity while agreeing to contest elections jointly. ⚠️ That is a structurally different instrument, and it was the appropriate one: in a society with no majority nationalism, the achievable unit of political agreement is an agreement among communities rather than a nation.

⚠️ And notice the British criterion. ✅ The election result "seemed to suggest stability", and the British were persuaded. ⚠️ Stability, not maturity, was the test - which is consistent with the Malayan case throughout: Britain's objection was never to independence but to independence delivered to the insurgents, and a fifty-one-out-of-fifty-two result by an inter-communal coalition removed the objection.

### 5. Malaysia, Brunei and Singapore: the unit revised twice more

✅ Malaya was running well under the Tunku's leadership, and its economy, based on exports of rubber and tin, was the most prosperous in south-east Asia. ✅ In **1961**, when the Tunku proposed that Singapore and three other British colonies - North Borneo (Sabah), Brunei and Sarawak - should join Malaya to form the **Federation of Malaysia**, Britain agreed. ✅ After a **United Nations investigation team** reported that a large majority of the populations concerned was in favour of the union, the Federation of Malaysia was officially proclaimed in **September 1963**. ✅ **Brunei decided not to join**, and eventually became an independent state within the Commonwealth in **1984**. ✅ Although **Singapore decided to leave the Federation to become an independent republic in 1965**, the rest of the Federation continued successfully.

⚠️ Three analytical points close the case.

⚠️ **Decolonisation did not end at independence.** ⚠️ The shape of the successor state remained contested for eight more years after 1957, and was settled in 1963 by enlargement and in 1965 by separation. ⚠️ This is the single most useful corrective in the lesson: independence is a date, but the successor-state question is a process that can continue long after it.

⚠️ **The instruments used were consent-based.** ✅ A United Nations investigation team reported a large majority in favour; ✅ Brunei simply decided not to join; ✅ Singapore left and the rest continued successfully. ⚠️ Compare Katanga's attempted secession in Lesson 19 and Biafra's in Lesson 23, both of which produced war. ⚠️ The difference is not that Malaysia's plural society was less divided but that the question of the unit remained politically negotiable rather than being fixed by force at independence.

⚠️ **The central government's firm overall control mattered.** ⚠️ Where the centre is strong, a member's departure removes a problem; where the centre is weak, as in Nigeria, a region's departure threatens the state's existence and is resisted by war.

### 6. Two control cases: the West Indies and Cyprus

⚠️ The same chapter that supplies Malaya supplies two further British experiments in assembling or partitioning successor states, and they are worth a compact paragraph each because they test the Malayan conclusion.

✅ **The West Indies.** ✅ A federation is where a number of states join together under a central or federal government which has overall authority, each state having its own parliament for internal affairs; ✅ many people thought it would be suitable for the British West Indies and for Malaya and neighbouring territories. ✅ Britain's West Indian possessions were a large assortment of islands with a population of around six million; ✅ some were very small - Grenada, St Vincent and Antigua about a hundred thousand each, St Kitts and Nevis only about sixty thousand between them - and there were doubts about whether they were viable as independent states. ✅ Some territories objected: Honduras and Guiana wanted nothing to do with a federation; ✅ Jamaica and Trinidad worried about coping with the problems of the smaller islands; ✅ some islands disliked the prospect of being dominated by Jamaica and Trinidad; ✅ and some of the smallest were not even sure they wanted independence at all. ✅ Britain went ahead anyway and established the **West Indies Federation in 1958**, excluding British Honduras and British Guiana, ✅ but it never really functioned successfully - the one thing they all had in common, a passionate commitment to cricket, was not enough, and there were constant squabbles about budget contributions and representation. ✅ Jamaica voted to leave in a referendum in **September 1961** and **Trinidad withdrew in January 1962**, after which the federation no longer seemed viable, and in **1962** Britain abandoned it and granted independence separately. ✅ By 1983 all parts had become independent except a few tiny islands: Jamaica and Trinidad and Tobago first in 1962, St Kitts and Nevis last in 1983; ✅ British Guiana became Guyana in 1966 and British Honduras took the name **Belize in 1973** and became independent in **1981**. ✅ Ironically, having rejected a fully-fledged federation, they soon found economic benefits in co-operation: the **Caribbean Free Trade Association in 1968** developed into the **Caribbean Community and Common Market in 1973**, which all the former British West Indian territories including Guyana and Belize joined.

⚠️ The West Indian case establishes the contrast with Malaya precisely. ⚠️ Both were British federation projects of the same years; ⚠️ Malaya's members wanted the same state and disagreed about who would run it, while the West Indian members did not want the same state and could not agree on the terms of sharing. ⚠️ The sequel - functional economic co-operation succeeding where political federation failed - is the same finding Lesson 5 reached about Pan-Africanism, and the two together make a general point worth stating: **shared function is a firmer basis for union than shared identity or administrative convenience.**

✅ **Cyprus.** ✅ The British Labour government of 1945-51 considered independence, but progress was delayed by complications, the most serious of which was the mixed population - about **eighty per cent** Greek-speaking Christians of the Orthodox Church, the rest Muslims of Turkish origin. ✅ The Greek Cypriots wanted the island to unite with Greece, **enosis**, and the Turks were strongly opposed. ✅ Churchill's government inflamed the situation in 1954 with self-government plans allowing far less power than Labour had intended; ✅ Eden then dropped the idea of independence altogether, believing Britain needed the island as a military base to protect her interests in the Middle East, and announced that Cyprus must remain permanently British. ✅ The Greek Cypriots under **Archbishop Makarios** pressed their demands, while a guerrilla organisation called **Eoka**, led by **General Grivas**, waged a campaign against the British, who declared a state of emergency in **1955** and deployed about **thirty-five thousand troops**; ✅ British policy also involved deporting Makarios and executing terrorists. ✅ In 1958 the Turks set up a rival organisation in support of dividing the island. ✅ To avoid possible civil war, **Macmillan** compromised: he appointed the sympathetic and tactful **Hugh Foot** as governor, who negotiated a deal with Makarios under which the Archbishop **dropped enosis** and Cyprus was granted full independence; ✅ Turkish interests were safeguarded; ✅ Britain retained **two military bases** and, with Greece and Turkey, guaranteed Cypriot independence; ✅ and Makarios became the first president with a Turkish Cypriot, **Fazil Kutchuk**, as vice-president in **1960**. ✅ It seemed the perfect solution, but it lasted only until **1963**, when civil war broke out between Greeks and Turks; ✅ in **1974** Turkey sent troops to help establish a separate Turkish state in the north, and the island has remained divided since, with Turks occupying roughly **one-third** of the island's area and Greeks the south, with UN troops keeping the peace. ✅ In the mid-1980s the UN pressed the idea of a federation as the most likely way of reconciling the two states, but this was rejected by the Greeks in 1987. ✅ In April 2003 the checkpoints along the frontier were opened so that both communities could cross the partition line for the first time since 1974, and ✅ in May 2004 the Republic of Cyprus joined the European Union while the island was still divided.

⚠️ Cyprus is the third answer to the successor-state question and the only one that failed outright. ⚠️ Malaya assembled a unit and made the franchise decide; the West Indies abandoned the attempt and separated amicably; Cyprus built a power-sharing constitution between two communities whose preferred outcomes were incompatible - union with Greece against partition - and it collapsed within three years. ⚠️ The variable is whether the communities agreed on what state they were in: in Malaya they did, in the West Indies the question was dissolved, and in Cyprus they did not.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Malaya is the principal Southeast Asian British case and the clearest instance of the syllabus's redrawal-of-boundaries clause operating during decolonisation itself; the Cypriot and West Indian experiments belong with it.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ The recurring demand is on the problems of decolonisation in the Malay Peninsula and how they differed from those of other colonies. ⚠️ The answer is: the difficulty was not extracting a departure but constructing a successor, so the examinable content is the unit, the franchise, the insurgency, the promise, the Alliance and the two later revisions of 1963 and 1965.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing the Emergency as a story of successful repression. ✅ Resettlement worked only in combination with a public commitment that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready, which kept the Malays firmly pro-British.
2. ⚠️ Treating the exclusion of Singapore as administrative tidiness. ⚠️ It altered the communal composition of the electorate in the unit that would vote, and universal adult suffrage then meant the Malays usually dominated affairs.
3. ⚠️ Calling the Alliance a nationalist movement. ✅ It was the Malay Party joining forces with the main Chinese and Indian groups - an inter-communal coalition, which is a structurally different thing.
4. ⚠️ Ending the story in 1957. ✅ The Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963, Brunei refused to join and became independent in 1984, and Singapore left in 1965.
5. ❌ Naming Malayan or Malaysian actors beyond those recorded. ✅ Chin Peng and Tunku Abdul Rahman are the two named here; do not add parties, riots or constitutional guarantees not in the sources.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Malaya was liberated from Japanese occupation in 1945; two problems had to be solved before British withdrawal.
2. ✅ It was a complex area difficult to organise: nine states each ruled by a sultan, two British settlements at Malacca and Penang, and Singapore, less than a mile from the mainland.
3. ✅ The population was multiracial - mostly Malays and Chinese, with some Indians and Europeans; the economy was based on exports of rubber and tin and was the most prosperous in south-east Asia.
4. ✅ The Federation of Malaya was formed in 1948, grouping the states and settlements while Singapore remained a separate colony; each state kept a legislature for local affairs and the sultans retained some power, but the central government had firm overall control.
5. ✅ All adults had the vote, which meant the Malays, the largest group, usually dominated affairs.
6. ✅ Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng, who had played a leading role in the resistance to the Japanese, stirred up strikes and violence in support of an independent communist state.
7. ✅ A state of emergency was declared in 1948 and remained in force until 1960; the tactic was to resettle into specially guarded villages all Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas.
8. ✅ It was made clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready for it, which ensured the Malays remained firmly pro-British and gave very little help to the communists, who were Chinese.
9. ✅ Tunku Abdul Rahman's Malay Party joined the main Chinese and Indian groups to form the Alliance Party, which won 51 of 52 seats in the 1955 elections; this seemed to suggest stability and the British were persuaded to grant full independence in 1957, when Malaya was admitted to the Commonwealth.
10. ✅ The Tunku proposed in 1961 that Singapore, North Borneo (Sabah), Brunei and Sarawak join Malaya; a United Nations investigation team reported a large majority in favour; the Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963.
11. ✅ Brunei decided not to join and became independent within the Commonwealth in 1984; Singapore left to become an independent republic in 1965, and the rest of the Federation continued successfully.
12. ✅ The West Indies Federation was set up in 1958, excluding British Honduras and British Guiana, and never functioned successfully; Jamaica voted to leave in September 1961 and Trinidad withdrew in January 1962, and Britain abandoned the federation in 1962, granting independence separately - Jamaica and Trinidad and Tobago in 1962, St Kitts and Nevis last in 1983; Guyana 1966; British Honduras renamed Belize in 1973 and independent in 1981; the Caribbean Free Trade Association of 1968 became the Caribbean Community and Common Market in 1973.
13. ✅ Cyprus: about eighty per cent Greek-speaking Orthodox Christians and the rest Muslims of Turkish origin; enosis demanded and opposed; Eden judged the island necessary as a military base and said it must remain permanently British; Eoka under General Grivas fought the British, who declared an emergency in 1955, deployed about 35,000 troops, deported Makarios and executed terrorists.
14. ✅ Macmillan compromised through Governor Hugh Foot: Makarios dropped enosis, Cyprus received full independence, Turkish interests were safeguarded, Britain retained two military bases and joined Greece and Turkey in guaranteeing independence, and Makarios became president with Fazil Kutchuk as vice-president in 1960.
15. ✅ Civil war broke out in 1963; Turkey sent troops in 1974 and the island has remained divided, Turks holding roughly one-third of its area, with UN troops keeping the peace; a federation was proposed by the UN in the mid-1980s and rejected by the Greeks in 1987; checkpoints opened in April 2003; the Republic of Cyprus joined the EU in May 2004 with the island still divided.
16. ⚠️ Master formulation: where the colonised society is plural, decolonisation is a contest over the successor state's boundaries and franchise rather than over the coloniser's departure - and a credible independence promise is a counter-insurgency weapon.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain how Britain defeated a communist insurgency in Malaya while conceding independence, and state what the case shows about the relationship between political concession and counter-insurgency.

**Model answer:** Britain defeated the insurgency by promising to the majority community the thing the insurgents were fighting for. Chin Peng's Chinese communist guerrillas, who had played a leading role in the resistance to the Japanese, demanded an independent communist state, and Britain declared a state of emergency in 1948 which remained in force until 1960. The coercive component was resettlement into specially guarded villages of all Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas. The decisive component was political: it was made clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready for it, which ensured that the Malays remained firmly pro-British and gave very little help to the communists, who were Chinese. The promise was credible because the unit and the franchise had already been settled in the Malays' favour - the Federation of Malaya of 1948 grouped the states and settlements while leaving Singapore out, and universal adult suffrage in that unit meant the largest group usually dominated affairs - so the majority community had more to gain from a British victory than from an insurgent one. The general proposition is that a credible political timetable is a counter-insurgency weapon, and its absence is an insurgent recruiting instrument: in Algeria ten years of peaceful campaigning achieved absolutely nothing and the struggle passed to the extremists, while in Malaya the promise of independence emptied the insurgency of the population it needed. It was completed politically rather than militarily, through the Alliance Party's fifty-one seats out of fifty-two in 1955 and independence in 1957.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating Malayan independence as complete in 1957. The successor-state question continued for another eight years: the Tunku proposed an enlarged union in 1961, the Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963 after a United Nations investigation team reported a large majority in favour, Brunei decided not to join and became independent in 1984, and Singapore left to become an independent republic in 1965, after which the rest of the Federation continued successfully. Where the society is plural, independence is a date within a longer process of deciding what the state is.

---

## Lesson 19 - Belgium, the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi

Progress: 19/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the two Belgian methods of control; the riots of January 1959 and the six-month timetable; seventeen graduates and no prepared successor; independence on 30 June 1960; the Katanga secession and the Belgian interest in it; Lumumba's arrest and murder; the United Nations operation and its limits; Mobutu; and Ruanda-Urundi's transfer of a colonial hierarchy into two independent states

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's Belgium section and its Congo chapter, covering the two methods of control, the January 1959 rioting in Leopoldville, the six-month announcement and the reasons for it, the seventeen graduates, the MNC's age, independence on 30 June 1960 with Lumumba and Kasavubu, the Katanga secession under Tshombe and the Belgian and American positions, Hammarskjold's refusals, Lumumba's arrest and murder, the twenty thousand UN troops and the ending of the secession, Mobutu's seizure of power in 1965, the 1981 Nationality Law, and the separate treatment of Ruanda-Urundi
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: a colonial method designed to prevent a successor

```text
   THE TWO BELGIAN METHODS OF CONTROL, as recorded
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   [1] "DENYING THE AFRICANS ANY ADVANCED EDUCATION. This would
        prevent them from coming into contact with nationalist ideas
        and deprive them of an educated professional class who could
        LEAD THEM TO INDEPENDENCE."
   [2] "USING TRIBAL RIVALRIES to their advantage by playing off
        different tribes against each other. This worked well in the
        huge Congo, which contained ABOUT 150 TRIBES; men from one
        tribe would be used to keep order in another tribal area.
        In Ruanda-Urundi the Belgians used the TUTSI tribe to help
        them control the other main tribal group, the HUTU."
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   BOTH methods were DESIGNED to make an orderly transfer IMPOSSIBLE.
   Neither was a failure of colonial policy. Both were the policy.

   THE TRIGGER AND THE TIMETABLE
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   Jan 1959  Widespread rioting in LEOPOLDVILLE, "protesting against
             unemployment and declining living standards"; disorder
             spreads throughout the country
                  |
             "The Belgians SUDDENLY CHANGED THEIR POLICY and
              announced that the Congo could become independent
              IN SIX MONTHS."
                  |
   WHY? TWO REASONS RECORDED:
        * "afraid of further bloodshed if they hesitated; there were
           OVER 100,000 BELGIANS in the country, who could be at risk"
        * "did not want to face the expense of a long anti-guerrilla
           campaign like the one dragging on in ALGERIA"
                  |
   WHAT THE SIX MONTHS HAD TO PRODUCE, from nothing:
        * an administrative class - but "there were only 17 GRADUATES
          in the entire country, and there were NO AFRICAN DOCTORS,
          LAWYERS, ENGINEERS OR OFFICERS IN THE ARMY"
        * a national political settlement - but the MNC under
          LUMUMBA "had been in existence LESS THAN A YEAR" and there
          were OVER 50 different groups
        * a workable unit - in a country of huge size and about 150
          ethnic groups
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   30 JUNE 1960  INDEPENDENCE. Lumumba prime minister, Kasavubu
                 president.
   WITHIN DAYS   army mutiny against the fact that ALL OFFICERS WERE
                 BELGIANS; KATANGA secedes under TSHOMBE
```

⚠️ The top box is the lesson. The Congo is usually cited as the case of inadequate preparation, which invites the inference that Belgium failed. It did not fail; it succeeded at what it had set out to do. The absence of a successor class was the deliberate product of a stated policy, and the crisis of 1960 is that policy's consequence rather than its refutation.

### 1. Two methods, both designed to prevent decolonisation

✅ Belgian control of their African possessions, the Belgian Congo and Ruanda-Urundi, ended in chaos, violence and civil war. ✅ The Belgians thought that the best ways to preserve their control were as follows.

✅ **Denying the Africans any advanced education.** ✅ This would prevent them from coming into contact with nationalist ideas and deprive them of an educated professional class who could lead them to independence.

✅ **Using tribal rivalries to their advantage by playing off different tribes against each other.** ✅ This worked well in the huge Congo, which contained about a hundred and fifty tribes; men from one tribe would be used to keep order in another tribal area. ✅ In Ruanda-Urundi the Belgians used the Tutsi to help them control the other main group, the Hutu.

⚠️ Take both of these as *stated colonial policy* and the whole case reorganises itself.

⚠️ **Method one names the dependency Lesson 9 identified and then weaponises it.** ⚠️ The Gold Coast could be transferred because it had a small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals; Central Africa was harder partly because settlers had ensured very little education was provided. ⚠️ Belgium did deliberately and systematically what the Central African settlers did as a side-effect. ⚠️ The consequence - only seventeen graduates in the entire country - is therefore not a measure of Congolese backwardness but of Belgian effectiveness.

⚠️ **Method two supplies the causal link that Lesson 23 needs.** ⚠️ Post-independence ethnic conflict is often explained by "ancient hatreds" or by the artificiality of borders. ⚠️ Here is a better explanation, recorded as policy: the colonial state deliberately organised its coercive apparatus so that one group policed another, over a century, in a territory containing about a hundred and fifty groups. ⚠️ That does not create hostility from nothing; it institutionalises it, attaches it to the state, and makes control of the state the thing every group must either hold or fear.

✅ In spite of all these efforts, nationalist ideas still began to filter in from neighbouring French and British colonies.

⚠️ Note that last sentence, because it is the demonstration effect of Lesson 9 breaching a deliberate quarantine. ⚠️ Belgium could suppress education and organisation inside the Congo; it could not suppress what was happening in the territories next door.

### 2. The six months

✅ The Belgians seemed taken by surprise when widespread rioting broke out in **January 1959** in the capital of the Congo, Leopoldville. ✅ The crowds were protesting against unemployment and declining living standards, and disorder soon spread throughout the country.

⚠️ Register the character of the trigger. ⚠️ It was not a nationalist rising led by a party; it was an economic protest about unemployment and falling living standards which became general. ⚠️ A colonial state that has suppressed political organisation will find that discontent arrives without leadership - which is worse for it, because there is nobody to negotiate with and nothing to concede short of everything.

✅ The Belgians suddenly changed their policy and announced that the Congo could become independent in six months. ✅ This was inviting disaster: the Belgians' own policies meant that there was no experienced group of Africans to which power could be handed over; ✅ the Congolese had not been educated for professional jobs - there were only **seventeen graduates** in the entire country, and there were **no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or officers in the army**. ✅ The **Congolese National Movement (MNC)**, led by **Patrice Lumumba**, had been in existence less than a year. ✅ The huge size of the country and the large number of ethnic groups would make it difficult to govern. ✅ Six months was far too short a time to prepare for independence.

✅ Why did the Belgians take this extraordinary decision? ✅ They were afraid of further bloodshed if they hesitated; there were over a hundred thousand Belgians in the country, who could be at risk. ✅ And they did not want to face the expense of a long anti-guerrilla campaign like the one dragging on in Algeria.

⚠️ Both reasons are worth a sentence each.

⚠️ **The settler-safety reason.** ⚠️ Over a hundred thousand Belgians in the country is a substantial European population, though not a landowning settler community of the Kenyan or Algerian type. ⚠️ Their presence produced the opposite effect from Kenya's settlers: instead of making withdrawal impossible, the fear for their safety made it immediate. ⚠️ The difference is that the Belgians in the Congo were an administrative and commercial population who could leave, while Kenya's and Algeria's settlers owned the land and could not.

⚠️ **The Algerian reason.** ✅ Belgium decided in 1959-60 on the basis of what was happening to France. ⚠️ This is the demonstration effect operating at metropolitan level, as Lesson 16 showed it operating on French policy in 1956. ⚠️ Empires learned from each other's wars, and the lesson Belgium drew from Algeria was to leave fast rather than to leave well.

⚠️ And here is the sentence that converts the case into an argument: **the Congo was not decolonised too quickly by accident; it was decolonised quickly because Belgium's own prior policy had left it with no option that was both cheap and orderly.** ⚠️ Having spent decades preventing the emergence of a successor, Belgium could not produce one in six months, and having seen the price of fighting in Algeria, it would not pay to stay.

### 3. Independence and its immediate collapse

✅ The Congo became independent on **30 June 1960**, with **Lumumba** as prime minister and **Joseph Kasavubu**, the leader of a rival nationalist group, as president.

✅ Unfortunately everything went wrong shortly after independence and the country was plunged into a disastrous civil war; ✅ order was not restored until 1964.

⚠️ Two things went wrong almost immediately and they are different in kind.

✅ **The army mutinied**, a few days after independence, against the fact that all its officers were Belgians.

⚠️ That is the education policy delivering its bill on the first page. ⚠️ An army with no African officers is an army whose command structure is foreign on the day the country becomes sovereign, and no post-colonial government can be secure in that position. ⚠️ It is also the first instance in this session of the proposition Lesson 23 will generalise: where the state's institutions are thin, the army is the most organised body in the country and therefore the most politically consequential.

✅ **Katanga seceded.** ✅ Encouraged by the Belgian copper-mining industry, Katanga declared itself independent under **Moise Tshombe**; ✅ this was the wealthiest part of the Congo, which the new state could not afford to lose.

⚠️ Note the agency in that sentence. ⚠️ The secession of the richest province was encouraged by the metropole's own mining interest, and ✅ many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga, which would be easier for them to influence, and wanted to continue their control of the copper mining. ⚠️ This is the clearest case in the session of Lesson 8's concentrated-economic-value variable: where the value sits in one province, the former coloniser's commercial interest favours detaching that province rather than retaining the whole.

⚠️ It is also the exact situation that the United Nations Declaration of December 1960, discussed in Lesson 6, was drafted against: ⚠️ the warning against any attempt aimed at the partial or total disruption of the national unity and territorial integrity of a country was written while Katanga's secession was in progress.

### 4. Lumumba, and the international politics of the crisis

✅ Lumumba, unable to rely on his mutinous army, appealed to the UN to help him preserve Congolese unity, and a **three-thousand-strong peacekeeping force** soon arrived.

✅ Lumumba wanted to use UN troops to force Katanga back into the Congo, but the situation was complex. ✅ He had already made himself unpopular with the Americans and British because of his outspoken socialism; ✅ the Americans in particular regarded him as a dangerous communist who would align the Congo on the side of the USSR in the Cold War. ✅ Many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga, which would be easier for them to influence, and they wanted to continue their control of the copper mining.

✅ Faced with all these pressures, the UN secretary-general, **Dag Hammarskjold**, refused to allow a UN attack on Katanga, though at the same time he refused to recognise Katangese independence. ✅ In disgust Lumumba appealed for help to the Russians, ✅ but this horrified Kasavubu, who, supported by **General Joseph Mobutu** and encouraged by the Americans and Belgians, had Lumumba arrested; ✅ he and two former ministers in his government were flown to Katanga, badly beaten, and then shot in **January 1961** by **Katangan gendarmes commanded by Belgian officers**, with Belgian and Katangan officials present. ⚠️ The textbook account says only "murdered by Belgian troops"; the exact agency - Katangan gendarmerie under Belgian command - is what an answer should state.

⚠️ Handle this passage carefully and completely, because it is the single most consequential sequence in African decolonisation after apartheid.

⚠️ **The sequence is self-reinforcing and should be laid out as such.** The prime minister cannot use his own army because its officers are foreign and it has mutinied; he therefore appeals to the United Nations; the United Nations will not act against the secession; he therefore appeals to the Soviet Union; that appeal is the evidence used to justify his removal; and he is killed. ⚠️ Each step is a rational response to the failure of the previous one, and the outcome was produced by the structure rather than by his character.

⚠️ **The charge and the evidence should be separated.** ✅ The account says he was regarded as a dangerous communist because of his outspoken socialism, and that the appeal to the Russians followed the refusal of the United Nations to act. ⚠️ The sequence therefore runs the opposite way from the accusation: the Soviet approach was a consequence of the western refusal, not the cause of it.

⚠️ **The killing is attributed, and the attribution has to be exact.** ✅ He and two former ministers were badly beaten and then shot in January 1961 by Katangan gendarmes under the command of Belgian officers, with Belgian and Katangan officials present. ❌ Do not write simply "Belgian troops", ❌ do not embroider the account, and ❌ do not extend the attribution to any government not named in the evidence.

✅ As the chaos continued, Hammarskjold realised that more decisive UN action was needed, and although he was killed in an air crash near **Ndola**, in Northern Rhodesia, in **September 1961** while flying to meet Tshombe, his successor **U Thant** followed the same line. ✅ By mid-1961 there were **twenty thousand UN troops** in the Congo; in September they invaded Katanga; the final operation opened in **December 1962** and the secession was formally ended on **15 January 1963**, with Tshombe going into exile. ⚠️ The textbook account closes the secession in December 1962; the announcement ending it came on 15 January 1963. ✅ Though successful, UN operations had been expensive, and within a few months all their troops were withdrawn. ✅ Ethnic rivalries aggravated by unemployment caused disorders to break out again almost immediately, and calm was not restored until **1965**, when **General Mobutu** of the Congolese army, **using white mercenaries and backed by the USA and Belgium**, crushed all resistance and took over the government himself.

⚠️ Note the United Nations record here honestly, because it cuts both ways and an answer gains from saying so. ⚠️ The organisation did end the secession, which is a real achievement and the largest peace-enforcement operation of the period. ⚠️ It also refused to act for over a year while the elected prime minister who had requested it was removed and killed, and it withdrew within months of success, after which the disorder resumed. ⚠️ Institutional capacity without political durability produces exactly that pattern.

### 5. Mobutu, and the citizenship law that converted colonial labour into a legal problem

✅ It was probably inevitable that if the Congo, with its many problems - an under-developed economy, ethnic divisions and a shortage of educated people - was to stay united, a strong authoritarian government was required; ✅ Mobutu provided exactly that. ✅ There was a gradual improvement in conditions as the Congolese gained experience of administration, and the economy began to look healthier after most of the European-owned mines were nationalised.

⚠️ Record that positive assessment, because balance requires it and because it identifies the trade-off the case actually presents: unity and administrative recovery were obtained at the price of authoritarian rule, and an answer should state the trade-off rather than pretending it does not exist.

✅ But troubles returned. ✅ In 1977 Katanga, now known as Shaba, was invaded by troops from Angola, apparently encouraged by the Angolan government, which resented Mobutu's earlier intervention in its affairs, and by the USSR, which resented American support for Mobutu; ✅ this was yet another extension of the Cold War. ✅ Zaire, as the country had been called since 1971, then ran into economic difficulties, mainly because of declining world copper prices and drought which made expensive food imports necessary. ✅ Mobutu came under increasing criticism outside Zaire for his authoritarian style of government and his huge personal fortune, ✅ and in May 1980 **Amnesty International** claimed that at least a thousand political prisoners were being held without trial and that several hundred had died from torture or starvation during 1978-9.

⚠️ Attribute that figure to Amnesty International and to the years it covers, as the account does.

✅ An important new measure, the **Nationality Law**, was introduced in **1981**. ✅ This restricted citizenship in Zaire to people who could demonstrate a family connection with the Congo **at the time of the Berlin Conference of 1885**. ✅ It aimed to deal with a problem dating back to the colonial era, when tens of thousands of migrant workers had moved into the Congo from neighbouring territories; ✅ the problem was exacerbated later by an influx of refugees from Uganda, Rwanda and Burundi. ✅ There was tension between the indigenous population and the immigrants, and the Nationality Law was passed in response to pressure from the indigenous Congolese. ✅ However, it was difficult to implement, and conflict between the two continued.

⚠️ This law deserves a paragraph in an answer on the legacies of colonialism, because it is an extraordinarily precise illustration of the argument. ⚠️ A post-colonial state defined its own citizenship by reference to the conference at which European powers had partitioned Africa. ⚠️ The colonial frontier of 1885 became the legal test of belonging in 1981, applied against populations that colonial labour recruitment had itself moved. ⚠️ Two colonial acts - drawing the border and moving the workers - were thereby converted into a twentieth-century citizenship conflict, and neither the border nor the movement had been chosen by anybody in the Congo.

✅ The resolution eventually chosen was different: in 2005 citizenship was awarded to everybody descended from ethnic groups present in the country at the time of independence in **1960**. ⚠️ Moving the qualifying date from 1885 to 1960 is a small constitutional act with a large meaning - the replacement of the partition as the foundational moment by independence.

⚠️ For completeness, and to mark the boundary: ✅ Mobutu allowed a multi-party system in 1990 with himself above politics as head of state and remained in power until **May 1997**, when he left the country and died later that year in exile in Morocco; ✅ **Laurent Kabila** became president and changed the country's name from Zaire to the **Democratic Republic of the Congo**. ⚠️ The wars that followed from August 1998, in which forces from six countries were involved, belong to the post-Cold-War order rather than to decolonisation, and this session records only the reported scale - ✅ that by the time Laurent Kabila was assassinated in January 2001, it was reported that almost three million people had lost their lives since the civil war began, most of them from starvation and disease in the rebel area in the east.

### 6. Ruanda-Urundi: a hierarchy transferred intact

✅ The other Belgian territory, Ruanda-Urundi, was given independence in **1962** and divided into two states - **Rwanda and Burundi** - **both governed by members of the Tutsi, as they had been throughout the colonial period**. ✅ Neither of the states had been properly prepared, and after independence both had a very unsettled history of bitter rivalry and violence between the Tutsi and the Hutu.

⚠️ That short passage contains the most important structural claim available about this case, and it is easy to miss. ⚠️ Belgium had used the Tutsi to help control the Hutu; at independence, both successor states were governed by members of the group Belgium had used. ⚠️ The colonial hierarchy was not dismantled at decolonisation; it was transferred intact into two sovereign states and thereby made permanent, with the difference that there was no longer an external power above it to arbitrate.

⚠️ And the account's own description of the two groups defeats any primordial explanation in advance. ✅ In both states there was an explosive mixture of two groups - the Tutsi and the Hutu - who **spoke the same language and looked very much alike**, and although the Hutu were in a majority, the Tutsi were the elite ruling group, and they followed different occupations: the Tutsi raised cattle - the word Tutsi means "rich in cattle" - whereas the Hutu were mainly farmers growing bananas and other crops, the word Hutu meaning "servant". ✅ There was continuous tension and skirmishing between the two right from independence day in 1962.

⚠️ Read that carefully. ⚠️ Same language, similar appearance, different occupations, a majority and an elite, and names that mean "rich in cattle" and "servant". ⚠️ That is the description of a **social and occupational hierarchy**, not of two nations. ⚠️ A hierarchy of this kind becomes a political identity when control of a state is the prize and when a colonial administration has spent decades using one side of it as its instrument.

> **Forward reference.** ⚠️ The killings in Burundi from October 1993 and the Rwandan genocide of April 1994 are taught in Lesson 23 as one of six post-independence mechanisms, with the attribution discipline applied in full. Do not narrate them here.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ The Congo is the account's standard example of collapse into state crisis immediately after independence, and it is the strongest available case for the proposition that post-colonial instability was a colonial product.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ As the unprepared half of a preparation comparison, as evidence in a question on post-independence African instability, or inside a question on the Cold War and the new states. ⚠️ The decisive move is to present the absence of a successor class as *achieved policy* rather than as neglect, which converts the case from an illustration of chaos into an argument about causation.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Writing that Belgium failed to prepare the Congo. ⚠️ Belgium had a stated policy of denying advanced education precisely in order to prevent an educated professional class who could lead the country to independence. The outcome was the policy working.
2. ⚠️ Explaining the crisis by Congolese incapacity. ✅ Seventeen graduates, no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers, a main party less than a year old and over fifty groups competing are colonial products, not national characteristics.
3. ⚠️ Treating Katanga's secession as spontaneous. ✅ It was encouraged by the Belgian copper-mining industry, and many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga they could influence more easily.
4. ⚠️ Reversing the Lumumba sequence. ✅ The appeal to the Russians followed the UN's refusal to act against Katanga; the charge of communism preceded it and rested on his outspoken socialism.
5. ❌ Extending or blurring the killing attribution. ✅ He and two former ministers were badly beaten and then shot in January 1961 by Katangan gendarmes under Belgian officers.
6. ⚠️ Treating Rwanda and Burundi's divisions as ancient. ✅ The two groups spoke the same language, looked very much alike and were distinguished by occupation, with a Tutsi elite the Belgians had used to control the Hutu majority - and both successor states were handed to that elite in 1962.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Belgian control of the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi ended in chaos, violence and civil war.
2. ✅ Method one: denying Africans any advanced education, to prevent contact with nationalist ideas and deprive them of an educated professional class who could lead them to independence.
3. ✅ Method two: using ethnic rivalries by playing groups off against each other; this worked well in the huge Congo with about 150 groups, men of one group being used to keep order in another's area; in Ruanda-Urundi the Tutsi were used to control the Hutu.
4. ✅ Nationalist ideas still filtered in from neighbouring French and British colonies.
5. ✅ January 1959: widespread rioting in Leopoldville against unemployment and declining living standards, spreading throughout the country.
6. ✅ The Belgians suddenly announced the Congo could become independent in six months.
7. ✅ There were only seventeen graduates in the entire country and no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers; the MNC under Lumumba had existed less than a year; over fifty groups competed; six months was far too short.
8. ✅ The two reasons: fear of further bloodshed with over 100,000 Belgians in the country at risk, and unwillingness to face the expense of a long anti-guerrilla campaign like Algeria's.
9. ✅ Independence 30 June 1960, Lumumba prime minister, Kasavubu president; within days the army mutinied against having only Belgian officers, and Katanga seceded under Tshombe, encouraged by the Belgian copper-mining industry.
10. ✅ Lumumba appealed to the UN, which sent a 3,000-strong force; Hammarskjold refused both to attack Katanga and to recognise its independence; the Americans regarded Lumumba as a dangerous communist because of his outspoken socialism; many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga they could influence.
11. ✅ Lumumba appealed to the Russians; Kasavubu, supported by Mobutu and encouraged by the Americans and Belgians, had him arrested; he and two former ministers were badly beaten and then shot in January 1961 by Katangan gendarmes under Belgian officers.
12. ✅ Hammarskjold was killed in an air crash near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia, in September 1961 while flying to meet Tshombe; U Thant continued the same line; by mid-1961 there were 20,000 UN troops; Katanga was invaded in September 1961 and the secession was formally ended on 15 January 1963; UN troops withdrew within months and disorder resumed.
13. ✅ In 1965 Mobutu, using white mercenaries and backed by the USA and Belgium, crushed all resistance and took over; the economy improved after most European-owned mines were nationalised.
14. ✅ 1977: Shaba invaded from Angola, encouraged by the Angolan government and the USSR - another extension of the Cold War; economic difficulties followed from declining copper prices and drought.
15. ✅ May 1980: Amnesty International claimed at least a thousand political prisoners held without trial and several hundred dead from torture or starvation during 1978-9.
16. ✅ The 1981 Nationality Law restricted citizenship to those who could show a family connection with the Congo at the time of the Berlin Conference of 1885, aimed at colonial-era migrant workers and later refugees; it was difficult to implement. ✅ In 2005 citizenship was awarded to all descended from groups present at independence in 1960.
17. ✅ Mobutu allowed a multi-party system in 1990 and left the country in May 1997, dying in exile in Morocco; Laurent Kabila renamed the country the Democratic Republic of the Congo; by January 2001 it was reported that almost three million people had lost their lives since the 1998 civil war began, most from starvation and disease in the rebel-held east.
18. ✅ Ruanda-Urundi became independent in 1962 as Rwanda and Burundi, both governed by Tutsi as throughout the colonial period; neither was properly prepared; tension and skirmishing began on independence day.
19. ✅ Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language and looked very much alike, the Hutu a majority and the Tutsi the elite ruling group, distinguished by occupation - "rich in cattle" against "servant".
20. ⚠️ Master formulation: the Congo's crisis was the intended consequence of a stated colonial policy of preventing a successor class, and Ruanda-Urundi's was the transfer of a colonial hierarchy into two sovereign states with no external arbiter left above it.

### Concept check

**Question:** The Congo is routinely cited as the case where a colonial power failed to prepare its territory for independence. Explain why "failed" is the wrong verb, and show how the same reasoning applies to Rwanda and Burundi.

**Model answer:** "Failed" is wrong because the absence of a successor class was the achieved object of a stated policy rather than an omission. The Belgians thought the best way to preserve their control was to deny Africans any advanced education, precisely in order to prevent them from coming into contact with nationalist ideas and to deprive them of an educated professional class who could lead them to independence. The result - only seventeen graduates in the entire country, and no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or officers in the army - was therefore the policy working as designed, and it delivered its consequences immediately: the army mutinied within days of independence on 30 June 1960 against the fact that all its officers were Belgians. The second Belgian method has the same character. Ethnic rivalries were used deliberately, with men from one group employed to keep order in another's area in a territory containing about a hundred and fifty groups, and in Ruanda-Urundi the Tutsi were used to help control the Hutu majority. When Ruanda-Urundi became independent in 1962 as Rwanda and Burundi, both states were governed by members of the Tutsi, as they had been throughout the colonial period - so the colonial hierarchy was not dismantled but transferred intact into two sovereign states, with no external power left above it to arbitrate, and tension and skirmishing began on independence day. In both territories the post-colonial crisis is the direct output of an identifiable colonial policy, which is why the causal claim is stronger here than in any other case in this topic.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading Lumumba's appeal to the Soviet Union as the cause of western hostility to him. The sequence runs the other way. He was already unpopular with the Americans and British because of his outspoken socialism and was regarded by the Americans in particular as a dangerous communist; he appealed to the United Nations first, and only turned to the Russians in disgust after Hammarskjold refused to allow a UN attack on Katanga - a refusal shaped in part by Belgian preference for an independent Katanga that would be easier to influence and would preserve their control of the copper mining. The appeal that was cited to justify his removal was produced by the refusal that preceded it.

## Lesson 20 - Portugal, Spain, Italy and the unfinished cases

Progress: 20/24 | Stage: Core | Subtopic: the Salazar regime's refusal and the three simultaneous wars; the cost that broke the metropole in 1974; independence for Guinea-Bissau, Mozambique and Angola; the immediate regional crisis this produced; East Timor as decolonisation reversed by invasion; Spanish Sahara as decolonisation never completed; the Italian territories disposed of by the victors; and where the Middle Eastern mandates hand over

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
