━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: three ways a decolonisation can fail to be a decolonisation

```text
   CASE A: LATE COLLAPSE - PORTUGUESE AFRICA
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   The metropole refuses for so long that the pressure destroys the
   METROPOLITAN REGIME before it releases the colonies.
       1956  only FIFTY AFRICANS in the whole of Mozambique had
             received any secondary education; nationalist groups
             formed but "remained insignificant"
       1961  fighting breaks out in ANGOLA (Neto's MPLA)
             -> spreads to GUINEA (Cabral) and MOZAMBIQUE
                (Mondlane's FRELIMO)
       1973  the government is spending FORTY PER CENT OF ITS BUDGET
             fighting THREE COLONIAL WARS AT ONCE
       1974  the SALAZAR DICTATORSHIP IS OVERTHROWN by a military coup
       1974  GUINEA-BISSAU independent (September)
       1975  MOZAMBIQUE and ANGOLA independent
   => the colonies outlasted the regime that held them.

   CASE B: REVERSAL - EAST TIMOR
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   Decolonisation happens and is then UNDONE by a neighbour.
       Sept 1975  FRETILIN wins a short civil war against the group
                  that wanted to stay with Portugal
       "after only a few weeks, Indonesian troops invaded, overthrew
        the government and incorporated East Timor into Indonesia"
       1999  UN-organised referendum: ALMOST 80 PER CENT for complete
             independence; militia violence follows
       2002  international recognition "after a struggle lasting more
             than a quarter of a century"

   CASE C: NEVER COMPLETED - SPANISH SAHARA
   ---------------------------------------------------------------
   The metropole leaves and the territory is DIVIDED BETWEEN
   NEIGHBOURS instead of becoming independent.
       1975  Franco dies; Spain agrees to release Sahara - but
             "instead of making it into an independent state ruled by
             its nationalist party, the POLISARIO FRONT, it was
             decided to DIVIDE IT between MOROCCO and MAURETANIA"
       1976  Polisario declares the Democratic Arab Republic of Sahara
       1979  Mauretania withdraws
       1990  the UN proposes a REFERENDUM; ceasefire from 6 Sep 1991;
             "the referendum was NEVER HELD"
   => and it is still on the United Nations list (Lesson 6).
```

⚠️ The three cases are arranged so that they answer one question between them: what exactly has to happen for a decolonisation to count as completed? Case A shows it happening late, Case B shows it happening and being undone, and Case C shows it not happening at all.

### 1. Portugal: why an authoritarian metropole cannot concede

✅ The main Portuguese possessions were in Africa: the two large areas of **Angola** and **Mozambique**, and the small West African colony of **Portuguese Guinea**. ✅ They also still owned the eastern half of the island of **Timor** in the East Indies.

✅ The right-wing Portuguese government of Dr **Salazar** blithely ignored nationalist developments in the rest of Africa, and for many years after 1945 the Portuguese colonies seemed quiet and resigned to their position. ✅ They were mainly agricultural; there were few industrial workers and the black populations were almost entirely illiterate. ✅ In **1956** there were only **fifty Africans in the whole of Mozambique** who had received any secondary education. ✅ Though nationalist groups were formed in all three colonies in 1956, they remained insignificant.

⚠️ That education figure belongs beside the Congo's seventeen graduates, and the pair should be quoted together whenever the argument about successor classes is made. ⚠️ Fifty people with secondary education in a whole territory is the most extreme number in this session, and it indicates a colonial system that had made the emergence of an organised nationalist leadership almost impossible.

⚠️ It also explains the delay. ⚠️ Portuguese Africa did not move in the 1950s because the material conditions for movement had been suppressed more thoroughly than anywhere else in this material. ⚠️ When it did move, it moved as armed insurgency rather than as constitutional politics, which is what happens when a colonial state has closed every other channel.

✅ Several factors changed the situation. ✅ By 1960 the nationalists were greatly encouraged by the large number of other African states winning independence. ✅ The Salazar regime, having learned nothing from the experiences of the other colonial powers, stepped up its repressive policies, but this only made the nationalists more resolute. ✅ Fighting broke out first in **Angola in 1961**, where **Agostinho Neto's MPLA** - the People's Movement for Angolan Liberation - was the main nationalist movement; ✅ violence soon spread to **Guinea**, where **Amilcar Cabral** led the resistance, and to **Mozambique**, where the **FRELIMO** guerrillas were organised by **Eduardo Mondlane**. ✅ The nationalists, who all had strong Marxist connections, received economic and military aid from the communist bloc.

⚠️ Note the last sentence carefully, because it explains a great deal about what happened after independence. ⚠️ The only external support available to movements fighting a NATO member's colonial wars came from the communist bloc, which meant that the future governments of Angola and Mozambique acquired Marxist alignments partly through the logistics of their liberation struggles. ⚠️ Those alignments then attracted the hostility documented in Lesson 6 and the invasions documented below. ⚠️ **The alignment of a liberation movement was frequently determined by who was willing to arm it, and that alignment then determined how it was treated after victory.**

✅ The Portuguese army found it impossible to suppress the nationalist guerrillas; the troops became demoralised and the cost escalated until **by 1973 the government was spending forty per cent of its budget fighting three colonial wars at once**. ✅ Still the Portuguese government refused to abandon its policy; but public opinion and many army officers were sick of the wars, and on **25 April 1974** the dictatorship - by then headed by **Marcello Caetano**, who had replaced the incapacitated Salazar in 1968 - was overthrown by a military coup. ⚠️ The textbook account calls it "the Salazar dictatorship"; the regime that fell was Salazar's Estado Novo under Caetano, Salazar himself having died in 1970, and an answer should say so. ✅ Soon all three colonies were granted independence: **Guinea-Bissau in September 1974**, and **Mozambique and Angola** the following year.

⚠️ This is the completed version of the mechanism named in Lesson 8. ⚠️ An authoritarian metropole has no election to lose, no opposition to take office and no press to force a reconsideration, so a policy that is failing cannot be changed by the normal means. ⚠️ Pressure therefore accumulates against the regime itself, and the colonies are released in the days after the regime falls. ⚠️ Forty per cent of a national budget is the figure that makes this concrete, and it should be quoted.

⚠️ Two further consequences of 1974-75 belong here, because they reach into other lessons.

✅ **The regional crisis.** This caused a serious crisis for Rhodesia and South Africa: they were now the only states left in Africa ruled by white minorities, and their governments felt increasingly threatened. ⚠️ Lesson 13 showed Mozambican independence breaking the Rhodesian sanctions position and supplying guerrilla bases; Lesson 22 shows the same event, together with the African takeover of Zimbabwe in 1980, leaving South Africa surrounded by hostile black states. ⚠️ **Portuguese decolonisation is the proximate cause of the ending of white minority rule in the two remaining settler states.**

✅ **The immediate invasion of Angola.** Now it was the turn of Angola to become a victim of outside interference and the Cold War. ✅ South African troops immediately invaded the country in support of **UNITA**, while ✅ General Mobutu of Zaire, with American backing, launched another invasion in support of the **FNLA**. ✅ The Americans thought that a joint Angolan government of these two groups would be more amenable and open to western influence than the Marxist MPLA. ✅ The MPLA received aid in the form of Russian weapons and a **Cuban army**; this enabled them to defeat both invasion forces by **March 1976**, and Neto was accepted as president of the new state. ✅ This proved to be only a temporary respite - further invasions followed and Angola was torn by civil war right through into the 1990s. ✅ The South Africans also interfered in Mozambique, sending raiding parties over the border and doing their best to destabilise the FRELIMO government.

> **Forward reference.** ⚠️ The Angolan civil war, the Namibia linkage and the settlement are taught in Lesson 23 as one of the six post-independence mechanisms. Here, note only that independence and invasion occurred in the same year.

### 2. East Timor: decolonisation reversed

✅ East Timor was half of a small island in the East Indies; the western half had belonged to the Netherlands and became part of Indonesia in 1949. ✅ East Timor's nationalist movement, **FRETILIN**, won a short civil war against the ruling group, which wanted to stay with Portugal, in **September 1975**. ✅ The USA denounced the new government as Marxist, **which was not entirely accurate**; ✅ after only a few weeks, Indonesian troops invaded, overthrew the government and incorporated East Timor into Indonesia.

✅ The USA continued to supply military goods to the Indonesians, who were guilty of appalling atrocities both during and after the war.

⚠️ **The mortality evidence here diverges inside the same account and must be reported as such.** ✅ In one place it is stated that it is estimated that about **one hundred thousand** people were killed, one-sixth of the population, while another **three hundred thousand** were put into detention camps. ✅ In another place it is stated that there was a long campaign of terror in which around **two hundred thousand** people were killed out of a total population of only **seven hundred thousand**.

⚠️ Apply the attribution discipline exactly. ❌ Do not select one figure. ❌ Do not average them. ❌ Do not present them as a range. ⚠️ Report both with the observation that a single account gives two different figures for the same episode, which is itself evidence about the state of the record for a territory the world had chosen not to examine. ⚠️ This is the clearest illustration in the session of why the discipline exists.

✅ The explanation the account gives for the international response is explicit and belongs in an answer on decolonisation and the Cold War. ✅ FRETILIN was thought to be Marxist, so that an independent East Timor might have socialist or even communist leanings; ✅ consequently US President Gerald Ford gave Suharto the go-ahead. ✅ FRETILIN continued to campaign for independence, but although the UN and the EU condemned Indonesia's action, East Timor was apparently too small and too unimportant, and the nationalists too left-wing, to warrant any sanctions being applied against Indonesia by the West; ✅ the USA consistently defended Indonesia's claim to East Timor and played down the violence.

⚠️ That is a very precise statement of how the international system actually treated the right of self-determination in this period, and it should be set against the 1960 Declaration from Lesson 6. ⚠️ The Declaration had been adopted without a single expressed dissent; fifteen years later a territory that had exercised that right had it annulled by invasion, with the acquiescence of the power that had done most to promote the Declaration. ⚠️ A candidate who can state that contrast has demonstrated exactly the critical reading that a twenty-mark question rewards.

✅ The turn came slowly. ✅ In **November 1991** two hundred and seventy-one people were killed in Dili, the capital, when Indonesian troops attacked a pro-independence demonstration; ✅ this helped to focus international attention on the campaign against Indonesian abuses of human rights and against US and UK arms sales to Indonesia. ✅ In **1996** the Roman Catholic Bishop of Dili, **Carlos Belo**, and the exiled FRETILIN spokesman **Jose Ramos-Horta** were jointly awarded the **Nobel Peace Prize**, in recognition of their long, non-violent campaign for independence. ✅ By **1999**, with international support mounting and the Cold War long since over, Indonesia at last began to give way and offered a referendum on special autonomy; ✅ organised by the UN in **August 1999**, it resulted in an almost **eighty per cent** vote for complete independence. ✅ The pro-Indonesian minority did their best to sabotage the elections, their militia, backed by Indonesian troops, intimidating voters; ✅ after the result was announced they ran wild in a furious outburst of revenge and destruction, killing **two thousand** and leaving **two hundred and fifty thousand** homeless. ✅ Violence was ended only by the arrival of a large Australian peacekeeping force. ✅ In **August 2001** elections for a Constituent Assembly were much calmer; FRETILIN won by a large majority and its leader **Xanana Gusmao** was elected the first president. ✅ In **May 2002** East Timor received international recognition as an independent state **after a struggle lasting more than a quarter of a century**.

⚠️ Two findings from this sequence deserve to be stated as propositions.

⚠️ **The Cold War's end was a necessary condition.** ✅ The account itself connects the 1999 concession to the Cold War being long since over. ⚠️ The Marxist label that had justified the invasion in 1975 had lost its power to justify anything by 1999, and the territory's position changed accordingly. ⚠️ This is the clearest case in the session of a decolonisation whose timing was set by the international system rather than by anything local.

⚠️ **Size determined treatment.** ✅ East Timor was apparently too small and too unimportant to warrant sanctions. ⚠️ Set that beside the international mobilisation against apartheid in Lesson 22 and the variable is not the gravity of the conduct but the significance of the state committing it and its alignment.

### 3. Spain: an exit that created a war instead of a state

✅ Spain owned some areas in Africa: the largest was **Spanish Sahara**, and there were also the small colonies of Spanish Morocco, Ifni and Spanish Guinea. ✅ General **Franco**, the right-wing dictator who ruled Spain from 1939 until 1975, showed little interest in the colonies. ✅ When nationalist movements developed, he did not resist long in the case of Spanish Morocco: when the French gave independence to French Morocco in 1956, Franco followed suit and Spanish Morocco became part of Morocco. ✅ Ifni was allowed to join Morocco, but not until **1969**; ✅ Guinea became independent as **Equatorial Guinea in 1968**.

✅ In Spanish Sahara, Franco resisted even longer, because the country was a valuable source of **phosphates**. ✅ Only after Franco's death in **1975** did the new Spanish government agree to release Sahara. ✅ Unfortunately the process was badly bungled: instead of making it into an independent state ruled by its nationalist party, the **Polisario Front**, it was decided to divide it between its two neighbouring states, **Morocco and Mauretania**.

⚠️ Stop here, because this is a distinct category of outcome and the session's typology needs it. ⚠️ Every other case in Lessons 9 to 19 ends with a sovereign successor state, however troubled. ⚠️ This one ends with the territory partitioned between neighbours. ⚠️ Decolonisation was not completed; the administering power was simply replaced.

✅ The Polisario Front, under its leader **Mohamed Abdelaziz**, declared the **Democratic Arab Republic of Sahara in 1976**, which was recognised by Algeria, Libya, the communist states and **India**. ✅ Algeria and Libya sent help, and in **1979** Mauretania decided to withdraw, making it easier for Sahara to struggle on against Morocco.

✅ However, the fact that Sahara had been officially recognised by the USSR was enough to arouse American suspicions. ✅ Just when it seemed that the Moroccans too were prepared to negotiate peace, the new American president, **Ronald Reagan**, encouraged them to continue the fight, stepping up aid to Morocco. ✅ The war dragged on through the 1980s; ✅ **yet another new Third World country had become a victim of superpower self-interest**.

⚠️ That verdict is the account's own and it should be quoted, because it states the general finding of this section in the account's own voice. ⚠️ Note precisely what the mechanism was: recognition by one superpower converted a colonial question into a Cold War question, and the other superpower then subsidised its continuation *at the moment a settlement had become possible*.

✅ In **1990** the UN proposed that a referendum should be held so that the people of Sahara could choose whether to be independent or become part of Morocco; ✅ a ceasefire took effect on **6 September 1991**, **but the referendum was never held**. ✅ During the 1990s the Polisario forces grew weaker as support was withdrawn by Algeria and Libya, mainly because they were preoccupied with their own problems; ✅ Sahara remained under Moroccan control, large numbers of Moroccan settlers began to move in, and many Saharans, including Polisario fighters, moved out of the country and were forced to live in refugee camps in Algeria.

⚠️ This is the clearest case in the session of a decolonisation that was never completed rather than one that ended badly: the administering power withdrew, no act of self-determination followed, and the territory passed to a neighbour. ⚠️ That the United Nations still carries a caseload of unfinished decolonisation is the institutional point recorded in Lesson 6; ❌ no current status for this particular territory is asserted here, because none was verified for this session.

### 4. Italy: the empire that was taken rather than given up

✅ It was officially decided in **1947** that the Italians, having supported Hitler and suffered defeat in the Second World War, must lose their overseas empire. ✅ Their African possessions were to be administered by France and Britain until the UN decided what to do with them, ✅ and the UN followed a policy of placing the territories under governments which would be sympathetic to western interests.

✅ **Ethiopia** had already been liberated in **1941**, and the Emperor **Haile Selassie**, forced into exile when the Italians invaded in 1935, was restored then; ✅ what the settlement of **1947** did was to record Italy's formal **renunciation of its claims**. ⚠️ The textbook account implies an Ethiopian restoration in 1947; the correct date for the restoration is 1941. ✅ **Libya** was given independence under **King Idris in 1951**. ✅ **Eritrea** was made part of Ethiopia in **1952**, though it was to have a large measure of self-government within a federal system. ✅ **Italian Somaliland** was merged with British Somaliland to form the independent state of **Somalia in 1960**.

⚠️ Italy is the control case for the whole causation argument of Lesson 3, and it is worth one sentence in any answer on why empires ended. ⚠️ Here the empire ended with no nationalist pressure and no metropolitan reconsideration: it was removed by the decision of the victorious powers, and the successor arrangements were made by the United Nations according to an explicit criterion - sympathy to western interests.

⚠️ And those arrangements generated their own conflicts. ✅ Some of them did not prove very successful: ✅ both Idris and Selassie became unpopular, Idris because he was thought to be too pro-West and Selassie because he made no attempt to modernise Ethiopia and did little to improve living standards; ✅ Selassie also made the mistake of cancelling **Eritrea's rights of self-government in 1962**, which prompted the Eritreans into launching a war for independence; ✅ Idris was overthrown in **1969** by a socialist revolutionary movement which nationalised the oil industry and began to modernise the country; ✅ and Selassie was overthrown in **1974**. ✅ New leaders emerged - **Colonel Gaddafi** in Libya and **Colonel Mengistu** in Ethiopia - both of whom turned to the USSR for economic aid.

⚠️ The Eritrean sequence is the most instructive and it repeats a pattern already seen twice. ⚠️ A federal guarantee of self-government was given in 1952 and cancelled in 1962, and the cancellation produced a war for independence. ⚠️ Compare Katanga, where the wealthiest province attempted to leave, and Biafra, where a federal region did. ⚠️ **Where a territory's autonomy is guaranteed and then withdrawn, the guarantee becomes the justification for secession.**

### 5. The Middle Eastern boundary, stated once

⚠️ The account's inventory for 1945 included territory in the Middle East, and this session owns the ending of that legal form and nothing beyond it.

✅ Britain and France still held land in the Middle East, taken from Turkey at the end of the First World War: ✅ Britain held Transjordan and Palestine, France held Syria; ✅ they were known as mandated territories, which meant that Britain and France were intended to look after them and prepare them for independence. ✅ **French Syria became independent in 1946**; ✅ **Transjordan, as Jordan, in 1946**; ✅ **the Palestine mandate ended in 1948**; ✅ **Sudan became independent in 1956**; ✅ **Libya in 1951**; and ✅ **Aden, as South Yemen, in 1967**.

> **Boundary.** ⚠️ The creation of Israel, the Arab-Israeli wars, the Suez crisis of 1956, Egyptian politics and the subsequent history of the region are owned by the Middle East material and are not taught, narrated or assessed in this session. ⚠️ What this topic owns is the proposition that the mandate was a distinct legal form carrying an explicit obligation to prepare for independence, that it therefore ended earliest, and that where the obligation was not discharged the consequence was a conflict rather than a transfer.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ These cases supply the syllabus's decolonisation clause with its latest, its reversed and its unfinished examples, and they are the strongest evidence that decolonisation was neither uniform nor complete.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Portuguese Africa appears in questions on why some decolonisations were late or violent; East Timor and Western Sahara appear where a question asks whether decolonisation was completed, or about the role of the superpowers. ⚠️ The strongest structure is the three-case typology: late collapse, reversal, non-completion.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Explaining Portuguese lateness by settler numbers. ⚠️ The operative variable is the authoritarian metropole, which had no mechanism for changing policy; the colonies were released days after the regime fell in 1974.
2. ⚠️ Selecting one East Timor mortality figure. ✅ The same account gives about one hundred thousand killed with three hundred thousand detained in one place, and around two hundred thousand killed out of a population of seven hundred thousand in another. Report both.
3. ⚠️ Treating Western Sahara as decolonised in 1975. ✅ It was divided between Morocco and Mauretania instead of becoming independent; the 1990 referendum was never held.
4. ⚠️ Omitting Italy from a causes answer. ✅ Its empire was removed in 1947 by the decision of the victors, with the UN placing the territories under governments sympathetic to western interests.
5. ❌ Importing Arab-Israeli or Suez material. ⚠️ Only the mandate form and its independence dates are owned here.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Portugal held Angola, Mozambique, Portuguese Guinea and the eastern half of Timor; Salazar's government blithely ignored nationalist developments elsewhere in Africa.
2. ✅ The colonies were mainly agricultural with few industrial workers and almost entirely illiterate black populations; in 1956 only fifty Africans in the whole of Mozambique had received any secondary education, and the nationalist groups formed that year remained insignificant.
3. ✅ By 1960 nationalists were encouraged by other African independences; the regime stepped up repression, making them more resolute.
4. ✅ Fighting broke out in Angola in 1961 under Neto's MPLA, spreading to Guinea under Amilcar Cabral and to Mozambique under Mondlane's FRELIMO; all had strong Marxist connections and received economic and military aid from the communist bloc.
5. ✅ The army could not suppress the guerrillas; by 1973 the government was spending forty per cent of its budget on three colonial wars at once; on 25 April 1974 the Estado Novo, by then led by Caetano rather than Salazar, was overthrown by a military coup.
6. ✅ Guinea-Bissau became independent in September 1974; Mozambique and Angola in 1975.
7. ✅ This caused a serious crisis for Rhodesia and South Africa, now the only states left in Africa ruled by white minorities.
8. ✅ South African troops invaded Angola in support of UNITA and Mobutu invaded with American backing in support of the FNLA; the MPLA, with Russian weapons and a Cuban army, defeated both by March 1976 and Neto was accepted as president; South Africa also raided Mozambique to destabilise FRELIMO.
9. ✅ East Timor: FRETILIN won a short civil war in September 1975 against the group that wanted to stay with Portugal; the USA denounced the new government as Marxist, which was not entirely accurate; Indonesian troops invaded within weeks and incorporated the territory.
10. ✅ Mortality is given twice and divergently: about one hundred thousand killed, one-sixth of the population, with another three hundred thousand in detention camps; and around two hundred thousand killed out of a population of only seven hundred thousand. Report both.
11. ✅ President Ford gave Suharto the go-ahead because FRETILIN was thought Marxist; East Timor was apparently too small and unimportant and its nationalists too left-wing for the West to apply sanctions; the USA defended Indonesia's claim and played down the violence.
12. ✅ November 1991: two hundred and seventy-one killed in Dili. ✅ 1996: Bishop Carlos Belo and Jose Ramos-Horta jointly awarded the Nobel Peace Prize. ✅ August 1999: a UN-organised referendum returned almost eighty per cent for independence; militia backed by Indonesian troops then killed two thousand and left two hundred and fifty thousand homeless; an Australian peacekeeping force ended the violence. ✅ August 2001 Constituent Assembly elections; Xanana Gusmao first president; May 2002 international recognition after a struggle of more than a quarter of a century.
13. ✅ Spain: Franco showed little interest; Spanish Morocco joined Morocco in 1956, Equatorial Guinea became independent in 1968, Ifni joined Morocco in 1969.
14. ✅ Spanish Sahara was held longest because it was a valuable source of phosphates; after Franco's death in 1975 it was divided between Morocco and Mauretania instead of becoming independent under the Polisario Front.
15. ✅ Polisario under Mohamed Abdelaziz declared the Democratic Arab Republic of Sahara in 1976, recognised by Algeria, Libya, the communist states and India; Mauretania withdrew in 1979.
16. ✅ Soviet recognition aroused American suspicions; Reagan encouraged Morocco to continue fighting just as it seemed ready to negotiate, stepping up aid - yet another new Third World country becoming a victim of superpower self-interest.
17. ✅ The UN proposed a referendum in 1990 and a ceasefire took effect on 6 September 1991, but the referendum was never held; Polisario weakened as Algerian and Libyan support was withdrawn; Moroccan settlers moved in and Saharans moved out into refugee camps in Algeria.
18. ✅ Italy lost its empire by decision in 1947; the territories were administered by France and Britain until the UN placed them under governments sympathetic to western interests: Ethiopia already liberated and Haile Selassie restored in 1941 with Italy renouncing its claims in 1947, Libya independent under King Idris in 1951, Eritrea made part of Ethiopia in 1952 with self-government in a federal system, Italian Somaliland merged into Somalia in 1960.
19. ✅ Selassie cancelled Eritrea's rights of self-government in 1962, prompting a war for independence; Idris was overthrown in 1969 and Selassie in 1974; Gaddafi and Mengistu both turned to the USSR.
20. ✅ Middle Eastern mandates: Britain held Transjordan and Palestine, France held Syria, both intended to prepare them for independence; Syria and Transjordan 1946, the Palestine mandate ended 1948, Libya 1951, Sudan 1956, Aden as South Yemen 1967. Everything beyond that is owned elsewhere.
21. ⚠️ Master formulation: three failure modes - the metropole that could not concede until its own regime fell; the decolonisation reversed by a neighbour with great-power permission; and the decolonisation replaced by partition and never completed.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Portugal held its African colonies for nearly twenty years after most of British and French Africa had become independent, and why they were then released within a few months of one another.

**Model answer:** Because an authoritarian metropole has no institutional route by which a failing colonial policy can become a former policy. Salazar's right-wing government blithely ignored nationalist developments elsewhere in Africa, and when fighting broke out in Angola in 1961 under Neto's MPLA and spread to Guinea under Cabral and to Mozambique under Mondlane's FRELIMO, the regime responded by stepping up repression, which only made the nationalists more resolute. There was no election to lose, no opposition party to take office and no free press to force reconsideration, so the pressure accumulated against the regime itself: the army could not suppress the guerrillas, troops became demoralised, and by 1973 the government was spending forty per cent of its budget fighting three colonial wars at once. The release therefore came as a consequence of regime change rather than policy change - public opinion and many army officers were sick of the wars, the Estado Novo under Caetano was overthrown by a military coup on 25 April 1974, and Guinea-Bissau became independent in September 1974 with Mozambique and Angola following in 1975. The preceding decades also explain why the movements were armed rather than constitutional: in 1956 only fifty Africans in the whole of Mozambique had received any secondary education, so the channels through which a negotiating leadership might have emerged had been closed, and the only external supporters of movements fighting a NATO member were in the communist bloc, which shaped the alignments of the successor governments and the invasions that followed.

**Misconception to avoid:** Selecting a single mortality figure for East Timor. The same account gives two: in one place about one hundred thousand people killed, one-sixth of the population, with another three hundred thousand placed in detention camps, and in another place around two hundred thousand killed out of a total population of only seven hundred thousand. They must not be averaged, combined or presented as a range. The divergence should be reported, because it is itself evidence about the quality of the record for a territory that, as the account says, was apparently too small and too unimportant, and whose nationalists were too left-wing, to warrant any western sanctions against the occupying power.

## Lesson 21 - Apartheid I: building a racial order, 1910-1976

### Visual: what apartheid inherited, what it added, and what it could never do

```text
   INHERITED FROM THE UNION OF 1910
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   POPULATION SHARES, as recorded:
        ~70%  black Africans        ~18%  whites (of these about 60%
        ~9%   "coloureds"                 Dutch, the rest British)
        ~3%   Asians
   * "The whites dominated politics and the economic life of the new
      state, and, with only a few exceptions, blacks were not allowed
      to vote."
   * RESERVED AREAS: "only about 7 PER CENT of the total area of
      South Africa", too small to grow enough food or pay taxes;
      blacks FORBIDDEN TO BUY LAND OUTSIDE the reserves
   * PASS LAWS controlling movement and employment
   * COMPOUNDS: in gold mining, "single-sex compounds with sometimes
      as many as 90 MEN SHARING A DORMITORY"
   * A LAW OF 1911: black workers FORBIDDEN TO STRIKE and BARRED FROM
      HOLDING SKILLED JOBS

   ADDED BY APARTHEID FROM 1948
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   [1] Complete separation at every level: reserves, townships,
       communities "uprooted and re-grouped", separate buses,
       coaches, trains, cafes, toilets, park benches, hospitals,
       beaches, picnic areas, sports and even churches; separate
       schools with "a much inferior education"
   [2] RACIAL CLASSIFICATION and an identity card for every person;
       strict pass laws; all travelling forbidden without police
       permission
   [3] MARRIAGE AND SEXUAL RELATIONS between whites and non-whites
       FORBIDDEN, with police surveillance
   [4] BANTU SELF-GOVERNMENT ACT 1959: seven "Bantustans"; Transkei
       given INTERNAL SELF-GOVERNMENT 1963 and declared "independent"
       on 26 OCTOBER 1976; Bophuthatswana and Venda by 1980;
       the areas covered about 13 PER CENT of the country for OVER
       8 MILLION people - "vastly overcrowded", "very little better
       than rural slums"; "the outside world dismissed this with
       contempt"
   [5] Africans LOST ALL POLITICAL RIGHTS; their parliamentary
       representation, held by white MPs, was ABOLISHED

   THE FLAW THE SYSTEM COULD NOT REMOVE
   -----------------------------------------------------------------
   "complete separation was IMPOSSIBLE because OVER HALF THE
    NON-WHITE POPULATION WORKED IN WHITE-OWNED MINES, FACTORIES AND
    OTHER BUSINESSES. THE ECONOMY WOULD HAVE COLLAPSED if all
    non-whites had been moved to reserves."
    -> plus: "virtually every white household had at least two
       African servants."
   => the system's stated principle and its economic foundation were
      in direct contradiction from the first day.
```

⚠️ The bottom box is the key to everything in this lesson and the next. Apartheid was not merely unjust; it was internally incoherent, because it demanded the removal of the people it depended on. Every subsequent development - the Bantustans, the reform attempts of the 1980s, the final negotiation - is an attempt to manage that contradiction, and the failure to manage it is why the system ended.

### 1. Why apartheid belongs in a decolonisation topic

⚠️ Begin with the question a candidate ought to ask: why is South Africa taught here at all, when no European power withdrew from it?

⚠️ The answer is in the record and it is the single most transferable point in the case. ✅ **When India and Pakistan were given independence in 1947, white South Africans became alarmed at the growing racial equality within the Commonwealth, and they were determined to preserve their supremacy.** ✅ The Afrikaner Nationalist Party under **Dr Malan** won the **1948** elections with promises to rescue the whites from the "black menace" and to preserve the racial purity of the whites, which would help to ensure continued white supremacy.

⚠️ So apartheid was a *reaction to decolonisation elsewhere*. ⚠️ Its formative moment was the demonstration effect of Asian independence, arriving in a state whose settler population had already won power and could therefore legislate against the process rather than negotiate with it. ⚠️ This is the sixth route from Lesson 8 - the state that never left - and it is the answer to the question of what colonialism looks like when the colonisers cannot be asked to go home.

⚠️ Two further reasons complete the case for teaching it here.

⚠️ **It is the fullest available example of the colonial relation made explicit in law.** ⚠️ Elsewhere in this session, racial hierarchy operated through practice, land allocation, franchise design and the colour bar. ⚠️ In South Africa it was written down: classification, pass laws, land reservation, marriage prohibition, separate inferior schooling. ⚠️ A candidate who wants to describe what colonial rule actually did to people has the statutory version here.

⚠️ **Its ending is the topic's most instructive negotiation.** ⚠️ Lesson 22 covers a transition to majority rule without civil war, achieved between two sides that had concluded the alternative was worse. ⚠️ Nothing comparable exists in any other case in this session.

### 2. What the Union of 1910 had already built

✅ The first Europeans to settle permanently were members of the Dutch East India Company, who founded a colony at the Cape of Good Hope in **1652**; ✅ it remained a Dutch colony until 1795, and during that time the Dutch - known as **Afrikaners** or **Boers**, meaning farmers - took land away from the native Africans and forced them to work as labourers, treating them as little better than slaves, ✅ and also brought more labourers in from Asia, Mozambique and Madagascar. ✅ In 1795 the Cape was captured by the British and the 1814 peace settlement decided it should remain British. ✅ The Dutch settlers became restless under British rule, especially when the British freed all slaves throughout the Empire in **1838**; ✅ many left Cape Colony in the Great Trek and set up the independent republics of the **Transvaal and Orange Free State (1835-40)**, while some moved into Natal. ✅ In the **Boer War (1899-1902)** the British defeated the Transvaal and the Orange Free State, and in **1910** these joined with Cape Colony and Natal to form the **Union of South Africa**.

✅ The population of the new state was mixed: approximately seventy per cent black Africans; eighteen per cent whites of European origin, of whom about sixty per cent were Dutch and the rest British; nine per cent of mixed race; and three per cent Asians.

⚠️ Note what those figures mean politically. ⚠️ The dominant group was itself divided between two European nationalities with a recent war between them, and the larger of the two - the Afrikaners - had lost that war. ⚠️ Afrikaner nationalism therefore had a grievance against the British as well as a supremacist programme against Africans, and that combination explains both the intensity of the 1948 project and its later willingness to leave the Commonwealth.

✅ Although they made up the vast majority of the population, black Africans suffered even worse discrimination than black people in the USA. ✅ The whites dominated politics and the economic life of the new state, and, with only a few exceptions, blacks were not allowed to vote. ✅ Black people had to do most of the manual work in factories, in the gold mines and on farms; the men mostly lived in barracks accommodation away from their wives and children. ✅ Black people generally were expected to live in areas reserved for them away from white residential areas; ✅ these reserved areas made up only about **seven per cent** of the total area of South Africa and were not large enough to enable Africans to produce sufficient food for themselves and to pay all their taxes; ✅ black Africans were forbidden to buy land outside the reserves. ✅ The government controlled the movement of blacks by a system of **pass laws**: a black person could not live in a town unless he had a pass showing he was working in a white-owned business, and an African could not leave the farm where he worked without a pass from his employer, nor get a new job unless his previous employer signed him out officially - ✅ so many workers were forced to stay in difficult working conditions, even under abusive employers. ✅ Living and working conditions were primitive; in gold mining, Africans had to live in single-sex compounds with sometimes as many as **ninety men sharing a dormitory**. ✅ By a law of **1911**, black workers were forbidden to strike and were barred from holding skilled jobs.

⚠️ Three of those provisions repay attention because they are labour law rather than racial law in form, and their combination is what made the system economically functional.

⚠️ **Seven per cent of the land, insufficient to feed or to pay taxes.** ⚠️ This is not simply dispossession; it is a mechanism for compelling wage labour. A reserve too small to support its population obliges its men to seek work in the white economy, which is exactly what the mines required.

⚠️ **The pass tied to a named employer.** ⚠️ A worker who cannot change jobs without his current employer's signature has no exit from a bad employer, which removes the main pressure that raises wages in a free labour market.

⚠️ **The 1911 strike ban and skilled-job bar.** ⚠️ Removing the right to strike removes collective bargaining; barring skilled work removes advancement. ⚠️ Together with the first two, this produces a workforce that is compelled to come, cannot leave, cannot combine and cannot rise.

⚠️ That is a complete description of a labour system, and it is the correct answer to any question about the economic content of racial rule in South Africa. ⚠️ Apartheid after 1948 **codified and extended this order; it did not invent it.**

### 3. Doctrine, party, church and secret society

✅ Under Prime Minister Malan from 1948 to 1954 a new policy called **apartheid**, meaning separateness, was introduced, tightening control over blacks still further.

✅ Most of the whites, especially those of Dutch origin, were against racial equality, but the most extreme were the **Afrikaner Nationalist Party** led by Dr Malan. ✅ They claimed that whites were a master race and that non-whites were inferior beings. ✅ The **Dutch Reformed Church**, the official state church of South Africa, supported this view and quoted passages from the Bible which they claimed proved their theory - ✅ this was very much out of line with the rest of the Christian churches, which believe in racial equality. ✅ The **Broederbond** was a secret Afrikaner organisation which worked to protect and preserve Afrikaner power.

⚠️ Four institutions, four functions, and an answer should name them as a set rather than as a list: a **doctrine** of racial hierarchy; a **party** to win elections on it; a **church** to supply theological legitimation; and a **secret organisation** to hold positions across the state and civil society. ⚠️ That combination is what distinguishes a system from a policy, and it explains the system's durability: four separate sources of authority reinforcing one another.

⚠️ It also explains why one specific later development was so damaging. ⚠️ If a church supplies your legitimation, its defection removes it - and Lesson 22 records the Dutch Reformed Church eventually condemning apartheid as incompatible with Christianity.

✅ Apartheid was continued and developed further by the prime ministers who followed Malan: **Strijdom (1954-8)**, **Verwoerd (1958-66)** and **Vorster (1966-78)**.

### 4. The five features, and the Bantustan device

⚠️ The five statutory features are set out in the visual and should be reproduced accurately, because precision here is the difference between describing a system and denouncing one. ⚠️ Two of them need development.

⚠️ **Separate and inferior education** is the feature with the longest reach. ✅ Black children went to separate schools and were given a much inferior education. ⚠️ An education system designed to limit a population's qualifications is the same instrument Belgium used in the Congo and Central African settlers used in Rhodesia, applied by a state to its own citizens rather than by a metropole to a colony. ⚠️ It is also the provision that produced the generation of 1976, and Lesson 22 shows the state's language policy in schools destroying its own position.

⚠️ **The Bantustans** deserve a full paragraph because they are the regime's attempted answer to its own contradiction.

✅ The **Bantu Self-Government Act of 1959** set up seven regions called Bantustans, based on the original African reserves. ✅ It was claimed that they would eventually move towards self-government, and the first of them, the **Transkei**, was given internal **self-government in 1963** and was then declared "independent" on **26 October 1976**; ✅ by 1980 two more homelands, **Bophuthatswana** and **Venda**, had received "independence". ⚠️ The textbook account dates the Transkei declaration to 1969; that date is wrong, and the corrected sequence - self-government in 1963, declared independence on 26 October 1976 - is the one used throughout this session. ✅ However, the outside world dismissed this with contempt, since the South African government continued to control the Transkei's economy and foreign affairs. ✅ The whole policy was criticised because the Bantustan areas covered only about **thirteen per cent** of the country's total area, with **over eight million** black people crammed into these relatively small areas, which were vastly overcrowded and unable to support their populations adequately; ✅ they became very little better than rural slums, but the government ignored the protests and continued its policy. ✅ Africans lost all political rights, and their representation in parliament, which had been by white MPs, was abolished.

⚠️ Read the Bantustan policy as a legal argument and its purpose becomes clear. ⚠️ If black South Africans are nationals of independent homelands, then they are not citizens of South Africa whose exclusion from the franchise requires justification; they are foreign nationals present for work. ⚠️ The device was designed to convert a domestic democratic deficit into an immigration arrangement.

⚠️ And it failed on three counts, all recorded. ⚠️ It failed **internationally**, because the outside world dismissed it with contempt, control over the Transkei's economy and foreign affairs having remained with Pretoria. ⚠️ It failed **materially**, because thirteen per cent of the land could not support over eight million people and the homelands became rural slums. ⚠️ And it failed **economically**, because the whole point of the migrant workforce was that it had to be present in the white economy, so the homelands could never absorb the population whose nationality they were meant to redefine.

⚠️ Note the numerical comparison that makes the point in one line: ✅ approximately seventy per cent of the population, assigned to about thirteen per cent of the land - an improvement on the seven per cent of the reserves that changed nothing about the arithmetic.

### 5. The contradiction, stated in the source's own words

✅ **But there was a flaw in the system: complete separation was impossible because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and other businesses. The economy would have collapsed if all non-whites had been moved to reserves.** ✅ In addition, virtually every white household had at least two African servants.

⚠️ This is the single most useful sentence available on apartheid and it should be used as the analytical spine of any answer on the subject.

```text
   THE SYSTEM'S PRINCIPLE:  complete separation of the races
   THE SYSTEM'S ECONOMY:    depends on the labour of the people
                            it proposes to separate
        -> separation can therefore only ever be PARTIAL and
           RESIDENTIAL, never economic
        -> the excluded population must be PRESENT DAILY in the
           spaces from which it is excluded
        -> which requires CONTINUOUS CONTROL of movement (passes),
           of residence (townships), of assembly (the strike ban)
           and of information (later, the emergency press bans)
        -> and control of that intensity is EXPENSIVE, VISIBLE and
           PERMANENTLY CONTESTED
   CONCLUSION: apartheid was not a stable equilibrium that was
   eventually overthrown. It was a system that had to be enforced
   with rising effort from the beginning, because it was at war
   with its own economy.
```

⚠️ That is why the reform attempts of the 1980s took the form they did, and why they failed. Lesson 22 develops it.

### 6. Resistance, and how repression changed its form

⚠️ The resistance sequence must be taught as a sequence with causes, not as a list of episodes. ⚠️ The organising claim is that **the form of resistance changed in response to the state's conduct**, and each change can be dated.

✅ **Phase one: legal and constitutional protest.** ✅ Inside South Africa opposition was difficult: anyone who objected, including whites, or broke the apartheid laws was accused of being a communist and severely punished under the **Suppression of Communism Act**; ✅ Africans were forbidden to strike and the **African National Congress** was helpless. ✅ In spite of this, protests took place. ✅ **Chief Albert Luthuli**, the ANC leader, organised a protest campaign in which black Africans stopped work on certain days. ✅ In **1952** Africans attempted a systematic breach of the laws by entering shops and other places reserved for whites: **over eight thousand** blacks were arrested and many were flogged; Luthuli was deprived of his chieftaincy and jailed for a time, and the campaign was called off.

✅ In **1955** the ANC formed a coalition with Asian and coloured groups, and at a massive open-air meeting at **Kliptown** near Johannesburg they announced a **Freedom Charter** before police broke up the crowd; it soon became the main ANC programme. ✅ It began: "South Africa belongs to all who live in it, black and white, and no government can claim authority unless it is based on the will of the people." ✅ It demanded equality before the law; freedom of assembly, movement, speech, religion and the press; the right to vote; the right to work, with equal pay for equal work; a forty-hour working week, a minimum wage and unemployment benefits; free medical care; and free, compulsory and equal education.

⚠️ Two features of the Charter carry marks. ⚠️ **Its opening sentence is inclusive**, claiming the country for all who live in it, black and white - which makes the movement's demand *inclusion* rather than reversal, and which is the reason a negotiated settlement was eventually possible. ⚠️ **Its content is largely social and economic** - equal pay, a forty-hour week, a minimum wage, unemployment benefit, free medical care and free education - which places labour at the centre of the anti-apartheid programme, exactly as the 1911 strike ban placed it at the centre of the system.

✅ Church leaders and missionaries, both black and white, spoke out against apartheid, including **Trevor Huddleston**, a British missionary working in South Africa since 1943. ✅ The ANC organised other protests, including the **1957 bus boycott**, in which instead of paying a fare increase on the route from their township to Johannesburg ten miles away, thousands of Africans walked to work and back **for three months** until fares were reduced.

⚠️ The bus boycott is worth a sentence because it shows the method at its most economical: a fare increase is reversed by ten miles of walking twice a day for three months by thousands of people. ⚠️ It demonstrates organisation, endurance and the fact that the system's daily operation depended on the co-operation of the people it excluded.

✅ **Phase two: the turning point.** ✅ Protests reached a climax in **1960** when a huge demonstration took place against the pass laws at **Sharpeville**, a township near Johannesburg. ✅ Police fired on the crowd, killing **sixty-nine** Africans and wounding many more. ⚠️ The textbook account gives sixty-seven; the figure established by the subsequent commission of inquiry is sixty-nine, and that is the figure used throughout this session. ✅ After this, **fifteen thousand** Africans were arrested and hundreds of people were beaten by police. ✅ This was an important turning point in the campaign: until then most of the protests had been non-violent, but **this brutal treatment by the authorities convinced many black leaders that violence could only be met with violence**.

⚠️ That is a causal claim and it should be stated as one. ⚠️ The armed turn was produced by state conduct, not by ideology or by external influence. ⚠️ The demonstration at Sharpeville was against the pass laws - the single most intrusive daily instrument of the system - and it was unarmed.

✅ **Phase three: armed struggle alongside the old methods.** ✅ A small action group of the ANC known as **Umkhonto we Sizwe**, the Spear of the Nation, or **MK**, was launched, with **Nelson Mandela** a prominent member; ✅ they organised a campaign of sabotaging strategic targets, and in **1961** there was a spate of bomb attacks in Johannesburg, Port Elizabeth and Durban. ✅ But the police soon clamped down, arresting most of the black leaders including Mandela, who was sentenced to **life imprisonment on Robben Island**. ✅ Chief Luthuli still persevered with non-violent protest, and after publishing his moving autobiography *Let My People Go* he was awarded the **Nobel Peace Prize**; ✅ he was killed in 1967, the authorities claiming that he had deliberately stepped in front of a train.

⚠️ Note the simultaneity. ⚠️ MK's sabotage campaign and Luthuli's non-violent leadership ran at the same time, and Luthuli's Nobel Prize belongs to the same period as the bomb attacks. ⚠️ ❌ Do not write a simple transition from non-violence to violence; ✅ both strategies persisted together, which is why the movement retained the moral standing that made the later negotiation possible.

✅ **Phase four: the generation the state created.** ✅ Discontent and protest increased again in the 1970s because the wages of Africans failed to keep pace with inflation. ✅ In **1976**, when the Transvaal authorities announced that **Afrikaans** - the language spoken by whites of Dutch descent - was to be used in black African schools, massive demonstrations took place at **Soweto**, a black township near Johannesburg. ✅ Although there were many children and young people in the crowd, police opened fire, killing **at least two hundred** black Africans. ✅ The protests did not die down; they spread over the whole country, and the government again responded with brutality: over the next six months a further **five hundred** were killed. ✅ Among the victims was **Steve Biko**, a young African leader encouraging young people to be proud of their blackness, who was **beaten to death by police in 1977**.

⚠️ The Soweto trigger is the most instructive fact in the entire apartheid section and it should never be omitted. ⚠️ The rising began over the **language of instruction in the schools the state had designed to keep black South Africans unqualified**. ⚠️ The state's own educational instrument produced the generation that broke it, and the immediate cause was an attempt to tighten that instrument further. ⚠️ Combine it with the wage grievance - wages failing to keep pace with inflation - and the 1976 rising is a combination of labour and educational grievance in a single event, which is precisely the combination the Freedom Charter had articulated twenty-one years earlier.

⚠️ The attribution discipline applies to the figures: ✅ sixty-nine killed at Sharpeville in 1960 with fifteen thousand arrested afterwards; ✅ at least two hundred killed at Soweto in 1976 and a further five hundred over the following six months; ✅ over eight thousand arrested in 1952. ❌ Do not total them and do not extend them.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ Apartheid is the fullest case in this material of a legally constructed racial order, of its social effects, of resistance and of its eventual dismantling; it is also the case that connects decolonisation to the politics of race directly, since it was adopted as a response to decolonisation elsewhere.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect demands to describe the system and its social effects, to assess resistance, or to explain its end. ⚠️ This lesson supplies the first two; Lesson 22 supplies the third. ⚠️ The structure that scores: inherited hierarchy, the 1947-48 trigger, the four institutions, the five statutory features, the Bantustan device and its threefold failure, the economic contradiction, and the four phases of resistance with the state conduct that caused each change of form.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Treating apartheid as the origin of South African racial discrimination. ✅ The Union of 1910 already had a seven per cent land reserve, pass laws, compounds and the 1911 ban on black strikes and skilled work; apartheid codified and extended an existing hierarchy.
2. ⚠️ Omitting the 1947 trigger. ✅ Indian and Pakistani independence alarmed white South Africans about growing racial equality within the Commonwealth, and the Nationalists won in 1948 on the promise of preserving supremacy.
3. ⚠️ Describing the Bantustans as a failed attempt at self-government. ⚠️ They were a device for redefining a disenfranchised majority as foreign nationals, and they failed internationally, materially and economically.
4. ⚠️ Writing a simple shift from non-violence to violence. ✅ MK's sabotage from 1961 ran alongside Luthuli's continued non-violent protest and Nobel Prize.
5. ⚠️ Omitting the Soweto trigger. ✅ The rising began over the imposition of Afrikaans as the language of instruction in black schools.
6. ❌ Aggregating the casualty figures. ✅ Report each with its event.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Dutch settlement at the Cape from 1652; British capture in 1795 confirmed in 1814; slave emancipation throughout the Empire in 1838 prompting the Great Trek and the republics of the Transvaal and Orange Free State (1835-40); the Boer War of 1899-1902; the Union of South Africa in 1910.
2. ✅ Union population: about 70 per cent black African, 18 per cent white (about 60 per cent of them Dutch), 9 per cent of mixed race, 3 per cent Asian.
3. ✅ Whites dominated politics and economic life; with few exceptions blacks could not vote; reserved areas were only about 7 per cent of the country, too small to feed their populations or pay taxes; blacks were forbidden to buy land outside them.
4. ✅ Pass laws tied residence and employment to a named white employer; gold-mining compounds held up to ninety men in a dormitory; a law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs.
5. ✅ Apartheid, meaning separateness, was introduced under Malan (1948-54) and developed by Strijdom (1954-8), Verwoerd (1958-66) and Vorster (1966-78).
6. ✅ Trigger: when India and Pakistan were given independence in 1947, white South Africans became alarmed at growing racial equality within the Commonwealth and were determined to preserve their supremacy.
7. ✅ The Afrikaner Nationalist Party under Malan claimed whites were a master race; the Dutch Reformed Church supported this with scriptural claims, out of line with the rest of the Christian churches; the Broederbond worked secretly to preserve Afrikaner power; the Nationalists won in 1948 promising to rescue whites from the "black menace" and preserve racial purity.
8. ✅ Feature one: complete separation as far as possible - reserves, townships, communities uprooted and re-grouped, separate buses, coaches, trains, cafes, toilets, park benches, hospitals, beaches, picnic areas, sports and churches, and separate schools with a much inferior education.
9. ✅ Feature two: racial classification and an identity card for every person, with strict pass laws and no travel without police permission.
10. ✅ Feature three: marriage and sexual relations between whites and non-whites forbidden, with police surveillance.
11. ✅ Feature four: the Bantu Self-Government Act of 1959 created seven Bantustans; Transkei self-governing 1963 and declared "independent" on 26 October 1976, Bophuthatswana and Venda by 1980; about 13 per cent of the country for over 8 million people, vastly overcrowded and little better than rural slums; the outside world dismissed this with contempt because Pretoria still controlled Transkei's economy and foreign affairs.
12. ✅ Feature five: Africans lost all political rights and their parliamentary representation by white MPs was abolished.
13. ✅ The flaw: complete separation was impossible because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and businesses, and the economy would have collapsed if all non-whites had been moved to reserves; virtually every white household had at least two African servants.
14. ✅ Opposition inside South Africa was punished under the Suppression of Communism Act; Africans were forbidden to strike and the ANC was helpless.
15. ✅ Luthuli organised stay-away protests; the 1952 defiance of the laws brought over 8,000 arrests and floggings; Luthuli lost his chieftaincy and was jailed.
16. ✅ 1955 Kliptown: the Freedom Charter - "South Africa belongs to all who live in it, black and white" - demanding equality before the law, freedoms of assembly, movement, speech, religion and press, the vote, the right to work with equal pay, a forty-hour week, a minimum wage, unemployment benefit, free medical care and free compulsory equal education.
17. ✅ Trevor Huddleston and other church figures spoke out; the 1957 bus boycott sustained a ten-mile walk to work for three months until fares were reduced.
18. ✅ Sharpeville, 1960: police fired on an anti-pass-law demonstration, killing 69 and wounding many; 15,000 arrests followed; the brutality convinced many black leaders that violence could only be met with violence.
19. ✅ Umkhonto we Sizwe (MK), with Mandela prominent, sabotaged strategic targets with bomb attacks in Johannesburg, Port Elizabeth and Durban in 1961; Mandela received life imprisonment on Robben Island; Luthuli continued non-violent protest, published *Let My People Go* and was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize.
20. ✅ 1976 Soweto: massive demonstrations against Afrikaans as the language of instruction in black schools; police killed at least 200; protests spread nationwide and a further 500 were killed over six months; Steve Biko, encouraging young people to be proud of their blackness, was beaten to death by police in 1977.
21. ⚠️ Master formulation: apartheid codified an existing labour system, was adopted in reaction to decolonisation elsewhere, and contained a contradiction it could not remove - it required daily the presence of the people it was designed to exclude.

### Concept check

**Question:** Identify the internal contradiction at the centre of apartheid, and explain how it shaped both the Bantustan policy and the system's long-term prospects.

**Model answer:** The contradiction is that the system's stated principle was complete separation while its economy depended on the labour of the people it proposed to separate. Complete separation was impossible because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and other businesses, and the economy would have collapsed if all non-whites had been moved to the reserves; in addition virtually every white household had at least two African servants. Separation could therefore only ever be residential and political, never economic, which meant that the excluded population had to be present daily in the very spaces from which it was excluded - and that required continuous, expensive and visible control of movement through the pass laws, of residence through the townships, of combination through the ban on black strikes dating from 1911, and eventually of information through emergency press bans. The Bantustan policy was the attempt to resolve this. The Bantu Self-Government Act of 1959 created seven homelands; the Transkei received internal self-government in 1963 and was declared "independent" on 26 October 1976, with Bophuthatswana and Venda following by 1980, so that black South Africans could be redefined as nationals of foreign states present for work rather than as disenfranchised citizens. It failed on all three of its own terms: internationally, because the outside world dismissed it with contempt while Pretoria still controlled Transkei's economy and foreign affairs; materially, because about thirteen per cent of the country could not support over eight million people and the homelands became little better than rural slums; and economically, because the workforce had to remain in the white economy, which is what the whole policy had been designed to disguise. The long-term consequence is that apartheid was never a stable equilibrium requiring only an eventual overthrow, but a system at war with its own economy, requiring rising enforcement from the start.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating 1948 as the beginning of racial discrimination in South Africa. The Union of 1910 had already confined the black majority to reserves covering about seven per cent of the country, forbidden them to buy land outside those reserves, controlled their movement and employment through pass laws tied to a named employer, housed mineworkers in single-sex compounds of up to ninety men, and, by a law of 1911, forbidden black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs. Apartheid codified and extended that order rather than creating it, and recognising this is what turns a description of injustice into an explanation of a labour system.

## Lesson 22 - Apartheid II: pressure, negotiation and after, 1976-94

Progress: 22/24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the gap between international condemnation and international trade; the regional collapse of 1975-80; Botha's reforms and why partial reform failed; the sanctions of 1986; the defection of the church and the growth of a black professional class; de Klerk, Mandela's release and the negotiation; the transition without civil war; and the continuities after 1994

⚠️ The two columns at the top are the answer to the question in the heading, and they should be the opening move of any answer on the ending of apartheid. Condemnation was universal and costless; trade and arms sales continued because South Africa was valued as an anti-communist position. The system survived on the gap between the two, and it ended when the gap closed.

### 1. The words: condemnation from 1960

✅ Outside South Africa there was opposition to apartheid from the rest of the Commonwealth. ✅ Early in 1960 the British Conservative prime minister **Harold Macmillan** had the courage to speak out against it in **Cape Town**, speaking of the growing strength of African nationalism: "the wind of change is blowing through the continent... our national policies must take account of it." ✅ His warnings were ignored, and shortly afterwards the world was horrified by the Sharpeville massacre.

✅ At the **1961 Commonwealth Conference** criticism of South Africa was intense and many thought the country would be expelled. ✅ In the end **Verwoerd withdrew South Africa's application for continued membership** - in 1960 it had become a republic instead of a dominion, severing the connection with the British crown, and had therefore had to apply for readmission - ✅ and it ceased to be a member of the Commonwealth.

⚠️ That is the first measurable consequence of decolonisation for South Africa, and it should be connected back to Lesson 21's trigger. ⚠️ Apartheid was adopted in 1948 because racial equality was growing within the Commonwealth; thirteen years later that same growth had made South Africa's membership untenable. ⚠️ The newly independent members had changed the institution, exactly as the newly independent members changed the United Nations General Assembly in Lesson 6.

✅ The **United Nations** and the **Organization of African Unity** condemned apartheid and were particularly critical of the continued South African occupation of South West Africa. ✅ The UN voted to place an economic boycott on South Africa in **1962**, ✅ but this proved useless because not all member states supported it.

### 2. The trade: why condemnation cost nothing

✅ **Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public, but continued to trade with South Africa. Among other things, they sold South Africa massive arms supplies, apparently hoping that it would prove to be a bastion against the spread of communism in Africa.** ✅ Consequently Verwoerd, until his assassination in 1966, and his successor Vorster were able to ignore the protests from the outside world until well into the 1970s.

⚠️ That passage should be quoted in full in any answer on international pressure, for three reasons.

⚠️ **It identifies the mechanism of survival.** A regime can absorb unlimited condemnation provided its trade and arms supply are unaffected, because condemnation imposes no cost.

⚠️ **It identifies the reason.** The motive was not indifference to apartheid but Cold War calculation: South Africa was valued as a bastion against the spread of communism in Africa. ⚠️ Lesson 6 showed anti-communism justifying intervention against decolonising governments; here it is shown sustaining a racial order for two decades.

⚠️ **It repeats the Rhodesian finding exactly.** ⚠️ Lesson 13 recorded states publicly condemning UDI and privately evading the embargo, with the USA buying Rhodesian chrome as the cheapest available and British oil companies breaking sanctions. ⚠️ Two cases, one pattern: **the states that wrote the resolutions were the states that made them ineffective.** ⚠️ Stating that as a general finding, supported from both cases, is a strong analytical move.

### 3. Botha, and why partial reform accelerated the crisis

✅ The system of apartheid continued without any concessions being made to black people until 1980. ✅ The new prime minister **P. W. Botha**, elected 1979, realised that all was not well with the system: he decided that he must reform apartheid, dropping some of the most unpopular aspects, **in an attempt to preserve white control**.

⚠️ Keep that final clause. ⚠️ The purpose of the reforms was the preservation of white control, and an answer that presents them as liberalisation has misread them.

✅ Four causes of the change are recorded. ✅ Criticism from abroad gathered momentum from the Commonwealth, the United Nations and the OAU; ✅ external pressures became much greater in 1975 when the white-ruled Portuguese colonies of Angola and Mozambique achieved independence, and the African takeover of Zimbabwe in 1980 removed the last of the white-ruled states sympathetic to the South African government - ✅ **now South Africa was surrounded by hostile black states**, and many Africans in these new states had sworn never to rest until their fellow Africans in South Africa had been liberated. ✅ There were economic problems: South Africa was hit by recession in the late 1970s and many white people were worse off; whites began to emigrate in large numbers while the black population increased, so that in 1980 whites made up only **sixteen per cent** of the population against **twenty-one per cent** between the two world wars. ✅ The African homelands were a failure: poverty-stricken, their rulers corrupt, and recognised by no foreign government as genuinely independent states. ✅ And the USA, which was treating its own black people better during the 1970s, began to criticise the South African government's racist policy.

⚠️ The regional cause is the one to emphasise, because it is the point at which this lesson joins the rest of the session. ⚠️ Portuguese decolonisation in 1975 and Zimbabwean independence in 1980 - Lessons 20 and 13 - are what removed apartheid's strategic buffer. ⚠️ **Apartheid was ended, in part, by the decolonisation of its neighbours.** ⚠️ That is the most satisfying causal connection available in this topic and it should be made explicitly.

✅ In a speech in September 1979 which astonished many of his Nationalist supporters, Botha said: "A revolution in South Africa is no longer just a remote possibility. Either we adapt or we perish. White domination and legally enforced apartheid are a recipe for permanent conflict."

⚠️ Note that this is a strategic judgement by the head of the government, not a moral conversion: apartheid is described as a recipe for permanent conflict, which is a statement about sustainability.

✅ The reforms followed. ✅ Blacks were allowed to join trade unions and to go on strike in **1979**. ✅ Blacks were allowed to elect their own local township councils, but not to vote in national elections, in **1981**. ✅ A new constitution set up two new houses of parliament, one for coloureds and one for Asians **but not for Africans**, weighted so that the whites kept overall control, coming into force in **1984**. ✅ Sexual relations and marriage were allowed between people of different races in **1985**. ✅ The hated pass laws for non-whites were abolished in **1986**.

⚠️ The 1979 union concession is the most consequential and is usually under-read. ⚠️ It reversed the ban of 1911 that Lesson 21 identified as the keystone of the labour system. ⚠️ Legalising black trade unions and the right to strike restored collective bargaining to the workforce on which the entire economy depended - and did so while the political exclusion remained total. ⚠️ The regime thereby created a legal, organised, economically indispensable black institution at precisely the moment it was refusing black political rights.

✅ This was as far as Botha was prepared to go. ✅ **He would not even consider the ANC's main demands - the right to vote and to play a full part in ruling the country.** ✅ Far from being won over by these concessions, black Africans were **incensed** that the new constitution made no provision for them, and were determined to settle for nothing less than full political rights.

⚠️ This is the central mechanism of the lesson and it should be stated as a proposition: **partial reform that concedes everything except power increases anger rather than reducing it**, because it demonstrates that the regime can change when it chooses and confirms that it has chosen not to change the one thing that matters. ⚠️ Every concession Botha made was an admission that the system was not immutable.

✅ Violence escalated, with both sides guilty of excesses. ✅ The ANC used the "necklace", a tyre placed round the victim's neck and set on fire, to murder black councillors and black police who were regarded as collaborators with apartheid. ✅ On the twenty-fifth anniversary of Sharpeville, police opened fire on a procession of black mourners going to a funeral near Uitenhage, killing **over forty** people in **March 1985**. ✅ In July a **state of emergency** was declared in the worst affected areas, and it was extended to the whole country in **June 1986**, giving police the power to arrest people without warrants and freedom from all criminal proceedings; thousands were arrested, and newspapers, radio and television were banned from reporting demonstrations and strikes.

⚠️ Report the "necklace" as the account does. ⚠️ Violence within the resistance, directed at black councillors and police regarded as collaborators, is part of the record and is required for balance in any critically-examine answer. ⚠️ The account's own formulation - both sides guilty of excesses - is the safe one.

✅ **However, as so often happens when an authoritarian regime tries to reform itself, it proved impossible to stop the process of change** - ✅ the same happened in the USSR when Gorbachev tried to reform communism.

⚠️ That generalisation is the account's own and it is worth carrying, because it links this case to an entirely different part of the syllabus and demonstrates comparative range. ⚠️ Its mechanism is the one just identified: reform reveals that the system is changeable, which destroys the fatalism on which acquiescence depended, and the demand then expands faster than the concessions.

### 4. Sanctions that finally bit, and a dispute worth reporting

✅ By the late 1980s international pressure was having more effect and internal attitudes had changed.

✅ In **August 1986** the **Commonwealth, except Britain**, agreed on a strong package of sanctions: no further loans, no sales of oil, computer equipment or nuclear goods to South Africa, and no cultural and scientific contacts. ✅ British prime minister **Margaret Thatcher** would commit Britain only to a voluntary ban on investment in South Africa, arguing that severe economic sanctions would worsen the plight of black Africans, who would be thrown out of their jobs. ✅ This caused the rest of the Commonwealth to feel bitter against Britain; ✅ **Rajiv Gandhi**, the Indian prime minister, accused Mrs Thatcher of "compromising on basic principles and values for economic ends".

✅ In **September 1986** the USA joined the fray when Congress voted, **over President Reagan's veto**, to stop American loans to South Africa, to cut air links and to ban imports of iron, steel, coal, textiles and uranium.

⚠️ Two points here, both examinable.

⚠️ **The Thatcher argument should be stated fairly and then answered.** ⚠️ The claim that sanctions would cost black South Africans their jobs is a serious argument and was made seriously. ⚠️ The response available from this material is that the same government's position had been maintained through two decades in which, as recorded above, the same five states condemned apartheid publicly and sold it massive arms supplies - so the concern for black employment appeared at the moment when the alternative was a measure with a cost. ⚠️ Rajiv Gandhi's formulation captures the objection precisely and should be quoted.

⚠️ **The American measure passed over a presidential veto**, which is worth a clause: the executive opposed sanctions and the legislature imposed them, which indicates how far public opinion had moved ahead of strategic calculation by 1986.

✅ Two further internal changes completed the shift. ✅ The black population was no longer just a mass of uneducated and unskilled labourers; there was a steadily growing number of well-educated, professional, middle-class black people, some of them holding important positions, like **Desmond Tutu**, who was awarded the **Nobel Peace Prize in 1984** and became Anglican archbishop of Cape Town in 1986. ✅ And the **Dutch Reformed Church, which had once supported apartheid, now condemned it as incompatible with Christianity**.

⚠️ The church's reversal is the most under-used fact in the case and it should be foregrounded. ⚠️ Lesson 21 identified four pillars: doctrine, party, church and secret society. ⚠️ The withdrawal of theological legitimation by the regime's own state church removed one of the four, and it removed the one that had answered the question of *why* the system was right rather than merely necessary.

✅ A majority of white South Africans now recognised that it was difficult to defend the total exclusion of blacks from the country's political life; ✅ so although they were nervous about what might happen, they became resigned to the idea of black majority rule at some time in the future, and white moderates were prepared to make the best of the situation and get the best deal possible.

### 5. De Klerk, the negotiation, and a transition without civil war

✅ The new president **F.
