W. de Klerk**, elected **1989**, had a reputation for caution, but privately he had decided that apartheid would have to go completely, and he accepted that black majority rule must come eventually. ✅ The problem was how to achieve it without further violence and possible civil war. ✅ With great courage and determination, and in the face of bitter opposition from right-wing Afrikaner groups, de Klerk gradually moved the country towards black majority rule.

✅ **Nelson Mandela was released after twenty-seven years in jail in 1990** and became leader of the ANC, which was made legal. ✅ Most of the remaining apartheid laws were dropped. ✅ **Namibia**, the neighbouring territory ruled by South Africa since 1919, was given independence under a black government in **1990**. ✅ Talks began in **1991** between the government and the ANC to work out a new constitution which would allow blacks full political rights.

⚠️ Note the Namibian item, because it closes a thread from Lesson 20 and Lesson 23. ✅ Namibia, formerly German South West Africa, had been handed to South Africa in 1919 to be prepared for independence, and the white South African government had ignored UN orders and delayed handing it over as long as possible; ✅ SWAPO under **Sam Nujoma** conducted a guerrilla campaign, based after 1975 in southern Angola. ⚠️ Namibia is therefore the last mandate of the First World War settlement to be decolonised, and it was decolonised as part of a Cold War bargain that Lesson 23 describes.

✅ Meanwhile the ANC was doing its best to present itself as a moderate party which had no plans for wholesale nationalisation, and to reassure whites that they would be safe and happy under black rule; ✅ **Nelson Mandela condemned violence and called for reconciliation between blacks and whites.**

✅ The negotiations were long and difficult. ✅ De Klerk had to face right-wing opposition from his own National Party and from various extreme white racialist groups who claimed that he had betrayed them. ✅ The ANC was involved in a power struggle with another black party, the Natal-based Zulu **Inkatha Freedom Party** led by **Chief Buthelezi**.

⚠️ Both constraints are essential to the analysis and neither should be omitted. ⚠️ Each negotiator faced a flank that regarded the negotiation itself as treachery, and the settlement's achievement is that both managed their own flank rather than that they agreed with each other. ⚠️ **In a negotiated transition the decisive skill is control of one's own extremists, not persuasion of the other side.**

✅ In the spring of **1993** the talks were successful and a power-sharing scheme was worked out to carry through the transition to black majority rule. ✅ A general election was held and the ANC won **almost two-thirds** of the votes. ✅ As had been agreed, a coalition government of the ANC, National Party and Inkatha took office, ✅ with **Mandela** as president in **1994** - and the transition to black majority rule was achieved **without civil war**.

⚠️ That last phrase is the analytical achievement and it is what the case is examined for. ⚠️ Compare every other settler case in this session: Algeria required a war and produced a settler exodus of about eight hundred thousand; Southern Rhodesia required fifteen years and a guerrilla war; Kenya required a state of emergency lasting from 1952 to 1960. ⚠️ South Africa, with the largest and most entrenched settler population of all, produced a negotiated constitution. ⚠️ The reason is that both sides had independently concluded that the alternative was civil war, and both had leaders able to hold their own side to the conclusion.

### 6. What did not end in 1994

✅ **The Truth and Reconciliation Commission** was one of Mandela's most successful initiatives, looking into human rights abuses during the apartheid regime; ✅ assisted by Archbishop Tutu, its approach was not one of taking revenge but of granting amnesties, with people encouraged to talk frankly, acknowledge their crimes and ask for forgiveness. ✅ This was one of the most admirable things about Mandela: although he had been kept in prison under apartheid for twenty-seven years, he still believed in forgiveness and reconciliation. ✅ He decided not to stand for re-election in 1999, retiring with his reputation high, almost universally admired for his statesmanship and restraint.

✅ The government faced daunting problems and was expected to deliver on the ANC programme, especially improving conditions for the black population, with plans for education, housing, health care, water and power supplies and sanitation; ✅ **but the scale of the problem was so vast that it would be many years before standards would show improvement for everybody.**

✅ **The delivery record.** ✅ Seventy per cent of black households had electricity; ✅ the number of people with access to pure water had increased by nine million since 1994; ✅ about two thousand new houses for poor people had been built; ✅ education was free and compulsory; ✅ and many black people said they felt they now had dignity instead of being treated like animals as they had been under apartheid. ✅ The economic situation appeared brighter: South Africa was diversifying its exports instead of relying on gold, there was a growing tourist industry, the budget deficit had fallen sharply and inflation was down to **four per cent**.

✅ **The persistent problems.** ✅ The crime rate soared; ✅ trade unions called strikes in protest against job losses, poor working conditions and the increasing rate of privatisation; ✅ the economic growth rate was slowing - in 2001 it was only **1.5 per cent** compared with **3.1** in 2000; ✅ the government came under special criticism for its handling of the AIDS epidemic, with **Thabo Mbeki** slow to recognise a crisis and refusing to declare a state of emergency that would have enabled cheaper medicines to be obtained; ✅ in 2005 South Africa was reported to be the country with the most HIV positive people in the world, **6.5 million**; ✅ there was an influx of migrants and refugees, mainly from Zimbabwe but also from Rwanda, the Congo, Somalia and Ethiopia, competing for jobs and housing, and in **May 2008** serious protest riots directed against immigrants killed **at least eighty** people; ✅ more left-wing ANC supporters felt there had been too little progress in the redistribution of wealth; ✅ and in May 2009 South Africa's unemployment rate had reached **twenty-five per cent**, with those out of work forced to live on less than **US$1.25 a day**.

⚠️ And one event closes the sequence in a way that connects it back to the very beginning of Lesson 21. ✅ In **August 2012** police shot and killed **thirty-four striking platinum miners** at the **Marikana** mine near Johannesburg; ✅ poorly paid and working in difficult conditions, the miners were demanding wage increases from the mine-owners, a British company; ✅ **two hundred and seventy** miners were then arrested and charged with the murder of their colleagues on the grounds that their behaviour had caused the police action, though the charges were later dropped, and a wave of outrage followed.

⚠️ Make the connection explicitly, because it is the strongest closing line available in this topic. ⚠️ The system described in Lesson 21 rested on a law of 1911 forbidding black mineworkers to strike, on compounds of up to ninety men, and on a land allocation that compelled men into the mines. ⚠️ Eighteen years after majority rule, black miners striking for higher wages at a British-owned platinum mine were shot by the police of a democratic state and then charged with their colleagues' deaths. ⚠️ **Political apartheid ended completely and at once; the structure of the mining economy did not** - which is the same "political independence without economic decolonisation" argument this session has made about every other case, applied to a state that never had to be decolonised in the formal sense at all.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This is the topic's fullest treatment of international pressure, of negotiated transition and of the limits of political emancipation, and it is a standard reference case for the redrawal of political orders without war.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "why did apartheid end", "assess the role of international pressure", or a critically-examine question on the transition. ⚠️ The structure that scores: condemnation without cost until the 1970s; regional collapse in 1975-80 as the decisive change; Botha's reform failing because it conceded everything but the vote; the 1986 sanctions and the defection of the church; de Klerk and Mandela each controlling their own flank; transition without civil war; and the economic continuities after 1994.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Crediting sanctions with ending apartheid. ✅ The 1962 UN boycott proved useless because not all member states supported it, and the five major western states condemned apartheid publicly while selling massive arms supplies until well into the 1970s. ✅ The 1986 packages mattered, but so did the regional collapse, the demographic shift, the failure of the homelands and the defection of the church.
2. ⚠️ Presenting Botha's reforms as liberalisation. ✅ Their stated purpose was to preserve white control, and he would not even consider the vote.
3. ⚠️ Omitting violence within the resistance. ✅ Both sides were guilty of excesses; the ANC used the necklace against black councillors and police regarded as collaborators.
4. ⚠️ Treating the negotiation as a meeting of two unified sides. ✅ De Klerk faced right-wing opposition inside his own party and from extreme white groups; the ANC was in a power struggle with Inkatha under Buthelezi.
5. ⚠️ Ending the answer in 1994. ✅ The delivery record and the persistent problems are both recorded, and Marikana in August 2012 shows the mining labour relation outlasting the political transition.
6. ❌ Asserting any current South African statistic. ⚠️ The figures above are dated and belong to the years given.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Macmillan's "wind of change" speech was delivered in Cape Town early in 1960; his warnings were ignored and Sharpeville followed shortly afterwards.
2. ✅ At the 1961 Commonwealth Conference criticism was intense; Verwoerd withdrew South Africa's application for continued membership after it became a republic in 1960, and it ceased to be a member.
3. ✅ The UN and OAU condemned apartheid and were particularly critical of the continued occupation of South West Africa; the UN voted an economic boycott in 1962 which proved useless because not all member states supported it.
4. ✅ Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public but continued to trade and sold massive arms supplies, apparently hoping South Africa would be a bastion against communism in Africa; Verwoerd and Vorster could therefore ignore outside protests until well into the 1970s.
5. ✅ Botha, elected 1979, decided to reform apartheid by dropping some unpopular aspects in an attempt to preserve white control.
6. ✅ Causes: mounting Commonwealth, UN and OAU criticism; the independence of Angola and Mozambique in 1975 and the African takeover of Zimbabwe in 1980 leaving South Africa surrounded by hostile black states; recession in the late 1970s with whites emigrating and falling from 21 per cent between the wars to 16 per cent by 1980; the failure of the homelands; and American criticism.
7. ✅ Botha, September 1979: "A revolution in South Africa is no longer just a remote possibility. Either we adapt or we perish. White domination and legally enforced apartheid are a recipe for permanent conflict."
8. ✅ The reforms: trade unions and strikes for blacks 1979; township councils but no national vote 1981; a tricameral constitution for whites, coloureds and Asians but not Africans, weighted for white control, in force 1984; mixed marriage and sexual relations allowed 1985; pass laws abolished 1986.
9. ✅ Botha would not even consider the ANC's main demands, the right to vote and to play a full part in ruling the country; far from being won over, black Africans were incensed that the new constitution made no provision for them.
10. ✅ Violence escalated with both sides guilty of excesses; the ANC used the necklace against black councillors and police regarded as collaborators; police killed over forty mourners near Uitenhage in March 1985; a state of emergency was declared in July 1985 and extended nationwide in June 1986, with arrest without warrant, freedom from criminal proceedings and a ban on reporting demonstrations and strikes.
11. ✅ As so often happens when an authoritarian regime tries to reform itself, it proved impossible to stop the process of change - the same happened in the USSR under Gorbachev.
12. ✅ August 1986: the Commonwealth except Britain agreed no further loans, no sales of oil, computer equipment or nuclear goods, and no cultural or scientific contacts; Thatcher committed Britain only to a voluntary investment ban, arguing severe sanctions would cost black Africans their jobs; Rajiv Gandhi accused her of compromising on basic principles and values for economic ends.
13. ✅ September 1986: the US Congress voted over Reagan's veto to stop American loans, cut air links and ban imports of iron, steel, coal, textiles and uranium.
14. ✅ A steadily growing black professional middle class had emerged, including Desmond Tutu, Nobel Peace Prize 1984 and Anglican archbishop of Cape Town 1986; the Dutch Reformed Church, which had once supported apartheid, now condemned it as incompatible with Christianity.
15. ✅ De Klerk, elected 1989, privately decided apartheid must go completely; Mandela was released in 1990 after twenty-seven years and the ANC legalised; most remaining apartheid laws were dropped; Namibia became independent under a black government in 1990; constitutional talks began in 1991.
16. ✅ The ANC presented itself as moderate with no plans for wholesale nationalisation and reassured whites; Mandela condemned violence and called for reconciliation.
17. ✅ De Klerk faced bitter opposition from right-wing Afrikaner groups and from his own National Party; the ANC was in a power struggle with the Zulu Inkatha Freedom Party under Chief Buthelezi.
18. ✅ Spring 1993: a power-sharing scheme; the ANC won almost two-thirds of the votes; a coalition of ANC, National Party and Inkatha took office with Mandela as president in 1994 - majority rule without civil war.
19. ✅ The Truth and Reconciliation Commission, assisted by Tutu, granted amnesties rather than taking revenge; Mandela, jailed twenty-seven years, still believed in forgiveness and reconciliation, and retired in 1999.
20. ✅ Delivery: 70 per cent of black households with electricity; access to pure water up by nine million since 1994; about two thousand new houses for poor people; free compulsory education; exports diversified away from gold; a growing tourist industry; the budget deficit sharply down; inflation at 4 per cent.
21. ✅ Persistent problems: a soaring crime rate; union strikes against job losses and privatisation; growth of 1.5 per cent in 2001 against 3.1 in 2000; contested handling of AIDS, with 6.5 million HIV positive people reported in 2005; migration from Zimbabwe, Rwanda, the Congo, Somalia and Ethiopia; anti-immigrant riots in May 2008 killing at least eighty; left-wing ANC criticism of too little redistribution; unemployment at 25 per cent in May 2009 with the jobless living on less than US$1.25 a day.
22. ✅ August 2012, Marikana: police shot and killed thirty-four striking platinum miners demanding wage increases from a British mine-owning company; two hundred and seventy miners were arrested and charged with their colleagues' murder before the charges were dropped.
23. ⚠️ Master formulation: apartheid survived on the gap between universal condemnation and uninterrupted trade, and ended when regional decolonisation, demographic change, homeland failure, sanctions with a cost and the defection of its own church closed that gap - while the economic structure it had enforced outlived the political system by a considerable margin.

### Concept check

**Question:** Explain why Botha's reforms of 1979-86 increased rather than reduced the pressure on the apartheid state, and identify the single external development that did most to make reform necessary.

**Model answer:** They increased pressure because they conceded everything except the thing at issue. Botha's stated purpose was to reform apartheid by dropping some of its most unpopular aspects in order to preserve white control, and the measures were substantial: black trade unions and the right to strike in 1979, township councils in 1981, a new constitution with houses for coloureds and Asians in 1984, the legalisation of mixed marriage and sexual relations in 1985 and the abolition of the pass laws in 1986. But he would not even consider the ANC's main demands, the right to vote and to play a full part in ruling the country, and far from being won over, black Africans were incensed that the new constitution made no provision for them. The mechanism is that reform destroys the fatalism on which acquiescence rests: each concession proved the system was changeable and therefore that its central exclusion was a choice, and the account generalises this by noting that when an authoritarian regime tries to reform itself it proves impossible to stop the process of change, as happened in the USSR under Gorbachev. The 1979 union concession was particularly consequential because it reversed the 1911 ban on black strikes and gave legal organisation to the workforce the economy depended on, while political rights were still refused. The external development that mattered most was the collapse of the regional buffer: Angola and Mozambique became independent from Portugal in 1975 and Zimbabwe passed to African rule in 1980, leaving South Africa surrounded by hostile black states, with many Africans in those states sworn never to rest until their fellow Africans in South Africa were liberated.

**Misconception to avoid:** Attributing the end of apartheid to international sanctions. Condemnation was universal from 1960 and cost nothing: the UN boycott of 1962 proved useless because not all member states supported it, and Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public while continuing to trade and selling massive arms supplies in the hope that South Africa would be a bastion against communism in Africa, which allowed Verwoerd and Vorster to ignore outside protest until well into the 1970s. Sanctions with a cost arrived only in August and September 1986 and arrived alongside four other changes - regional decolonisation, a white population falling from twenty-one to sixteen per cent, the visible failure of the homelands, and the Dutch Reformed Church's condemnation of apartheid as incompatible with Christianity. Sanctions were one component of a convergence, not the cause.

---

## Lesson 23 - After independence: five structures and six mechanisms

Progress: 23/24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: the five problems common to the new African states; the 1980s debt and structural adjustment crisis; and six named cases each isolating a different mechanism - Ghana, Nigeria and Biafra, Tanzania, Angola, Rwanda and Burundi, and Zimbabwe - together with the counter-evidence that prevents the structural argument from becoming determinism

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's chapter on the problems facing the new African nations, covering the four common problems and their detail, the IMF structural adjustment programmes of the 1980s and the drought of 1982-5; and its case sections on Ghana under Nkrumah, Busia and Rawlings, on Nigeria's civil war and recovery, on Tanzania's Arusha Declaration and its results, on Angola's civil war and the Lisbon and Namibia settlements, on Burundi and Rwanda, and on Zimbabwe under Mugabe
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the inheritance, and the six things that could be done with it

```text
   THE INHERITANCE - four problems "common to all the states"
   ================================================================
   (a) ETHNIC DIFFERENCE: groups "held together by the foreign
       colonial rulers" and united in the nationalist struggle;
       "as soon as the Europeans withdrew, there was little
       incentive to stay together"
   (b) ECONOMIC UNDER-DEVELOPMENT: "very little industry; this had
       been A DELIBERATE POLICY BY THE COLONIAL POWERS" - colonies
       were to buy manufactures from Europe or the USA and supply
       food and raw materials; after independence, reliance on ONE
       OR TWO COMMODITIES, so "a fall in the world price of their
       products was a major disaster"; shortage of capital and
       skills; population growth over 2 PER CENT a year; loans
       leaving them heavily in debt; exporting to repay loans made
       food for home consumption scarcer  => NEO-COLONIALISM
   (c) POLITICAL INEXPERIENCE: politicians "lacked experience of how
       to work the systems of parliamentary democracy left behind by
       the Europeans"; one-party states, which "worked well" in
       Kenya and Tanzania; where legal opposition was impossible,
       "violence was the only answer" and MILITARY COUPS became
       common
   (d) ECONOMIC AND NATURAL DISASTERS: 1980s world recession cutting
       demand for oil, copper and cobalt; DROUGHT 1982-5 with crop
       failures, livestock deaths, famine and starvation; then a
       severe DEBT CRISIS and IMF-imposed ESAP austerity - currency
       devaluation, reduced food subsidies, higher food prices with
       rising unemployment and falling wages, and cuts to education,
       health and social services

   PLUS: Cold War military intervention against governments thought
   too left-wing - Angola invaded by South Africa and Zaire

   THE SIX MECHANISMS, each isolated by one case
   ================================================================
   1  GHANA          preparation is not enough when one commodity
                     funds the state and politics is closed
   2  NIGERIA        federal design meets territorial identity
   3  TANZANIA       a coherent alternative model, defeated by the
                     world economy it could not exit
   4  ANGOLA         external patrons lengthening a war they did
                     not start
   5  RWANDA/BURUNDI identity made rather than inherited, then
                     converted into mass killing
   6  ZIMBABWE       a settlement that deferred the land question
```

⚠️ The structure of this lesson is deliberate and should be reproduced in answers. The inheritance sets the constraints; the six cases show what different governments did inside them; and the counter-evidence at the end shows that the outcomes were not determined. An answer that gives only the inheritance is fatalistic; an answer that gives only the cases is anecdotal.

### 1. The four common problems, read as colonial products

✅ **Ethnic difference.** ✅ Each state contained a number of different groups which had only been held together by the foreign colonial rulers and which had united in the nationalist struggle for freedom from the foreigners; ✅ as soon as the Europeans withdrew there was little incentive to stay together, and they tended to regard loyalty to the group as more important than loyalty to their new nation. ✅ In Nigeria, the Congo, Burundi and Rwanda these differences became so intense that they led to civil war.

⚠️ Handle this carefully, because it is the point at which weak answers become circular. ⚠️ The statement "there was little incentive to stay together" is a statement about incentives, not about hatred, and the incentives were produced by two colonial facts already established in this session: ⚠️ the frontiers had been drawn by Europeans, so the state was not the expression of any group's political history; ⚠️ and colonial administration had in several cases institutionalised the divisions - most explicitly in the Belgian method of using men from one group to keep order in another's area, and in Ruanda-Urundi's use of the Tutsi to control the Hutu.

⚠️ The formulation to use: **colonial rule did not invent African ethnic identities, but it fixed them, ranked them and attached them to the apparatus of the state; independence then made control of that apparatus the only prize worth having.**

✅ **Economic under-development.** ✅ Most African territories had very little industry; ✅ this had been a deliberate policy by the colonial powers, whose intention was that the colonies should buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and provide food and raw materials. ✅ After independence they relied on only one or two commodities for export, so that a fall in the world price of their products was a major disaster - ✅ Nigeria, for example, relied heavily on its oil exports, which produced about **eighty per cent** of its annual income. ✅ There was a shortage of capital and skills of all kinds, and the population was growing at a rate of **over two per cent a year**. ✅ Loans from abroad left them heavily in debt, and as they concentrated on increasing exports to pay for the loans, food for home consumption became scarcer. ✅ All this left the African nations heavily dependent on western European countries and the USA for both markets and investment, and enabled those countries to exert some control over African governments - **neo-colonialism**.

⚠️ That paragraph is the single most important passage in the lesson and it should be reproduced almost in full in any answer on post-colonial economic difficulty, because it states a *designed* structure rather than a condition.

⚠️ Note the chain, which is worth drawing out explicitly:

```text
   deliberate colonial policy: no industry; food and raw materials only
        -> at independence, one or two export commodities
        -> a world price fall becomes a national fiscal crisis
        -> borrowing to cover the gap
        -> debt service requires MORE exports
        -> land and labour shift further towards export crops
        -> FOOD FOR HOME CONSUMPTION BECOMES SCARCER
        -> dependence on western markets and investment
        -> "enabled those countries to exert some control over
            African governments" = NEO-COLONIALISM
```

⚠️ The step most often missed is the second from the end. ⚠️ Debt repayment does not merely cost money; it changes what a country grows, and it does so in the direction of less food. ⚠️ That mechanism explains why economic difficulty in post-colonial states so frequently appeared as food insecurity rather than as a simple fiscal problem.

✅ **Political problems.** ✅ African politicians lacked experience of how to work the systems of parliamentary democracy left behind by the Europeans; faced with difficult problems they often failed to cope, and governments became corrupt. ✅ Most African leaders who had taken part in guerrilla campaigns before independence had been influenced by Marxist ideas, which often led them to set up one-party states as the only way to achieve progress. ✅ **In many states, such as Kenya and Tanzania, this worked well, providing stable and effective government.** ✅ On the other hand, since it was impossible to oppose such governments by legal means, violence was the only answer, and military coups to remove unpopular rulers became common. ✅ President Nkrumah of Ghana, for example, was removed by the army in 1966 after two assassination attempts had failed. ✅ Where the army was unable or unwilling to stage a coup, as in Malawi, the one-party system flourished at the expense of freedom and genuine democracy.

⚠️ The sentence about Kenya and Tanzania should not be dropped, because it prevents the paragraph from becoming a blanket condemnation and because it is the more interesting finding. ⚠️ One-party government produced stable and effective administration in some states and closed the only peaceful route to change in all of them. ⚠️ **The trade-off is the analytical content: a one-party state buys stability at the price of making every subsequent change of government a coup.**

✅ **Economic and natural disasters.** ✅ In the 1980s the whole of Africa was beset by economic and natural disasters. ✅ The world recession reduced demand for African exports such as oil, copper and cobalt, and there was a severe **drought from 1982 to 1985** which caused crop failures, deaths of livestock, famine and starvation. ✅ The drought ended in 1986 and much of the continent had record harvests that year. ✅ However, by this time Africa, like the rest of the world, was suffering from a severe **debt crisis**, and had been forced by the **International Monetary Fund** to economise drastically in return for further loans. ✅ In a number of cases the IMF prescribed the **Economic Structural Adjustment Programme (ESAP)**, which often forced countries to devalue their currency and reduce food price subsidies, leading to increased food prices at a time when unemployment was rising and wages were falling; ✅ governments were also forced to cut their spending on education, health and social services as part of the austerity programme.

⚠️ Three consequences of structural adjustment recur in the cases below and should be recognised when they appear. ⚠️ ✅ In Ghana, Rawlings turned to the IMF reluctantly and had to agree to austerity measures, after which production rose by seven per cent and inflation fell to forty per cent early in 1985. ⚠️ ✅ In Tanzania an IMF debt-relief package in April 2000 came with the condition that parents contribute part of their children's school fees, which was totally unrealistic for a poor country and led to a sharp fall in primary-school numbers. ⚠️ ✅ In Zimbabwe, Mugabe accepted an IMF loan and agreed, very much against public opinion, to abide by the ESAP, which involved unpopular cuts in public spending on social services and jobs.

⚠️ Note that the three outcomes differ: recovery in one case, a measurable social cost in the second, and a political liability in the third. ⚠️ **Structural adjustment is therefore not a single verdict but a variable whose effects depended on the state applying it**, and saying so is more accurate than either endorsing or condemning it wholesale.

### 2. Mechanism one: Ghana, and why preparation was not enough

✅ **Kwame Nkrumah ruled Ghana from independence in 1957 until his removal by the army in 1966.**

✅ **The achievements first, because they were real.** ✅ He was a socialist in outlook and wanted his people to enjoy a higher standard of living, which would come from efficient organisation and industrialisation. ✅ Production of **cocoa**, Ghana's main export, doubled; ✅ forestry, fishing and cattle-breeding expanded; ✅ the country's modest deposits of **gold and bauxite** were more effectively exploited; ✅ the building of a dam on the **River Volta**, begun in **1961**, provided water for irrigation and hydro-electric power, producing enough electricity for the towns as well as for a new plant for smelting Ghana's large deposits of bauxite; ✅ and government money was provided for village projects in which local people built roads and schools. ✅ He also gained international prestige, supporting the pan-African movement and the Organization of African Unity, keeping Ghana in the Commonwealth - Queen Elizabeth II made a state visit in 1961 - while forging links with the USSR, East Germany and China.

✅ **Then the failures.** ✅ He tried to introduce industrialisation too quickly and borrowed vast amounts of capital from abroad, hoping to balance the budget from increased exports. ✅ Unfortunately Ghana was still uncomfortably dependent on cocoa exports, and **a steep fall in the world price of cocoa left her with a huge balance-of-payments deficit**. ✅ The smelting plant was a disappointment because the American corporation that built and owned it insisted on buying bauxite from abroad instead of using Ghanaian bauxite. ✅ There was criticism that too much money was being wasted on unnecessary projects, such as a ten-mile stretch of motorway from Accra to Tema and some grandiose building projects.

⚠️ The smelter detail is the most precise illustration of neo-colonialism available in this session and it should be used. ⚠️ Ghana built a smelting plant to process its own bauxite; the plant was built and owned by a foreign corporation; the corporation bought its bauxite abroad. ⚠️ The physical infrastructure of industrialisation existed on Ghanaian soil and did not industrialise Ghana, because the decision about inputs lay with the owner. ⚠️ **Ownership, not location, determines whether investment develops a country.**

✅ **The political failure.** ✅ Probably the most important reason for his downfall was that he gradually began to abandon parliamentary government in favour of a one-party state and personal dictatorship. ✅ He justified this on the grounds that the opposition parties, based on ethnic differences, were not constructive and merely wanted more power in their own areas; ✅ as Nkrumah himself wrote: "Even a system based on a democratic constitution may need backing up in the period following independence by emergency measures of a totalitarian kind." ✅ The **Preventive Detention Act of 1958** created the power to detain without trial, and from **1959** onwards opponents could be deported or imprisoned for up to five years without trial; ⚠️ name the statute and its year, because the textbook account dates only the practice; ✅ the respected opposition leader **J. B. Danquah** was arrested in 1961 and died in prison; ✅ in **1964** all parties except Nkrumah's were banned, and even within his own party no criticism was allowed. ✅ He built up an image of himself as the "father of the nation", with slogans such as "Nkrumah is our Messiah, Nkrumah never dies" and numerous statues of the "saviour"; ✅ this struck many people as absurd, but he justified it on the grounds that the population could identify itself better with a single personality than with vague notions of the state.

✅ All this, plus the fact that he was believed to have amassed a personal fortune through corruption, was too much for the army, which seized control when Nkrumah was on a visit to China in **1966**. ✅ **The American CIA gave the coup its full backing, because the USA disapproved of Nkrumah's links with communist states.** ⚠️ That sentence is the textbook account's, and it should be carried as a contested claim rather than as an established fact. ⚠️ American hostility to Nkrumah, and American foreknowledge and encouragement of his removal, are well attested; "full backing" states more than the declassified record settles, and the extent of operational American involvement in the coup of February 1966 remains disputed. ✅ Write it as "removed by the army with American encouragement, and on this account's reading with full CIA backing - a claim that is contested".

⚠️ The full mechanism, which is the one to write:

```text
   single-commodity export base
        -> world price falls
        -> fiscal crisis the government cannot absorb
        -> the leader answers the crisis by CLOSING POLITICS
           (1959 detention without trial; 1964 all other parties banned)
        -> closed politics leaves the ARMY as the only remaining
           instrument of change
        -> a coup, with EXTERNAL BACKING supplied because of the
           leader's foreign alignment
```

⚠️ And the decisive comparative point: ⚠️ **Ghana had the best preparation in British Africa** - the five supervised years the account calls unique - **and failed anyway**. ⚠️ Therefore "they were not ready" cannot be the explanation for post-colonial failure, because the readiest state failed for reasons preparation did not touch.

✅ The subsequent Ghanaian record confirms the structural reading rather than a personal one. ✅ **Dr Kofi Busia**, elected in October 1969, was overthrown by the army in January 1972; an academic who had studied economics at Oxford and had a reputation for honesty, he faced rising unemployment, rising prices, the low world price of cocoa and massive debts, and **debt repayments were using up about forty per cent of Ghana's export profits**; ✅ in 1971 imports were limited and the currency devalued by nearly fifty per cent. ✅ **Colonel Acheampong** followed, then **General Akuffo**, then **Jerry J. Rawlings** in June 1979, who returned Ghana to civilian rule under **Dr Hilla Limann** in September 1979; ✅ during 1981 inflation ran at **125 per cent** with widespread labour unrest, and Rawlings removed Limann in December 1981. ✅ Rawlings reluctantly turned to the IMF, production rose by seven per cent and inflation was down to forty per cent early in 1985; ✅ he was elected president in November 1992 with over fifty-eight per cent of the votes, re-elected in 1996, and could not stand again in 2000; ✅ **John Kufuor** took over in January 2001, continuing stability and prosperity, diversifying the economy, reforming the National Health System and starting the Ghana School Feeding Programme in 2005; ✅ the NDC's J. E. A. Mills won the narrowest of victories in 2008. ✅ Ghana continued to be regarded as one of the most stable, prosperous and generally successful democracies in the whole of Africa.

⚠️ That last sentence is essential and is routinely omitted. ⚠️ The state whose first republic collapsed in 1966 was, by the 2000s, among the continent's most successful democracies. ⚠️ **Post-colonial difficulty was a phase, not a destiny**, and the Ghanaian trajectory across fifty years is the best available evidence for that proposition.

### 3. Mechanism two: Nigeria, and federalism meeting territorial identity

⚠️ Lesson 10 taught the design and its failure. ⚠️ Here the war and its aftermath complete the mechanism.

✅ It took more than the "short surgical police action" Gowon had described, as the Biafrans fought back vigorously. ✅ **Britain and the USSR supplied Gowon with arms, and France supplied Biafra.** ✅ It was a bitter and terrible war, in which **Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in the fighting**. ✅ Neither the UN, the Commonwealth nor the Organization of African Unity was able to mediate, and the Biafrans held on to the bitter end as Nigerian troops closed in on all sides. ✅ The final surrender came in **January 1970**. ✅ Nigerian unity had been preserved.

⚠️ Two observations. ⚠️ First, on the arming: ✅ Britain and the Soviet Union armed the same side while France armed the other, which cuts directly across the Cold War alignment and is a useful corrective to any assumption that African conflicts mapped onto the superpower division. ⚠️ Second, on the casualties: ✅ Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in the fighting. ❌ Do not attach a total; the account gives none. ⚠️ The composition of the loss is the examinable fact, and it identifies the war's decisive weapon as blockade rather than battle.

✅ **The counter-evidence is as important as the war and must be given.** ✅ Recovery after the war was **remarkably swift**. ✅ There were pressing problems - famine in Biafra, inter-ethnic bitterness, unemployment and economic resources strained by the war - but **Gowon showed considerable statesmanship**. ✅ There was no revenge-taking, as the Ibos had feared, and Gowon made every effort to reconcile them, persuading them to return to their jobs in other parts of the country. ✅ He introduced a new federal system of **twelve states, later increased to nineteen**, to give more recognition of local differences - a pragmatic move in a country with so much ethnic diversity. ✅ Nigeria was able to take advantage of rising oil prices in the mid-1970s, which gave it a healthy balance of payments.

⚠️ This is the single most important corrective in the whole lesson. ⚠️ **Institutional redesign worked.** ⚠️ The three-region federation had made every distributive dispute a secession question, because three units of comparable weight mean that any two can dominate the third. ⚠️ Twelve, and then nineteen, states broke the arithmetic that had made secession rational. ⚠️ Any answer claiming that ethnic division condemns post-colonial states must explain why the redesign after 1970 succeeded where the design of 1954 failed - and the answer is the number of units, not the depth of the divisions.

✅ The later record shows the commodity mechanism operating exactly as in Ghana. ✅ During 1981 the economy got into difficulties: Nigeria had relied too heavily on oil exports, world oil prices fell, and the healthy trade balance of 1980 became a deficit in 1983; ✅ President Shagari, re-elected in August 1983, was removed by a military coup that December, the new leader Major-General Bukhari alleging mismanagement of the economy, financial corruption and rigging of the election; ✅ Bukhari was himself overthrown in August 1985; ✅ a further dramatic fall in oil prices in 1986 reached a record low of **ten dollars a barrel** in June against a budget calculated on **$23.50 a barrel**, forcing a World Bank loan. ✅ Corruption on a large scale is recorded, including reports that over **twelve billion dollars** in oil revenues went missing during Babangida's period in power. ✅ Civilian rule returned with Obasanjo in May 1999; ✅ UN statistics showed **between sixty-six and seventy per cent** of the population living in poverty by 2004, compared with **48.5 per cent** as recently as 1998; ✅ and by 2004 Nigeria had been exporting oil for more than thirty years, earning over **$250 billion** in revenue, while ordinary people had seen very little benefit and the ruling elites had amassed huge fortunes.

⚠️ That final comparison - thirty years of oil exports, $250 billion earned, very little benefit to ordinary people - is the most economical statement available of the difference between revenue and development, and it belongs in any answer on resource dependence.

### 4. Mechanism three: Tanzania, and the alternative that the world economy defeated

✅ **Tanganyika became independent in 1961** and was joined in **1964** by Zanzibar to form **Tanzania**, ruled by **Dr Julius Nyerere** of TANU, who faced formidable problems: ✅ Tanzania was one of the poorest states in the whole of Africa; ✅ there was very little industry, few mineral resources and a heavy dependence on coffee production; ✅ and later Tanzania became involved in expensive military operations to overthrow President Idi Amin of Uganda and provided help and training for nationalist guerrillas from countries like Zimbabwe. ✅ On the other hand, ethnic problems were not as serious as elsewhere, and the **Swahili** language provided a common bond.

✅ **The programme.** ✅ Nyerere's approach was different from that of any other African ruler. ✅ He began conventionally by expanding the economy: during the first ten years of independence, production of coffee and cotton doubled and sugar production trebled, while health services and education expanded. ✅ But he was not happy that Tanzania seemed to be developing along the same lines as Kenya, with an ever-widening gulf between the wealthy elite and the resentful masses. ✅ His solution was set out in a remarkable document known as the **Arusha Declaration**, published in **1967**: all human beings should be treated as equal; the state must have effective control over the means of production and intervene in economic life to ensure people were not exploited and that poverty and disease were eliminated; there must be no great accumulations of wealth, or society would no longer be classless; and bribery and corruption must be eliminated. ✅ According to Nyerere, Tanzania was at war and the enemy was poverty and oppression; the way to victory was not through money and foreign aid but through **hard work and self-reliance**, and the first priority was to improve agriculture so the country could be self-sufficient in food.

✅ **The instruments.** ✅ All important enterprises, including those owned by foreigners, were nationalised; five-year development plans were introduced; ✅ village projects were encouraged and given government aid, involving **ujamaa** - "familyhood", or self-help - in which families in each village pooled resources and farmed in co-operatives, small but viable units operating collectively and able to use more modern techniques. ✅ Foreign loans and investments as well as imports were reduced to a minimum to avoid running into debt. ✅ Politically, Nyerere's brand of socialism meant a one-party state run by TANU, but elections were still held, and **some elements of genuine democracy existed, since voters in each constituency had a choice of two TANU candidates and every election resulted in a large proportion of MPs losing their seats**. ✅ Nyerere himself provided dignified leadership and, with his simple lifestyle and complete indifference to wealth, set the perfect example.

⚠️ That detail about two TANU candidates per constituency and large numbers of MPs losing their seats is important and rarely used. ⚠️ It shows that "one-party state" covers a range of arrangements, from Nkrumah's ban on all other parties in 1964 to a system with real intra-party competition and high incumbent turnover. ⚠️ **Do not treat single-party government as a single category.**

✅ **The results, at retirement in 1985.** ✅ At an international conference on the Arusha Declaration in December 1986, President Mwinyi gave social statistics that few other African countries could match: **3.7 million children in primary school; two universities with over 4,500 students in total; a literacy rate of 85 per cent; 150 hospitals and 2,600 dispensaries; infant mortality down to 137 per thousand; life expectancy up to 52.**

✅ **The failures.** ✅ Corruption crept in because many officials were not as high-minded as Nyerere himself. ✅ There was insufficient investment in agriculture so that production was far below what was expected. ✅ The nationalisation of the sisal estates carried out in the 1960s was a failure, and Nyerere reversed it in May 1985. ✅ From the end of 1978 Tanzania was in difficulties because of the fall in world prices of coffee and tea, its main exports; rising oil prices, which used up almost half its export earnings; and the expense of the war against Amin in Uganda, **at least £1,000 million**. ✅ Loans from the IMF only brought the added problem of meeting the interest repayments. ✅ **Tanzania was nowhere near being a socialist state, nor was it self-sufficient - two major aims of the Declaration.**

✅ The verdict the account offers is the one to quote: ✅ **Nyerere's socialist experiment might have worked well in a closed economy, but unfortunately Tanzania was becoming part of the "global village", exposed to the vagaries of the world economy.**

⚠️ That judgement is the mechanism. ⚠️ Self-reliance is a coherent strategy only if the terms on which a country meets the world economy are within its control. ⚠️ Tanzania's coffee and tea prices, its oil import bill and its interest rates were all set elsewhere, and a doctrine of self-reliance cannot make an export economy self-reliant by resolving to be so. ⚠️ **The Arusha experiment failed at the point where every other case in this lesson failed - the terms of trade - and the difference is only that it had tried hardest to avoid them.**

⚠️ Nyerere's own standing should be recorded, because it is part of the historical judgement. ✅ He was deservedly highly respected both as an African and as a world statesman, as an enemy of apartheid, and as an outspoken critic of the world economy and the way it exploited poor countries; ✅ he played a vital role in the overthrow of Idi Amin; ✅ and his prestige was at its height when he was chosen as chairman of the OAU for 1984-5.

### 5. Mechanism four: Angola, and the lengthening of a war

✅ Angola was engulfed by civil war immediately after gaining independence from Portugal in **1975**. ✅ Part of the problem was that there were **three different liberation movements** which started to fight each other almost as soon as independence was declared. ✅ The **MPLA** was a Marxist-style party which tried to appeal across ethnic divisions to all Angolans and claimed to be the new government, with **Agostinho Neto** as president. ✅ **UNITA**, under **Jonas Savimbi**, drew much of its support from the Ovimbundu in the south. ✅ The **FNLA**, much weaker than the other two, drew much of its support from the Bakongo in the north-west.

✅ Alarm bells immediately rang in the USA, which did not like the look of the Marxist MPLA; the Americans decided to back the FNLA, also supported by Mobutu of Zaire, providing advisers, cash and armaments and encouraging it to attack the MPLA. ✅ UNITA also launched an offensive. ✅ **Cuba sent troops** to help the MPLA, while **South African troops**, supporting the other two groups, invaded Angola via neighbouring **Namibia**, and **Mobutu also sent troops in from Zaire**.

✅ **The verdict to memorise:** ✅ **"No doubt there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War to Angola certainly made the conflict much worse."**

⚠️ That formulation is the model of balance for this whole topic and it should be adopted as a template. ⚠️ It concedes the internal cause, denies that the external one created the conflict, and attributes to the external one a precise effect - making it worse. ❌ Never write that the Cold War caused African civil wars. ⚠️ Write that external patronage converted finite internal contests into indefinitely resourced ones, because neither side can be defeated while its supplier remains.

✅ **The Namibian link.** ✅ Namibia, formerly German South West Africa, had been handed to South Africa in 1919 to be prepared for independence; ✅ the white South African government ignored UN orders and delayed handing it over as long as possible; ✅ the Namibian liberation movement **SWAPO**, under **Sam Nujoma**, began a guerrilla campaign; ✅ after 1975 the MPLA allowed SWAPO to have bases in southern Angola, which is why South Africa was so hostile to the MPLA.

⚠️ Note the shape of that: a First World War mandate, still undischarged in the 1970s, became the reason a Cold War patron invaded a newly decolonised neighbour. ⚠️ Three periods of the syllabus intersect in one sentence, and saying so is genuine value addition.

✅ **The settlement, and what made it possible.** ✅ The civil war dragged on through the 1980s until changing international circumstances brought the possibility of peace. ✅ In **December 1988** the UN managed to arrange a settlement in which **South Africa agreed to withdraw from Namibia provided that the 50,000 Cuban troops left Angola**. ✅ This went ahead: Namibia became independent under Nujoma in **1990**; ✅ the end of the Cold War and of communist rule in eastern Europe meant that all communist support for the MPLA ceased, all Cuban troops had gone home by **June 1991**, and South Africa was ready to end her involvement; ✅ the UN, the OAU, the USA and Russia all played a part in setting up peace talks in **Lisbon in May 1991**, agreeing a ceasefire followed by UN-monitored elections.

⚠️ That bargain is worth stating precisely because it is the clearest evidence in the session that the Cold War was the binding constraint. ⚠️ One decolonisation - Namibia's - was traded against the withdrawal of one patron's army. ⚠️ **The last mandate of 1919 became independent as the price of ending a proxy deployment.**

✅ **The failure of the peace, and where blame lies.** ✅ Elections took place in September 1992: the MPLA won 58 per cent and 129 seats, UNITA 31 per cent and 70 seats; in the presidential contest **dos Santos** won 49.57 per cent against Savimbi's 40.07 per cent, and the leader of the UN team reported that the election had been **generally free and fair**. ✅ Tragically UNITA, instead of accepting defeat gracefully, renewed the civil war. ✅ By the end of January 1994 the UN reported **3.3 million refugees** and an average of **a thousand people a day, mainly civilians, dying**. ✅ **This time the outside world could not be blamed for the civil war: this was clearly the fault of UNITA.** ✅ However, many observers blamed the USA for encouraging UNITA, since shortly before the Lisbon agreement President Reagan had officially met Savimbi in the USA, making him seem an equal of the MPLA government rather than a rebel leader, and the USA did not recognise the MPLA as Angola's legal government until **May 1993**, six months after UNITA had resumed the war.

⚠️ Note the account's own even-handedness: it says plainly that the renewed war was UNITA's fault, and then records the American conduct that had encouraged it. ⚠️ That is the model for how to handle responsibility in a proxy conflict - primary agency local, aggravating conduct external.

✅ A ceasefire came in October 1994 and a peace agreement in November, ✅ but Savimbi financed his forces with the proceeds of **illicit diamond sales** and continued until **February 2002**, when he was killed in an ambush by government troops; ✅ his death changed the situation dramatically and a ceasefire was signed in April 2002.

✅ **The cost, with attribution.** ✅ At the end of the twentieth century inflation was running at **240 per cent**; ✅ according to the **IMF**, over **four billion dollars** of oil receipts had disappeared from the treasury since 1996; ✅ **Human Rights Watch** reported that UNITA had employed **86,000 child soldiers** and government forces **3,000**; ✅ the two armies between them had laid some **15 million landmines**; ✅ and it was calculated that about **four million people**, a third of the population, had been forced to leave their homes and were homeless in 2002, while **1.5 million had been killed**.

⚠️ Carry each figure with its owner: the IMF for the missing oil receipts, Human Rights Watch for the child soldiers, and the UN report of January 1994 for the refugee and daily mortality figures. ❌ Do not merge them.

### 6. Mechanism five: Rwanda and Burundi, and identity made rather than inherited

⚠️ Lesson 19 established the colonial origin. ⚠️ Here is what followed, and the evidence must be handled with the greatest care of anything in this session.

✅ **The framing that defeats a primordial reading.** ✅ The Tutsi and the Hutu **spoke the same language and looked very much alike**; although the Hutu were in a majority, the Tutsi were the elite ruling group, and they followed different occupations - the Tutsi raised cattle, the word Tutsi meaning "rich in cattle", whereas the Hutu were mainly farmers growing bananas and other crops, the word Hutu meaning "servant". ✅ There was continuous tension and skirmishing between the two right from independence day in **1962**. ✅ The Belgians had left both states, like the Congo, **completely unprepared for independence**.

✅ **Burundi.** ✅ In **1993** the country held its first democratic elections and for the first time a Hutu president was chosen. ✅ **Tutsi soldiers soon murdered the new president, in October 1993**, but other members of the Hutu government were able to escape. ✅ As Hutu carried out reprisal killings against Tutsi, massacre followed massacre: ✅ **around fifty thousand Tutsi were killed** and the country disintegrated into chaos. ⚠️ That figure is the textbook account's; it names no producing body and no closing date for the count, so it must be reported as that account's statement rather than as a verified total, and ❌ it must never be merged with any other. ✅ Eventually the army imposed a power-sharing agreement with a Tutsi prime minister and a Hutu president, though most of the power was concentrated in the hands of the Tutsi prime minister. ✅ Fighting continued into 1996, and the **Organization of African Unity**, which sent a peacekeeping force - **the first time it had ever taken such action** - was unable to prevent the continuing massacres and ethnic cleansing. ✅ The economy was in ruins and agricultural production seriously reduced because much of the rural population had fled. ✅ **The outside world and the great powers showed little concern - their interests were not involved or threatened - and the conflict in Burundi was not given much coverage in the world's media.**

⚠️ That last sentence should be quoted whenever a question asks about international responses to African conflicts. ⚠️ It states the operating rule of the period plainly: intervention followed interest, and where no interest was engaged there was neither intervention nor coverage.

✅ Later, mediation by several former African presidents including **Nyerere** and **Mandela** produced an agreement at **Arusha in October 2001** with a three-year transitional period and an international peacekeeping force; ✅ the main Hutu rebel party signed a ceasefire in December 2002; ✅ and under President **Nkurunziza** a ceasefire with the last of the rebel militias was reached in 2006, with new policies to safeguard the rights of women and children and free primary education.

✅ **Rwanda.** ✅ Ethnic warfare began in 1959 before independence and reached its first big climax in 1963, when the Hutu, fearing a Tutsi invasion from Burundi, massacred thousands of Rwandan Tutsi and overthrew the Tutsi government. ✅ In 1990 fighting broke out between the Tutsi-dominated **Rwandese Patriotic Front (FPR)**, based over the border in Uganda, and the Hutu-dominated official Rwandan army. ✅ This lasted off and on until **1993**, when the UN helped to negotiate a peace settlement at **Arusha** in Tanzania providing for a more broadly based government including the FPR, with **2,500 UN troops** sent to monitor the transition in October 1993.

✅ The more extreme Hutu were bitterly opposed to the Arusha peace plan and shocked by the murder of the Hutu president of Burundi; ✅ extremist Hutu who had formed their own militia, the **Interahamwe**, decided to act. ✅ **The aircraft bringing the moderate Hutu President Habyarimana of Rwanda and the Burundian president back from talks in Tanzania was brought down by a missile, apparently fired by extremist Hutu, as it approached Kigali, killing both presidents in April 1994.** ✅ With the president dead, nobody was sure who was giving the orders, and this gave the Interahamwe the cover they needed to launch **a campaign of genocide**.

✅ **Altogether about eight hundred thousand Tutsi and moderate Hutu who tried to help their neighbours were brutally murdered in what was clearly a deliberate and carefully planned attempt to wipe out the entire Tutsi population of Rwanda, and it was backed by the Hutu government of Rwanda.** ⚠️ The figure of about eight hundred thousand is the textbook account's; it names no producing body and no counting period, and published estimates for the hundred days from April 1994 differ from one another. ✅ Report it as that account's figure for 1994, or withhold it; ❌ never present it as a verified count and ❌ never combine it with the Burundian figure. ✅ A favourite technique was to persuade Tutsi to take sanctuary in churches and then destroy the church buildings and the sheltering Tutsi; even nuns and clergy were caught up in the massacre. ✅ The Tutsi FPR responded by marching on the capital; ✅ UN observers reported that the streets of Kigali were literally running with blood. ✅ **The small UN force was not equipped to deal with violence on this scale, and it soon withdrew.** ✅ In addition to those killed, about **a million Tutsi refugees** had fled into neighbouring Tanzania and Zaire.

✅ **The failures of prevention are documented and must be included.** ✅ The historian **Linda Melvern** has shown how the warning signs were ignored by all those who might have prevented the genocide; ✅ she claims that Belgium and France both knew what was being planned, and that as early as the spring of 1992 the Belgian ambassador told his government that extremist Hutu were planning the extermination of the Tutsi of Rwanda once and for all and to crush the internal Hutu opposition. ✅ The French continued to supply the Hutu with arms throughout the genocide. ✅ And it was not only the West and the UN that turned a blind eye: **the Organization of African Unity did not even condemn the genocide, let alone try to prevent it, nor did any other African state take any action or issue public condemnation**; ✅ arguably African attention was focused on the new democracy in South Africa rather than on halting the genocide in Rwanda.

⚠️ **The conceptual discipline required here is absolute.** ⚠️ **Genocide** means an attempt to destroy a group as such, evidenced here by intent, planning and state backing - the account states that it was clearly a deliberate and carefully planned attempt to wipe out the entire Tutsi population, backed by the Hutu government. ⚠️ **Apartheid** was a statutory system of racial subordination, not extermination. ⚠️ **Civil war** is an organised armed contest for control of a state or for secession, as in Nigeria in 1967-70 or Angola from 1975. ⚠️ ❌ These three words are not interchangeable, and using one for another is a serious error rather than a stylistic one. ⚠️ Rwanda in 1994 contained a civil war **and** a genocide, which is why both words are needed and why neither alone is sufficient.

✅ **After.** ✅ A power-sharing arrangement made the moderate Hutu **Pasteur Bizimungu** president with **Paul Kagame**, a Tutsi, as vice-president, though Kagame was the real policy decider; Bizimungu was removed in 2000 and Kagame took over. ✅ Jails were overflowing with well over **a hundred thousand prisoners** awaiting trial for involvement in the genocide, and in January 2003 Kagame ordered the release of around **forty thousand**, to the consternation of survivors. ✅ A new constitution in 2003 provided for a balance of political power between Hutu and Tutsi, with no party able to hold more than half the seats in parliament, and outlawed the incitement of ethnic hatred. ✅ In February 2004 a reconciliation policy released those who admitted guilt and asked forgiveness before 15 March 2004, except those accused of organising the genocide - hoped, like the South African Truth and Reconciliation Commission, to help Rwandans come to terms with the past. ✅ Economic and social conditions improved: corruption and crime were reduced; **between 2000 and 2008 per capita income doubled**; **almost half the country's children were receiving a full primary education, compared with twenty per cent before Kagame came to power**; life expectancy rose markedly; antiretroviral drugs became available in health centres; tea and coffee exports increased and tourism became an important source of revenue. ✅ In 2009 Rwanda was accepted as a member of the Commonwealth, an attempt to distance the country from its Belgian past. ✅ Kagame was decisively re-elected in August 2010, although observers expressed doubts about how free the elections really were, several opposition supporters and journalists were killed during the campaign and press freedom was limited, and the UN, the European Union and the USA all expressed concerns.

⚠️ Report both halves. ⚠️ A recovery that is real and an electoral practice that is contested are both in the record, and an answer that gives only one has selected.

### 7. Mechanism six: Zimbabwe, and the question the settlement deferred

⚠️ Lesson 13 taught the settlement. ⚠️ Here is the mechanism it created.

✅ **The first decade earns praise.** ✅ Mugabe had been an uncompromising guerrilla leader with Marxist opinions, but he soon showed that he was capable of moderation and pledged himself to work for reconciliation and unity, calming the fears of the white farmers and businessmen who had remained and **who were necessary for the economy to flourish**. ✅ He formed a coalition with Nkomo's ZAPU, kept the Lancaster House promise of twenty guaranteed white seats in the hundred-seat parliament, and introduced wage increases, food subsidies and better social services, health care and education. ✅ Many commentators felt that in his first few years he showed great statesmanship and deserved credit for keeping his country relatively peaceful.

✅ **The internal conflict.** ✅ The most serious early problem was the long-standing hostility between ZANU, supported mainly by the Shona, and ZAPU, supported by the Ndebele in Matabeleland. ✅ In 1982 Nkomo was accused of planning a coup; Mugabe forced him to resign and had many leading ZAPU members arrested. ✅ Nkomo's supporters in Matabeleland retaliated with violence and **were brutally suppressed**. ✅ Resistance continued until the **Unity Accord of 1987**, under which ZANU and ZAPU united as ZANU-PF, Mugabe became executive president with Nkomo a vice-president, and the reserved white seats were abolished.

✅ **The economic pressure.** ✅ Although in years of good harvests Zimbabwe was regarded as the breadbasket of southern Africa, success depended heavily on the weather, and the 1980s brought more than the usual periods of drought together with high world oil prices. ✅ After the collapse of the USSR, Mugabe abandoned most of his Marxist policies and attempted western free-market methods, accepting an IMF loan and agreeing, very much against public opinion, to abide by the ESAP, which involved unpopular cuts in public spending on social services and jobs. ✅ A severe drought in 1992 compounded the difficulties.

✅ **The land question, deferred in 1979, returns.** ✅ **About four thousand white farmers had stayed on after independence, and between them they owned about half the country's arable land.** ✅ Squatters occupied hundreds of white-owned farms, the government encouraged them and the police gave the farmers no protection, so the occupied areas were not cultivated, adding to the food supply problem. ✅ From **February 2000** men claiming to be veterans of the war for independence began the **systematic and violent occupation** of white-owned farms, continuing for four years and **clearly a deliberate policy organised by the government**. ✅ When the UK protested, Mugabe claimed it was the fault of the British, who had broken their Lancaster House promise to provide adequate compensation to white farmers; ✅ Britain declared itself willing to pay extra compensation provided that the confiscated land went to **ordinary peasant farmers rather than to members of Mugabe's ruling elite**, and that the elections due in June 2000 were free and fair.

✅ **The political timing matters.** ✅ In February 2000 the people had rejected a new pro-Mugabe draft constitution, a clear indication that his popularity had dwindled, which probably led him to take whatever measures were necessary to win the June elections. ✅ There was widespread violence and intimidation of the opposition before and during the election and international observers were severely restricted; even so the result was close, with ZANU-PF winning 62 seats in a 150-seat parliament and the opposition **Movement for Democratic Change** 57, though the president's right to nominate 30 members preserved a comfortable majority. ✅ The MDC had support from trade unions and from the prominent, mainly white, **Commercial Farmers' Union**.

✅ The consequences followed. ✅ The EU condemned the policy and imposed sanctions in February 2002; the Commonwealth expelled Zimbabwe for one year; and the World Bank cut off funding because of debt arrears which had risen to over **$380 million**. ✅ President Mbeki of South Africa claimed the land seizures were a violation of the rule of law and ought to stop, but urged a conciliatory approach and refused economic sanctions, which would only ruin an already ailing economy; ✅ the majority of black African states expressed sympathy and support for Mugabe. ✅ Internally, press and judicial independence were curtailed: the one remaining independent daily newspaper's journalists were harassed, MDC leader **Morgan Tsvangirai** was charged with plotting to overthrow the president, three Supreme Court judges who criticised the land policy were sacked and replaced by the president's nominees, public meetings were banned except those of Mugabe's supporters, and it became an offence to undermine the authority of the president by publishing statements that provoke hostility. ✅ Mugabe won the March 2002 presidential election with 56 per cent against Tsvangirai's 42 per cent; Tsvangirai called it the biggest electoral fraud he had seen in his life.

⚠️ **The mechanism, stated cleanly:** a settlement that secures political power while leaving the colonial distribution of productive assets untouched leaves the land question available as a political instrument twenty years later, to be deployed by a government under electoral pressure. ⚠️ Both halves are needed. ⚠️ The grievance was genuine - four thousand farmers owning about half the arable land is a colonial distribution surviving into the twenty-first century. ⚠️ And the timing was political - the seizures began in the month a pro-Mugabe constitution was rejected, and Britain's stated condition was precisely that the land should go to ordinary peasant farmers rather than to the ruling elite.

### 8. The counter-evidence: why this is not determinism

⚠️ Everything above could be read as an argument that post-colonial states were doomed. ⚠️ That reading is wrong and the same evidence base refutes it. ⚠️ Assemble the refutation, because a twenty-mark answer that stops at the structural argument has not finished.

| ⚠️ Fatalistic reading | ✅ Counter-evidence from the same record |
|---|---|
| Ethnic division makes federations unviable | ✅ Nigeria's recovery after 1970 was remarkably swift; Gowon took no revenge, reconciled the Ibos and expanded the federation from twelve states to nineteen |
| One-party rule always degrades government | ✅ In Kenya and Tanzania it worked well, providing stable and effective government, and Tanzanian voters chose between two candidates with large numbers of MPs losing their seats |
| Post-colonial states could not build democracies | ✅ Ghana came to be regarded as one of the most stable, prosperous and generally successful democracies in the whole of Africa, with power changing hands by election in 2001 and 2008 |
| The Cold War caused African civil wars | ✅ The account's own formulation: there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference made the conflict much worse |
| Mass atrocity was inevitable given ethnic difference | ✅ Rwanda's post-1994 record - corruption and crime reduced, per capita income doubled between 2000 and 2008, primary education rising from twenty per cent to almost half of children, a constitution capping any party at half the seats and outlawing incitement |
| Colonial inheritance determines outcomes | ⚠️ Ghana had the best preparation in British Africa and failed in 1966; the Congo had the worst and failed too; Botswana, Kenya and Tanzania inherited comparable constraints with different results |

⚠️ The formulation that holds both halves together, and which is the strongest available conclusion to any question on post-colonial African instability: **the inheritance set the constraints and choices made inside them determined the outcome.** ⚠️ Undiversified economies, thin administrative classes, territorially concentrated identities, unresolved land distribution and external intervention narrowed the options available to every new government; closed parties, rigged elections, personal enrichment and coercive state-building explain why some states deteriorated more sharply than others; and Gowon's reconstruction after 1970 shows that inherited difficulty did not make failure inevitable.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This is the syllabus's "effect on society" clause in its fullest form, and the standard demand is on why newly independent African states experienced instability.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect "why did independent Africa face coups, civil wars and one-party rule so often", or a quotation about decolonisation transferring sovereignty faster than it built stable states. ⚠️ Structure: the four inherited problems with the deliberate-policy point foregrounded; two or three named cases at ten marks, three or four at fifteen, five with a counter-case at twenty; and the inheritance-and-choice formulation as the verdict.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Explaining instability by ethnic difference alone. ✅ The account attributes it to borders, weak institutions, military politics and outside intervention, and records the deliberate colonial suppression of industry.
2. ⚠️ Using post-colonial difficulty to argue that decolonisation was premature. ⚠️ These were state-building crises whose causes were largely colonial products, and Ghana's exceptional preparation did not prevent its collapse.
3. ⚠️ Claiming the Cold War caused African civil wars. ✅ Outside interference made the Angolan conflict much worse; it did not create it, and the renewed war of 1992 was clearly the fault of UNITA.
4. ⚠️ Confusing genocide, apartheid and civil war. ⚠️ Rwanda 1994 was a genocide, on evidence of intent, planning and government backing, running concurrently with a civil war.
5. ❌ Merging attributed figures, or reporting them without a producer. ✅ Around fifty thousand Tutsi killed in Burundi in 1993 and about eight hundred thousand murdered in Rwanda in 1994 with about a million refugees - both figures as stated by the textbook account, which names no producing body for either; 1.5 million killed and four million displaced in Angola by 2002, on a calculation reported at the time; each belongs to its own event and source.
6. ⚠️ Omitting the recoveries. ✅ Nigeria after 1970, Ghana by the 2000s and Rwanda after 1994 all recorded substantial recovery.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Common problem one - ethnic difference: groups held together only by colonial rulers, with little incentive to stay together once the Europeans withdrew; in Nigeria, the Congo, Burundi and Rwanda this led to civil war.
2. ✅ Common problem two - economic under-development: very little industry as a deliberate colonial policy, colonies to buy manufactures from Europe or the USA and supply food and raw materials; dependence on one or two export commodities, so a price fall was a major disaster; Nigeria's oil producing about eighty per cent of annual income; shortage of capital and skills; population growth over two per cent a year; debt; export-led repayment making food scarcer at home; dependence on western markets and investment enabling control - neo-colonialism.
3. ✅ Common problem three - political inexperience: unfamiliarity with parliamentary systems; corruption; one-party states influenced by Marxist ideas, which worked well in Kenya and Tanzania; where legal opposition was impossible violence was the only answer and military coups became common; Nkrumah removed by the army in 1966 after two failed assassination attempts; in Malawi the one-party system flourished at the expense of freedom.
4. ✅ Common problem four - disasters: the 1980s world recession cutting demand for oil, copper and cobalt; drought 1982-5 with crop failures, livestock deaths, famine and starvation; record harvests in 1986; a severe debt crisis; IMF-imposed ESAP austerity with devaluation, reduced food subsidies, higher food prices, rising unemployment, falling wages and cuts to education, health and social services.
5. ✅ Ghana's achievements: cocoa production doubled; forestry, fishing and cattle-breeding expanded; gold and bauxite better exploited; the Volta dam begun 1961 for irrigation, hydro-electricity and bauxite smelting; village road and school projects; international prestige and Commonwealth membership alongside links with the USSR, East Germany and China.
6. ✅ Ghana's failures: too-rapid industrialisation on vast foreign borrowing; continued cocoa dependence and a steep price fall producing a huge balance-of-payments deficit; the smelter's American owner buying bauxite abroad; wasteful projects.
7. ✅ Ghana's political closure: the Preventive Detention Act of 1958, with detention or deportation without trial from 1959; Danquah arrested 1961 and dying in prison; all other parties banned in 1964; the "father of the nation" cult; belief that he had amassed a personal fortune; the army's coup in 1966 during a visit to China, with American encouragement because of his links with communist states, which the textbook account calls full CIA backing though the claim is contested.
8. ✅ Busia's fall in January 1972 with debt repayments using about forty per cent of export profits and a near-fifty-per-cent devaluation in 1971; Acheampong, Akuffo, Rawlings from June 1979, Limann, 125 per cent inflation in 1981, Rawlings again from December 1981; IMF austerity, seven per cent production growth and forty per cent inflation by early 1985; Rawlings elected 1992 with over 58 per cent and re-elected 1996; Kufuor from January 2001; the Ghana School Feeding Programme from 2005; Mills's narrow win in 2008; Ghana regarded as one of Africa's most stable and successful democracies.
9. ✅ Biafra: Britain and the USSR armed Gowon, France armed Biafra; Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in fighting; the UN, Commonwealth and OAU could not mediate; surrender January 1970 with Nigerian unity preserved.
10. ✅ Nigeria's recovery was remarkably swift: no revenge-taking, Gowon reconciling the Ibos, and a federal system expanded from twelve states to nineteen.
11. ✅ Later Nigeria: oil dependence producing a 1983 deficit, coups in 1983 and 1985, oil at a record low of $10 a barrel in June 1986 against a budget assuming $23.50; over $12 billion of oil revenues reported missing under Babangida; Obasanjo from May 1999; UN figures of 66-70 per cent in poverty by 2004 against 48.5 per cent in 1998; over $250 billion earned from oil in thirty years with very little benefit to ordinary people.
12. ✅ Tanzania: Tanganyika independent 1961, joined by Zanzibar in 1964; one of the poorest states in Africa, with little industry, few minerals and heavy dependence on coffee, but fewer ethnic problems and Swahili as a common bond.
13. ✅ The Arusha Declaration of 1967: equality; state control of the means of production; no great accumulations of wealth; elimination of bribery and corruption; victory through hard work and self-reliance rather than money and foreign aid; first priority self-sufficiency in food.
14. ✅ Instruments: nationalisation of all important enterprises including foreign-owned ones; five-year plans; ujamaa village co-operatives; foreign loans, investment and imports minimised; a one-party state under TANU but with two TANU candidates per constituency and large numbers of MPs losing their seats.
15. ✅ Results reported in December 1986: 3.7 million children in primary school; two universities with over 4,500 students; 85 per cent literacy; 150 hospitals and 2,600 dispensaries; infant mortality down to 137 per thousand; life expectancy 52.
