16. ✅ Failures: corruption; insufficient agricultural investment; the sisal nationalisation reversed in May 1985; from late 1978 falling coffee and tea prices, oil prices using almost half export earnings, and the Uganda war costing at least £1,000 million; IMF interest repayments; neither socialist nor self-sufficient. ✅ Verdict: the experiment might have worked in a closed economy, but Tanzania was exposed to the vagaries of the world economy.
17. ✅ Angola: three movements - the MPLA under Neto appealing across ethnic divisions, UNITA under Savimbi based on the Ovimbundu, and the weaker FNLA on the Bakongo; American advisers, cash and armaments to the FNLA; Cuban troops for the MPLA; South African invasion via Namibia; Mobutu's troops from Zaire.
18. ✅ The verdict: there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War certainly made the conflict much worse.
19. ✅ Namibia, German South West Africa handed to South Africa in 1919 to be prepared for independence, was withheld; SWAPO under Sam Nujoma fought a guerrilla campaign with bases in southern Angola after 1975; the December 1988 UN settlement traded South African withdrawal from Namibia against the departure of the 50,000 Cuban troops; Namibia independent 1990; Cuban troops home by June 1991; Lisbon accords May 1991.
20. ✅ September 1992 elections: MPLA 58 per cent and 129 seats, UNITA 31 per cent and 70 seats; dos Santos 49.57 per cent against Savimbi's 40.07 per cent; the UN team reported the election generally free and fair; UNITA renewed the war, which was clearly its fault, though Reagan's official meeting with Savimbi and the delay of American recognition of the MPLA until May 1993 had encouraged it; by end-January 1994 the UN reported 3.3 million refugees and about a thousand deaths a day, mainly civilians; Savimbi financed the war with illicit diamond sales until killed in February 2002.
21. ✅ Angolan costs: inflation 240 per cent; the IMF recording over $4 billion of oil receipts missing since 1996; Human Rights Watch reporting 86,000 UNITA child soldiers and 3,000 in government forces; some 15 million landmines laid; about four million displaced and 1.5 million killed on a calculation reported in 2002.
22. ✅ Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language and looked very much alike, differing by occupation - "rich in cattle" against "servant" - with a Tutsi elite and a Hutu majority; tension from independence day in 1962; the Belgians left both states completely unprepared.
23. ✅ Burundi: first democratic elections in 1993 produced a Hutu president, murdered by Tutsi soldiers in October 1993; reprisal killings followed and around fifty thousand Tutsi were killed, on the textbook account's unattributed figure; the OAU's first-ever peacekeeping deployment could not stop the massacres; the great powers showed little concern because their interests were not involved, and the conflict received little media coverage; Arusha agreement October 2001 with Nyerere's and Mandela's mediation; ceasefire with the last militias in 2006 under Nkurunziza, with rights for women and children and free primary education.
24. ✅ Rwanda: fighting from 1990 between the Tutsi-dominated FPR based in Uganda and the Hutu-dominated army; an Arusha settlement in 1993 with 2,500 UN troops; the aircraft carrying the Rwandan and Burundian presidents brought down by a missile in April 1994; the Interahamwe launched a campaign of genocide; about 800,000 Tutsi and moderate Hutu murdered in a deliberate and carefully planned attempt to wipe out the entire Tutsi population, backed by the Hutu government - the figure as stated by the textbook account, with no producing body named; churches used as death traps; the small UN force was not equipped and withdrew; about a million Tutsi refugees fled to Tanzania and Zaire.
25. ✅ Linda Melvern showed the warning signs were ignored; Belgium and France knew what was planned, the Belgian ambassador reporting it in spring 1992; France continued supplying arms during the genocide; the OAU did not even condemn it and no African state acted or issued public condemnation.
26. ✅ Rwanda after: power-sharing under Bizimungu then Kagame; over 100,000 awaiting trial with about 40,000 released in January 2003; a 2003 constitution capping any party at half the seats and outlawing incitement of ethnic hatred; a 2004 reconciliation policy; corruption and crime reduced; per capita income doubled between 2000 and 2008; primary education rising from twenty per cent of children to almost half; Commonwealth membership in 2009; Kagame re-elected in August 2010 amid killings of opposition supporters and journalists and limits on press freedom, with UN, EU and US concern.
27. ✅ Zimbabwe: moderation, reconciliation, a ZANU-ZAPU coalition, the twenty guaranteed white seats honoured, wage increases, food subsidies and better social services, health care and education; then Nkomo's forced resignation in 1982, brutal suppression in Matabeleland and the 1987 Unity Accord creating ZANU-PF with Mugabe as executive president and the white seats abolished.
28. ✅ Zimbabwe's economy: breadbasket in good years but weather-dependent; 1980s droughts and high oil prices; after the USSR's collapse, IMF loans and ESAP cuts against public opinion; severe drought in 1992.
29. ✅ About 4,000 white farmers owned about half the arable land; squatter occupations with government encouragement and no police protection; from February 2000 systematic violent occupation clearly organised by the government; Mugabe blamed Britain for breaking a Lancaster House compensation promise; Britain offered extra compensation provided the land went to ordinary peasant farmers rather than Mugabe's elite and the June 2000 elections were free and fair.
30. ✅ In February 2000 a pro-Mugabe draft constitution was rejected; the June 2000 election saw widespread violence and intimidation, ZANU-PF winning 62 of 150 seats against the MDC's 57, with 30 presidential nominees preserving a majority; the MDC drew support from trade unions and the mainly white Commercial Farmers' Union; EU sanctions February 2002, Commonwealth suspension for a year, World Bank funding cut over arrears above $380 million; Mbeki called the seizures a violation of the rule of law but refused sanctions; most black African states supported Mugabe; press and judicial independence curtailed; Mugabe won 56 per cent in March 2002 against Tsvangirai's 42 per cent.
31. ⚠️ Master formulation: the inheritance set the constraints and choices made inside them determined the outcome - which is why Ghana, the best-prepared state, failed in 1966, and why Nigeria, having fought a civil war, recovered remarkably swiftly by redesigning its federation from three regions into nineteen states.

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate argues that post-colonial African instability shows the new states were not ready for independence. Refute this using three separate pieces of evidence, and give the formulation you would use instead.

**Model answer:** The argument fails on three separate counts. First, the best-prepared state failed. Ghana had a small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals who gained five years of government under British supervision, an experience the account calls unique to Ghana, and Nkrumah's government was nevertheless removed by the army in 1966, after detention without trial from 1959, the banning of all other parties in 1964, a balance-of-payments crisis produced by a steep fall in the world cocoa price, and external interest in his alignment - the account states full CIA backing for the coup because of his links with communist states, which should be carried as a contested allegation rather than a settled fact, since American hostility, foreknowledge and encouragement are well attested while "full backing" states more than the declassified record establishes. None of those causes is one that preparation could have addressed. Second, the constraints themselves were colonial products rather than national deficiencies. Most African territories had very little industry because this had been a deliberate policy by the colonial powers, whose intention was that the colonies should buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and provide food and raw materials; the frontiers were European creations; and the successor classes had in some cases been suppressed by design, most explicitly in the Belgian Congo with its seventeen graduates. Third, states demonstrably succeeded once given the chance to redesign. Nigeria's recovery after January 1970 was remarkably swift: Gowon took no revenge, reconciled the Ibos and expanded the federation from twelve states to nineteen to give more recognition of local differences, and Ghana came to be regarded as one of the most stable, prosperous and generally successful democracies in the whole of Africa, with power changing hands by election in 2001 and 2008. The formulation to use is that the inheritance set the constraints and the choices made inside them determined the outcome: undiversified economies, thin administrative classes, territorially concentrated identities and external intervention narrowed the options for every government, while closed parties, rigged elections and personal enrichment explain why some states deteriorated more sharply than others.

**Misconception to avoid:** Writing that the Cold War caused Africa's civil wars. The account's own formulation on Angola is the model and should be copied exactly: there would no doubt have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War certainly made the conflict much worse. The same source also records that when the war resumed in 1992 after an election the UN team had reported generally free and fair, this was clearly the fault of UNITA. External patronage lengthened and armed conflicts whose origins were local, because neither side could be defeated while its supplier remained - which is a precise claim, unlike a general attribution of African violence to the superpowers.

---

## Lesson 24 - Neo-colonialism, the verdict debate and answer architecture

Progress: 24/24 | Stage: Advanced | Subtopic: neo-colonialism as a recorded practice and as Nkrumah's own concept; the gains that decolonisation did deliver; the case against empire from Brendon, Orwell, Russell and Gott; Ferguson's defence; Wood and Hall on formal rights against social rights; the numerical transformation from 74 states to 192; and the five master theses with the directive map that converts them into marks

━━━ PRE-TEACH CHECKLIST ━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
Book context: queried - the textbook account's verdict section on decolonisation in full, including the gains recorded for the new states, the negative assessments from Brendon, Orwell, Russell and Gott with their named evidence, the four limits on the supposed benefits, the Ellen Wood argument on passive citizenship, the record of interventions against socialist or pro-communist governments, the Ferguson defence of 2003, the concluding judgement, Anthony Hall's formulations, Nkrumah's quotation from his book on neo-colonialism and the figures for the growth in the number of nation-states; plus the account's end-of-chapter questions and the answer architecture used throughout this session
CA search: not sought - the session's single current linkage was used in Lesson 6 and is not repeated
CA found: none carried for this lesson
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

### Visual: the verdict debate as a balance sheet with a hinge

```text
   THE GAINS, as recorded                THE LIMITS, as recorded
   ======================                =======================

     more control over what went on        European countries and the
     inside their frontiers"               USA "still exerted a great
   * "some gains for ordinary people,      deal of control over the new
     such as advances in EDUCATION         states, which continued to
     and SOCIAL SERVICES, and a            need the markets and the
     political culture which allowed       investment that the West
     them to VOTE"                         could provide"
   * 1946: 74 nation-states              * BAD OR NO PREPARATION, with
     1995: 192 nation-states                artificial frontiers and
     "thanks to decolonization"             little incentive for groups
                                            to stay together (Nigeria,
                                            the Belgian Congo)
                                          * Nyasaland at withdrawal:
                                            "only THREE SECONDARY
                                            SCHOOLS for 3 MILLION
                                            Africans, and NOT ONE
                                            SINGLE INDUSTRIAL FACTORY"
                                          * Portugal, forced out of
                                            Mozambique, "DELIBERATELY

                                            machinery in revenge"
                                          * ELITE CAPTURE: governments
                                            run by local political elite
                                            groups; "no social revolution
                                            and no guarantee that
                                            ordinary people would be any
                                            better off"
                                          * INTERVENTION against
                                            socialist or pro-communist
                                            governments: Indo-China,
                                            Indonesia, East Timor, Chad,
                                            Angola, Mozambique, Zaire,
                                            Jamaica

                                            commodities, debt; Africa
                                            "the only area of the world
                                            where, in 1987, incomes were
                                            on average LOWER THAN IN 1972"

   THE HINGE - Ellen M. Wood:
   the new political rights and citizenship were "essentially PASSIVE".
   "The whole point of this strategy is TO PUT FORMAL POLITICAL RIGHTS
    IN PLACE OF SOCIAL RIGHTS, and to put as much of social life as
    possible out of the reach of democratic accountability."

   => the balance sheet does not balance across categories. What was
      delivered was POLITICAL and FORMAL; what was withheld was
      SOCIAL and ECONOMIC. The verdict question is whether the first
      is worth having without the second.
```

⚠️ Do not treat the two columns as items to be counted against each other. The account's own conclusion is that they are categorically different, and recognising that is what turns a list into a judgement.

### 1. Neo-colonialism: three statements, one concept

⚠️ This session has met the term three times, in three different registers, and assembling them is the first task of this lesson.

✅ **As a British expectation, recorded at the start of the African withdrawal.** ✅ The British, especially the Labour governments of 1945-51, were quite willing to allow independence and were confident that they would still be able to exercise influence through trade links, which they hoped to preserve by including the new states as members of the Commonwealth; ✅ this practice of exercising influence over former colonies after independence by economic means became known as **neo-colonialism**, and it became widespread in most of the new states of the Third World.

✅ **As a French intention, recorded at the moment of transfer.** ✅ This new independence was not quite so complete as the new states had hoped - de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism - ✅ and all the states except Guinea found that France still influenced their economic and foreign policies, and any independent action was almost out of the question.

✅ **As a structural condition, recorded in the aftermath.** ✅ Dependence on western European countries and the USA for both markets and investment **enabled those countries to exert some control over African governments** - neo-colonialism.

⚠️ The three together give the concept its full definition, and the definition is precise enough to examine: **neo-colonialism is the exercise of effective influence over the economic and foreign policy of a formally sovereign former colony, by economic means, arising from that state's dependence on the former metropole or its allies for markets, investment, aid and manufactures.**

⚠️ Four boundaries keep the concept usable rather than rhetorical.

⚠️ **It requires formal sovereignty.** ❌ Continuing formal rule is simply colonialism. ⚠️ Neo-colonialism names what happens *after* the flag comes down.

⚠️ **Its instrument is economic, not administrative.** ⚠️ Markets, investment, aid conditions, commodity prices and debt service - not governors and district officers.

⚠️ **It was designed as well as inherited.** ⚠️ The British and French statements above are intentions held before and during transfer, not diagnoses made afterwards by critics.

⚠️ **It is not a theory attributed here to a single author.** ✅ The account records it as the name given to a practice. ⚠️ ✅ It does separately quote Nkrumah's own formulation from his book on the subject - **"For those who practise neo-colonialism, it means power without responsibility and for those who suffer from it, it means exploitation without redress"** - and that quotation may be used with its attribution. ❌ Do not build a theoretical apparatus around the term beyond what the sources support.

⚠️ The best single piece of concrete evidence for the concept remains the Ghanaian smelter from Lesson 23: ✅ a plant built to process Ghanaian bauxite, built and owned by an American corporation, which insisted on buying bauxite from abroad. ⚠️ Sovereignty over the territory did not carry decision rights over the plant.

### 2. What decolonisation did deliver

⚠️ A verdict that records only the limits is not a verdict. ✅ The account is explicit that although some states, particularly Britain with the exception of Kenya, handled decolonisation better than others, in general it was not a pleasant experience for the colonies and there was no simple happy ending - ✅ but there were some gains for the new states, which now had much more control over what went on inside their frontiers; ✅ and some gains for ordinary people, such as advances in education and social services, and a political culture which allowed them to vote.

⚠️ Three of these should be pressed harder than the account presses them, using its own evidence from elsewhere.

⚠️ **Control inside the frontiers was real and it mattered.** ⚠️ Compare what the absence of it looked like: ✅ pass laws tying a South African worker's residence to a named employer; ✅ Kenyan restrictions on what the Kikuyu could grow; ✅ Algerian settlers holding a third of the most fertile land while African living standards fell. ⚠️ Sovereignty removed the legal capacity of outsiders to legislate on such matters, and where it was used the effect was immediate - ✅ coffee became a main Kenyan crop as soon as the restrictions went.

⚠️ **The social delivery is measurable in the record.** ✅ Tanzania by 1986: 3.7 million children in primary school, 85 per cent literacy, 150 hospitals and 2,600 dispensaries, infant mortality down to 137 per thousand, life expectancy 52. ✅ Rwanda between 2000 and 2008: per capita income doubled and primary education rose from twenty per cent of children to almost half. ✅ South Africa after 1994: seventy per cent of black households with electricity, nine million more people with access to pure water, free compulsory education. ⚠️ These are not trivial and should be cited against any claim that independence changed nothing.

⚠️ **The numerical transformation is the largest single fact.** ✅ **In 1946 there were seventy-four nation-states on the planet; in 1995, thanks to decolonisation, the number had risen to one hundred and ninety-two.** ⚠️ That is the reconstitution of the international system itself, and Lesson 6 showed the consequence: the new members captured the General Assembly and legislated the norm that governed the rest of the process.

### 3. The case against empire, with its named evidence

⚠️ The account records the critical position at length, and an answer should use its named evidence rather than general condemnation.

✅ It soon became fashionable to dismiss the entire colonial and imperial experience as a disaster in which European nations, with supreme arrogance, imposed control over their subject peoples, exploited them ruthlessly and then withdrew unwillingly, leaving them impoverished and facing new problems.

✅ **Piers Brendon** points out that this was not really surprising, since "the British Empire's real purpose was not to spread sweetness and light but to increase Britain's wealth and power. Naturally its coercive and exploitative nature must be disguised." ✅ The same applied to other European empires, except perhaps that they were not as good as the British in disguising it. ✅ **George Orwell** remarked that empire was "a despotism with theft as its final object". ✅ **Bertrand Russell** called the British Empire "a cesspool for British moral refuse", by which he apparently meant that many of the British administrators and officials were racist bullies.

✅ There is plenty of evidence to support this negative view. ✅ Although by no means all officials were racist bullies, there is no doubt that most of them treated the native peoples with arrogance and considered them to be inferior beings. ✅ Brendon shows that "the history of India is punctuated by famines which caused tens of millions of deaths"; ✅ during a severe famine in Bengal in **1942-3**, Churchill refused to divert shipping to take food supplies to Calcutta, and **the result was that over three million people died from starvation**. ✅ Much more can be added to the debit list: the slaughter of thousands of Aborigines in Australia and Maoris in New Zealand; ✅ during the **Boer War of 1899-1902** the British set up concentration camps in which **about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died**; ✅ and whenever there was any resistance, retribution was usually swift and disproportionate - Afghanistan, Ceylon, Jamaica, Burma, Kenya and Iraq were all ruthlessly subjugated.

✅ **Richard Gott**, in *Britain's Empire: Resistance, Repression and Revolt* (2012), goes along with what is probably the majority view, presenting a long catalogue of crimes against humanity committed by British imperialists: slavery, famine, prison, repression, battles, massacre, devastation and extermination.

⚠️ Two points of method.

⚠️ **Attribute the Bengal figure and its circumstance.** ✅ Brendon establishes the pattern of famines causing tens of millions of deaths; ✅ the 1942-3 Bengal famine with Churchill's refusal to divert shipping and over three million deaths is the named instance. ❌ Do not generalise the figure or apply it elsewhere.

⚠️ **Note that Gott's position is described as probably the majority view.** ⚠️ That is a statement about the state of the historiography, and reporting it demonstrates awareness that the critical position is now the scholarly mainstream rather than a dissenting opinion.

### 4. Ferguson's defence, stated fairly

⚠️ A genuinely critical answer reports the strongest opposing case, and here it is.

✅ In **2003**, the historian **Niall Ferguson** brought out a strong defence of the British Empire and its legacy. ✅ While admitting that Britain's record as a colonial power was not without blemish, he argued that the benefits of British rule were considerable: ✅ in the nineteenth century the British "pioneered free trade, free capital movements and, with the abolition of slavery, free labour"; ✅ in addition they developed a global network of modern communications, spread a system of law and order and "maintained a global peace unmatched before or since". ✅ When the Empire came to an end, the former British territories were left with the successful structures of liberal capitalism, the institutions of parliamentary democracy and the English language, which today is a vitally important medium of global communication. ✅ "What the British Empire proved", Ferguson concludes controversially, "is that empire is a form of international government which can work - and not just for the benefit of the ruling power. It sought to globalize not just an economic but a legal and ultimately a political system too."

⚠️ Now test the claims against this session's own evidence, which is what an examiner wants rather than a counter-assertion.

| ✅ Ferguson's claim | ⚠️ The test from this session's evidence |
|---|---|
| Free labour, through the abolition of slavery | ⚠️ Slavery was abolished throughout the Empire in 1838; ✅ yet South Africa's Union of 1910 confined black Africans to reserves covering about seven per cent of the country, tied residence and employment to a named employer through pass laws, housed miners in compounds of up to ninety men, and by a law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs. ⚠️ That is not free labour by any usable definition |
| A system of law and order | ⚠️ The law is the instrument by which the above was enforced; ✅ and in Kenya the British government hid from people at home a detention system whose scale was published only in 2005, Anderson recording more than a thousand hangings and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting, and Elkins separately and contestedly estimating up to a hundred thousand deaths in the camps |
| Institutions of parliamentary democracy | ✅ Delivered in several territories; ⚠️ but the same power created the Central African Federation in 1953 with a parliament heavily weighted to favour a white minority of about 300,000 out of 8.5 million, and offered Kenya six African seats in a council of fifty-four |
| Free trade and free capital movements | ✅ The USA regarded imperially protected markets as giving the British an unfair advantage, which is evidence that the trade was protected rather than free; ⚠️ and the colonial policy of preventing industry so that colonies would buy manufactures and supply raw materials is the opposite of free exchange |
| A global peace unmatched before or since | ⚠️ Within the period taught here: Malaya 1948-60, Kenya 1952-60, Cyprus from 1955, Central Africa 1959, and the Rhodesian war to 1980 |
| Structures of liberal capitalism as a legacy | ⚠️ Testable both ways. ✅ Ghana became one of Africa's most stable and successful democracies; ✅ Nigeria's federation was successfully redesigned after 1970. ⚠️ But the capitalism inherited was the single-commodity export model that made a world price movement a national fiscal crisis |

⚠️ The honest conclusion, and the one that reads best under examination, is not that Ferguson is simply wrong. ⚠️ It is that **the claimed legacies must be tested against who received them, when, and at what cost**, and that on this evidence base they were, as the account puts it, **at best thinly spread**.

⚠️ And one methodological point deserves stating: ⚠️ a legacy is only a benefit if it survives the accounting. ⚠️ Law, railways and English exist in former colonies; so do borders that produced wars, economies built not to industrialise, and a professional class deliberately kept small in some territories. ⚠️ Both sets are legacies, and a defence that counts only one set is not a balance sheet.

### 5. The limits, and the hinge that organises them

✅ The account then sets out what the supposed benefits actually amounted to, and the evidence suggests that at best these were thinly spread.

✅ **Neo-colonialism** meant that western European countries and the USA still exerted a great deal of control over the new states, which continued to need the markets and the investment that the West could provide.

✅ **Preparation.** Many new states, especially in Africa, had been badly prepared or not prepared at all; ✅ their frontiers were often artificial ones forced on them by the Europeans, and there was little incentive for different groups to stay together; ✅ in Nigeria and the Belgian Congo these differences helped to cause civil war. ✅ **When the British withdrew from Nyasaland there were only three secondary schools for three million Africans, and not one single industrial factory.** ✅ **When the Portuguese were forced to withdraw from Mozambique, they deliberately destroyed installations and machinery in revenge.**

⚠️ Those two sentences are the sharpest pieces of evidence in the whole verdict section and both should be quoted. ⚠️ Three secondary schools for three million people, after decades of rule, is a measurable statement about what colonial administration had chosen to provide. ⚠️ Deliberate destruction on withdrawal is a statement about the relationship at its end.

✅ **Elite capture.** Although the people of the newly independent states were now able to vote, in most cases the governments which took over were run by the local political elite groups; ✅ there was no social revolution and no guarantee that ordinary people would be any better off.

✅ **The hinge, from Ellen M. Wood.** ✅ Many historians, including Ellen M. Wood, have pointed out that the new political rights and citizenship were essentially **passive**: people were allowed to vote from time to time, but in practice it hardly made any difference to the way the country was run. ✅ "The whole point of this strategy", she writes, "is **to put formal political rights in place of social rights**, and to put as much of social life as possible out of the reach of democratic accountability."

⚠️ This is the most important analytical sentence in the verdict debate and it should be understood rather than merely quoted. ⚠️ Wood's claim is not that independence was fraudulent. ⚠️ It is that the *domain* of self-government was defined so as to exclude the questions that determine most people's lives: who owns the land, what the country produces, on what terms it trades, who receives investment and on what conditions. ⚠️ A population can vote, and the terms of trade will not appear on the ballot.

⚠️ Test it against this session's cases and it holds with uncomfortable regularity. ⚠️ ✅ Ghana voted and the cocoa price was set elsewhere. ⚠️ ✅ The twelve French African territories voted in 1958 in a referendum in which taxation and foreign affairs were reserved to France. ⚠️ ✅ Zimbabwe achieved majority rule in 1980 with about four thousand white farmers still holding about half the arable land. ⚠️ ✅ South Africa achieved majority rule in 1994 and thirty-four striking miners were shot at Marikana in 2012.

✅ **Intervention.** In countries where new governments were prepared to introduce socialist policies such as nationalising resources or foreign businesses, or where governments showed any sign of being pro-communist, the western countries disapproved; ✅ they often responded by cutting off aid or helping to destabilise the government, and in some cases even overthrowing governments - ✅ this happened in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica. ✅ The East Timorese case is given as the illustration, with President Ford giving Suharto the go-ahead because FRETILIN was thought to be Marxist, and around two hundred thousand people killed out of a total population of only seven hundred thousand in the long campaign of terror that followed.

⚠️ Notice how that item completes Wood's argument rather than merely adding to it. ⚠️ Where a population did use its formal rights to reach beyond the permitted domain - to nationalise, to redistribute, to realign - the response was aid withdrawal, destabilisation or removal. ⚠️ **The domain was not merely narrow; it was enforced.**

✅ **Poverty.** All the Third World states faced intense poverty; they were economically underdeveloped and often relied on exports of only one or two commodities, so a fall in the world price was a major disaster, and loans from abroad left them heavily in debt. ✅ As usual, Africa was worst hit: **it was the only area of the world where, in 1987, incomes were on average lower than in 1972.**

⚠️ That final statistic is the most damaging single line available and it should be used at the end of a verdict paragraph rather than the beginning. ⚠️ A region in which average incomes fell over fifteen years, across the first generation of independence, is the strongest quantitative support Wood's argument receives.

### 6. The account's conclusion, and Hall's formulations

✅ **In conclusion, it seems fair to say that so many limitations were placed on the independence given to the former colonies after the Second World War that the result was to divide people's political rights from any chance of expressing their rights in social and economic affairs.** ✅ True, they were now able to vote, but this did not necessarily enable them to improve their standards of living, since governments were still dominated by wealthy privileged elites.

✅ The Canadian historian **Anthony J. Hall** calls this "the great betrayal of humanity's democratic promise", ✅ and observes that there was much unevenness in the outcomes: the evidence is overwhelming that the frontier expansions of global corporations, along with the exercise of coercive authority centred in the military-industrial complex of the United States, intensifies the disparities of wealth and power that continue to reside at the core; ✅ class exploitation and colonial exploitation are two sides of the same coin; ✅ and it all tends to favour the interests of small, local oligarchies rather than to deliver on the ideals of broad-ranging liberation that the winds of change seemed initially to promise.

✅ Against which stands the numerical fact: ✅ in 1946 there were seventy-four nation-states on the planet; in 1995, thanks to decolonisation, the number had risen to one hundred and ninety-two.

⚠️ **The formulation that holds both:** decolonisation was a real and irreversible transfer of sovereignty, accomplished on a scale that reconstituted the international system, and a much weaker transfer of economic control - which is why the second half of the twentieth century produced political independence alongside continuing dependence. ⚠️ ❌ Do not resolve this into a single verdict. ⚠️ The two halves are true simultaneously and the whole skill of the topic is holding them together.

### 7. The five master theses

⚠️ These are the load-bearing claims of the session. Each should be recallable in about fifteen seconds and each answers a different demand.

**Thesis 1 - Causation.** ⚠️ Empire ended where the cost of holding it came to exceed the benefit, and colonial nationalism's decisive achievement was to raise that cost until metropolitan publics and treasuries were unwilling to pay it - which is why a defeated insurgency in Kenya and an undefeated one in Algeria produced the same result, and why Portugal's colonies outlasted the regime that held them.

**Thesis 2 - Route.** ⚠️ The form decolonisation took was determined less by the coloniser's intentions than by whether settlers, strategic assets, an authoritarian metropole or an unsettled successor question made withdrawal non-negotiable.

**Thesis 3 - Agency.** ⚠️ The decision was usually taken in Europe, but the price that made the decision necessary was set in the colony - and the colonised world then organised internationally, captured the General Assembly and legislated the norm that governed the remainder of the process.

**Thesis 4 - The post-colonial state.** ⚠️ Independence transferred sovereignty into structures designed for extraction and control, so the instability that followed was an inheritance rather than a verdict on self-rule; but the inheritance set constraints while choices made inside them determined outcomes, as Nigeria's post-1970 redesign and Ghana's later democracy both show.

**Thesis 5 - Continuity.** ⚠️ Political decolonisation was rapid and economic decolonisation was not, which is why formal sovereignty coexisted with commodity dependence, debt, aid conditionality and intervention - the practice the sources themselves call neo-colonialism and which both Britain and France designed before the transfer.

### 8. The directive map

⚠️ Match the demand to the thesis, the body of evidence and the structure.

| ⚠️ If the question says | ⚠️ It is testing | ⚠️ Lead with | ❌ Do not write |
|---|---|---|---|
| Why did the European empires collapse? | The *interaction* of metropolitan weakness, colonial nationalism and international climate | Thesis 1, then the Holland dispute from Lesson 3, then three territories placed at different points on the scale | A list of five causes with no weighting |
| Assess the reasons for the growth of nationalism after 1945 | The five wartime mechanisms and the social bases | Lesson 2's asymmetry between Asia and Africa, then Lesson 4's elite-plus-strike combination | "The war weakened the European powers" as a complete answer |
| Compare a peaceful and a violent case | The variable that decides the route | Thesis 2, then Gold Coast against Algeria or Kenya, naming settlers, strategic value and successor design | Two case narratives side by side |
| Explain the problems of decolonisation in the Malay Peninsula and how they differed | The plural-society successor-state problem | Lesson 18: the unit, the franchise, the insurgency, the promise, the Alliance, then 1963 and 1965 | An Emergency narrative |
| Why did independent African states face coups and civil wars? | Inherited structures plus choices | Thesis 4, then two to five named cases from Lesson 23, ending with the counter-evidence table | "They were not ready" |
| Did decolonisation bring the benefits Africans hoped for? | The formal-versus-social rights distinction | The gains, then Wood's hinge, then the 1987 income statistic, then a graded verdict | An undifferentiated list of failures |
| Assess decolonisation / was it a transfer or a rupture? | Thesis 5 | The three neo-colonialism statements, then Ferguson tested against evidence, then Hall and the 74-to-192 figure | A verdict without evidence |
| The role of Western-educated Africans in African nationalism | Elite leadership plus urban mass action | Lesson 4: education supplying vocabulary and grievance together, then the 1945 and 1950 general strikes | A list of leaders |
| Apartheid: system, resistance, end | The statutory system, its contradiction, and the convergence that ended it | Lessons 21-22: the 1947 trigger, the four institutions, the economic contradiction, the four phases of resistance, then the 1975-80 regional collapse | Moral condemnation without mechanism |
| The redrawal of national boundaries | The frontier as colonial product, underwritten by the 1960 Declaration | Lesson 6's territorial-integrity clause, then Katanga, Biafra, Eritrea and Western Sahara | Border determinism |

### 9. Mark-scaled structures

| Marks | ⚠️ Structure |
|---|---|
| **10** | Thesis in one sentence -> two causes or two cases, each with a named mechanism -> one qualification -> graded verdict. Two named territories is the target; three is usually too many at this length |
| **15** | Thesis -> why empire became unsustainable, with the metropolitan-agency dispute stated -> the route variable with two contrasting named cases -> why instability followed, with one named post-independence case -> verdict |
| **20** | All of the above -> **plus** settler against non-settler variation, the border legacy and the 1960 territorial-integrity clause, the Cold War handled with the Angolan formulation, and the formal-versus-social-rights argument with the 1987 income statistic -> a verdict that holds both halves of Thesis 5 |

⚠️ **The selection rule.** ⚠️ This session contains far more evidence than any answer can use. ⚠️ Choose two or three bodies of evidence - causation, routes, a named case, the aftermath, apartheid, the verdict debate - and develop them. ⚠️ Breadth without mechanism loses marks; the examiner is testing whether you can explain one thing properly, not whether you can name twenty.

### 10. Factual-risk controls

⚠️ These are the specific statements this session forbids. Check an answer against them before submitting.

1. ❌ Do not say all colonies became independent peacefully. ✅ Algeria, Indo-China, Kenya, Malaya, Cyprus, Portuguese Africa and Southern Rhodesia all involved sustained armed conflict.
2. ❌ Do not say empire ended because Europeans became generous. ✅ Cost, nationalist pressure and international climate are the recorded causes, and both Britain and France designed post-independence influence in advance.
3. ❌ Do not use post-colonial instability as an argument that decolonisation was premature. ✅ These were state-building crises whose causes were largely colonial products.
4. ❌ Do not treat partition as the whole story of Asian decolonisation. ✅ Indonesia, Indo-China and Malaya show diverse trajectories.
5. ❌ Do not give independence dates other than those sourced: ✅ Syria and Transjordan 1946; India and Pakistan 1947; Burma, Ceylon and the end of the Palestine mandate 1948; Indonesia 1949; Libya 1951; Sudan, Morocco, Tunisia and Spanish Morocco 1956; Ghana and Malaya 1957; Guinea 1958; Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus, the Belgian Congo, the eleven French West and Equatorial African territories that voted yes in 1958, and Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar 1960; Tanganyika and Sierra Leone 1961; Algeria, Uganda, Jamaica, Trinidad and Tobago and Ruanda-Urundi 1962; Zanzibar on 10 December 1963, Kenya and the Federation of Malaysia 1963; Malawi, Zambia, Malta and Tanzania's formation 1964; Singapore's separation and the Gambia 1965; Guyana, Barbados and Botswana 1966; Aden as South Yemen 1967; Equatorial Guinea 1968; Guinea-Bissau 1974; Angola, Mozambique, Surinam and Spanish Sahara's transfer 1975; Zimbabwe 1980; Belize 1981; St Kitts and Nevis 1983; Brunei 1984; Namibia 1990; East Timor 2002.
6. ❌ Do not state mortality, detention or displacement figures without their source and period. ✅ Anderson for the Kenyan hangings and combat deaths, and Elkins, separately and as a contested estimate, for the camp mortality; "some estimates put the total as high as" for the Algerian aftermath; "at least half a million" for the Indonesian purge; the two divergent East Timor figures reported together; the IMF and Human Rights Watch for Angola; "around fifty thousand" for Burundi in 1993 and "about eight hundred thousand" for Rwanda in 1994, both as the textbook account's own figures with no producing body named; Amnesty International for Zaire in 1978-9.
7. ❌ Do not confuse genocide, apartheid, civil war and neo-colonialism.
8. ❌ Do not supply Indian transfer-of-power or partition detail, Arab-Israeli or Suez material, Cold War system-level narrative, or the post-1954 history of Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos. ⚠️ Each is owned elsewhere.
9. ❌ Do not attribute the term neo-colonialism to a named theorist as its originator from this evidence base. ✅ It is recorded as the name given to a practice; Nkrumah's own quotation may be used with attribution.
10. ❌ Do not assert any current African or Asian economic, demographic or political indicator. ⚠️ The one dated current item in this session is in Lesson 6 and is limited there.

### Examination use

**Where it sits in the syllabus.** ✅ This lesson answers the evaluative half of the syllabus's decolonisation clause and supplies the concepts - neo-colonialism, formal against social rights - without which the "effect on society" demand cannot be met.

**How it is framed.** ⚠️ Expect a quotation inviting agreement or disagreement, most often on whether decolonisation delivered what Africans hoped for, or whether it transferred sovereignty faster than it built states. ⚠️ Both are answered by the balance sheet plus Wood's hinge plus a graded verdict.

**Traps.**

1. ⚠️ Counting gains against losses as though they were commensurable. ⚠️ What was delivered was political and formal; what was withheld was social and economic, which is the account's own conclusion.
2. ⚠️ Dismissing Ferguson rather than testing him. ⚠️ Test each claimed legacy against evidence from the cases.
3. ⚠️ Treating neo-colonialism as a slogan. ⚠️ Define it precisely: effective influence over economic and foreign policy, by economic means, over a formally sovereign former colony.
4. ❌ Quoting the 1987 income statistic without its comparison. ✅ Africa was the only area of the world where, in 1987, incomes were on average lower than in 1972.
5. ⚠️ Forgetting the scale of the achievement. ✅ Seventy-four states in 1946 and one hundred and ninety-two in 1995.

### Revision notes

1. ✅ Neo-colonialism recorded three times: as a British expectation of influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership; as de Gaulle's stated intention in 1958-60; and as the structural dependence on western markets and investment that enabled control over African governments.
2. ⚠️ Definition: effective influence over the economic and foreign policy of a formally sovereign former colony, exercised by economic means through dependence on markets, investment, aid, manufactures and debt.
3. ✅ Nkrumah's own formulation, from his book on the subject: "For those who practise neo-colonialism, it means power without responsibility and for those who suffer from it, it means exploitation without redress."
4. ✅ Gains recorded: much more control over what went on inside their frontiers; advances in education and social services; a political culture which allowed them to vote.
5. ✅ In 1946 there were 74 nation-states; in 1995, thanks to decolonisation, there were 192.
6. ✅ Brendon: the British Empire's real purpose was to increase Britain's wealth and power, and its coercive and exploitative nature had to be disguised; the same applied to other empires, which were less good at disguising it.
7. ✅ Orwell: empire was "a despotism with theft as its final object". ✅ Russell: "a cesspool for British moral refuse", meaning many administrators were racist bullies.
8. ✅ Brendon: the history of India is punctuated by famines causing tens of millions of deaths; in the Bengal famine of 1942-3 Churchill refused to divert shipping to Calcutta and over three million died of starvation.
9. ✅ Further debit items: thousands of Aborigines and Maoris slaughtered; in the Boer War of 1899-1902 British concentration camps in which about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died; swift and disproportionate retribution in Afghanistan, Ceylon, Jamaica, Burma, Kenya and Iraq.
10. ✅ Richard Gott (2012) presents a long catalogue of crimes against humanity - slavery, famine, prison, repression, battles, massacre, devastation and extermination - and this is described as probably the majority view.
11. ✅ Ferguson (2003): Britain pioneered free trade, free capital movements and, with the abolition of slavery, free labour; developed global communications; spread law and order; maintained a global peace unmatched before or since; and left liberal capitalism, parliamentary institutions and the English language, proving that empire is a form of international government which can work.
12. ✅ The limits: neo-colonialism; bad or absent preparation with artificial frontiers; only three secondary schools for three million Africans in Nyasaland and not one industrial factory; Portugal deliberately destroying installations and machinery on leaving Mozambique; governments run by local political elites with no social revolution; and intense poverty on one or two commodities with heavy debt.
13. ✅ Ellen M. Wood: the new political rights and citizenship were essentially passive; the whole point of the strategy is to put formal political rights in place of social rights, and to put as much of social life as possible out of the reach of democratic accountability.
14. ✅ Intervention against socialist or pro-communist governments in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica, including Ford's go-ahead to Suharto over East Timor.
15. ✅ Africa was the only area of the world where, in 1987, incomes were on average lower than in 1972.
16. ✅ The conclusion: so many limitations were placed on independence that the result was to divide people's political rights from any chance of expressing their rights in social and economic affairs. ✅ Anthony J. Hall calls this "the great betrayal of humanity's democratic promise", and holds that class exploitation and colonial exploitation are two sides of the same coin, favouring small local oligarchies rather than broad-ranging liberation.
17. ⚠️ The five master theses: cost imposition; the route variable; decision in Europe but price set in the colony, followed by collective international action; inheritance setting constraints with choices determining outcomes; and rapid political with slow economic decolonisation.
18. ⚠️ Master formulation: decolonisation reconstituted the international system and did not redistribute economic power, and an answer must hold both halves at once.

### Concept check

**Question:** Niall Ferguson argues that the British Empire left its former territories free trade, free labour, law and order, parliamentary institutions and a global peace unmatched before or since. Test three of these claims against evidence taught in this session, and state the conclusion the evidence supports.

**Model answer:** Take free labour first. Slavery was abolished throughout the Empire in 1838, but the Union of South Africa in 1910 confined the black majority to reserves covering about seven per cent of the country, forbade them to buy land elsewhere, tied residence and employment to a named white employer through pass laws so that a worker could not change jobs without his previous employer's signature, housed gold miners in single-sex compounds holding up to ninety men, and by a law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs. A workforce compelled to seek wage labour, unable to leave an employer, unable to combine and unable to be promoted is not free labour. Take parliamentary institutions second. They were genuinely delivered in several territories, including the Gold Coast, where a constitution with universal adult suffrage, an elected Assembly and eight of eleven Executive Council members chosen by that Assembly preceded independence in 1957. But the same power authorised the Central African Federation in 1953 with a parliament at Salisbury heavily weighted to favour about three hundred thousand Europeans out of a population of about eight and a half million, and for years offered Kenya only six African seats on a Legislative Council of fifty-four. Take global peace third. Within the period taught here Britain declared emergencies in Malaya in 1948, Kenya in 1952, Cyprus in 1955 and Nyasaland and Southern Rhodesia in 1959, and the Rhodesian war ran to 1980. The conclusion the evidence supports is the account's own: the supposed benefits were at best thinly spread, and a claimed legacy is only a benefit once it has been tested against who received it, when, and at what cost.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the verdict as a balance sheet in which gains and losses can be added up against each other. They belong to different categories, which is precisely the account's conclusion: so many limitations were placed on independence that the result was to divide people's political rights from any chance of expressing their rights in social and economic affairs. What decolonisation delivered was political and formal - sovereignty, the vote, advances in education and social services, and an increase in the number of nation-states from seventy-four in 1946 to one hundred and ninety-two in 1995. What it withheld was social and economic, which is why Africa was the only area of the world where incomes in 1987 were on average lower than in 1972. The correct answer holds both halves rather than choosing one.
