## Probable framings recorded in the study material and by the textbook account

⚠️ Since no question is owned, the best available guide to how this topic would be examined is the set of framings recorded in the study material for this topic and by the account itself. ⚠️ All of them are covered by the original Mains practice below.

✅ **From the topic's study material:** why the European colonial empires collapsed after the Second World War; why many newly independent African states faced severe political instability; a comparison of a relatively peaceful case of decolonisation with a violent one; the problems of decolonisation in the Malay Peninsula and how they differed from those of other colonies; post-independence problems with named cases; apartheid as system, resistance and ending; the role of Western-educated Africans in the rise of African nationalism; whether decolonisation was a transfer or a rupture; and the comparison with Latin American independence as a different era, colonial type, leadership and social base.

✅ **From the advanced material for this topic:** "Decolonization transferred sovereignty faster than it built stable states." Discuss; analyse the causes of the collapse of the European empires after 1945; and why independent Africa faced coups, civil wars and one-party rule so often.

✅ **From the textbook account's own end-of-chapter questions:** whether, without de Gaulle's masterly handling of the situation, the Algerian crisis would probably have plunged France into civil war; whether decolonisation brought the benefits for the majority of the African people which they had hoped for; whether Indian independence was a gift from the British or "the hard-won fruit of struggle and sacrifice"; why it was thought necessary to divide India; and an assessment of the reasons for the growth of nationalism in the European colonies after the Second World War, with the importance of nationalism in bringing about decolonisation.

⚠️ Note that two of those five end-of-chapter questions concern India's transfer of power and partition, which are owned by the Modern Indian History material. ⚠️ This session supplies, for such a question, only the historiographical structure taught in Lesson 3 - the four separable claims of intention, interest, causation and conduct, with the positions of Sarkar and Anita Inder Singh, the drain-on-resources reading, Brasted's middle view, and the divergent assessments of the handover from Attlee, Menon, Brasted and Wilson. ❌ It supplies no Indian narrative.

## What a topic with no owned question requires

⚠️ **Analytical inference:** a nil return over nine years should change preparation rather than reduce it, and three things follow.

1. ⚠️ **The syllabus clause covers this topic directly.** ✅ The official General Studies Paper I syllabus names colonisation and decolonisation and the redrawal of national boundaries among its examples of world history from the eighteenth century. ⚠️ Decolonisation is therefore a named term with no question yet asked against it in the held span, which makes it a standing candidate rather than a low-probability one.
2. ⚠️ **The topic is examined through other papers and other parts of the syllabus.** ✅ Every Africa-related question in the span belongs to International Relations or Geography. ⚠️ A candidate who treats a world-history nil return as permission to skip will meet Africa elsewhere without the historical depth that makes an answer good - and the colonial origin of commodity dependence, arbitrary frontiers and thin institutions is exactly the kind of background that distinguishes a first-class contemporary answer.
3. ⚠️ **Rehearse architectures, not answers.** ⚠️ Lesson 24 lists the five master theses and the directive map. ⚠️ The two that transfer fastest to an unanticipated stem are the cost-imposition thesis, which answers any causation demand, and the formal-against-social-rights distinction, which answers any evaluation demand.

# CUMULATIVE CONCEPT CHECKS

These run across lesson blocks rather than within a single lesson. Attempt each from memory before reading the model answer; the misconception line names the error that most often costs marks in that block.

## Block A - the causes, Lessons 1 to 8

### Cumulative check A1 - one process, five different clocks

**Question:** Decolonisation is often described as a single post-war process. Show that it ran on at least five different timetables, and give the reason for each.

**Model answer:** The Middle Eastern mandates went first, because the mandate form carried an explicit obligation to prepare the territory for independence: French Syria and Transjordan became independent in 1946 and the Palestine mandate ended in 1948. South and South-East Asia followed immediately, because nationalist movements were decades old - the Indian National Congress had agitated since 1885 and Vietnamese nationalists since the 1920s - and because the Japanese conquests had destroyed the European claim to protect its subjects while leaving behind trained guerrillas; India and Pakistan became independent in 1947, Burma and Ceylon in 1948 and Indonesia in 1949. Non-settler Africa came a decade later, because the war's African effect was political awakening without armed organisation, so parties, newspapers and general strikes had to be built first: the Gold Coast became Ghana in 1957 and the 1960 cluster followed. Settler Africa came later still, because settlers converted withdrawal into expropriation and could act against their own metropole: Kenya in 1963, Algeria in 1962 after a war, and Southern Rhodesia not until 1980 after a unilateral declaration in 1965. The Portuguese territories came last of all, because an authoritarian metropole has no election, opposition or free press through which a failing policy can be changed, so pressure accumulated until the regime fell in 1974 and the colonies were released in 1974 and 1975. Italy is the outlier that proves the point about agency: its empire was removed by decision of the victors in 1947 without nationalist pressure at all.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating the 1960 cluster as coincidence or as the natural maturing of separate local processes. Fourteen French-administered territories, Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus and the Belgian Congo became independent in one calendar year, which is the strongest single piece of evidence for the metropolitan thesis: de Gaulle's referendum was a single Paris design applied to twelve territories at once, and Belgium's six-month timetable was decided in Brussels after riots in Leopoldville in January 1959.

### Cumulative check A2 - the variable, tested against its apparent exceptions

**Question:** State the single best predictor of a territory's decolonisation route, and show how it copes with the two cases that appear to refute it.

**Model answer:** The best predictor is whether a substantial settler population owned land and held political privilege in the territory. The account itself organises British Africa on this basis: West Africa had relatively few Europeans, and those administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend, which made the move to independence comparatively straightforward; East Africa was complicated by the settler factor, especially in Kenya; and in Central Africa the settler factor was at its most serious, particularly in Southern Rhodesia. The outcomes match without exception - the Gold Coast 1957, Nigeria 1960, Sierra Leone 1961 and the Gambia 1965 with no colonial war; Tanganyika 1961 and Uganda 1962 by negotiation but Kenya 1963 after an emergency from 1952; Malawi and Zambia 1964 but Southern Rhodesia 1980. The two apparent exceptions are governed by other conditions that make withdrawal non-negotiable. Portuguese Africa had comparatively few settlers and thirteen years of war, because the operative variable there was the authoritarian metropole: Salazar's regime met the fighting that began in Angola in 1961 with intensified repression until forty per cent of the budget was going on three wars by 1973, and only a coup at home in 1974 released the colonies. Malaya had no settler class and a twelve-year emergency, because the operative variable there was the contested successor state: the population was multiracial and the insurgents were Chinese communists under Chin Peng, so the question was which community would inherit, and Britain answered it by combining resettlement with a credible promise that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready. The general rule is therefore that violence follows from any condition making withdrawal non-negotiable, of which settler property is the commonest form.

**Misconception to avoid:** Organising the comparison by imperial power. Britain declared emergencies in Malaya in 1948, Kenya in 1952, Cyprus in 1955 and Central Africa in 1959, while France negotiated Tunisian and Moroccan independence in 1956 in the middle of the Algerian war. The coloniser's identity is a weak predictor; the condition in the territory is a strong one.

### Cumulative check A3 - agency, evidenced

**Question:** A candidate writes that decolonisation was something Europe decided and Asia and Africa received. Give four pieces of evidence that refute this, drawn from four different territories.

**Model answer:** First, Guinea in 1958. De Gaulle offered twelve territories self-government inside a French Community in which France would decide taxation and foreign affairs, with aid for members and none for any territory choosing independence, and he was confident that none would dare face the future without French help. Guinea under Sekou Toure voted ninety-five per cent against, took immediate independence, had all French aid stopped and survived - after which the other eleven territories, together with Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar, demanded full independence and received it in 1960. Second, Nigeria in 1945. Azikiwe had founded newspapers since 1937 without moving British policy, and his impressive general strike of 1945 was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence. Third, the Gold Coast in 1950. Boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike led to Nkrumah's imprisonment, after which the British, realising he had mass support, released him and conceded a constitution with universal adult suffrage, an elected Assembly and eight of eleven Executive Council members chosen by that Assembly. Fourth, Bandung and the General Assembly. Twenty-nine countries met at Bandung from 18 to 24 April 1955 and adopted ten principles claiming sovereignty, equality and non-intervention for all nations large and small; the resulting coalition, styled variously the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77 or the Bandung group, held a working majority by 1960 and carried the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples on 14 December 1960 without a single expressed dissent.

**Misconception to avoid:** Over-correcting into the claim that the metropolitan thesis is worthless. Robert Holland's position - that the powers divested themselves not because internal pressures left no choice but through a revisionist process in which imperial roles came to look incongruent with modern foreign and economic goals - has real evidence behind it, above all the 1960 cluster and the wholesale character of de Gaulle's 1958 design. The account's resolution is that all the factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories, which is an instruction to weigh them case by case.

## Block B - the routes and the cases, Lessons 9 to 20

### Cumulative check B1 - the successor-state problem in four answers

**Question:** Four territories in this session faced the problem of what the successor state should be, and answered it differently. Name them, state each answer and its outcome, and identify the variable that separates success from failure.

**Model answer:** Nigeria adopted a three-region federation under the 1954 constitution, with regional self-government preceding national independence in 1960; the regions felt the centre in Lagos did not safeguard their interests, and after a recession, a rigged election in 1964 and a coup in January 1966 the eastern region seceded as Biafra in May 1967, producing a war that lasted to January 1970. Kenya considered federalism because KADU under Ronald Ngala wanted it, formed a coalition with KANU in 1962, and then abandoned the federal idea after KANU won the May 1963 elections; independence followed in December 1963. Malaya assembled the unit before transferring it: the Federation of Malaya of 1948 grouped nine sultanates and two settlements while leaving Singapore out, gave each state a local legislature and the sultans some power but left the central government with firm overall control, and universal adult suffrage in that unit meant the Malays usually dominated affairs; the Federation of Malaysia followed in September 1963 and survived Brunei's refusal to join and Singapore's departure in 1965. Cyprus built a power-sharing constitution in 1960 in which Makarios dropped enosis and a Turkish Cypriot became vice-president; civil war broke out in 1963, Turkey intervened in 1974 and the island has remained divided. The variable is whether the communities agreed on what state they were in. Malaya's did and its central government held firm overall control, so a member's departure removed a problem; Nigeria's centre was weaker than its regions, so a region's departure threatened the state; and in Cyprus the two communities' preferred outcomes - union with Greece against partition - were incompatible from the start.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that federation is a poor instrument for plural societies. Nigeria's own recovery disproves it: after the war Gowon took no revenge, reconciled the Ibos and expanded the federation from twelve states to nineteen to give more recognition of local differences, and the recovery was remarkably swift. The three-region design failed because three units of comparable weight allow any two to dominate the third; more units broke that arithmetic.

### Cumulative check B2 - the moderate in jail

**Question:** Identify the mechanism by which imprisoning a moderate nationalist leader damaged the colonial power that did it, and give three cases, one from each of three different empires.

**Model answer:** The mechanism is that imprisonment removes the only interlocutor with whom a settlement can be made, transferring leadership to people whose position does not depend on obtaining concessions, so the metropole must eventually release the moderate on worse terms. The French case is the clearest because the reasoning is recorded: New Destour was banned and Bourguiba jailed in 1952, seventy thousand troops failed to crush the guerrillas, and the French then became aware that with the moderate leaders in jail the movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate; they concluded that with a moderate like Bourguiba at the head of the country there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence, released him and let him form the government of an independent Tunisia in March 1956. The British case is the Gold Coast: Nkrumah and other leaders were imprisoned after the boycotts, demonstrations and general strike of 1950, and the British, realising he had mass support, soon released him, conceded a constitution with universal adult suffrage and invited him to form a government in 1952. The Kenyan variant shows the cost of failing to apply the lesson: the settlers refused to negotiate with the gradualist Kenyatta, his party's radical Forty Group argued for force, and after the 1952 uprising Kenyatta was jailed for six years despite publicly condemning violence and denying his party's involvement; he was not released until 1961 and was prime minister by May 1963. The Algerian variant is the endpoint of the same logic: Ben Bella was imprisoned in 1956 and released in 1962 to attend the Evian talks that granted independence.

**Misconception to avoid:** Reading the releases as concessions to justice. The French reasoning was explicitly about post-independence leverage, and the British released Nkrumah once mass support had been demonstrated. In every case the calculation was about which interlocutor would produce the most favourable settlement, which is why this mechanism belongs with the neo-colonial design of Lessons 16 and 24 rather than with a narrative of moral progress.

### Cumulative check B3 - sanctions, twice

**Question:** International sanctions were applied to Rhodesia from 1965 and to South Africa from 1962. Explain why both failed for two decades and what changed in each case.

**Model answer:** Both failed because the states writing the resolutions did not enforce them and because regional geography supplied alternatives. Against Rhodesia, Britain ruled out force and relied on economic measures, stopping purchases of sugar and tobacco, and the UN called for a complete trade embargo; but South Africa and Portuguese-ruled Mozambique refused to comply, so a landlocked state simply traded through them, many countries condemned publicly and evaded privately, the United States bought Rhodesian chrome because it was the cheapest available, and companies including British oil companies broke sanctions - while Zambia, enforcing them, suffered more than Rhodesia did. Against South Africa, the UN voted an economic boycott in 1962 which proved useless because not all member states supported it, and Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public while continuing to trade and selling massive arms supplies, apparently hoping South Africa would prove a bastion against the spread of communism in Africa - so Verwoerd and Vorster could ignore outside protest until well into the 1970s. What changed in Rhodesia was regional and military: Mozambican independence in June 1975 removed a sanctions-breaking neighbour and supplied guerrilla bases, the front-line states provided training camps, South Africa recalculated after its Angolan invasion was called off on American orders in October 1975, and by 1978 guerrillas controlled large areas of the countryside. What changed in South Africa was a convergence: the same regional collapse in 1975 and 1980 left it surrounded by hostile black states, whites fell from twenty-one per cent between the wars to sixteen per cent by 1980, the homelands had visibly failed, the Commonwealth except Britain agreed a real package in August 1986 and the US Congress voted sanctions over Reagan's veto in September 1986, and the Dutch Reformed Church condemned apartheid as incompatible with Christianity.

**Misconception to avoid:** Concluding that sanctions do not work. The finding supported by both cases is narrower and more useful: sanctions failed while the target had compliant neighbours and while the sanctioning states permitted their own firms to trade, and they contributed to the outcome once the regional configuration changed and the measures carried a cost to the states imposing them.

## Block C - apartheid, the aftermath and the verdict, Lessons 21 to 24

### Cumulative check C1 - writing about mass death in this topic

**Question:** Set out the rules this topic requires for writing about mortality and detention, and apply them to five episodes.

**Model answer:** The rules are four. Every figure travels with the name of whoever produced it and the period it covers; no figure is rounded, totalled, averaged, updated or combined across sources; qualifying phrases such as "nobody knows how many" and "some estimates put the total as high as" are retained because they record the state of the evidence; and responsibility is attributed to named governments, parties, armies, militias and officials rather than to nations or peoples. Applied to five episodes: for Kenya, the historians David Anderson and Caroline Elkins revealed the full details only in 2005, recording that during the Emergency the British hanged more than a thousand Kikuyu, killed some twenty thousand in combat and that up to a hundred thousand died in detention camps, against fewer than a hundred whites killed - and the concealment of the scale from people at home is itself part of the finding. For Algeria, Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some two hundred thousand who had served in the French army, were denounced as traitors by the FLN, and nobody knows how many were executed or murdered, though some estimates put the total as high as a hundred and fifty thousand. For Indonesia, the New Order purge of communists is recorded as one in which at least half a million people were murdered, with few protests from the West because in the Cold War atmosphere an anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable. For East Timor the same account gives two figures for the same episode - about a hundred thousand killed, one-sixth of the population, with another three hundred thousand detained, and around two hundred thousand killed out of a total population of only seven hundred thousand - and both must be reported with the divergence stated. For Rwanda, about eight hundred thousand Tutsi and moderate Hutu were murdered in a deliberate and carefully planned attempt to wipe out the entire Tutsi population, backed by the Hutu government, with about a million Tutsi refugees fleeing to Tanzania and Zaire; in Burundi in 1993 around fifty thousand Tutsi were killed after Tutsi soldiers murdered the newly elected Hutu president in October.

**Misconception to avoid:** Producing a compromise figure in the interest of balance, particularly for East Timor. Averaging incompatible testimony creates a number no source supports, which is fabrication rather than moderation. The honest form of balance is to report each estimate with its author, its period and the conditions under which it was formed - and, where a single account diverges from itself, to say so, because that divergence is evidence about how little the world chose to know.

### Cumulative check C2 - the boundaries this session observes

**Question:** Name the five subject boundaries this session holds, state what each neighbouring topic owns, and say what this session nevertheless supplies in each case.

**Model answer:** First, India's transfer of power and partition belong to Modern Indian History. This session supplies only the historiographical structure of the dispute - the official preparation narrative against Sarkar's and Anita Inder Singh's view that the 1919 and 1935 Acts postponed rather than prepared independence, the drain-on-resources reading, Brasted's middle view, and the divergent judgements of the handover - and the demonstration effect, since independence in 1947 alarmed white South Africans about growing racial equality within the Commonwealth and helped produce the Nationalist victory of 1948. Second, the Cold War as a system belongs to the Cold War topic. This session teaches superpower conduct only where it entered a decolonising society: American and Belgian encouragement of Lumumba's removal, the CIA's involvement in the Indonesian events of 1965 and its alleged and contested backing for the Ghanaian coup of 1966, Cuban troops and South African invasions in Angola, and Reagan's encouragement of Morocco in Western Sahara. Third, the communist regimes of China, Korea, Vietnam, Cambodia and Laos belong to the topic on Asian communism; this session teaches the anti-colonial war against France and stops at Geneva in July 1954. Fourth, the conquest that created these empires, the drawing of the frontiers and the colonial fixing of identities belong to the topic on the New Imperialism; this session inherits their consequences, most explicitly in the Belgian methods of denying advanced education and playing groups against each other. Fifth, the Middle East after the mandates - the creation of Israel, the Arab-Israeli wars, Suez and Egyptian politics - belongs to the Middle East material; this session owns only the mandate as a legal form carrying an obligation to prepare for independence, and its dates.

**Misconception to avoid:** Treating a boundary as a gap. Each handover is stated in the lesson as ordinary teaching, and the material each neighbour covers is available for use in an answer through that part of the syllabus. What is forbidden is asserting the neighbour's content from this session's evidence base, which would mean writing Indian partition detail, Cold War system narrative or Arab-Israeli material from sources that were never read for it.

### Cumulative check C3 - the argument that runs from 1910 to 2012

**Question:** Trace the single continuing argument that connects the South African land and labour legislation of 1910-11 to the shooting of striking miners at Marikana in August 2012, and state what it demonstrates about decolonisation generally.

**Model answer:** The argument concerns whether political emancipation reaches the structures that determine most people's lives. The Union of 1910 confined the black majority to reserves covering about seven per cent of the country, too small to feed their populations or pay their taxes, and forbade them to buy land elsewhere, which compelled wage labour; the pass laws tied residence and employment to a named employer so a worker could not change jobs without his previous employer's signature; miners lived in single-sex compounds holding as many as ninety men; and a law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs. Apartheid from 1948 codified and extended this while adding classification, marriage prohibition, inferior schooling and the Bantustans, and it could never achieve complete separation because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and businesses and the economy would have collapsed without them. The Freedom Charter of 1955 answered in the same register, demanding the right to work with equal pay, a forty-hour week, a minimum wage, unemployment benefit and free medical care alongside the vote. Botha's most consequential concession in 1979 was to allow blacks to join trade unions and to strike, reversing the 1911 ban while still refusing the vote. Majority rule arrived in 1994 and delivered measurably - seventy per cent of black households with electricity, nine million more people with access to pure water, free compulsory education - while unemployment reached twenty-five per cent by May 2009 and the more left-wing ANC supporters complained of too little redistribution of wealth. In August 2012 police of a democratic South Africa shot and killed thirty-four platinum miners striking for higher wages against a British mine-owning company, and two hundred and seventy miners were charged with their colleagues' murder before the charges were dropped. What it demonstrates is Ellen Wood's argument in its strongest form: political rights can be delivered completely and at once while the social and economic structures they were expected to transform survive the transition, which is the same "political independence without economic decolonisation" finding this session reaches in Ghana, in French West Africa and in Zimbabwe.

**Misconception to avoid:** Using the continuity to argue that the political transition did not matter. It mattered enormously and the record says so - transition to black majority rule without civil war, the end of the statutory racial order, the Truth and Reconciliation Commission, and measurable delivery in electricity, water and education. The correct claim is not that nothing changed but that what changed was political and formal while what persisted was social and economic, and that these are different categories rather than opposite entries in one ledger.

# ORIGINAL 10-, 15- AND 20-MARK MAINS MODEL PRACTICE

⚠️ All six questions are original. ⚠️ Because this topic owns no verified previous-year question, they are constructed to cover the framings recorded in the study material for this topic and by the textbook account, including the two pre-2018 recurring demands rendered neutrally in the section above. ⚠️ Each model answer is written to the stated ceiling and should be read as a demonstration of structure and evidence density, not as a passage to be memorised.

## Question 1 - 10 marks, 150 words

**Examine why decolonisation took a violent form in some territories and a negotiated form in others.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Examine* - identify the components of the difference and inspect their causes; two case narratives will not satisfy it.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** The decisive variable was not the coloniser's intention but whether withdrawal was negotiable. Where Europeans were administrators rather than landowning settlers, as in the Gold Coast, Britain could concede: boycotts, demonstrations and a general strike in 1950 produced a constitution with universal adult suffrage, and independence followed in 1957. Where settlers held the best land and had metropolitan allies, withdrawal meant expropriating citizens who could resist. In Algeria over a million pieds noirs held about a third of the most fertile land in a territory legally part of France, so ten years of Messali Hadj's peaceful campaigning achieved nothing and the struggle passed to the extremists; independence came in July 1962 after seven hundred thousand French troops had been deployed. Two further conditions do the same: an authoritarian metropole with no mechanism for conceding, as in Portugal, and an unsettled successor question, as in Malaya.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the mark is earned by refusing the empire-by-empire framing in the first sentence and naming a variable instead. Note also the two supplementary conditions in the last sentence, which pre-empt the obvious objection that Portugal had few settlers - a ten-mark answer that anticipates its own counter-example reads as controlled rather than partial.

## Question 2 - 10 marks, 150 words

**"The educated elite led African nationalism, but it did not win independence on its own." Discuss.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Discuss* - state what is true in the claim, what limits it, and the reason for the pattern; a verdict without the reason is incomplete.

**Model answer (150-word ceiling).** The claim is accurate and its reason is structural. African nationalism spread rapidly after 1945 because more Africans were educated in Britain and the United States, where they were made aware of racial discrimination - one journey supplying both the vocabulary of rights and the grievance. Nkrumah, educated in London and the USA, led the Convention People's Party from 1949; Azikiwe, educated in the USA, founded newspapers from 1937; Nyerere came from Edinburgh. But leadership did not move timetables. Azikiwe's papers had run eight years when his general strike of 1945 was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence; Nkrumah's imprisonment ended when the British, realising he had mass support after the 1950 general strike, released him. French Africa confirmed it in 1949 by arresting trade unionists alongside politicians. The elite supplied argument and legitimacy; the towns and the strike supplied pressure.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the discriminator is the sentence explaining why education produced nationalism - because it delivered the metropolitan vocabulary of rights and the experience of racial discrimination simultaneously. Most scripts assert that education produced leaders; this one explains the mechanism, and then tests it by showing that the newspapers achieved nothing until the strike.

## Question 3 - 15 marks, 250 words

**Analyse the causes of the collapse of the European colonial empires after 1945.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Analyse* - separate the causes, weigh them and show how they interacted; a list will not satisfy the directive.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The dispute is the starting point. Robert Holland's metropolitan thesis holds that the powers divested themselves not because internal pressures left no choice but through a revisionist process in which imperial roles came to look incongruent with modern foreign and economic goals. Against it stands the question whether Britain would have left East and Central Africa for purely metropolitan reasons had there been no nationalist movements there. The resolution: all these factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories - weigh them case by case.

The war supplied the accelerant, unevenly. Japan's conquest of Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong, Burma, the Dutch East Indies and Indo-China destroyed the European claim to protect its subjects and left trained guerrillas. In Africa the effect was awakening without arms: some 374,000 Africans served in British forces and returned with a standard of comparison.

Metropolitan capacity fell. Porter notes the British Empire had always been a cheapskate affair, run by devolving rule and cost to settlers and chiefs; Macleod concluded Africa could not possibly have been held by force.

Colonial nationalism supplied the price. Azikiwe's strike of 1945 prompted preparation for Nigerian independence; Guinea refused de Gaulle's Community by ninety-five per cent in 1958 knowing all aid would stop.

External pressure worked on both sides at once, raising the cost of holding empire and lowering that of resisting it.

Empire ended where the cost of holding it exceeded the benefit.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** two features carry the marks. First, opening with the historiographical dispute rather than with a cause, which signals that the candidate understands causation to be contested. Second, the closing sentence, which is a thesis rather than a summary - and it is earned, because the preceding paragraphs have established supply of coercion, demand for coercion and the international environment separately.

## Question 4 - 15 marks, 250 words

**"The problems of decolonisation in the Malay Peninsula differed fundamentally from those elsewhere." Examine this statement.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Examine* - test the claim against comparable cases; the word "fundamentally" must be addressed rather than assumed.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The statement is correct, and the difference is precise: elsewhere the problem was extracting a departure, while in Malaya it was constructing a successor.

There was no single polity to transfer: nine states each under a sultan, two British settlements at Malacca and Penang, and Singapore, less than a mile from the mainland; the population was multiracial, mostly Malays and Chinese with some Indians and Europeans; and the economy, based on rubber and tin, was the most prosperous in south-east Asia. Britain had to decide the unit and the franchise before it could hand anything over, and those were one decision: the Federation of Malaya of 1948 grouped the states and settlements while leaving Singapore out, and universal adult suffrage in that unit meant the Malays, as the largest group, usually dominated affairs.

The insurgency was a challenge to that answer rather than to British departure. Chin Peng's Chinese communist guerrillas, veterans of the anti-Japanese resistance, fought for an independent communist state; Britain declared an emergency in 1948, resettled suspected supporters into guarded villages, and crucially made clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready - which kept the Malays firmly pro-British.

The instrument was correspondingly distinctive: not a mass national movement but the inter-communal Alliance Party, which won fifty-one of fifty-two seats in 1955.

The qualification: the difference is of degree. Cyprus and Nigeria faced the same question; Malaya answered it more successfully.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the two-questions-are-one-answer formulation in the second paragraph is the single highest-value sentence available on this demand, because it shows that excluding Singapore was a demographic decision rather than an administrative one. Note also the qualification in the final sentence: conceding that the difference is of degree, after arguing that it is fundamental, is what turns assertion into examination.

## Question 5 - 20 marks, 250 words

**"Decolonisation transferred sovereignty faster than it built stable states." Critically examine.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Critically examine* - test both halves and state the relation between them; two lists side by side will not satisfy "critically".

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** Both halves are true and the interesting question is how they are related.

The transfer was genuine and rapid. In 1946 there were seventy-four nation-states; by 1995 there were one hundred and ninety-two. New states gained real control inside their frontiers: Kenya lifted restrictions on what the Kikuyu could grow and coffee became a main crop.

The instability was equally real, and its causes were largely inherited. Most African territories had very little industry because this had been a deliberate colonial policy, the colonies being intended to buy manufactures and supply raw materials, so a fall in one commodity price became a fiscal crisis - Nigeria's oil supplied about eighty per cent of its income. Frontiers were European creations; Belgium left the Congo with seventeen graduates; Nyasaland had three secondary schools for three million people and no industrial factory.

But the relation is not one of simple determination. Ghana had the best preparation in British Africa - five supervised years the account calls unique - and produced a one-party state by 1964 and a coup in 1966 attributed to contested CIA backing. The Congo had the worst and collapsed too. Nigeria fought a civil war to January 1970 and then recovered remarkably swiftly, Gowon taking no revenge and expanding the federation from twelve states to nineteen.

The inheritance set the constraints; choices made inside them determined outcomes. Sovereignty was transferred completely; the economic structure that made sovereignty hard to use was not.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the closing two-sentence formulation is the difference between a twenty-mark answer and an extended fifteen. Stating that the inheritance set constraints while choices determined outcomes converts two descriptive halves into a causal argument, and the Ghana-Congo pairing is the evidence that makes it defensible: the best-prepared and the worst-prepared states both failed, which rules out preparation as the operative variable.

## Question 6 - 20 marks, 250 words

**Evaluate the claim that political independence was achieved without economic decolonisation, with reference to at least three territories.**

⚠️ **Directive:** *Evaluate* - judge the proposition including what is right in the opposing view; three territories is a floor, and a survey of six will lose depth.

**Model answer (250-word ceiling).** The proposition is well supported, with one genuine qualification.

The design is recorded rather than inferred. Britain expected influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership, a practice which became known as neo-colonialism and widespread in most new Third World states.

French West Africa is the clearest case. De Gaulle's 1958 plan offered self-government inside a French Community in which France would take all important decisions about taxation and foreign affairs, with aid withheld from any territory refusing. Guinea refused by ninety-five per cent and lost all aid; the remaining territories became independent in 1960, and the account records de Gaulle as intent on neo-colonialism, all of them except Guinea finding France still influencing their economic and foreign policies.

Ghana shows the mechanism physically. A smelting plant was built to process Ghanaian bauxite; its American owner insisted on buying bauxite abroad. When the world cocoa price fell, the resulting deficit was unmanageable.

Zimbabwe shows the land version. Majority rule came in April 1980 with about four thousand white farmers still holding roughly half the arable land - a colonial distribution surviving the political settlement by twenty years.

The qualification is that sovereignty was not nominal. Guinea escaped the influence precisely because it refused the aid; Tanzania nationalised foreign enterprises; Sukarno seized Dutch property.

But the option was punished. Western states cut aid, destabilised or overthrew governments in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica.

⚠️ **Scoring note for this answer:** the qualification paragraph is what makes this an evaluation rather than an assertion. Conceding that sovereignty was real, evidencing the concession with Guinea, Tanzania and Indonesia, and then showing that the exercise of that sovereignty was punished, demonstrates the capacity to hold a position against its strongest counter-evidence - which is precisely what "evaluate" demands and what most scripts on this question omit.

# REMEDIATION

⚠️ These are the error families that recur in scripts on this topic. ⚠️ Each entry names the error, gives the correction from evidence taught above, and states the diagnostic that prevents recurrence.

## Error family A - dates and their phenomena

1. ⚠️ **Error:** dating Ghana's independence to the first African independence of any kind. ✅ **Correction:** the Gold Coast was the first black African state **south of the Sahara** to win independence after the Second World War, taking the name Ghana in 1957. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** carry the qualifier that the source carries.
2. ⚠️ **Error:** treating Malayan independence as complete in 1957. ✅ **Correction:** the Federation of Malaya was formed in 1948, independence came in 1957, the Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963, Brunei declined to join and became independent in 1984, and Singapore separated in 1965. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** in plural societies, independence is a date inside a longer process.
3. ⚠️ **Error:** dating the Algerian war from de Gaulle's return. ✅ **Correction:** the FLN launched its guerrilla war towards the end of 1954; de Gaulle returned in May 1958 and independence came in July 1962. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** separate the start of a conflict from the metropolitan crisis it produced.
4. ⚠️ **Error:** dating the end of the Central African Federation to the independence of Malawi and Zambia. ✅ **Correction:** the Federation was terminated in December 1963; Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia became independent the following year. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** dissolution and independence are distinct acts.
5. ⚠️ **Error:** placing Zimbabwe's independence at the unilateral declaration. ✅ **Correction:** UDI was November 1965; the Lancaster House Conference ran September to December 1979; independence came in April 1980. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** an unrecognised declaration is not an independence.
6. ⚠️ **Error:** dating Namibia's decolonisation to its mandate. ✅ **Correction:** German South West Africa was handed to South Africa in 1919 to be prepared for independence, and independence came only in 1990, under the December 1988 settlement trading South African withdrawal against the departure of the fifty thousand Cuban troops from Angola.
7. ⚠️ **Error:** confusing the two Congolese independence dates with the Rwandan one. ✅ **Correction:** the Belgian Congo became independent on 30 June 1960; Ruanda-Urundi was divided into Rwanda and Burundi in 1962.

## Error family B - figures and attribution

8. ⚠️ **Error:** "a hundred thousand Kenyans died in British camps", or "Anderson and Elkins record a hundred thousand deaths". ✅ **Correction:** the evidence appeared in 2005 in two separate books. Anderson records more than a thousand Kikuyu hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting; Elkins separately estimated up to a hundred thousand deaths in the detention camps, an estimate contested by demographic work that puts the figure far lower, with no reconciled total. Fewer than a hundred whites were killed, and before 2005 the British government had hidden the scale from people at home. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** if the sentence has no author in it, it is not yet finished; if it has two authors sharing one figure, it is wrong.
9. ⚠️ **Error:** "150,000 harkis were killed after Algerian independence". ✅ **Correction:** Algerian Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some two hundred thousand who had served in the French army, were denounced as traitors; nobody knows how many were executed or murdered, but some estimates put the total as high as a hundred and fifty thousand. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** retain the phrase "nobody knows how many".
10. ⚠️ **Error:** giving one figure for East Timor. ✅ **Correction:** the same account states in one place that about a hundred thousand were killed, one-sixth of the population, with another three hundred thousand in detention camps, and in another that around two hundred thousand were killed out of a total population of only seven hundred thousand. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** report the divergence; it is itself a finding.
11. ⚠️ **Error:** merging African mortality figures into a continental total. ✅ **Correction:** around fifty thousand Tutsi killed in Burundi in 1993 and about eight hundred thousand Tutsi and moderate Hutu murdered in Rwanda in 1994 with about a million refugees, both as stated by the textbook account, which names no producing body or counting period for either; a calculation reported in 2002 putting Angola's dead at 1.5 million with about four million displaced; almost three million reported lost in the Congo since 1998, most from starvation and disease. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask what each figure counts and who counted it.
12. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Indonesian coup killed half a million communists". ✅ **Correction:** the New Order purge is recorded as one in which at least half a million people were murdered and the Communist Party was broken, with few protests from the West because in the Cold War atmosphere the anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** "at least" is a floor, not a total.
13. ⚠️ **Error:** attributing Angola's missing oil revenue or child-soldier figures generically. ✅ **Correction:** the IMF recorded over four billion dollars of oil receipts missing since 1996; Human Rights Watch reported 86,000 UNITA child soldiers and 3,000 in government forces. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** name the body.
14. ⚠️ **Error:** stating Nyasaland's educational provision loosely. ✅ **Correction:** when the British withdrew there were only three secondary schools for three million Africans and not one single industrial factory; and in 1956 only fifty Africans in the whole of Mozambique had received any secondary education. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** these are exact and their exactness is the point.

## Error family C - mechanisms and causation

15. ⚠️ **Error:** "European exhaustion caused decolonisation". ✅ **Correction:** exhaustion reduced the supply of coercion; colonial nationalism raised the demand for it. France, the Netherlands and Portugal were all exhausted and all chose to fight first. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** name which side of the equation the factor operates on.
16. ⚠️ **Error:** "a defeated insurgency achieved nothing". ✅ **Correction:** Kenya's uprising was defeated by 1960, and by then the British, encouraged by the wind of change and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** insurgencies work by cost imposition, not by victory.
17. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Cold War caused African civil wars". ✅ **Correction:** there would no doubt have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War to Angola certainly made the conflict much worse - and when the war resumed in 1992 after a generally free and fair election, that was clearly the fault of UNITA. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** copy the account's own formulation.
18. ⚠️ **Error:** "Britain gave independence where it was ready and fought where it was not". ✅ **Correction:** Britain's own classification was by settler presence; and the concessions consistently followed demonstrations of strength - the release of Nkrumah after the 1950 strike, the preparation of Nigeria after the 1945 strike, and the change of Kenyan policy after an expensive campaign.
19. ⚠️ **Error:** "Botha's reforms were the beginning of the end of apartheid because they liberalised the system". ✅ **Correction:** their stated purpose was to preserve white control; they failed because they conceded everything except the vote, and black Africans were incensed that the new constitution made no provision for them. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask what the reform was for.
20. ⚠️ **Error:** "the Congo failed because it was unprepared". ✅ **Correction:** the absence of a successor class was the achieved object of a stated Belgian policy of denying Africans advanced education precisely to deprive them of a professional class who could lead them to independence. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether the deficiency was a by-product or the design.
21. ⚠️ **Error:** "sanctions ended white minority rule". ✅ **Correction:** in Rhodesia neighbouring states refused to comply and sanctioning states' own firms evaded; in South Africa the 1962 UN boycott was useless and the major western states sold massive arms supplies until well into the 1970s. Both outcomes required regional and internal change as well.

## Error family D - categories and comparisons

22. ⚠️ **Error:** using genocide, apartheid and civil war interchangeably. ✅ **Correction:** Rwanda 1994 was a genocide, evidenced by intent, planning and government backing, running concurrently with a civil war; apartheid was a statutory system of racial subordination, not extermination; civil war is an organised armed contest for the state or for secession, as in Nigeria in 1967-70. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask what the perpetrator was trying to achieve.
23. ⚠️ **Error:** using "neo-colonialism" for continuing formal rule. ✅ **Correction:** it names influence exercised by economic means over a formally sovereign former colony. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** if the flag is still flying, the word is colonialism.
24. ⚠️ **Error:** treating settlers as agents of their metropole. ✅ **Correction:** Kenyan settlers refused to negotiate and provoked confrontation to force Britain's hand; Massu and Salan prepared to airlift troops into Paris in May 1958; Smith declared independence against the wishes of Britain in November 1965. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether the settlers ever acted against their own metropole.
25. ⚠️ **Error:** treating "one-party state" as a single category. ✅ **Correction:** in Kenya and Tanzania it provided stable and effective government, and Tanzanian constituencies offered a choice of two TANU candidates with large numbers of MPs losing their seats, while Ghana banned all other parties in 1964. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether there was competition inside the party.
26. ⚠️ **Error:** using "tribe" as an analytical category. ✅ **Correction:** the source itself notes these are the groups as they now tend to be called ethnic groups; Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language, looked very much alike and were distinguished by occupation. ⚠️ **Diagnostic:** ask whether the identity was fixed by colonial administration.
27. ⚠️ **Error:** placing all federations on a single success-failure spectrum. ✅ **Correction:** the West Indies Federation failed because members did not want the same state; the Central African Federation failed because members did not consent to its purpose; the Federation of Malaysia survived because the central government had firm overall control.

## Error family E - boundaries and scope

28. ⚠️ **Error:** narrating Indian partition inside a world-history decolonisation answer. ✅ **Correction:** that material belongs to Modern Indian History; this session supplies the historiographical structure and the demonstration effect on white South Africa in 1947-48.
29. ⚠️ **Error:** carrying Indo-China past 1954. ✅ **Correction:** the French exit ends at the Geneva Conference of July 1954; what followed is owned by the Cold War and Asian communism material.
30. ⚠️ **Error:** importing Suez or Arab-Israeli material. ✅ **Correction:** this session owns the mandate form and its independence dates only.
31. ⚠️ **Error:** asserting a contemporary African or Asian statistic. ✅ **Correction:** every figure in this session is dated and belongs to its stated year; the only current item is the dated United Nations report in Lesson 6.
32. ⚠️ **Error:** treating the New Imperialism as part of this topic. ✅ **Correction:** the conquest, the frontier-drawing and the colonial fixing of identities are owned elsewhere; this session inherits their consequences and says so.

## Map 2 - The causal chain from colonial economy to post-colonial crisis

```text
   COLONIAL POLICY: "very little industry; this had been a DELIBERATE
   POLICY by the colonial powers" - colonies to BUY MANUFACTURES from
   Europe/USA and to SUPPLY FOOD AND RAW MATERIALS
                              |
                              v
   AT INDEPENDENCE: reliance on ONE OR TWO EXPORT COMMODITIES
   (Nigeria: oil ~80% of annual income; Ghana: cocoa;
    Tanzania: coffee and tea; Zambia: copper)
                              |
        +---------------------+---------------------+
        |                                           |
        v                                           v
   WORLD PRICE FALLS                          POPULATION GROWS
   (Ghana's cocoa; Nigeria's oil at $10        at over 2% a year
   in June 1986 against a $23.50 budget;
   Tanzania's coffee and tea from 1978)
        |                                           |
        v                                           |
   BALANCE-OF-PAYMENTS CRISIS                       |
        |                                           |
        v                                           |
   BORROWING ABROAD -> HEAVY DEBT                   |
        |                                           |
        v                                           |
   DEBT SERVICE REQUIRES MORE EXPORTS <-------------+
   (Busia: about 40% of Ghana's export profits)
        |
        v
   LAND AND LABOUR SHIFT FURTHER TO EXPORT CROPS
        |
        v
   "food for home consumption became SCARCER"
        |
        v
   IMF CONDITIONALITY (ESAP): devaluation; food subsidies cut;
   spending on education, health and social services cut
        |                                    |                    |
        v                                    v                    v
   GHANA: recovery - production up      TANZANIA: school fee   ZIMBABWE: cuts
   7%, inflation down to 40% by 1985    condition in April     "very much
                                        2000, primary numbers  against public
                                        FALL SHARPLY           opinion"
        |
        v
   POLITICAL CONSEQUENCE: the leader answers the crisis by CLOSING
   POLITICS -> the ARMY becomes the only instrument of change -> COUP
   (Ghana 1966; Nigeria 1966, 1983, 1985)
        |
        v
   DEPENDENCE on western markets and investment "enabled those
   countries to exert some control over African governments"
   = NEO-COLONIALISM
        |
        v
   RESULT: "Africa... was the only area of the world where, in 1987,
   incomes were on average LOWER THAN IN 1972."
```

## Map 4 - The settler ladder

```text
   INCREASING SETTLER WEIGHT ->  INCREASING DIFFICULTY OF WITHDRAWAL

   [0] NO SETTLERS OF CONSEQUENCE
       West Africa: Europeans "administrators rather than permanent
       settlers with profitable estates to defend"
       => Gold Coast 1957, Nigeria 1960, Sierra Leone 1961,
          Gambia 1965 - NO COLONIAL WAR
                              |

       Tunisia ~250,000 (1945); Morocco ~300,000 (1945)
       => negotiated exits in 1956, with an indigenous authority

                              |

       Kenya: 66,000 whites against 10 million Africans, but "it was
       the white settlers who had the political influence over the
       British government"; only SIX African seats in a council of 54
       => emergency 1952-60; independence December 1963
                              |
   [3] SETTLERS AGGREGATING ACROSS TERRITORIES
       Central African Federation 1953: ~300,000 Europeans out of
       ~8.5 million; parliament "heavily weighted to favour the
       whites"; the goal was DOMINION STATUS
       => dissolved December 1963 by the exit clause
                              |
   [4] SETTLERS IN ARMED REBELLION AGAINST THE METROPOLE
       Southern Rhodesia: under 200,000 whites, 4 million Africans;
       UDI November 1965
       => 15 years; Lancaster House 1979; independence April 1980
                              |
   [5] SETTLERS LEGALLY FUSED WITH THE METROPOLE
       Algeria: over 1,000,000 settlers holding about a third of the
       most fertile land; a "province of metropolitan France"
       => war 1954-62; 700,000 French troops by 1960; the Fourth
          Republic destroyed; about 800,000 settlers leave
                              |
   [6] SETTLERS IN POWER, LEGISLATING THEIR SUPREMACY
       South Africa: apartheid from 1948
       => no transfer at all; 46 years; a negotiated constitution
          and majority rule in 1994
```

## Map 6 - Retrieval drills

⚠️ Use these as timed recall exercises rather than as reading. ⚠️ Each should be answerable in under a minute once the session has been worked through.

**Drill 1 - the date ladder.** Recite in order with the phenomenon named: 1941, 1944, 1946, 1947, 1948, 1949, 1951, 1952, 1953, May 1954, July 1954, April 1955, 1956, 1957, 1958, January 1959, 14 December 1960, 30 June 1960, 1961, July 1962, September 1963, December 1963, November 1965, 1967, January 1970, 1974, 1975, 1976, 1979, April 1980, 1986, December 1988, 1990, 1994, 2002.

**Drill 2 - the five wartime mechanisms.** Name them, say which continent each bit hardest, and give one piece of evidence for each.

**Drill 3 - the six routes.** Name them with a defining case and the decisive condition for each.

**Drill 4 - the settler ladder.** Recite the seven rungs of Map 4 with the numbers for each.

**Drill 5 - the causation dispute.** State Holland's metropolitan thesis, the test question against it, and the resolution, then place three territories on the scale.

**Drill 6 - the attribution table.** For each of the seven episodes of mass death or detention in this session, state the figure with its author or reporting body and its period, without merging any of them.

**Drill 7 - the four common problems.** Recite them with the deliberate-policy point and the 1980s disaster sequence.

**Drill 8 - the four institutions of apartheid and the five statutory features**, then the contradiction, then the four phases of resistance with the state conduct that caused each change of form.

**Drill 9 - the three neo-colonialism statements.** British expectation, French intention, structural condition - with the Ghanaian smelter as the physical illustration.

**Drill 10 - the five master theses.** Recite them from memory; each should take about fifteen seconds.

**Drill 11 - the boundary list.** Name the five subject boundaries and what each neighbour holds.

**Drill 12 - the directive map.** Given any of the ten demands in Lesson 24 section 8, state the thesis to lead with and the trap to avoid.

# COMPLETE CONSOLIDATED REGISTER NOTES

⚠️ These notes are for revision after the lessons, not instead of them. ⚠️ Each section compresses one lesson into the form a script actually needs: the spine of the argument, the dates and figures worth carrying, the evidence with its attribution, and the error the examiner is most likely to reward you for avoiding.

## 1. The 1945 map, and the four legal forms

⚠️ **Spine:** the form of rule predicts the exit better than the identity of the ruler does.
✅ **Holdings.** Britain the largest in area - India, Burma, Ceylon, Malaya, enormous tracts of Africa, Cyprus, Hong Kong, the West Indies, the Falklands, Gibraltar. France second - Africa, Indo-China, the West Indies. Netherlands - the Dutch East Indies, Surinam, West Indian islands. Belgium - the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi. Portugal - Angola, Mozambique, Guinea, East Timor. Spain - Spanish Sahara, Spanish Morocco, Ifni, Spanish Guinea. Italy - Libya, Somalia, Eritrea.
✅ **Four forms.** Colony under direct rule; protectorate with an indigenous ruler retained (Tunisia under a bey, Morocco under a king); mandate carrying an express obligation to prepare for independence; and integration, where the territory is declared part of the metropole. ⚠️ Only the mandate carries a written end-point, which is why mandates went first - Transjordan and French Syria in 1946, the Palestine mandate closing in 1948.
✅ **Settler territories named by the account:** Algeria, Kenya, Tanganyika, Uganda and Rhodesia, where large numbers of whites had settled and were relentlessly hostile to independence under black rule.
✅ **Integration case:** Algeria was treated not as a colony or protectorate but as an extension or province of metropolitan France.
✅ **The Commonwealth** was continuity designed in advance: Britain granted independence when territories were felt to be ready, most new states stayed for the advantages to be gained, and Britain expected influence to survive through the trade links membership preserved - the expectation later named neo-colonialism.
✅ **Poor-metropole inversion.** The Netherlands, Belgium, Spain and Portugal held on harder, probably because, being less wealthy, they lacked the resources to sustain neo-colonialism.
✅ **Cluster to quote:** 1960 alone produced fourteen French-administered territories - the **eleven** of French West and Equatorial Africa that voted yes in 1958, with **Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar** - plus Nigeria, Somaliland, Cyprus and the Belgian Congo. ⚠️ Guinea belongs to **1958**, not to this cluster. ✅ Italy's empire was removed by a decision of 1947 - Ethiopia having already been liberated and Haile Selassie restored in 1941, with Italy renouncing its claims in 1947; Libya 1951, Eritrea joined to Ethiopia 1952, Italian Somaliland merged into Somalia 1960.
❌ **Trap:** treating "British = peaceful, French = violent". Britain fought in Kenya, Malaya and Cyprus; France negotiated Tunisia and Morocco in 1956.

## 2. The war as accelerant: five mechanisms

⚠️ **Spine:** an accelerant is not a cause. The war did not create the demand; it changed the price of refusing it.
✅ **Mechanism 1 - prestige.** Japanese forces conquered Malaya, Singapore, Hong Kong, Burma, the Dutch East Indies and French Indo-China; Britain, France and Holland had failed miserably to protect their subjects, destroying any claim to legitimacy they might have had.
✅ **Mechanism 2 - capacity.** Nationalists continued to fight with guerrilla tactics learned against the Japanese, in Indo-China, the Dutch East Indies, Malaya and Burma. ✅ Some Asian leaders worked with the Japanese expecting better independence prospects; Sukarno gained governing experience, declared an independent republic on 18 August 1945 and was its first president from that date, Dutch recognition following only in 1949.
✅ **Mechanism 3 - comparison.** Some 374,000 Africans were recruited into the British armed forces; most had never left home and were appalled at the contrast between conditions in Africa and in the forces. ⚠️ This mechanism is African, which is part of why Africa's wave followed Asia's by roughly a decade.
✅ **Mechanism 4 - promises.** Atlantic Charter, 1941: no territorial expansion at others' expense, and all peoples to have the right to choose their own form of government. ✅ Churchill later said it applied only to victims of Hitler's aggression, but hopes had already been raised; Roosevelt insisted it applied to all peoples and, with Truman, pressed Britain on India. ⚠️ A promise cannot be withdrawn once it has been heard.
✅ **Mechanism 5 - solvency.** The war left European states not militarily or economically strong enough to hold their empires against really determined campaigns; Britain recognised this first. ✅ Porter: the British Empire had always been "a cheapskate affair", managed by devolving rule and expense to settlers or chiefs, which diluted Britain's own power. ✅ Macleod: "we could not possibly have held by force our territories in Africa; the march of men towards freedom cannot be halted; it can only be guided."
✅ **Presentation.** The British concentrated on making withdrawals look good - in control, intended all along, not scuttling away. ✅ France went the other way: the Brazzaville Declaration of 1944 ruled out autonomy or self-government even at a distant date.
❌ **Trap:** writing "the war made decolonisation inevitable". France, the Netherlands, Spain and Portugal all fought first and conceded later, which is exactly what inevitability would have made unnecessary.

## 3. Causation: the metropolitan thesis and its critics

⚠️ **Spine:** the decision was usually taken in Europe; the price that made the decision necessary was usually set in the colony.
✅ **Why the debate exists now.** More documents became available during the 1990s, enabling deeper investigation of European motives and methods of withdrawal.
✅ **The question.** How far was decolonisation caused by local nationalist movements, and how far by outside political and economic considerations?
✅ **Pole A - Holland's metropolitan thesis.** The powers divested themselves not because internal pressures left no choice, but through a revisionist process that made imperial roles look incongruent with modern foreign and economic goals.
✅ **Pole B - agency.** More credit to local nationalist movements; in some cases the imperial power was simply expelled by sheer force.
✅ **The test question.** Would Britain have left East and Central Africa for purely metropolitan reasons if there had been no nationalist movements there?
✅ **The resolution given by the account:** no simple answer; all these factors were present in varying degrees in all colonial territories. ⚠️ Read that as an instruction to differentiate territory by territory, not as a refusal to decide.
✅ **Metropolitan evidence:** the 1960 cluster; de Gaulle's 1958 referendum design; Britain's expectation of influence through trade and Commonwealth; Italy's empire removed by the victors in 1947.
✅ **Agency evidence:** Dien Bien Phu, May 1954; 700,000 French troops in Algeria by 1960; four years of Dutch war to 1949; forty per cent of Portugal's budget on three colonial wars by 1973; Nkrumah's general strike of 1950; Azikiwe's of 1945.
✅ **Indian dispute, used only as the model of the argument's shape:** the official careful-preparation version; Sarkar and Anita Inder Singh's rejoinder that the 1919 and 1935 Acts postponed rather than prepared, and that independence was "the hard-won fruit of struggle and sacrifice"; the drain-on-resources reading; Brasted's middle view citing Labour's pre-war policy and the Attlee-Nehru discussion of 1938. ✅ Conduct assessments diverge too - Attlee blaming Indian disagreement, Menon crediting Britain with universal respect and goodwill, Brasted noting worse was possible, Wilson faulting Mountbatten on peacekeeping and frontiers.
⚠️ **The four separable claims:** intention, interest, causation and conduct. They can all be true at once, and a good answer says which one it is answering.
❌ **Trap:** accepting the metropole's own preparation narrative as evidence. The British deliberately made withdrawals look controlled and intended, so that narrative needs corroboration before it is used.

## 4. The social bases: elite, press, strike, town

⚠️ **Spine:** two supplies had to meet - an elite that supplied argument, legitimacy and an international audience, and a town-and-mine population that supplied pressure. Neither moved a timetable alone.
✅ **The elite mechanism.** African nationalism spread rapidly after 1945 because more Africans were educated in Britain and the USA, where they were made aware of racial discrimination. ✅ Colonialism was seen as the humiliation and exploitation of blacks by whites, and the mass of Africans in the new towns were particularly receptive.
✅ **Four formations, four predictions.** Nkrumah - London and the USA, CPP from 1949, boycotts, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950, imprisoned then released once the British realised he had mass support. Azikiwe - educated in the USA, newspaper editor in the Gold Coast, back in Nigeria in 1937 founding a series of newspapers, his general strike of 1945 enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria. Nyerere - Edinburgh, TANU, insisting government must be African while assuring whites they had nothing to fear, conceded independence with black majority rule in 1961. Kenyatta - born 1894, Kikuyu, in Britain during the 1930s, back in 1947, leading the KAU and wanting gradual majority rule, with the radical Forty Group wanting to drive the British out by force.
⚠️ **The moderate-in-jail rule.** Imprisoning the moderate transfers the movement to people the metropole cannot negotiate with. ✅ France banned New Destour and jailed Bourguiba in 1952, found the guerrilla movement becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate, and released him. ✅ Kenyatta was jailed six years although he had publicly condemned violence and said the KAU had not organised the rebellion.
✅ **Three instruments:** the newspaper, the party - the CPP won 34 of 38 seats in 1951 - and the general strike. ✅ Labour repression is the mirror: France clamped down in 1949, arresting nationalist leaders and trade unionists and often denouncing them as communist agitators without much evidence; Moroccan trade unions played an important role; South Africa's law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs.
✅ **Land and labour grievance.** Kenya's fertile highland plateau reserved to white settlers. Algeria's settlers holding about a third of the most fertile land and growing export crops and vines while African living standards fell. South Africa's reserves at about seven per cent of the country, a ban on buying land outside them, and compounds holding up to ninety men.
✅ **Structural cause of the town.** Very little industry in most African territories was a deliberate colonial policy: colonies were to buy manufactured goods from Europe or the USA and supply food and raw materials.
⚠️ **Women, stated honestly.** The evidence base read for this session records what colonial and apartheid law did to women - pass laws and compounds separating families, the prohibition of interracial marriage and sexual relations with police surveillance, inferior schooling - and records demands and later policies touching women, in the Freedom Charter's equal pay, medical care and education, in Burundi's post-settlement provisions for women and children, and in the record that Sharia punishments were especially severe on women in twelve northern Nigerian states by the end of 2002, opposed by the federal government. ❌ It does not record women's organisation inside the nationalist movements. That is logged as an open gap and is not filled from memory.

## 5. Pan-Africanism, and its defeat by the states it helped create

⚠️ **Spine:** Pan-Africanism supplied the pre-national identity that made territorial nationalism possible, and was then defeated by the territorial states it had helped to create.
✅ **Definition.** All people of African descent, wherever they lived, united by the same cultural and spiritual heritage.
✅ **Origin outside Africa.** First publicised by people of African origin living outside Africa - Marcus Garvey, a self-educated Jamaican and founder of the Universal Negro Improvement Association, and W. E. B. Du Bois, the first African American to earn a doctorate from Harvard. ✅ By the end of the Second World War some African students, mainly from British colonies, had taken it up.
✅ **The double ambition.** Encouragement to independence, and, after independence, abandoning the artificial frontiers set up by the Europeans in favour of a federal United States of Africa on the American model. ⚠️ The second half is the radical half, because it proposes to undo the colonial map rather than to inherit it.
✅ **The campaign and the vote against it.** Nkrumah, first prime minister of a semi-independent Gold Coast and first president of Ghana, organised conferences of African leaders. Supporters: Guinea, Mali and Morocco, with an economic union formed with Guinea and Mali though nothing much came of it. Opponents: a majority who, having just won independence, saw little point in surrendering a large proportion of it - strongest were Ethiopia and Liberia, independent for generations, together with Nigeria, Sierra Leone and almost all the former French colonies. ✅ Some leaders suspected Nkrumah of delusions of grandeur, seeing himself as president of a federal Africa.
✅ **Addis Ababa, 1963.** The prospect had disappeared; the conference decided instead on the Organization of African Unity, a much less binding arrangement while still displaying a sort of unity.
✅ **Record in practice.** Unable to mediate in Nigeria's civil war; its first-ever peacekeeping deployment, to Burundi, could not prevent the massacres; it did not even condemn the Rwandan genocide. ✅ Effective where interests aligned: it condemned apartheid and was particularly critical of South Africa's continued occupation of South West Africa, and Nyerere chaired it in 1984-5.
✅ **The account's verdict:** Pan-Africanism had not been totally irrelevant - it had been an important influence on the rise of nationalist movements in many of the former colonies.
❌ **Trap:** scoring Pan-Africanism only against the federation it failed to build. Its measurable achievement is upstream, in the movements it helped produce.

## 6. External pressure: Washington, Moscow, the United Nations

⚠️ **Spine:** external pressure was the only factor operating on both sides of the equation at once - raising the cost of holding empire and lowering the cost of resisting it - which is why the process synchronised globally.
✅ **Washington, four motives.** Anti-imperial self-image, remembering that the USA had been the earliest part of the British Empire to declare independence in 1776; the containment argument that delay would encourage communism in Asia and Africa, which Porter qualifies as having some truth for Asia but comparatively little application to Africa, where communist influence was small; irritation at Churchill's imperialism, which Peter Clarke records as making Americans determined not to help Britain keep its empire; and, the account judges more important, the view of new nations as potential markets into which American firms could force their way, since imperially protected markets gave Europeans an unfair advantage.
✅ **The other half of American conduct.** A pattern of intervention against successor governments in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica. ✅ Named instances: CIA involvement in the Indonesian coup of 1965; American encouragement of the 1966 coup against Nkrumah, which the textbook account calls full CIA backing though the claim is contested; American advisers, cash and armaments to the FNLA in Angola; Ford's go-ahead to Suharto over East Timor; Reagan's encouragement to Morocco in Western Sahara.
✅ **Moscow.** Constant denunciation of imperialism; economic and military aid from the communist bloc to the nationalists in the Portuguese colonies; Russian weapons and a Cuban army enabling the MPLA to defeat both invasions by March 1976. ✅ Soviet recognition of the Saharan republic aroused American suspicions, and the account's verdict on that case is that yet another new Third World country had become a victim of superpower self-interest.
✅ **The United Nations.** Under American influence it came out firmly against imperialism and demanded a step-by-step programme for decolonisation. ✅ Resolution 1514 (XV), the Declaration on the Granting of Independence to Colonial Countries and Peoples, adopted 14 December 1960 without a single expressed dissent. ✅ Its principles: alien subjugation denies fundamental human rights; all peoples have the right to self-determination including economic, social and cultural development; armed action against dependent peoples is to cease; immediate steps are to transfer all powers in Trust and Non-Self-Governing Territories; and national unity and territorial integrity are not to be disrupted.
✅ **Why 1960 and not 1950.** The Assembly had changed - sixteen new states admitted in 1955 taking the total to seventy-six, and seventeen more during 1960, sixteen of them on 20 September and Nigeria on 7 October, taking membership from eighty-two to ninety-nine - forming a coalition styled variously the Non-Aligned bloc, the Group of 77, the Bandung group or the Third World. ✅ The Special Committee on Decolonization, the C-24, was established in 1961 to monitor progress under Chapter XI of the Charter.
📰 **The one current item in this session, 16 February 2026:** seventeen Non-Self-Governing Territories remain, and more than eighty former colonies comprising some 750 million people have gained independence since 1945. ⚠️ Used once, in Lesson 6, and not carried into any historical argument.
❌ **Trap:** reading 1514 as the cause of the 1960 cluster. The cluster and the resolution are the same phenomenon at two levels - new states made the majority that passed the norm.

## 7. Bandung, Belgrade and Global South agency

⚠️ **Spine:** three successive framings by one generation - continental federation, Afro-Asian conference, non-aligned posture - each broader and less binding, because sovereign states co-operate exactly as far as co-operation does not diminish sovereignty.
✅ **Bandung.** Asian-African Conference, Bandung, Indonesia, 18-24 April 1955, twenty-nine countries, adopting a ten-point declaration, the Dasasila Bandung. ✅ Its principles cover human rights and the Charter; sovereignty and territorial integrity; equality of all races and of all nations large and small; non-intervention; the right of self-defence under the Charter; abstention from collective defence arrangements serving big-power interests and from pressure between countries; no aggression or threat of force; peaceful settlement; mutual interests and co-operation; and respect for justice and international obligations.
⚠️ **Read the document's strategy.** It claims the existing rules of sovereignty and equality for everybody rather than proposing a new order - the strongest available position for a weak party, because it makes the opponent's own law the standard.
✅ **Belgrade.** First Non-Aligned summit, Belgrade, Yugoslavia, 1-6 September 1961, twenty-five founding states, with Tito, Nehru, Nasser, Nkrumah and Sukarno the associated leaders.
⚠️ **The structural difference.** Bandung was identity-based, Afro-Asian; Belgrade was posture-based, non-aligned - which is why Yugoslavia could lead the second and not the first, and why a posture-based movement can recruit and bargain when an identity-based one cannot.
⚠️ **The self-reinforcing chain:** decolonisation creates states, states form a bloc, the bloc legislates the norm, the norm accelerates decolonisation. ✅ The bloc carried 1514 (XV) in December 1960 without a single expressed dissent.
✅ **Limits, stated honestly.** Indonesia, Bandung's host, annexed East Timor in 1975.
