Sukarno was displaced from 1965 by a military takeover involving the CIA, authority passing to Suharto under Supersemar in March 1966 and the presidency being removed by the MPRS in March 1967, with his leadership of the non-aligned movements among the stated American objections. The OAU could not mediate in Nigeria, could not stop the Burundi massacres, and did not condemn the Rwandan genocide. ⚠️ The economic claim went unmet, because the principles contain no mechanism for altering commodity dependence or debt.
❌ **Trap:** treating Bandung and NAM as the same body. Different dates, different membership criteria, different organising logic.

## 8. The route typology and the settler variable

⚠️ **Spine:** the form decolonisation took was determined less by the coloniser's intentions than by whether settlers, strategic assets or an unsettled successor question made withdrawal negotiable.
✅ **The account's own regional classification of British Africa.** West Africa, with relatively few Europeans and those administrators rather than permanent settlers with profitable estates to defend, making the move comparatively straightforward. East Africa - Kenya, Uganda, Tanganyika - complicated, especially in Kenya, by settlers who feared for their future under black governments. Central Africa - Nyasaland, Northern and Southern Rhodesia - where the settler factor was at its most serious, settlers most firmly entrenched with huge and profitable estates, and confrontation most bitter.
✅ **The outcomes match the classification.** West Africa 1957-65 with no colonial war; Tanganyika 1961 and Uganda 1962 by negotiation but Kenya 1963 after an emergency declared in 1952; Malawi and Zambia 1964 but Southern Rhodesia 1980.
⚠️ **Why settlers change everything, in four steps.** Withdrawal becomes expropriation rather than a change of administration; settlers hold political influence inside the metropole; they advance a claim of belonging that no compensation can buy out; and they can act against their own metropole.
✅ **Settler autonomy in evidence:** Kenyan settlers refusing to negotiate with Kenyatta and provoking confrontation; Massu and Salan's Operation Resurrection of May 1958; Smith's unilateral declaration of November 1965; the Nationalist victory of 1948 in South Africa.
⚠️ **Six routes:** negotiated transfer; delayed transfer with counter-insurgency; hurried exit into a vacuum; settler revolt or long delay; settler-state war; and the state that never left.
⚠️ **Two cases the settler variable does not explain.** Portuguese Africa is explained by metropolitan regime type - the Salazar regime had no mechanism for conceding, so pressure broke the regime in 1974 before it released the colonies. Malaya is explained by a plural society and a contested successor state.
✅ **Secondary variables.** Strategic value - Eden judged Cyprus necessary as a military base and said it must remain permanently British, and the 1960 settlement gave independence while Britain retained two bases. Concentrated economic value - Spanish Sahara's phosphates, and Katanga's copper, with many Belgians preferring an independent Katanga they could influence. Metropolitan wealth, inverted - the poorer metropoles held on hardest.
✅ **Settler numbers to quote.** Kenya some 66,000 whites against 10 million Africans, plus around 200,000 Indians and 35,000 Muslim Arabs. Southern Rhodesia fewer than 200,000 whites, about 20,000 Asians and 4 million Africans. The Central African Federation about 300,000 Europeans out of about 8.5 million. Algeria over a million settlers and 9 million Muslim Algerians. Tunisia about 250,000 and Morocco about 300,000 in 1945.
❌ **Trap:** using the typology as a taxonomy. It earns marks only when the sorting question - was there a settler community with land to lose? - is stated and then applied.

## 9. The Gold Coast to Ghana, 1947-57

⚠️ **Spine:** a ladder of concessions, each rung following a demonstration of strength - and five supervised years that were an unusual advantage whose limits Ghana itself then demonstrated.
✅ **Position.** West Africa - the Gold Coast, Nigeria, Sierra Leone, the Gambia - had relatively few Europeans, and those were administrators rather than permanent settlers with estates to defend. ✅ The Gold Coast was the first black African state south of the Sahara to win independence after the Second World War, taking the name Ghana in 1957, the first British territory in Africa to do so, and a great source of inspiration for other African colonies.
✅ **The campaign.** Nkrumah, educated in London and the USA, led the Convention People's Party from 1949: boycotts of European goods, violent demonstrations and a general strike in 1950, followed by the imprisonment of Nkrumah and other leaders.
✅ **The concession.** Realising he had mass support, the British soon released him and agreed a new constitution - the vote for all adults, an elected Assembly, and an eleven-man Executive Council of which eight were chosen by the Assembly. ⚠️ Read the numbers: eight of eleven is the concession, and it is the detail an answer should quote.
✅ **The ladder.** February 1951 elections, the first under the new constitution - the CPP won 34 seats out of 38; Nkrumah released in February 1951 immediately after the result, invited to form a government, prime minister in 1952 with self-government but not full independence; full independence in 1957.
✅ **The five years.** A small but well-educated group of politicians and professionals gained five years' experience of government under British supervision; the account calls this experience unique to Ghana and says it might possibly have helped avoid chaos and mismanagement elsewhere.
⚠️ **The comparison that gives the five years their meaning:** the Congo received six months' notice, had only seventeen graduates, no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers, and a nationalist movement less than a year old.
✅ **Why other regions lacked the same elite.** Central Africa had far fewer well-educated Africans than West Africa because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided - settler obstruction operating indirectly as well as directly.
⚠️ **The honest qualification.** Preparation did not guarantee stability: Ghana banned all parties except Nkrumah's in 1964 and its government was removed by the army in 1966 with American encouragement and, on the textbook account's contested reading, full CIA backing.
❌ **Trap:** writing the Gold Coast as a gift. Every rung followed pressure, and the released-prisoner-to-prime-minister sequence is the proof.

## 10. Nigeria, Sierra Leone and the Gambia

⚠️ **Spine:** a federation survives by procedure; the 1964 election destroyed the procedure. Ethnic difference supplied the fault line, recession supplied the pressure, and the discredited ballot removed the remedy.
✅ **Scale.** Nigeria was easily the largest British African colony, with a population of over sixty million, and more difficult than Ghana because of great size and regional differences - the vast Muslim north dominated by the Hausa and Fulani, the western region of the Yoruba, the eastern region of the Ibo. ✅ Extensive oil resources had been discovered in the eastern coastal area.
✅ **The press before the party.** Nnamdi Azikiwe, "Zik", educated in the USA, newspaper editor in the Gold Coast, returned to Nigeria in 1937 and founded a series of newspapers, gaining enormous prestige; his impressive general strike of 1945 was enough to prompt the British to begin preparing Nigeria for independence.
✅ **The design and the sequence.** A federal system was judged most suitable; the 1954 constitution created local assemblies for the three regions with a central federal government in Lagos; the regions assumed self-government first; the country as a whole became independent in 1960; Nigeria became a republic in 1963 with Azikiwe as president and Balewa as prime minister.
✅ **Four pressures, only one of them ethnic.** The regions felt Lagos did not safeguard their interests, with Yoruba and Ibo complaining of northern domination even though Azikiwe was an Ibo; by 1964 prices had risen fifteen per cent, unemployment was rising and wages were well below the calculated minimum living wage; Balewa arrested Chief Awolowo, premier of the western region until 1959 and by then federal leader of the opposition, and the west for a time seemed likely to break away; and the central government was accused of corruption after blatantly trying to fix the 1964 elections.
✅ **The collapse.** January 1966 - a military coup by mainly Ibo officers killed Balewa; savage massacres of Ibos followed in the north; General Ironsi, an Ibo, was murdered by northern soldiers; Colonel Gowon, a northerner, emerged supreme; Ibos fled back to the east; Colonel Ojukwu announced secession as Biafra in May 1967; Gowon launched what he called a "short surgical police action".
✅ **The two quiet transfers.** Sierra Leone became independent in 1961 and the Gambia in 1965, without serious incident. ⚠️ They matter as controls: the same region and the same coloniser, without the scale and the oil.
❌ **Trap:** explaining Biafra by ethnicity alone. Ethnicity was present in 1960 too; what changed was the collapse of the procedure by which regional claims could be settled.

## 11. Kenya, Tanganyika and Uganda

⚠️ **Spine:** Kenya shows a defeated insurgency achieving its objective by cost imposition, and shows a settler community acting as a third party against both the colonised and its own metropole.
✅ **The expectation and the correction.** Britain expected East African independence later than West Africa's and in the form of multiracial governments with European and Asian settlers playing a significant part. ✅ Macmillan, Cape Town, 1960: "the wind of change is blowing through the continent... this growth of national consciousness is a political fact, and our national policies must take account of it."
✅ **The deadlock.** Ten million Africans, some 66,000 white settlers violently opposed to black majority rule, around 200,000 Indians and 35,000 Muslim Arabs, with the settlers holding political influence over the British government. ✅ The main grievance was land - the most fertile farming land was on the highland plateau and only white settlers were allowed to farm there - plus the colour bar, made more unacceptable by wartime service in which Africans had received equal treatment. ✅ Settlers claimed they had worked hard, saw themselves as white Africans, regarded Kenya as their homeland, and expected to keep all their privileges even under independence; they refused to negotiate with Kenyatta and provoked a confrontation, hoping violence would destroy the African party, supported by certain big-business interests in Britain. ✅ The only British concession for years was six Africans on a Legislative Council of fifty-four.
✅ **Kenyatta.** Born 1894, Kikuyu, in Britain during the 1930s, returned 1947, led the KAU, wanted gradual majority rule; the radical Forty Group wanted to drive the British out by force. ✅ He was jailed six years despite publicly condemning violence and denying KAU involvement in the rebellion.
✅ **The emergency.** The 1952 uprising was organised by the Mau Mau secret society, attacking European-owned farms and black workers; a state of emergency was declared in 1952; Operation Anvil in April 1954 used about 5,000 troops and 1,000 police and screened over 50,000 people; the Hola detention camp scandal of 1959 left eleven dead after savage beatings.
✅ **Attribution, exactly as recorded, and separately.** The British government hid the scale at home; the evidence was published in 2005 in two separate books. ✅ **Anderson:** more than a thousand Kikuyu hanged and some twenty thousand killed in the fighting. ✅ **Elkins, separately:** up to one hundred thousand dead in detention camps, where there was a culture of brutality, routine beatings, killings and torture. ⚠️ Elkins's camp figure is a contested estimate; demographic critiques put it far lower and no reconciled total exists. ✅ Fewer than a hundred whites were killed. ❌ These figures are never merged, averaged or jointly attributed.
✅ **Why Britain changed course.** The uprising was defeated by 1960, but by then the British, encouraged by the wind of change and by the expense of the anti-terrorist campaign, had changed their attitude; Macmillan judged it impossible and indefensible to prolong the privileged position of a group making up no more than five per cent of the population.
✅ **The ladder that followed.** African settlement on the fertile highland plateau; restrictions lifted on Kikuyu crops, making coffee a main crop; eight African seats in 1957; an African majority on the council and four of ten ministries in 1960; Kenyatta released in 1961.
✅ **The successor-state question in miniature.** KANU wanted a strong centralised government, KADU under Ronald Ngala wanted federalism; a coalition in 1962; KANU won in May 1963; federalism was abandoned; full independence in December 1963; a republic a year later with Kenyatta president and Odinga vice-president. ✅ Kenyatta favoured reconciliation, whites who took Kenyan citizenship were fairly treated, and Kenya became one of the most pro-British former colonies - while internal divisions persisted, with Mboya assassinated in 1969 and Odinga sacked and imprisoned for two years.
✅ **The two contrasts.** Tanganyika - Nyerere, Edinburgh-educated, led TANU, insisted government must be African while assuring whites; independence with black majority rule in 1961; union with Zanzibar as Tanzania in 1964; president until 1985. Uganda - delayed by the kabaka of Buganda's objection to democracy, solved by a federal constitution leaving the kabaka some powers, independent in 1962 under Milton Obote.
❌ **Trap:** calling Mau Mau a military success. It was defeated by 1960; its effect was on the British calculation of cost.

## 12. The Central African Federation, 1953-63

⚠️ **Spine:** the Federation was a constitutional weapon rather than a constitutional arrangement, and it was defeated by a procedural right its own sponsors had been forced to accept.
✅ **The setting.** Central Africa was the most troublesome region for Britain because settlers were most numerous and most deeply entrenched, particularly in Southern Rhodesia; well-educated Africans were far fewer than in West Africa because the settlers had ensured that very little education was provided.
✅ **The device.** Alarmed at the spread of nationalism, the whites decided to combine resources and persuaded Churchill's government in 1953 to allow a union of Nyasaland and Northern and Southern Rhodesia. ✅ Its aim was to preserve the supremacy of the white minority - about 300,000 Europeans out of a total population of about 8.5 million - and its federal parliament at Salisbury, the capital of Southern Rhodesia, was heavily weighted to favour the whites, who hoped for full independence from Britain with dominion status.
⚠️ **The arithmetic that defeats it.** Aggregation cannot change a territory's internal ratio, only the unit in which the ratio is counted. An exit clause therefore destroys a federation built on aggregation.
✅ **The African response.** Dr Hastings Banda in Nyasaland, Kenneth Kaunda in Northern Rhodesia and Joshua Nkomo in Southern Rhodesia campaigned for black majority rule; 1959 brought violence, a state of emergency in Nyasaland and Southern Rhodesia, and mass arrests of Africans. ✅ There was much support for the Africans in Britain, especially in the Labour Party, and the Conservative colonial secretary Iain Macleod was sympathetic.
✅ **The clause that killed it.** The Monckton Commission of 1960 recommended votes for Africans, an end to racial discrimination, and the right of territories to leave the Federation; new constitutions in Nyasaland and Northern Rhodesia in 1961-62 in effect gave the Africans their own parliaments; both wanted to leave; the Federation was terminated in December 1963 - a defeat for the settlers. ✅ The following year Nyasaland became Malawi and Northern Rhodesia became Zambia, fully independent.
⚠️ **Consequence.** Dissolution concentrated the settler problem in Southern Rhodesia, whose whites then assumed they would receive the same treatment and requested independence.
❌ **Trap:** dating the end of the Federation to the independence of Malawi and Zambia. Dissolution in December 1963 and independence in 1964 are distinct acts.

## 13. Southern Rhodesia to Zimbabwe, 1965-80

⚠️ **Spine:** sanctions failed because the region and the sanctioning states' own firms did not enforce them; the war succeeded because it attacked the settlers' agricultural asset once the regional buffer had gone.
✅ **The residue.** Fewer than 200,000 whites, about 20,000 Asians and 4 million black Africans; the Rhodesia Front, a right-wing white racist party, determined never to surrender control, with African parties banned. ✅ Britain would grant independence only if the constitution gave black Africans at least a third of parliamentary seats; Smith, prime minister from April 1964, refused, and Wilson from 1964 also refused independence without preparation for majority rule. ✅ Smith's stated reasons were the problems of new black governments elsewhere in Africa and the bitter divisions among Zimbabwe nationalists.
✅ **UDI, November 1965, and the failure of sanctions.** Britain ruled out force and relied on sanctions, stopping purchases of Rhodesian sugar and tobacco; the UN condemned UDI and called for a complete trade embargo. ✅ South Africa and Portuguese-ruled Mozambique refused to comply, so Rhodesia traded through them; many states evaded privately; the USA bought Rhodesian chrome as the cheapest available; British oil companies broke sanctions; the economy suffered but not enough to topple Smith.
✅ **Commonwealth strain.** Ghana and Nigeria wanted force and offered troops; Zambia and Tanzania preferred sanctions; Zambia suffered more from them than Rhodesia did. ✅ Wilson met Smith aboard HMS Tiger in 1966 and HMS Fearless in 1968; Smith rejected both sets of proposals.
✅ **Intermediate steps.** 1969 - the World Council of Churches programme to combat racism encouraged and financed the nationalists. 1970 - Rhodesia declared itself a republic and black citizens' rights were whittled away.
✅ **1975 as the hinge.** Mozambique's independence in June 1975 was a blow to sanctions and allowed guerrillas to operate from there; the front-line states - Zambia, Botswana, Tanzania and Mozambique - supported the armed struggle and provided training camps; South Africa became less inclined to support Rhodesia after its October 1975 invasion of Angola was called off on American orders; the USA and South Africa urged Smith to concede, fearing Soviet and Cuban involvement. ✅ By 1978 guerrillas controlled large areas of the countryside, white farmers were attacked, rural schools closed or were burnt, and Smith hired foreign mercenaries.
✅ **Nationalist division.** ZAPU under Nkomo, ZANU under Sithole, the UANC under Muzorewa, with Mugabe emerging as ZANU's unchallenged leader; the Geneva Conference of 1976 produced the loose Patriotic Front, hence ZANU-PF and PF-ZAPU. ✅ Smith's Internal Settlement, signed on 3 March 1978, produced elections in April 1979 and a Zimbabwe/Rhodesia under Muzorewa as prime minister from June 1979, but the parties with mass support continued the war.
✅ **Lancaster House, September-December 1979.** A new constitution for black majority rule; a hundred-seat parliament with twenty seats reserved for whites, uncontested; Muzorewa to step down; the war to end. ✅ ZANU won 57 of the 80 black African seats; Mugabe became prime minister at independence in April 1980; ZANU and ZAPU merged in 1987 and Mugabe became executive president.
⚠️ **The deferred question.** About 4,000 white farmers owned about half the arable land at independence, and the settlement of 1979 left that distribution untouched. ⚠️ The later history of that grievance is in register note 23, where its evidence sits.
❌ **Trap:** treating the twenty reserved seats as the settlement's core. The unresolved item was land, and it returned twenty years later.

## 14. Indo-China, Tunisia and Morocco

⚠️ **Spine:** France abandoned formal rule wherever informal influence could be preserved through a negotiable leader, and fought wherever it could not - which is why one government conceded Tunisia and Morocco in 1956 while escalating in Algeria.
✅ **The starting position.** The French began by trying to suppress all nationalist agitation, regarding it as high treason. ✅ Brazzaville Declaration, 1944: the colonising work of France makes it impossible to accept any idea of autonomy, and even at a distant date there will be no self-government in the colonies.
✅ **Indo-China.** Vietnamese nationalists had campaigned against French rule since the 1920s; Vietnamese independence was unacceptable to the French; an eight-year armed struggle culminated in the French defeat at Dien Bien Phu in May 1954. ✅ The defeat was a humiliating blow that caused a political crisis; the government resigned; Mendes-France, realising public opinion was turning against the war, decided to withdraw. ✅ The Geneva Conference of July 1954 provided that Vietnam, Laos and Cambodia should become independent. ⚠️ The Americans were unwilling to allow all Vietnam under Ho Chi Minh and a more bloody struggle developed - that struggle is owned elsewhere and this session stops at Geneva.
⚠️ **Four consequences of Dien Bien Phu for this topic:** it changed a metropolitan government; it forced the Maghreb concessions; it encouraged the formation of the FLN later in 1954; and it committed the French army to restoring its reputation in Algeria.
✅ **The protectorate route.** Tunisia and Morocco were protectorates, under a bey and a king respectively; settlers numbered about 250,000 and about 300,000 in 1945 and were committed to the French connection that guaranteed their privileged position.
✅ **Tunisia.** New Destour under Bourguiba, with widespread support among rural dwellers and townspeople who believed independence would improve living standards; a guerrilla campaign; the party banned and Bourguiba jailed in 1952; 70,000 French troops deployed and failing to crush the guerrillas. ✅ The French noticed that with the moderates jailed the movement was becoming more left-wing and less willing to negotiate, and that with a moderate like Bourguiba in charge there would be more chance of maintaining French influence after independence; Bourguiba was released, Mendes-France allowed him to form a government, and Tunisia became fully independent in March 1956.
✅ **Morocco.** Istiqlal, with King Mohammed himself in the forefront of opposition and the new trade unions playing an important role; the king was deposed in 1953, provoking violent demonstrations and a guerrilla campaign; faced with the prospect of yet another long and expensive anti-guerrilla war, the French bowed to the inevitable, the king returned, and Morocco became independent in 1956.
✅ **The repression that preceded both.** In 1949 the French clamped down on all nationalist movements and arrested many nationalist leaders and trade unionists, often denouncing them as communist agitators without much evidence.
❌ **Trap:** reading 1956 as a change of French principle. The principle was constant; only the calculation about retained influence changed.

## 15. Algeria, 1954-62

⚠️ **Spine:** where a territory is legally part of the metropole and a million settlers hold its best land, there is no act of transfer available - the only routes are enfranchisement that would transform the metropole or separation that would divide it, and the second required a war.
✅ **The legal fact.** Algeria was treated not as a colony or protectorate but as an extension or province of metropolitan France, and the nine million Muslim Arab Algerians were allowed no say in the government of their country.
✅ **The settler economy.** Over a million French settlers, the pieds noirs, controlled something like a third of all the most fertile land, taken from the original Algerian owners during the century before 1940; they exported most crops and grew vines for winemaking, so there was less food for a growing African population whose standard of living was clearly falling.
✅ **The rigged assembly.** 120 members with limited powers - a million whites elected sixty, nine million Muslims elected the other sixty, and European corruption usually gave them a majority anyway.
✅ **The failure of peaceful nationalism.** Messali Hadj led an active but peaceful movement that achieved absolutely nothing in almost ten years after the war; the stubbornness of the settlers and their refusal even to talk meant the struggle would be decided by the extremists. ✅ The FLN, led by Ben Bella, was formed after the French defeat in Indo-China and launched a guerrilla war towards the end of 1954, while promising that the pieds noirs would be treated fairly. ✅ On the textbook account's figure, by 1960 France had 700,000 troops engaged; estimates vary and the figure should carry its source.
✅ **Four effects in France.** The realisation that military victory could not secure control while the FLN retained popular support; a split in public opinion bringing France to the verge of civil war; an army determined to restore its reputation after the Second World War and Indo-China, with some generals ready to stage a coup; and the Algiers demonstrations of May 1958 by Massu and Salan with Operation Resurrection.
✅ **De Gaulle.** The government resigned; de Gaulle condemned the Fourth Republic's "regime of the parties" and declared himself ready to assume the powers of the Republic; Coty called him; he became prime minister on condition of drawing up a new constitution, and the Fourth Republic ended. ⚠️ Historians dispute how far he knew of Resurrection; what seems clear is that he knew of the plan and had hinted to Massu and Salan that he would be happy for it to proceed if Coty refused him power. ✅ The constitutional referendum of 28 September 1958 returned over eighty per cent in favour in France and 96.6 per cent in favour in Algeria, where Muslim Algerians voted on equal terms with whites for the first time; de Gaulle was elected president of the Fifth Republic in December 1958.
✅ **The OAS.** Set up under Salan in 1961, running a terrorist campaign in Algeria and France and repeatedly attempting to assassinate de Gaulle, including in August 1962 after independence; it seized power in Algeria when the Evian talks were announced; de Gaulle appeared on television in full general's uniform and denounced it; the army split and the rebellion collapsed.
✅ **Independence.** Ben Bella, imprisoned since 1956, was released for the Evian talks; independence was agreed for July 1962 and he was elected first president the following year; about 800,000 settlers left in and around 1962 and the new government took over most of their land and businesses. ⚠️ The settler population is given as over a million and the 800,000 is a departure figure; the two quantities are different and must not be presented as one.
✅ **Aftermath, with attribution.** Algerian Muslims who had remained loyal to France, including some 200,000 who had served in the French army, were denounced as traitors; nobody knows how many were executed or murdered, but some estimates put the total as high as 150,000. ❌ No total is asserted.
✅ **The verdict given.** Of all the wars of independence waged against a colonial power this was one of the most bloody; given the intransigence of the settlers, of elements of the rebel army and eventually of the FLN, it is difficult to imagine any other politician handling it better, and the flawed process arguably saved France from civil war.
❌ **Trap:** dating the war from de Gaulle's return. The FLN began at the end of 1954; de Gaulle returned in May 1958.

## 16. The 1958 referendum and neo-colonialism by design

⚠️ **Spine:** where aid is made conditional on a constitutional choice, a referendum measures dependence rather than preference - which is why de Gaulle's plan won eleven votes to one and collapsed within two years once one territory proved the penalty survivable.
✅ **The territories.** French West Africa - Dahomey, Guinea, Ivory Coast, Mauretania, Niger, Senegal, Sudan, Upper Volta. French Equatorial Africa - Chad, Gabon, Middle Congo, Oubangui-Shari. Plus Cameroun and Togo, former German colonies held as mandates from 1919, and Madagascar.
✅ **The doctrine and the sham.** French policy after 1945 was to treat these territories as if they were part of France - a sham, since Africans were not treated on equal terms and moves towards more privileges were opposed by French settlers. ✅ In 1949 the French clamped down on all nationalist movements, arresting leaders and trade unionists and often denouncing them as communist agitators without much evidence.
✅ **The reluctant concession.** Pressure from Indo-China, from the Maghreb and from Britain's preparation of the Gold Coast and Nigeria forced a change; in 1956 the twelve colonies each received self-government for internal affairs, but pressed for full independence.
✅ **The 1958 plan as an instrument.** Self-government with local parliaments; membership of a new French Community; France to take all important decisions on taxation and foreign affairs; economic aid for members; a referendum in each colony; independence available but with no French aid. ✅ De Gaulle was confident none would dare face the future without French help.
✅ **The result.** Of the twelve territories that voted, eleven voted in favour; Guinea under Sekou Toure returned a ninety-five per cent vote against, took independence immediately in 1958 and had all French aid stopped, while the eleven that voted yes became independent during 1960. ✅ Guinea's brave stand encouraged the other eleven plus Togo, Cameroun and Madagascar; all demanded full independence, de Gaulle agreed, and all became independent republics during 1960.
✅ **The design that survived the collapse.** The new independence was not quite so complete as hoped - de Gaulle was intent on neo-colonialism - and all the states except Guinea found France still influenced their economic and foreign policies, with independent action almost out of the question.
✅ **The territories that never left.** Martinique, Guadeloupe and French Guiana were not given independence; they became overseas departements, voting in French elections with representatives in the National Assembly.
⚠️ **Comparison with Britain.** The Commonwealth design survived formally and the French Community collapsed formally, and both metropoles substantially achieved the underlying objective - because the work was done by aid, trade and commodity dependence rather than by the institutional wrapper.
❌ **Trap:** presenting the eleven-to-one vote as evidence of contentment. The penalty attached to one option is the explanation, and Guinea's survival is the proof.

## 17. Indonesia, 1945-65

⚠️ **Spine:** a small metropole defeated by cost rather than in battle; a partial settlement that left a retained province to be fought over for fourteen years; and a Cold War that supplied both the intervention and the permission.
✅ **The holding.** The Dutch East Indies included Sumatra, Java, Celebes, West Irian and about two-thirds of Borneo; the Netherlands also held West Indian islands and Surinam. ✅ The Dutch operated in a way similar to the French in Algeria - growing crops for export and doing very little to improve living standards.
✅ **The Japanese interval.** Nationalist groups had campaigned throughout the 1930s and many leaders, including Sukarno, were arrested; the Japanese invaded in 1942, released Sukarno and others, allowed them a part in the administration and promised independence after the war.
✅ **The republic and the war.** On the Japanese defeat in 1945 Sukarno declared an independent Republic of Indonesia, not expecting resistance from a Netherlands that had itself been defeated and occupied; Dutch troops soon arrived and made determined efforts to regain control.
✅ **Five reasons the Dutch negotiated in 1949.** Crippling expense for a small country; outright victory still a long way off; strong United Nations pressure; the USA and Australia pressing so that they could exert their own influence once exclusive Dutch control ended; and Dutch hopes of preserving the link and maintaining some influence.
✅ **The settlement and its residue.** The Netherlands recognised the independence of the United States of Indonesia in 1949 with Sukarno as president, excluding West Irian; Sukarno accepted a Netherlands-Indonesia Union under the Dutch crown and Dutch troops withdrew. ✅ The following year he broke away from the Union, pressed for West Irian, seized Dutch-owned property and expelled Europeans; in 1963 the Dutch gave way and West Irian became part of Indonesia.
✅ **1965-67, with attribution.** Sukarno was displaced by a right-wing military takeover - executive authority transferred to Suharto by the Supersemar order of 11 March 1966, and the presidency removed by the MPRS in March 1967 - apparently because he was thought too much under the influence of communist China and of the Indonesian Communist Party, the largest communist party outside the USSR and China. ✅ The USA, operating via the CIA, was involved, objecting to his toleration of the Communist Party and to his leadership of the non-aligned and anti-imperialist movements of the Third World. ✅ General Suharto's "New Order" involved a purge of communists during which at least half a million people were murdered and the Communist Party was broken. ✅ The regime had all the hallmarks of a brutal military dictatorship, but there were few protests from the West because in the Cold War atmosphere the anti-communist campaign was perfectly acceptable.
✅ **The rest of the Dutch empire.** Surinam became an independent republic in 1975; the Dutch West Indian islands were treated as part of the Netherlands with some control over internal affairs.
⚠️ **Recorded gap.** The evidence base read for this session gives no casualty figure for the Dutch-Indonesian war of 1945-49. ❌ None is supplied from elsewhere.
❌ **Trap:** writing 1949 as a clean transfer. West Irian was withheld, and the union under the Dutch crown was abandoned within a year.

## 18. Malaya, Malaysia and the successor-state question

⚠️ **Spine:** where the colonised society is plural, decolonisation becomes a contest over the successor state's boundaries and franchise rather than over the coloniser's departure - and a credible independence promise is a counter-insurgency weapon.
✅ **The unit that did not exist.** Malaya was liberated from Japanese occupation in 1945; it was a complex area difficult to organise - nine states each ruled by a sultan, two British settlements at Malacca and Penang, and Singapore, less than a mile from the mainland. ✅ The population was multiracial, mostly Malays and Chinese with some Indians and Europeans, and the economy, based on exports of rubber and tin, was the most prosperous in south-east Asia.
✅ **The 1948 settlement.** The Federation of Malaya grouped the states and settlements while Singapore remained a separate colony; each state kept a legislature for local affairs and the sultans retained some power, but the central government had firm overall control; all adults had the vote, which meant the Malays, the largest group, usually dominated affairs. ⚠️ Demography was written into the constitution, and Singapore's exclusion was part of that arithmetic.
✅ **The insurgency and the counter-insurgency.** Chinese communist guerrillas led by Chin Peng, who had played a leading role in the resistance to the Japanese, stirred up strikes and violence in support of an independent communist state; a state of emergency was declared in 1948 and remained in force until 1960; the tactic was to resettle into specially guarded villages all Chinese suspected of helping the guerrillas. ✅ It was made clear that independence would follow as soon as the country was ready, which ensured the Malays remained firmly pro-British and gave very little help to the communists, who were Chinese.
✅ **The Alliance and independence.** Tunku Abdul Rahman's Malay party joined the main Chinese and Indian groups to form the Alliance Party, which won 51 of 52 seats in the 1955 elections; this seemed to suggest stability and the British were persuaded to grant full independence in 1957, when Malaya was admitted to the Commonwealth.
✅ **The unit revised twice more.** The Tunku proposed in 1961 that Singapore, North Borneo (Sabah), Brunei and Sarawak join Malaya; a United Nations investigation team reported a large majority in favour; the Federation of Malaysia was proclaimed in September 1963; Brunei decided not to join and became independent within the Commonwealth in 1984; Singapore left to become an independent republic in 1965, and the rest of the Federation continued successfully.
✅ **Control case one - the West Indies.** The Federation was set up in 1958, excluding British Honduras and British Guiana, and never functioned successfully; Jamaica voted to leave in September 1961 and Trinidad withdrew in January 1962, and Britain abandoned the federation in 1962, granting independence separately - Jamaica and Trinidad and Tobago in 1962, St Kitts and Nevis last in 1983, with Guyana in 1966 and Belize - renamed from British Honduras in 1973 - independent in 1981; the Caribbean Free Trade Association of 1968 became the Caribbean Community and Common Market in 1973.
✅ **Control case two - Cyprus.** About eighty per cent Greek-speaking Orthodox Christians and the rest Muslims of Turkish origin; enosis demanded and opposed; Eden judged the island necessary as a military base and said it must remain permanently British; Eoka under General Grivas fought the British, who declared an emergency in 1955, deployed about 35,000 troops, deported Makarios and executed terrorists. ✅ Macmillan compromised through Governor Hugh Foot - Makarios dropped enosis, Cyprus received full independence, Turkish interests were safeguarded, Britain retained two military bases and joined Greece and Turkey in guaranteeing independence, and Makarios became president with Fazil Kutchuk as vice-president in 1960. ✅ Civil war broke out in 1963; Turkey sent troops in 1974 and the island has remained divided, Turks holding roughly one-third of its area, with UN troops keeping the peace; a federation proposed by the UN in the mid-1980s was rejected by the Greeks in 1987; checkpoints opened in April 2003; the Republic of Cyprus joined the EU in May 2004 with the island still divided.
❌ **Trap:** calling the Malayan Emergency a purely military success. The decisive instrument was a credible promise of independence that separated the largest community from the insurgency.

## 19. The Congo and Ruanda-Urundi

⚠️ **Spine:** the Congo's crisis was the intended consequence of a stated colonial policy of preventing a successor class, and Ruanda-Urundi's was the transfer of a colonial hierarchy into two sovereign states with no external arbiter left above it.
✅ **The verdict, stated first.** Belgian control of the Congo and Ruanda-Urundi ended in chaos, violence and civil war.
✅ **Method one - education denied.** Africans were denied any advanced education, to prevent contact with nationalist ideas and to deprive them of an educated professional class who could lead them to independence.
✅ **Method two - divide and rule.** Ethnic rivalries were used by playing groups off against each other; this worked well in the huge Congo with about 150 groups, men of one group being used to keep order in another's area, and in Ruanda-Urundi the Tutsi were used to control the Hutu. ✅ Nationalist ideas still filtered in from neighbouring French and British colonies.
✅ **The six months.** January 1959 brought widespread rioting in Leopoldville against unemployment and declining living standards, spreading throughout the country; the Belgians suddenly announced the Congo could become independent in six months. ✅ There were only seventeen graduates in the entire country and no African doctors, lawyers, engineers or army officers; the MNC under Lumumba had existed less than a year; over fifty groups competed; six months was far too short. ✅ Two reasons for the haste: fear of further bloodshed with over 100,000 Belgians in the country at risk, and unwillingness to face the expense of a long anti-guerrilla campaign like Algeria's.
✅ **Collapse.** Independence on 30 June 1960 with Lumumba prime minister and Kasavubu president; within days the army mutinied against having only Belgian officers, and Katanga seceded under Tshombe, encouraged by the Belgian copper-mining industry.
✅ **The international politics.** Lumumba appealed to the UN, which sent a 3,000-strong force; Hammarskjold refused both to attack Katanga and to recognise its independence; the Americans regarded Lumumba as a dangerous communist because of his outspoken socialism; many Belgians preferred an independent Katanga they could influence. ✅ Lumumba appealed to the Russians; Kasavubu, supported by Mobutu and encouraged by the Americans and Belgians, had him arrested; he and two former ministers were badly beaten and then shot in January 1961 by Katangan gendarmes under Belgian officers. ✅ Hammarskjold was killed in an air crash near Ndola, Northern Rhodesia, in September 1961 while flying to meet Tshombe; U Thant continued the same line; by mid-1961 there were 20,000 UN troops; Katanga was invaded in September 1961 and the secession was formally ended on 15 January 1963; UN troops withdrew within months and disorder resumed.
✅ **Mobutu.** In 1965, using white mercenaries and backed by the USA and Belgium, he crushed all resistance and took over; the economy improved after most European-owned mines were nationalised. ✅ In 1977 Shaba was invaded from Angola, encouraged by the Angolan government and the USSR - another extension of the Cold War - and economic difficulties followed from declining copper prices and drought. ✅ In May 1980 Amnesty International claimed at least a thousand political prisoners held without trial and several hundred dead from torture or starvation during 1978-9.
✅ **Citizenship as a colonial residue.** The Nationality Law of 1981 restricted citizenship to those who could show a family connection with the Congo at the time of the Berlin Conference of 1885, aimed at colonial-era migrant workers and later refugees; it was difficult to implement. ✅ In 2005 citizenship was awarded to all descended from groups present at independence in 1960.
✅ **After Mobutu.** He allowed a multi-party system in 1990 and left the country in May 1997, dying in exile in Morocco; Laurent Kabila renamed the country the Democratic Republic of the Congo; by January 2001 it was reported that almost three million people had lost their lives since the civil war began in 1998, most from starvation and disease in the rebel-held east.
✅ **Ruanda-Urundi.** Independent in 1962 as Rwanda and Burundi, both governed by Tutsi as throughout the colonial period; neither was properly prepared; tension and skirmishing began on independence day. ✅ Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language and looked very much alike, the Hutu a majority and the Tutsi the elite ruling group, distinguished by occupation - "rich in cattle" against "servant".
❌ **Trap:** narrating the Congo crisis as Cold War rivalry alone. The vacuum was created by a stated colonial policy before any superpower entered it.

## 20. Portugal, Spain, Italy and the unfinished cases

⚠️ **Spine:** three failure modes - the metropole that could not concede until its own regime fell; the decolonisation reversed by a neighbour with great-power permission; and the decolonisation replaced by partition and never completed.
✅ **Portugal.** It held Angola, Mozambique, Portuguese Guinea and the eastern half of Timor, and Salazar's government blithely ignored nationalist developments elsewhere in Africa. ✅ The colonies were mainly agricultural with few industrial workers and almost entirely illiterate black populations; in 1956 only fifty Africans in the whole of Mozambique had received any secondary education, and the nationalist groups formed that year remained insignificant. ✅ By 1960 nationalists were encouraged by other African independences; the regime stepped up repression, making them more resolute.
✅ **The wars and the coup.** Fighting broke out in Angola in 1961 under Neto's MPLA, spreading to Guinea under Amilcar Cabral and to Mozambique under Mondlane's FRELIMO; all had strong Marxist connections and received economic and military aid from the communist bloc. ✅ The army could not suppress the guerrillas; by 1973 the government was spending forty per cent of its budget on three colonial wars at once; on 25 April 1974 the Estado Novo, by then led by Caetano rather than Salazar, was overthrown by a military coup. ✅ Guinea-Bissau became independent in September 1974, Mozambique and Angola in 1975.
✅ **The regional consequence.** This caused a serious crisis for Rhodesia and South Africa, now the only states left in Africa ruled by white minorities. ✅ South African troops invaded Angola in support of UNITA and Mobutu invaded with American backing in support of the FNLA; the MPLA, with Russian weapons and a Cuban army, defeated both by March 1976 and Neto was accepted as president; South Africa also raided Mozambique to destabilise FRELIMO.
✅ **East Timor - decolonisation reversed.** FRETILIN won a short civil war in September 1975 against the group that wanted to stay with Portugal; the USA denounced the new government as Marxist, which was not entirely accurate; Indonesian troops invaded within weeks and incorporated the territory. ⚠️ **Mortality is given twice and divergently in the same account:** about one hundred thousand killed, one-sixth of the population, with another three hundred thousand in detention camps; and around two hundred thousand killed out of a population of only seven hundred thousand. ❌ Both are reported; neither is preferred and no average is taken.
✅ **Permission and its price.** President Ford gave Suharto the go-ahead because FRETILIN was thought Marxist; East Timor was apparently too small and unimportant and its nationalists too left-wing for the West to apply sanctions; the USA defended Indonesia's claim and played down the violence. ✅ November 1991 - two hundred and seventy-one killed in Dili. ✅ 1996 - Bishop Carlos Belo and Jose Ramos-Horta jointly awarded the Nobel Peace Prize. ✅ August 1999 - a UN-organised referendum returned almost eighty per cent for independence, after which militia backed by Indonesian troops killed two thousand and left two hundred and fifty thousand homeless, and an Australian peacekeeping force ended the violence. ✅ August 2001 Constituent Assembly elections, Xanana Gusmao first president, and international recognition in May 2002 after a struggle of more than a quarter of a century.
✅ **Spain.** Franco showed little interest; Spanish Morocco joined Morocco in 1956, Equatorial Guinea became independent in 1968, Ifni joined Morocco in 1969. ✅ Spanish Sahara was held longest because it was a valuable source of phosphates; after Franco's death in 1975 it was divided between Morocco and Mauretania instead of becoming independent under the Polisario Front. ✅ Polisario under Mohamed Abdelaziz declared the Democratic Arab Republic of Sahara in 1976, recognised by Algeria, Libya, the communist states and India; Mauretania withdrew in 1979. ✅ Soviet recognition aroused American suspicions, and Reagan encouraged Morocco to continue fighting just as it seemed ready to negotiate, stepping up aid - yet another new Third World country becoming a victim of superpower self-interest. ✅ The UN proposed a referendum in 1990 and a ceasefire took effect on 6 September 1991, but the referendum was never held; Polisario weakened as Algerian and Libyan support was withdrawn; Moroccan settlers moved in and Saharans moved out into refugee camps in Algeria.
✅ **Italy.** It lost its empire by decision in 1947; the territories were administered by France and Britain until the UN placed them under governments sympathetic to western interests - Ethiopia already liberated and Haile Selassie restored in 1941 with Italy renouncing its claims in 1947, Libya independent under King Idris in 1951, Eritrea made part of Ethiopia in 1952 with self-government in a federal system, Italian Somaliland merged into Somalia in 1960. ✅ Selassie cancelled Eritrea's rights of self-government in 1962, prompting a war for independence; Idris was overthrown in 1969 and Selassie in 1974; Gaddafi and Mengistu both turned to the USSR.
✅ **The Middle Eastern boundary, stated once.** Britain held Transjordan and Palestine and France held Syria, both intended to prepare them for independence; Syria and Transjordan 1946, the Palestine mandate ended 1948, Libya 1951, Sudan 1956, Aden as South Yemen 1967. ⚠️ Everything beyond that - the creation of Israel, the Arab-Israeli wars, Suez, Egyptian politics - is owned elsewhere.
❌ **Trap:** grouping Portugal with the settler cases. The explanatory variable there is metropolitan regime type, not settler numbers.

## 21. Apartheid I: building a racial order, 1910-1976

⚠️ **Spine:** apartheid codified an existing labour system, was adopted in reaction to decolonisation elsewhere, and contained a contradiction it could never remove - it required daily the presence of the people it was designed to exclude.
✅ **The inheritance.** Dutch settlement at the Cape from 1652; British capture in 1795 confirmed in 1814; slave emancipation throughout the Empire in 1838, prompting the Great Trek and the republics of the Transvaal and Orange Free State between 1835 and 1840; the Boer War of 1899-1902; the Union of South Africa in 1910.
✅ **The Union's arithmetic and law.** About 70 per cent black African, 18 per cent white - about sixty per cent of them Dutch - 9 per cent of mixed race and 3 per cent Asian. ✅ Whites dominated politics and economic life; with few exceptions blacks could not vote; reserved areas were only about 7 per cent of the country, too small to feed their populations or pay taxes, and blacks were forbidden to buy land outside them. ✅ Pass laws tied residence and employment to a named white employer; gold-mining compounds held up to ninety men in a dormitory; a law of 1911 forbade black workers to strike and barred them from skilled jobs.
✅ **The doctrine and its carriers.** Apartheid, meaning separateness, was introduced under Malan (1948-54) and developed by Strijdom (1954-8), Verwoerd (1958-66) and Vorster (1966-78). ✅ The Afrikaner Nationalist Party under Malan claimed whites were a master race; the Dutch Reformed Church supported this with scriptural claims, out of line with the rest of the Christian churches; the Broederbond worked secretly to preserve Afrikaner power; the Nationalists won in 1948 promising to rescue whites from the "black menace" and preserve racial purity.
✅ **The decolonisation trigger.** When India and Pakistan were given independence in 1947, white South Africans became alarmed at growing racial equality within the Commonwealth and were determined to preserve their supremacy.
✅ **The five features.** Complete separation as far as possible - reserves, townships, communities uprooted and re-grouped, separate buses, coaches, trains, cafes, toilets, park benches, hospitals, beaches, picnic areas, sports and churches, and separate schools with a much inferior education. Racial classification with an identity card for every person, strict pass laws and no travel without police permission. Marriage and sexual relations between whites and non-whites forbidden, with police surveillance. The Bantu Self-Government Act of 1959 creating seven Bantustans - Transkei self-governing in 1963 and declared "independent" on 26 October 1976, Bophuthatswana and Venda by 1980 - about 13 per cent of the country for over 8 million people, vastly overcrowded and little better than rural slums, dismissed by the outside world with contempt because Pretoria still controlled Transkei's economy and foreign affairs. And the loss by Africans of all political rights, with their parliamentary representation by white MPs abolished.
✅ **The contradiction, in the source's own terms.** Complete separation was impossible because over half the non-white population worked in white-owned mines, factories and businesses, and the economy would have collapsed if all non-whites had been moved to reserves; virtually every white household had at least two African servants.
✅ **Resistance, and how repression changed its form.** Opposition inside South Africa was punished under the Suppression of Communism Act, Africans were forbidden to strike and the ANC was helpless. ✅ Luthuli organised stay-away protests; the 1952 defiance of the laws brought over 8,000 arrests and floggings, and Luthuli lost his chieftaincy and was jailed. ✅ At Kliptown in 1955 the Freedom Charter declared that "South Africa belongs to all who live in it, black and white", demanding equality before the law, freedoms of assembly, movement, speech, religion and press, the vote, the right to work with equal pay, a forty-hour week, a minimum wage, unemployment benefit, free medical care and free compulsory equal education. ✅ Trevor Huddleston and other church figures spoke out; the 1957 bus boycott sustained a ten-mile walk to work for three months until fares were reduced. ✅ At Sharpeville in 1960 police fired on an anti-pass-law demonstration, killing 69 and wounding many, with 15,000 arrests following; the brutality convinced many black leaders that violence could only be met with violence. ✅ Umkhonto we Sizwe, with Mandela prominent, sabotaged strategic targets with bomb attacks in Johannesburg, Port Elizabeth and Durban in 1961; Mandela received life imprisonment on Robben Island; Luthuli continued non-violent protest, published *Let My People Go* and was awarded the Nobel Peace Prize. ✅ At Soweto in 1976 massive demonstrations against Afrikaans as the language of instruction in black schools were met with police killing at least 200, with protests spreading nationwide and a further 500 killed over six months; Steve Biko, who encouraged young people to be proud of their blackness, was beaten to death by police in 1977.
❌ **Trap:** dating racial subordination from 1948. The land, pass and labour law of 1910-11 came first; 1948 systematised it.

## 22. Apartheid II: pressure, negotiation and after, 1976-94

⚠️ **Spine:** apartheid survived on the gap between universal condemnation and uninterrupted trade, and ended when regional decolonisation, demographic change, homeland failure, sanctions with a cost and the defection of its own church closed that gap - while the economic structure it had enforced outlived the political system by a considerable margin.
✅ **The words.** Macmillan's "wind of change" speech was delivered in Cape Town early in 1960; his warnings were ignored and Sharpeville followed shortly afterwards. ✅ At the 1961 Commonwealth Conference criticism was intense; Verwoerd withdrew South Africa's application for continued membership after it became a republic in 1960, and it ceased to be a member. ✅ The UN and the OAU condemned apartheid and were particularly critical of the continued occupation of South West Africa; the UN voted an economic boycott in 1962 which proved useless because not all member states supported it.
✅ **The trade.** Britain, the USA, France, West Germany and Italy condemned apartheid in public but continued to trade and sold massive arms supplies, apparently hoping South Africa would be a bastion against communism in Africa; Verwoerd and Vorster could therefore ignore outside protests until well into the 1970s.
✅ **Botha's reform, and its causes.** Elected in 1979, he decided to reform apartheid by dropping some unpopular aspects in an attempt to preserve white control. ✅ Causes: mounting Commonwealth, UN and OAU criticism; the independence of Angola and Mozambique in 1975 and the African takeover of Zimbabwe in 1980, leaving South Africa surrounded by hostile black states; recession in the late 1970s with whites emigrating and falling from 21 per cent of the population between the wars to 16 per cent by 1980; the failure of the homelands; and American criticism. ✅ Botha, September 1979: "A revolution in South Africa is no longer just a remote possibility. Either we adapt or we perish. White domination and legally enforced apartheid are a recipe for permanent conflict."
✅ **The reforms.** Trade unions and strikes for blacks in 1979; township councils but no national vote in 1981; a tricameral constitution for whites, coloureds and Asians but not Africans, weighted for white control, in force in 1984; mixed marriage and sexual relations allowed in 1985; pass laws abolished in 1986. ✅ Botha would not even consider the ANC's main demands - the right to vote and to play a full part in ruling the country - and far from being won over, black Africans were incensed that the new constitution made no provision for them.
✅ **Escalation.** Violence grew with both sides guilty of excesses; the ANC used the necklace against black councillors and police regarded as collaborators; police killed over forty mourners near Uitenhage in March 1985; a state of emergency was declared in July 1985 and extended nationwide in June 1986, with arrest without warrant, freedom from criminal proceedings and a ban on reporting demonstrations and strikes. ⚠️ The account's own generalisation: as so often happens when an authoritarian regime tries to reform itself, it proved impossible to stop the process of change - the same happened in the USSR under Gorbachev.
✅ **Sanctions that bit, and a dispute worth reporting.** August 1986 - the Commonwealth except Britain agreed no further loans, no sales of oil, computer equipment or nuclear goods, and no cultural or scientific contacts; Thatcher committed Britain only to a voluntary investment ban, arguing severe sanctions would cost black Africans their jobs, and Rajiv Gandhi accused her of compromising on basic principles and values for economic ends. ✅ September 1986 - the US Congress voted over Reagan's veto to stop American loans, cut air links and ban imports of iron, steel, coal, textiles and uranium.
✅ **Internal defection.** A steadily growing black professional middle class had emerged, including Desmond Tutu, Nobel Peace Prize in 1984 and Anglican archbishop of Cape Town in 1986; the Dutch Reformed Church, which had once supported apartheid, now condemned it as incompatible with Christianity.
✅ **The negotiation.** De Klerk, elected in 1989, privately decided apartheid must go completely; Mandela was released in 1990 after twenty-seven years and the ANC was legalised; most remaining apartheid laws were dropped; Namibia became independent under a black government in 1990; constitutional talks began in 1991. ✅ The ANC presented itself as moderate with no plans for wholesale nationalisation and reassured whites, and Mandela condemned violence and called for reconciliation. ✅ De Klerk faced bitter opposition from right-wing Afrikaner groups and from his own National Party, while the ANC was in a power struggle with the Zulu Inkatha Freedom Party under Chief Buthelezi. ✅ A power-sharing scheme was agreed in spring 1993; the ANC won almost two-thirds of the votes; a coalition of ANC, National Party and Inkatha took office with Mandela as president in 1994 - majority rule without civil war. ✅ The Truth and Reconciliation Commission, assisted by Tutu, granted amnesties rather than taking revenge; Mandela, jailed twenty-seven years, still believed in forgiveness and reconciliation, and retired in 1999.
✅ **Delivery.** 70 per cent of black households with electricity; access to pure water up by nine million since 1994; about two thousand new houses for poor people; free compulsory education; exports diversified away from gold; a growing tourist industry; the budget deficit sharply down; inflation at 4 per cent.
✅ **What did not end.** A soaring crime rate; union strikes against job losses and privatisation; growth of 1.5 per cent in 2001 against 3.1 in 2000; contested handling of AIDS, with 6.5 million HIV positive people reported in 2005; migration from Zimbabwe, Rwanda, the Congo, Somalia and Ethiopia; anti-immigrant riots in May 2008 killing at least eighty; left-wing ANC criticism of too little redistribution; unemployment at 25 per cent in May 2009 with the jobless living on less than US$1.25 a day. ✅ August 2012 at Marikana - police shot and killed thirty-four striking platinum miners demanding wage increases from a British mine-owning company, and two hundred and seventy miners were arrested and charged with their colleagues' murder before the charges were dropped.
❌ **Trap:** attributing the end of apartheid to sanctions alone. Sanctions mattered from 1986; the regional collapse of 1975-80, the demographic slide, the homelands' failure and the church's defection are all part of the same account.

## 23. After independence: the inheritance and what was done with it

⚠️ **Spine:** the inheritance set the constraints and the choices made inside them determined the outcomes - which is why Ghana, the best-prepared state, failed in 1966, and why Nigeria, having fought a civil war, recovered remarkably swiftly by redesigning its federation from three regions into nineteen states.
✅ **Problem one - ethnic difference.** Groups held together only by colonial rulers, with little incentive to stay together once the Europeans withdrew; in Nigeria, the Congo, Burundi and Rwanda this led to civil war.
✅ **Problem two - economic under-development.** Very little industry as a deliberate colonial policy, colonies being required to buy manufactures from Europe or the USA and to supply food and raw materials; dependence on one or two export commodities, so that a price fall was a major disaster, with Nigeria's oil producing about eighty per cent of annual income; shortage of capital and skills; population growth over two per cent a year; debt; export-led repayment making food scarcer at home; and dependence on western markets and investment enabling control - neo-colonialism.
✅ **Problem three - political inexperience.** Unfamiliarity with parliamentary systems; corruption; one-party states influenced by Marxist ideas, which worked well in Kenya and Tanzania; where legal opposition was impossible, violence was the only answer and military coups became common - Nkrumah was removed by the army in 1966 after two failed assassination attempts, and in Malawi the one-party system flourished at the expense of freedom.
✅ **Problem four - disasters.** The world recession of the 1980s cutting demand for oil, copper and cobalt; drought from 1982 to 1985 with crop failures, livestock deaths, famine and starvation; record harvests in 1986; a severe debt crisis; and IMF-imposed ESAP austerity with devaluation, reduced food subsidies, higher food prices, rising unemployment, falling wages and cuts to education, health and social services.
✅ **Ghana.** Achievements - cocoa production doubled; forestry, fishing and cattle-breeding expanded; gold and bauxite better exploited; the Volta dam begun in 1961 for irrigation, hydro-electricity and bauxite smelting; village road and school projects; international prestige with Commonwealth membership alongside links with the USSR, East Germany and China. Failures - too-rapid industrialisation on vast foreign borrowing; continued cocoa dependence and a steep price fall producing a huge balance-of-payments deficit; the smelter's American owner buying bauxite abroad; wasteful projects. Political closure - the Preventive Detention Act of 1958 and detention or deportation without trial from 1959, Danquah arrested in 1961 and dying in prison, all other parties banned in 1964, the "father of the nation" cult, the belief that he had amassed a personal fortune, and the army's coup in 1966 during a visit to China, with American encouragement because of his links with communist states, which the textbook account calls full CIA backing though the claim is contested. After - Busia's fall in January 1972 with debt repayments using about forty per cent of export profits and a near-fifty-per-cent devaluation in 1971; Acheampong, Akuffo, Rawlings from June 1979, Limann, 125 per cent inflation in 1981, Rawlings again from December 1981; IMF austerity, seven per cent production growth and forty per cent inflation by early 1985; Rawlings elected in 1992 with over 58 per cent and re-elected in 1996; Kufuor from January 2001; the Ghana School Feeding Programme from 2005; Mills's narrow win in 2008; and Ghana regarded as one of Africa's most stable and successful democracies.
✅ **Nigeria and Biafra.** Britain and the USSR armed Gowon, France armed Biafra; Biafra lost more civilians from disease and starvation than troops killed in fighting; the UN, the Commonwealth and the OAU could not mediate; surrender came in January 1970 with Nigerian unity preserved. ✅ Recovery was remarkably swift - no revenge-taking, Gowon reconciling the Ibos, and a federal system expanded from twelve states to nineteen. ✅ Later - oil dependence producing a 1983 deficit, coups in 1983 and 1985, oil at a record low of $10 a barrel in June 1986 against a budget assuming $23.50, over $12 billion of oil revenues reported missing under Babangida, Obasanjo from May 1999, UN figures of 66-70 per cent in poverty by 2004 against 48.5 per cent in 1998, and over $250 billion earned from oil in thirty years with very little benefit to ordinary people.
✅ **Tanzania.** Tanganyika independent in 1961, joined by Zanzibar in 1964; one of the poorest states in Africa, with little industry, few minerals and heavy dependence on coffee, but fewer ethnic problems and Swahili as a common bond. ✅ The Arusha Declaration of 1967 - equality; state control of the means of production; no great accumulations of wealth; elimination of bribery and corruption; victory through hard work and self-reliance rather than money and foreign aid; and self-sufficiency in food as the first priority. ✅ Instruments - nationalisation of all important enterprises including foreign-owned ones, five-year plans, ujamaa village co-operatives, minimised foreign loans, investment and imports, and a one-party state under TANU but with two TANU candidates per constituency and large numbers of MPs losing their seats. ✅ Results reported in December 1986 - 3.7 million children in primary school, two universities with over 4,500 students, 85 per cent literacy, 150 hospitals and 2,600 dispensaries, infant mortality down to 137 per thousand and life expectancy 52. ✅ Failures - corruption, insufficient agricultural investment, the sisal nationalisation reversed in May 1985, falling coffee and tea prices from late 1978, oil prices using almost half of export earnings, the Uganda war costing at least £1,000 million, and IMF interest repayments; neither socialist nor self-sufficient. ✅ The verdict - the experiment might have worked in a closed economy, but Tanzania was exposed to the vagaries of the world economy.
✅ **Angola.** Three movements - the MPLA under Neto appealing across ethnic divisions, UNITA under Savimbi based on the Ovimbundu, and the weaker FNLA on the Bakongo; American advisers, cash and armaments to the FNLA; Cuban troops for the MPLA; a South African invasion via Namibia; Mobutu's troops from Zaire. ✅ The verdict - there would have been fighting and bloodshed anyway, but outside interference and the extension of the Cold War certainly made the conflict much worse. ✅ Namibia, German South West Africa handed to South Africa in 1919 to be prepared for independence, was withheld; SWAPO under Sam Nujoma fought a guerrilla campaign with bases in southern Angola after 1975; the UN settlement of December 1988 traded South African withdrawal from Namibia against the departure of the 50,000 Cuban troops; Namibia became independent in 1990, Cuban troops were home by June 1991, and the Lisbon accords followed in May 1991. ✅ September 1992 elections - MPLA 58 per cent and 129 seats, UNITA 31 per cent and 70 seats, dos Santos 49.57 per cent against Savimbi's 40.07 per cent, with the UN team reporting the election generally free and fair; UNITA renewed the war, which was clearly its fault, though Reagan's official meeting with Savimbi and the delay of American recognition of the MPLA until May 1993 had encouraged it; by the end of January 1994 the UN reported 3.3 million refugees and about a thousand deaths a day, mainly civilians; Savimbi financed the war with illicit diamond sales until he was killed in February 2002. ✅ Costs, each with its reporting body - inflation 240 per cent; the IMF recording over $4 billion of oil receipts missing since 1996; Human Rights Watch reporting 86,000 UNITA child soldiers and 3,000 in government forces; some 15 million landmines laid; and about four million displaced and 1.5 million killed on a calculation reported in 2002.
✅ **Rwanda and Burundi.** Tutsi and Hutu spoke the same language and looked very much alike, differing by occupation - "rich in cattle" against "servant" - with a Tutsi elite and a Hutu majority; tension began on independence day in 1962 and the Belgians left both states completely unprepared. ✅ Burundi - the first democratic elections in 1993 produced a Hutu president, murdered by Tutsi soldiers in October 1993; reprisal killings followed and around fifty thousand Tutsi were killed, on the textbook account's unattributed figure; the OAU's first-ever peacekeeping deployment could not stop the massacres; the great powers showed little concern because their interests were not involved and the conflict received little media coverage; the Arusha agreement of October 2001 followed Nyerere's and Mandela's mediation, and a ceasefire with the last militias came in 2006 under Nkurunziza, with rights for women and children and free primary education. ✅ Rwanda - fighting from 1990 between the Tutsi-dominated FPR based in Uganda and the Hutu-dominated army; an Arusha settlement in 1993 with 2,500 UN troops; the aircraft carrying the Rwandan and Burundian presidents brought down by a missile in April 1994; the Interahamwe launching a campaign of genocide; about 800,000 Tutsi and moderate Hutu murdered in a deliberate and carefully planned attempt to wipe out the entire Tutsi population, backed by the Hutu government - the figure as stated by the textbook account, with no producing body or counting period named - with churches used as death traps; the small UN force not equipped and withdrawing; and about a million Tutsi refugees fleeing to Tanzania and Zaire. ✅ Linda Melvern showed the warning signs were ignored - Belgium and France knew what was planned, the Belgian ambassador reporting it in spring 1992, France continued supplying arms during the genocide, the OAU did not even condemn it and no African state acted or issued public condemnation. ✅ After - power-sharing under Bizimungu then Kagame; over 100,000 awaiting trial with about 40,000 released in January 2003; a 2003 constitution capping any party at half the seats and outlawing incitement of ethnic hatred; a 2004 reconciliation policy; corruption and crime reduced; per capita income doubled between 2000 and 2008; primary education rising from twenty per cent of children to almost half; Commonwealth membership in 2009; and Kagame re-elected in August 2010 amid killings of opposition supporters and journalists and limits on press freedom, with UN, EU and US concern.
✅ **Zimbabwe.** Moderation, reconciliation, a ZANU-ZAPU coalition, the twenty guaranteed white seats honoured, wage increases, food subsidies and better social services, health care and education; then Nkomo's forced resignation in 1982, brutal suppression in Matabeleland, and the 1987 Unity Accord creating ZANU-PF with Mugabe as executive president and the white seats abolished. ✅ Economy - a breadbasket in good years but weather-dependent, with 1980s droughts and high oil prices, IMF loans and ESAP cuts against public opinion after the USSR's collapse, and severe drought in 1992. ✅ Land - about 4,000 white farmers owned about half the arable land; squatter occupations proceeded with government encouragement and no police protection; from February 2000 the occupation was systematic, violent and clearly organised by the government; Mugabe blamed Britain for breaking a Lancaster House compensation promise, and Britain offered extra compensation provided the land went to ordinary peasant farmers rather than Mugabe's elite and the June 2000 elections were free and fair. ✅ Politics - a pro-Mugabe draft constitution was rejected in February 2000; the June 2000 election saw widespread violence and intimidation, ZANU-PF winning 62 of 150 seats against the MDC's 57, with 30 presidential nominees preserving a majority; the MDC drew support from trade unions and the mainly white Commercial Farmers' Union; EU sanctions followed in February 2002, with Commonwealth suspension for a year and World Bank funding cut over arrears above $380 million; Mbeki called the seizures a violation of the rule of law but refused sanctions; most black African states supported Mugabe; press and judicial independence were curtailed; and Mugabe won 56 per cent in March 2002 against Tsvangirai's 42 per cent.
❌ **Trap:** writing the post-independence record as determined by the inheritance. Nigeria's recovery and Ghana's reversal are both inside the same inheritance.

## 24. Neo-colonialism, the verdict debate and answer architecture

⚠️ **Spine:** decolonisation reconstituted the international system and did not redistribute economic power, and an answer must hold both halves at once.
✅ **Neo-colonialism recorded three times.** As a British expectation of influence through trade links preserved by Commonwealth membership; as de Gaulle's stated intention in 1958-60; and as the structural dependence on western markets and investment that enabled control over African governments. ⚠️ **Definition:** effective influence over the economic and foreign policy of a formally sovereign former colony, exercised by economic means through dependence on markets, investment, aid, manufactures and debt. ✅ Nkrumah's own formulation, from his book on the subject: "For those who practise neo-colonialism, it means power without responsibility and for those who suffer from it, it means exploitation without redress."
✅ **What decolonisation did deliver.** Much more control over what went on inside their frontiers; advances in education and social services; a political culture which allowed them to vote. ✅ In 1946 there were 74 nation-states; in 1995, thanks to decolonisation, there were 192.
✅ **The case against empire, with its named evidence.** Brendon - the British Empire's real purpose was to increase Britain's wealth and power, and its coercive and exploitative nature had to be disguised, the same applying to other empires which were less good at disguising it. Orwell - empire was "a despotism with theft as its final object". Russell - "a cesspool for British moral refuse", meaning many administrators were racist bullies. Brendon again - the history of India is punctuated by famines causing tens of millions of deaths, and in the Bengal famine of 1942-3 Churchill refused to divert shipping to Calcutta and over three million died of starvation. Further debit items - thousands of Aborigines and Maoris slaughtered; in the Boer War of 1899-1902 British concentration camps in which about one-sixth of the entire Boer population died; and swift and disproportionate retribution in Afghanistan, Ceylon, Jamaica, Burma, Kenya and Iraq. ✅ Richard Gott in 2012 presents a long catalogue of crimes against humanity - slavery, famine, prison, repression, battles, massacre, devastation and extermination - and this is described as probably the majority view.
✅ **Ferguson's defence, stated fairly.** Writing in 2003, he holds that Britain pioneered free trade, free capital movements and, with the abolition of slavery, free labour; developed global communications; spread law and order; maintained a global peace unmatched before or since; and left liberal capitalism, parliamentary institutions and the English language, proving that empire is a form of international government which can work.
✅ **The limits, which are the hinge.** Neo-colonialism; bad or absent preparation with artificial frontiers; only three secondary schools for three million Africans in Nyasaland and not one industrial factory; Portugal deliberately destroying installations and machinery on leaving Mozambique; governments run by local political elites with no social revolution; and intense poverty resting on one or two commodities with heavy debt. ✅ Ellen M. Wood - the new political rights and citizenship were essentially passive, and the whole point of the strategy is to put formal political rights in place of social rights and to put as much of social life as possible out of the reach of democratic accountability. ✅ Intervention against socialist or pro-communist governments in Indo-China, Indonesia, East Timor, Chad, Angola, Mozambique, Zaire and Jamaica, including Ford's go-ahead to Suharto over East Timor. ✅ Africa was the only area of the world where, in 1987, incomes were on average lower than in 1972.
✅ **The conclusion, and Hall's formulations.** So many limitations were placed on independence that the result was to divide people's political rights from any chance of expressing their rights in social and economic affairs; Anthony J. Hall calls this "the great betrayal of humanity's democratic promise", and holds that class exploitation and colonial exploitation are two sides of the same coin, favouring small local oligarchies rather than broad-ranging liberation.
⚠️ **The five master theses, for recall in fifteen seconds each.** Nationalism usually worked by cost imposition rather than by military victory. The route was set by the settler, strategic and successor variables rather than by the coloniser's identity. The decision was taken in Europe while the price was set in the colony, after which collective international action generalised the norm. The inheritance set the constraints and the choices made inside them determined the outcomes. Political decolonisation was rapid and economic decolonisation was not.
⚠️ **Directive discipline.** Match the thesis to the demand - a "why" demand takes the cost-imposition or route thesis, a "compare" demand takes the route typology with its sorting question, an "assess" or "examine" demand on consequences takes the inheritance-and-choices thesis, and any demand touching independence's value takes the two-halves thesis with Ferguson stated fairly before it is limited.
❌ **Trap:** writing the verdict as a verdict on the British alone, or resolving it. The account supplies a balance sheet with a hinge, and the hinge is whether political rights reached social and economic life.
